“Wooed and married and a’.”

“Edith!” Margaret called softly, her voice barely above a whisper. But as she had half expected, Edith was already asleep—curled up like a contented cat on the crimson damask sofa in the back drawing-room of 96 Harley Street. She looked ethereal in her white muslin dress, tied with delicate blue ribbons, her silken curls spilling over the armrests. If Titania had ever been dressed in such finery and fallen into a peaceful slumber in a London parlor, she might have passed for this sleeping beauty. Margaret paused, struck anew by her cousin’s grace and charm. They had grown up together, side by side, and though everyone else had long admired Edith’s loveliness, Margaret had never truly noticed it—until now. With Edith’s marriage just days away, every feature, every quiet gesture seemed to glow with a deeper significance.

They had been talking—whispering, really—about wedding dresses and ceremonies, about Captain Lennox and his tales of life in Corfu, where his regiment was stationed. They’d discussed the challenge of keeping a piano in tune (a concern Edith treated as one of the greatest perils of married life), and what gowns she would need for their immediate trip to Scotland after the wedding. But the conversation had grown sleepy, the voices hushed, until finally Edith had drifted off, wrapped in soft muslin and dreams.

Margaret had been about to share some thoughts of her own—visions of her future life at Helstone parsonage, where her parents lived, and where she had spent all her happiest holidays. Though she had lived with her aunt Shaw for the past ten years, Helstone remained her true home, the place where she felt most herself. Now, however, with Edith leaving soon, that sense of belonging was tinged with melancholy. The thought of returning there alone stirred both joy and sorrow—joy at the prospect of simplicity, peace, and family; sorrow at the thought of parting from her gentle aunt and dear cousin.

From the next room came fragments of conversation. Mrs. Shaw was speaking with the usual circle of neighbors—women she called friends not out of deep affection, but because they dined together often enough to form a routine. Their husbands still lingered in the dining room, enjoying Mr. Grey’s wit, the man who always sat at the bottom of the table and whose jokes were as predictable as they were popular.

Mrs. Shaw had invited them for a farewell dinner in honor of Edith’s upcoming marriage—a gesture Edith had hesitated over. Captain Lennox was expected to arrive late that evening, and she had wished to wait until he was there. But Edith, though indulged and spoiled, lacked strong will. When her mother insisted—especially when she ordered those seasonal delicacies said to soothe grief—Edith gave in. She merely leaned back in her chair, played with her food, and stared ahead with a look of distant seriousness, while others laughed at Mr. Grey’s latest anecdote.

Then came the request: “Edith, my dear, would you play something for us?”

But Edith did not stir.

And so, without warning, Mrs. Shaw called again—this time louder, more urgently: “Edith! Edith!”

She paused, breathless, as if the effort had exhausted her.

Margaret stepped forward. “She’s asleep, Aunt Shaw. Is there anything I can do?”

A chorus of sympathy followed. “Poor child!” they murmured. Even Tiny, the lapdog curled in Mrs. Shaw’s arms, began to bark, as if disturbed by the sudden wave of pity.

“Hush, Tiny,” Mrs. Shaw said gently. “You naughty little thing—you’ll wake your mistress.” Then, turning to Margaret: “Would you mind asking Newton to bring down the shawls? Perhaps you could go, dear?”

Margaret climbed the stairs to the old nursery at the top of the house—the same room where she had first arrived nine years before, a wild child from the country, thrust into London life. Newton was busy arranging laces for the wedding. As she waited, Margaret looked around. This was the first room in the house she had known, dim and quiet, presided over by a strict nurse who demanded clean hands and perfect behavior. She remembered her first tea here—alone, high above her father and aunt, who dined far below, seemingly miles away in the depths of the house. At home, her mother’s dressing room had been her nursery; meals had been shared with her parents, life simple and close-knit.

Now, as an eighteen-year-old girl, she recalled the night she had wept beneath the bedclothes, afraid to cry too loudly, afraid to disturb Miss Edith. And how the nurse had scolded her, how her father had come upstairs quietly, how her aunt had comforted her—softly, gently—until she had stopped sobbing, pretending to sleep so as not to sadden her father.

Now, the room stood empty, stripped of its former liveliness. Yet Margaret loved it. She looked around with a quiet sadness, knowing she would leave it forever in three days.

“Ah, Newton,” she said, “I think we’ll all miss this old room.”

Newton grumbled under her breath. “Indeed, miss, I shan’t. My eyes aren’t what they used to be, and the light’s poor. I can only mend laces by the window—and even then, it’s cold enough to freeze a man’s bones.”

“Well,” Margaret replied, “you’ll have better light and warmth in Naples. Keep your mending till then. Thank you—I’ll take the shawls down.”

She descended the stairs, wrapped in the rich scent of Indian silk—spicy, warm, exotic. Her aunt asked her to stand as a model, since Edith was still asleep. No one questioned it. Margaret, tall and slender in her black mourning dress, stood beneath the chandelier, silent and still, while her aunt draped the shawls over her shoulders. The folds fell like water, shimmering with gold and crimson, far too grand for Edith’s delicate frame.

Occasionally, she caught sight of herself in the mirror—her familiar face, calm and composed, framed by the luxurious fabric. She smiled faintly, enjoying the moment—not as a queen, but as a girl delighted by a fleeting splendor.

Then the door opened.

Mr. Henry Lennox was announced.

The ladies started slightly, as if caught in a moment of girlish excess. Mrs. Shaw greeted him warmly, but Margaret remained still, expecting to be needed again as a shawl holder. Instead, she met his gaze with a quiet amusement—knowing he would see the absurdity of the situation.

Her aunt was instantly absorbed in questions about the bridegroom, the bridesmaid, the Lennox family—all the details of the coming union. Margaret, no longer required, turned her attention to the other guests.

Just then, Edith appeared from the back drawing-room—blinking, yawning, her curls slightly tousled. She looked like a fairy awakened from a dream. Even in sleep, she had sensed the arrival of someone important.

She rushed into the room, eager to question her brother-in-law-to-be about Janet, the sister-in-law from Scotland, whom she adored with such intensity that Margaret couldn’t help but feel a flicker of envy—though she wouldn’t admit it.

As Edith engaged Henry Lennox in conversation, Margaret slipped into the background. She saw him glance toward a vacant chair near her, and knew he would claim it as soon as he could. She hadn’t been sure he’d come tonight—her aunt’s account of his plans had been vague. His arrival was a surprise, and a welcome one.

He liked the same things she did—books, music, honest talk. He didn’t pretend to care about fashion or ceremony.

When he finally sat beside her, she smiled—openly, without hesitation.

“Well,” he said, “I suppose you’re all buried in ladies’ business. Very different from my work—real law, real paperwork. Playing with shawls is nothing compared to drafting settlements.”

“Oh, I knew you’d find this amusing,” she replied. “But really, Indian shawls are beautiful. Perfect, even.”

“Perfect in price, too,” he said, laughing. “No flaws there.”

The gentlemen trickled in, the noise rising.

“This is your last dinner party, isn’t it?” someone asked. “No more before Thursday?”

“No,” Mrs. Shaw replied. “After tonight, I’ll finally breathe easy. All the arrangements are done. I’ll have time to think—something I haven’t had in weeks. And I’m sure Edith will, too.”

“I’m not so sure about her,” Henry said. “But I can imagine you will. You’ve been swept along by other people’s whirlwinds lately.”

“Yes,” Margaret said, a touch of weariness in her voice. “I wonder if every marriage must be preceded by such chaos. Or if sometimes—just sometimes—there could be calm instead.”

“Cinderella’s godmother planning the trousseau, writing invitations, organizing the breakfast,” Henry said, laughing. “But are all these necessary?”

“Of course,” he added, growing serious. “There are forms to go through—not for ourselves, but to satisfy the world. Without them, life feels incomplete. But how would you plan a wedding?”

“I’ve never thought much about it,” she said. “But I’d want a fine summer morning. A walk to church through trees. Few bridesmaids. No breakfast. I suppose I’m rejecting everything that’s caused me trouble lately.”

“No,” he said, “you’re not. The idea of stately simplicity suits you well.”

She winced slightly. She didn’t like being analyzed—especially when he praised her character in a way that felt like flattery disguised as insight.

She cut him short. “It’s natural to think of Helstone church, walking through the woods, rather than driving up to a London church in the middle of a paved street.”

“Tell me about Helstone,” he said. “You’ve never described it to me. I’d like to know what you’ll be living in, when 96 Harley Street feels dull and shut up.”

“Oh, it’s just a hamlet,” she said. “Not even a village. Just a church and a few cottages on the green—roses climbing over the walls.”

“And flowering all year round, especially at Christmas,” he added, teasing.

“No,” she said, annoyed. “I’m not making a picture. I’m describing reality. You shouldn’t say that.”

“I’m sorry,” he said. “But it *does* sound like a scene from a story.”

“And so it is,” she replied, warming. “All the places I’ve seen seem hard and plain after the New Forest. Helstone feels like a poem—like Tennyson’s poetry. But I won’t try to describe it further. You’d only laugh.”

“I wouldn’t,” he said. “But you’re determined not to tell me.”

“You’ve made me angry,” she said. “So I won’t tell you about Helstone—or my home.”

“But you could,” he said gently. “Tell me what you do there. Here, you read, study, walk. What will you do in Helstone? Ride? Drive? Walk?”

“Walk,” she said firmly. “We don’t have a horse—even for Papa. He walks to the farthest end of his parish. The paths are so beautiful—it would be a shame to drive.”

“What about gardening? That’s proper for young ladies in the country.”

“I don’t know,” she said. “I’m afraid it’s too hard.”

“Archery parties? Picnics? Hunt balls?”

“Oh no,” she laughed. “Papa’s income is small. Even if we were near such things, I doubt I’d go.”

“I see,” he said. “You won only tell me what you won’t do.”

“I’ll tell you when you visit,” she said. “Before vacation ends, I hope you’ll come. Then you’ll see for yourself how beautiful Helstone is.”

She rose. “Now I must go. Edith is playing, and I know just enough music to turn the pages.”

Edith played brilliantly. Halfway through the piece, the door creaked open. Captain Lennox stood there, uncertain whether to enter.

Edith dropped her music and rushed out, leaving Margaret standing, blushing, trying to explain to the astonished guests what had startled her cousin.

Captain Lennox had arrived earlier than expected—or perhaps it was simply very late. Watches were consulted, expressions shocked, and the guests took their leave.

Then Edith returned, glowing, leading in her tall, handsome fiancé. His brother shook hands, Mrs. Shaw welcomed him with her usual kindness—soft, gentle, yet tinged with a quiet sorrow, born of years spent feeling trapped in an unloving marriage.

Now, with the General gone, she had wealth and comfort, yet still found reasons to worry—most recently, her health. A nervous cough, a doctor’s prescription, a winter in Italy. She preferred to believe she was obeying necessity, not desire. It allowed her to complain softly, to mourn her fate, while secretly delighting in the journey.

She began to speak of her plans to Captain Lennox, who nodded politely, his eyes fixed on Edith, who was busily re-arranging the tea-table, ordering up cakes and sweets despite his protests that he had eaten recently.

Henry Lennox leaned against the chimney-piece, watching the scene with quiet amusement. He was plain in a family of good looks, but his face was sharp, intelligent, alive. Margaret sometimes wondered what he was thinking—what thoughts passed behind those observant eyes.

He watched Edith, who insisted on doing everything herself, proud to show her lover how capable she was. She discovered the urn’s water was cold and sent for the kitchen kettle. When it arrived, she tried to carry it in—but it was too heavy. She stumbled in, pouting, a smudge on her gown, her hand marked by the handle. She showed it to Captain Lennox like a child with a bruise—and, naturally, the remedy was the same: Margaret lit the spirit-lamp.

It worked perfectly. Not romantic, not like a gypsy camp—yet practical, efficient.

After that evening, the house became a hive of activity—preparing, packing, planning—until the wedding day arrived.

“By the soft green light in the woody glade,
On the banks of moss where thy childhood played,
By the household tree, thro’ which thine eye
First looked in love to the summer sky.”

Margaret Hale sat quietly beside her father in the railway carriage, dressed simply for the journey home. The wedding at Harley Street had ended, and now they were returning to Helstone parsonage, where peace and familiar routines awaited them. Her mother had stayed behind, citing a host of vague excuses—none of which anyone truly understood, except Mr. Hale himself, who knew full well that his wife had refused to wear the grey satin gown he’d suggested, halfway between new and old, because she lacked the means for a fresh wardrobe. And so, with no new dress to wear on such a special occasion, Mrs. Hale had chosen not to attend at all.

If Mrs. Shaw had known the real reason, she might have sent gowns by the dozen. But it had been nearly twenty years since Mrs. Shaw was the pretty Miss Beresford, and time had softened her memory of past hardships. Now, her only lingering complaint was the sorrow of marrying someone older—a grievance she could talk about for hours. She remembered how her sister Maria had married the man of her dreams, just eight years her senior, with a gentle nature and those rare, dark-blue eyes. Mr. Hale, too, was one of the most moving preachers she’d ever heard, a model of pastoral devotion. So when she reflected upon her sister’s life, she concluded: “Married for love—what more could dearest Maria possibly desire?”

Mrs. Hale, if she spoke honestly, might have replied: “A silver-grey glacé silk, a white chip bonnet, dozens of things for the wedding, hundreds for the house.” But Margaret didn’t know the truth behind her mother’s absence—only that she hadn’t come, and she wasn’t sorry. It was better to greet her parents in the quiet comfort of their home than amid the chaos of London, where she’d spent the last two days playing hostess, running from room to room, managing guests and schedules, until her body ached and her mind felt strained.

Now, as the train rolled through the countryside, she let go of the past. The farewells had been swift, the goodbyes bittersweet. The people she’d lived with for so long—friends, servants, neighbours—were slipping away, and she felt a deep ache for what was lost. Not because those times had been perfect, but because they were gone forever. Yet, as the night drew near, she pulled herself back from melancholy, focusing instead on the future—on the warmth of home, the green woods, the quiet joy of being free again.

She watched her father, asleep in the corner seat. His once-dark hair was now threaded with grey, thinning over his brow. The bones of his face stood out clearly—sharp, almost severe, yet softened by the grace of fine features. His expression was still, but not at rest. There was a weariness beneath the calm, an undercurrent of worry that settled into the lines around his mouth and eyes. Margaret sighed softly. *Poor Frederick,* she thought. *If only he had become a clergyman instead of joining the navy…* She wished she knew the truth of what had happened—the story Aunt Shaw never told, only hinted at. Why couldn’t Frederick come home? What terrible affair had severed him from them?

Her heart tightened at the sight of her father’s quiet suffering. She wanted nothing more than to be there for him, to ease his burden, to bring light into their home. When he stirred, she smiled brightly—no trace of fatigue in her eyes. He returned her smile, but faintly, as though it cost him effort. Then, slowly, his face slipped back into its usual look of concern. He had a habit of half-opening his mouth as if to speak, then stopping—leaving his lips uncertain, his thoughts unspoken.

But his eyes—large, soft, slow-moving—were hers. They held depth and dignity, framed by delicate, translucent lids. Margaret bore more resemblance to her father than to her mother. Some said she wasn’t beautiful—too plain, too serious—but others saw something stronger: a presence that commanded attention without pretence. Her mouth was wide, not delicate like a rosebud, but full of life, curved in rich red lips. Her skin, though not pale, had a smooth, ivory-like quality. When she spoke to her father, her face lit up—dimples appearing, laughter sparkling in her eyes. She was young, yes, but already carried within her a quiet strength, a hopeful spirit.

It was late July when she arrived home. The forest stood thick and dark, the ferns glowing under slanting sunlight. The air was still, heavy with heat. Margaret used to walk beside her father, crushing the ferns beneath her feet with a kind of joyful cruelty, inhaling their earthy scent as they released it. She loved these walks—out across the broad commons, where wild creatures roamed freely, basking in the sun, feasting on flowers and herbs. This life—these walks—were everything she’d dreamed of. The forest was hers. Its people were hers. She made friends with them, learned their ways, spoke their language. She visited cottages tucked in the shade, nursed sick children, read aloud to the elderly, brought food to the ill. She even planned to teach at the village school—though her father often found himself distracted, drawn away from his duties to check on some friend or family in need.

Outside, her life was perfect. Inside, however, shadows lingered.

She felt a childlike shame when she realized how sharply she noticed the cracks in their domestic peace. Her mother—so tender, so loving—was often discontented. She complained that the bishop neglected his duties, failing to give Mr. Hale a better living. She gently reproached her husband for not seeking advancement, for not speaking up for himself. And every time he sighed and said, “If I can do what I ought here, I am thankful,” she pressed harder.

Each plea seemed to deepen his silence. Margaret tried to mediate, to remind her mother of the beauty of Helstone, of the simplicity that could be cherished. But her mother insisted the place was unhealthy—too many trees, too much damp. Margaret would coax her out onto the sun-dappled common, hoping the open air would lift her spirits. It helped for a while. But as autumn approached, the weather turned unpredictable, and her mother grew more restless. She resented the fact that her husband, more learned than Mr. Hume, more devoted than Mr. Houldsworth, had not received the recognition they both deserved.

This constant discontent—this quiet erosion of peace—was what Margaret hadn’t expected. She’d imagined giving up luxuries as a form of freedom, not as a source of sorrow. In London, she’d enjoyed comforts, but also felt constrained by them. Here, she could breathe, walk, think—yet the weight of her parents’ unhappiness pressed down on her.

There had been murmurs before—small complaints, fleeting regrets—but in the glow of happy memories, she’d forgotten them. Now, they returned, louder and clearer.

By mid-September, the rains came. Margaret was forced to stay indoors more often. Helstone was isolated, far from any refined society. “It is undoubtedly one of the most out-of-the-way places in England,” her mother said, sighing. “Papa has no one to associate with. He’s wasted here, seeing only farmers and labourers week after week.”

“If we lived on the other side of the parish,” Margaret offered, “we’d be near the Stansfields. The Gormons would be within walking distance.”

“The Gormons?” her mother asked, raising an eyebrow. “Those merchants from Southampton?”

“Oh, I’m glad we don’t visit them,” Margaret said firmly. “I don’t care for shopkeepers. I’d rather know only honest workers, people without pretension.”

“You mustn’t be so fastidious, dear,” her mother replied, thinking of a handsome young Mr. Gormon she’d once met.

“I like people who work the land,” Margaret insisted. “Soldiers, sailors, scholars—I admire them. But I don’t want you to think I should praise butchers and candlestick-makers.”

“But the Gormons are coach-builders,” her mother pointed out.

“Still a trade,” Margaret said. “And a far less useful one than baking bread or slaughtering meat. Oh, I used to hate riding in Aunt Shaw’s carriage! I longed to walk.”

And walk she did—despite the rain. She moved through the wind and mist as if carried by the breeze itself, light as a leaf swept along by autumn’s breath.

But evenings were harder. After tea, her father retreated to his small library. Margaret and her mother were left alone. Mrs. Hale never read much—she’d discouraged her husband early in their marriage from reading aloud while she worked. They’d tried backgammon once, but as Mr. Hale became more involved in his parish, his duties began to clash with her expectations. She saw interruptions as burdens, not as part of his calling. So he withdrew, finding solace in philosophy and metaphysics, books that few others would touch.

When Margaret had come home before, she’d brought a trunk full of books—recommended by teachers, assigned by governesses—and spent her summer racing through them. Now, the drawing-room shelves held only a few well-bound English classics: Thomson’s *Seasons*, Hayley’s *Cowper*, Middleton’s *Cicero*. These were the lightest, newest, most enjoyable among them. But they weren’t enough.

So Margaret told her mother about London—every detail, every person, every moment. Her mother listened, sometimes amused, sometimes wistful, comparing her sister’s comfortable life to their own modest circumstances. On such nights, Margaret would fall silent, listening to the steady *drip-drip* of rain on the lead roof. Once or twice, she found herself counting the drops, wondering if she dared ask—just once—about Frederick.

Where was he now? What was he doing? How long had it been since they’d heard from him?

But she hesitated. She knew the mutiny—the incident that had taken Frederick away—had marked a turning point. Her mother’s health issues, her dislike of Helstone, all began then. And the full story had never been told. It seemed buried, like a secret too painful to unearth.

When she was with her mother, she thought her father might answer. When she was with him, she believed she could approach her mother. But each time, she stopped short. Perhaps there was nothing new to hear. In a letter from before she left London, her father had written that they’d heard from Frederick—he was still in Rio, healthy, sending his love. But that was cold comfort. It wasn’t the living news she craved.

Frederick was always spoken of as “Poor Frederick.” His room remained untouched—cleaned weekly by Dixon, the maid, who treated it with reverence. Dixon had once served Lady Beresford, and she still carried the memory of the young Miss Beresfords, the belles of Rutlandshire. To her, Mr. Hale was the ruin of her mistress’s fate. If Miss Beresford hadn’t rushed into marriage with a poor country priest, who knew what she might have become? Yet Dixon stayed, loyal, protective—even if she saw herself as the fairy battling the wicked giant, Mr. Hale.

Master Frederick had been her favourite. Every week, she entered his room with quiet care, arranging it as if he might return that very evening.

Margaret suspected there was news—something her mother didn’t know—that weighed heavily on her father. But Mrs. Hale seemed unaware of any change. Her husband’s spirits were gentle, easily touched by sorrow—depressed after a deathbed, shaken by a crime. But now, Margaret noticed a new absence—an emptiness, as if his mind were occupied by something beyond daily tasks.

He didn’t go out among the villagers as much. He stayed in his study, waiting anxiously for the postman—whose arrival was signaled by a knock on the kitchen shutter. Once, this call was ignored, forgotten. Now, Mr. Hale lingered in the garden or stood by the window, watching the postman pass, nodding half-respectfully, half-confidentially, before retreating inside with a heavy heart.

Yet Margaret was young. And youth, when faced with uncertainty, often finds escape in sunshine. When October brought fourteen brilliant, clear days, her worries lifted like thistledown. She thought only of the forest—the golden leaves, the crisp air, the open glades now accessible after the rain.

The fern harvest was over, but the woods still held wonders. She had studied drawing with Edith, and during the rainy weeks, she’d regretted not capturing the beauty of the woods while she could. Now, determined to make up for lost time, she prepared her sketchboard one morning.

Just then, Sarah, the housemaid, flung open the drawing-room door and announced, “Mr. Henry Lennox.”

“Learn to win a lady’s faith
Nobly, as the thing is high;
Bravely,5 as for life and death—
With a loyal gravity.
Lead her from the festive boards,
Point her to the starry skies,
Guard her, by your truthful words,
Pure from courtship’s flatteries.”

“Mr. Henry Lennox.”

Margaret had just been thinking of him—of his quiet inquiry into how she might spend her days at home—when his name was announced. It was as if the sun had suddenly broken through clouds: her face lit up with warmth and surprise as she set down her drawing board and stepped forward to greet him.

“Tell mamma, Sarah,” she said. “Mamma and I have so many questions to ask you about Edith. I’m truly grateful you’ve come.”

“Did not I say I would?” he replied, lowering his voice slightly. “But I heard you were far away in the Highlands, and never imagined Hampshire could be within reach.”

“Oh!” he answered, more lightly now, “our young couple were behaving like children—climbing mountains, sailing on lakes, risking everything. I thought they needed a guide, a mentor, someone to keep them out of trouble. And indeed they did—my uncle was nearly driven mad, fearing for them every hour. Once I saw how reckless they were, I felt it my duty not to leave until I’d seen them safely aboard the ship at Plymouth.”

“Have you been to Plymouth? Oh! Edith never mentioned that. Of course, she’s been writing in such haste lately. Did they really sail on Tuesday?”

“They did,” he said, “and I’m relieved to say it. I’ve brought messages from Edith—here’s a little note.” He pulled out a folded slip of paper.

“Oh, thank you!” Margaret exclaimed. Then, half wishing to read it alone, she made an excuse: “I must go tell mamma again—Sarah must have mistaken something—that Mr. Lennox is here.”

As she left the room, he began to look around, his eyes sharp and observant. The small drawing-room glowed in the morning sunlight. The bow window stood open, framing climbing roses and scarlet honeysuckle. Outside, the lawn sparkled with verbenas and geraniums in vivid hues. Yet the brightness outside only made the room seem duller—the carpet worn, the chintz faded, the whole space smaller and humbler than he had expected. For Margaret, who seemed so regal in spirit, this setting felt inadequate.

He picked up one of the books on the table. It was *The Divine Comedy* by Dante, bound in old Italian vellum and gold. Beside it lay a dictionary and several pages of handwritten notes—words copied out neatly in Margaret’s hand. They were dry, scholarly entries, yet somehow they pleased him. He sighed softly as he laid them down.

“The living is clearly as modest as she described. Strange, though—Beresford is a family of standing.”

Meanwhile, Margaret found her mother in the hall, wrapped in her usual apron, looking tired and preoccupied. It was one of Mrs. Hale’s restless days, when even simple tasks felt like burdens.

“It’s most unfortunate,” she said. “We’re dining early, and all we have is cold meat—so the servants can finish their ironing. Still, of course, we must invite him. He’s Edith’s brother-in-law, after all. And your father—he’s in such low spirits today. I went into his study earlier, and he had his head buried in his hands. I told him Helstone air didn’t agree with him any more than it does with me, and he suddenly looked up and begged me not to speak against Helstone. Said it was the one place he loved on earth. But I know it’s the damp and sluggish air.”

A chill passed over Margaret, as if the sun had been dimmed. She listened quietly, hoping her mother might find relief in speaking. But soon it was time to turn her thoughts back to Mr. Lennox.

“Papa likes Mr. Lennox,” she said gently. “They got on well at the wedding breakfast. His visit might cheer papa up. And don’t worry about dinner, dear mamma. Cold meat will do perfectly well—especially since Mr. Lennox likely sees a two o’clock meal as a light lunch.”

“But what shall we do with him till then? It’s only half-past ten.”

“I’ll ask him to sketch with me,” Margaret suggested. “I know he draws. It will take him away from you, mamma. Please come in now—he’ll think it odd if you don’t.”

Mrs. Hale removed her apron, smoothed her hair, and greeted Mr. Lennox warmly, as one might welcome a near relation. He clearly expected to stay the day, and accepted the invitation with such cheerful eagerness that Mrs. Hale wished she could offer more than cold beef. He admired everything—the house, the garden, the idea of sketching together. He insisted on not disturbing Mr. Hale, knowing they would meet soon at dinner.

Margaret brought out her art supplies. After selecting paper and brushes, the two set off, full of good humor.

“Now, please stop here for a moment,” Margaret said. “These cottages haunted me during the rainy fortnight—like reproaches for not having drawn them.”

“Before they vanished,” he replied. “If they’re worth sketching—and they are very picturesque—we should not wait until next year. Where shall we sit?”

“Oh!” she laughed. “You could have stepped straight from chambers in the Temple, after two months in the Highlands! Look at this tree trunk—left just right for the light. I’ll put my plaid over it—it’ll make a fine forest throne.”

“And your feet in that puddle for a royal footstool!” he teased. “Wait—I’ll move. You can come closer this way. Who lives in these cottages?”

“They were built by squatters fifty or sixty years ago. One is empty—foresters plan to tear it down once the other passes on. Poor old man! There he is—I must go speak to him. He’s deaf, so you’ll hear all our secrets.”

The old man stood bareheaded in the sun, leaning on his cane. His weathered face softened into a slow smile as Margaret approached.

Mr. Lennox quickly sketched the two figures, adding them as secondary elements to the landscape—something Margaret noticed only when they packed up. She laughed and blushed. He watched her face closely.

“Now, I call that treacherous,” she said. “I never dreamed you were making us subjects when you asked me to talk to Isaac.”

“It was irresistible,” he admitted. “You wouldn’t understand how tempting it was. I hardly dare say how much I’ll treasure this sketch.”

She walked to the brook to wash her palette. He wasn’t sure if she heard the last part. When she returned, her cheeks were flushed, but she looked innocent and unaware. He was glad—because the words had slipped out unguardedly, rare for a man as deliberate as Henry Lennox.

Back at the house, the atmosphere was bright again. The clouds on Mrs. Hale’s brow had lifted, thanks to a neighbor’s gift of two carp. Mr. Hale had returned from his morning walk and waited just outside the garden gate. He looked like a gentleman—though his coat was threadbare, his hat worn. Margaret was proud of him, always moved by how strangers respected her father. Yet her quick eye caught traces of unease beneath his calm.

Mr. Hale asked to see their sketches.

“I think you’ve made the thatch too dark,” he said, handing back Margaret’s. “Don’t you?”

“No, papa,” she replied, peering over his shoulder. “The house-leek and stone-crop turned darker in the rain. Is it like, papa?”

“Yes, very like,” he said. “Your figure and posture are excellent. And that’s exactly how poor old Isaac holds himself—stooped, stiff with rheumatism. What’s hanging from that branch? Not a bird’s nest?”

“Oh no,” she smiled. “That’s my bonnet. I can’t draw with it on—it makes my head too hot. I wonder if I could manage figures. There are so many people here I’d love to sketch.”

“I should say that a likeness you deeply wish to capture would always succeed,” Mr. Lennox remarked. “I have great faith in the power of will. I believe I’ve succeeded fairly well in yours.”

Mr. Hale had already entered the house. Margaret lingered, picking roses to adorn her gown for dinner.

“A London girl would catch the meaning behind that,” Mr. Lennox thought. “She’d read between the lines, detect the compliment. But I don’t believe Margaret—no, I won’t believe it!” He stopped himself. “Let me help you.” He reached above her, gathering velvety crimson roses, then placed two in his buttonhole before sending her inside, smiling and content.

Dinner passed pleasantly. Questions flowed—news of Mrs. Shaw in Italy, shared memories. In the warmth of conversation, Mr. Lennox forgot the slight disappointment he’d first felt upon seeing the truth in Margaret’s description of her father’s modest income.

“Margaret, my child,” Mr. Hale said, as wine was poured, “you might have gathered some pears for dessert.”

Mrs. Hale was taken aback. Desserts were rare at the parsonage. Yet behind Mr. Hale, biscuits and marmalade sat neatly arranged on the sideboard. But the idea of pears had seized him.

“There are brown beurrés against the south wall—worth any foreign fruit. Run, Margaret, and gather some.”

“I propose we move into the garden,” Mr. Lennox suggested. “Nothing beats biting into crisp, sun-warmed fruit. Though the worst is, the wasps are bold enough to challenge you at the very peak of pleasure.”

He rose, waiting for Mrs. Hale’s permission. She preferred to finish dinner properly—especially since Dixon had fetched the finger-glasses from storage. But Mr. Hale stood, ready to go, so she yielded.

“I shall arm myself with a knife,” Mr. Hale declared. “Such primitive eating is beyond me. I must pare and quarter it first.”

Margaret made a plate from a beetroot leaf, which brought out the golden hue of the pears beautifully. Mr. Lennox watched her more than the fruit. Her father, meanwhile, chose the ripest pear with care and sat on the bench to savor it slowly.

Margaret and Mr. Lennox strolled along the terrace under the south wall, where bees hummed among the hives.

“What a perfect life you lead here,” he said. “I used to mock poets who dreamed of ‘a cot beside a hill.’ Now I feel like a true Cockney. Twenty years of law study might be repaid by one year of this peace—these skies!” He looked upward. “This amber and crimson foliage, still as stone!”

“You must remember,” she said gently, “our skies aren’t always this blue. We have rain. Leaves fall and grow wet. Still, I think Helstone is one of the finest places in the world. Do you recall how you once dismissed it as ‘a village in a tale’?”

“Scorned, Margaret? That’s a strong word.”

“Perhaps. But I felt I had so much to say, and you—what shall I call it?—spoke disrespectfully of Helstone.”

“I will never do so again,” he said, earnestly.

They turned the corner of the path.

“I could almost wish, Margaret—” he hesitated. So unusual for a man of his fluency that she looked up, startled.

In an instant, she wished she were anywhere else—back with her parents, safe. She sensed he was about to say something she couldn’t answer. But pride surged within her. She would not shrink. She could respond—calmly, firmly. To fear such a moment was weak.

Then he took her hand—suddenly, decisively—forcing her to stop.

“Margaret,” he said, “I wish you didn’t love Helstone so much—didn’t seem so happy here. For three months, I’ve hoped to find you missing London—missing its friends—enough to listen kindly to one who has little to offer—only hopes—but who loves you, Margaret, almost despite himself.”

She tried to pull her hand free, quietly but firmly.

“Margaret,” he continued, seeing her lips tremble, “have I frightened you?”

She fought to control herself. When she could speak, she said: “I was startled. I didn so know you cared in that way. I’ve always thought of you as a friend. I prefer to keep it so. I don’t like being spoken to like this. I cannot answer as you want. Yet I’d grieve if I hurt you.”

“Do you”—he began, looking into her eyes—“love anyone else?”

He stopped. It felt like an insult to those clear, honest eyes.

“Forgive me,” he said. “I’ve been too sudden. I am punished. But let me hope. Tell me—you’ve never seen anyone whom you could—”

He paused. He couldn’t finish.

Margaret felt deep regret. If only he hadn’t formed this feeling!

“If only you’d never thought this,” she said. “It was such joy to think of you as a friend.”

“But may I hope,” he asked, “that someday you’ll think of me as a lover? Not now—there’s no rush—but someday—”

She was silent, searching her heart. Then: “I’ve never thought of you but as a friend. I like that. I’m certain I could never think of you otherwise. Let us both forget this conversation.”

He paused. Then, coldly: “Of course. Your feelings are clear, and this has clearly upset you. It’s best forgotten. But in theory, forgetting pain is noble. In practice—well, it will be hard for me.”

“You’re disappointed,” she said sadly. “Yet how can I help it?”

Her sorrow touched him. He struggled, then spoke more cheerfully, though with stiffness: “You should consider the mortification—not just of a lover, but of a man not given to romance. Prudent, worldly, as some say. Carried out of habit by passion—well, let’s say no more. But in the one outlet for deeper feeling, he meets rejection. I’ll console myself by mocking my own folly. A struggling barrister dreaming of marriage!”

She couldn’t reply. His tone annoyed her—highlighting every difference that had ever distanced her from him. Yet he had been her most understanding friend in London.

A faint scorn curled her lip.

Thankfully, they came upon Mr. Hale, still enjoying his pear, peeled in one long, delicate strip.

Margaret felt stunned, unable to join the trivial talk that followed.

She remained grave, wondering when Mr. Lennox would leave—when she could return to her thoughts.

He was eager to go, too. But he stayed, forcing light conversation—a sacrifice to his wounded pride.

He glanced at her often—her sad, thoughtful face.

“I am not indifferent,” he thought. “Not as she believes. I do not give up hope.”

Within minutes, he fell into sarcasm—comparing city and country life, aware of his own mockery.

Mr. Hale was puzzled. This man was different—lighter, sharper, more worldly—dissonant to his own nature.

All were relieved when Mr. Lennox said he must leave to catch the five o’clock train.

They walked back to the house to bid Mrs. Hale farewell.

At the last moment, Henry Lennox’s true self broke through.

“Margaret,” he said, “don’t despise me. I have a heart—despite all this careless talk. Proof? I love you more than ever—if I don’t hate you—for the disdain in your eyes these past thirty minutes. Good-bye, Margaret—Margaret!”

“Cast me upon some naked shore,
Where I may track
Only the print of some sad wrack;
If thou be there though the seas roare,
I shall no gentler calm implore.”

He was gone. The house had closed for the evening. No more clear blue skies or golden hues at dusk. Margaret ascended to prepare for tea, only to find Dixon in a sour mood—irritated by the unexpected interruption of a visitor on a busy day. Her displeasure showed itself in sharp, impatient strokes as she brushed Margaret’s hair, pretending haste to attend to Mrs. Hale. Yet despite this pretense, Margaret waited long into the evening before her mother descended from her room.

She sat alone by the fire, candles unlit behind her, lost in thought. The day had held so much—joyful walks, sketching under open skies, a pleasant dinner with laughter and warmth—and then, the heavy silence of their walk in the garden. How different men were from women! She felt deeply wounded, unsettled by the impossibility of refusal when her heart had stirred in response to his proposal. And yet he—within minutes of being rejected—had spoken as if nothing mattered but success, briefs, fine houses, polished society. As if those were all that defined a man’s life. Oh, how she could have loved him—if only he had been different, not just in manner, but in depth, in soul.

Then came the thought: perhaps his lightness was not real at all, but a mask. A shield against pain too deep to show. If she had loved and been turned away, would not her own heart have cracked beneath such grief? The idea brought a strange comfort—almost a kind of justice.

Her mother entered the drawing-room before she could settle these thoughts. Margaret had to push aside the weight of what had passed, and become an attentive listener as her mother recounted trivial grievances—Dixon’s complaint about a burnt ironing blanket, Susan Lightfoot seen wearing artificial flowers, a sign, she insisted, of vanity and frivolity.

Mr. Hale sipped his tea in silence, lost in thought. Margaret answered for both parents. She wondered how they could forget so completely the man who had shared their day—the one whose presence had once seemed so vital. Then it struck her: he had not proposed to them. He had not asked for their consent. That explained their silence.

After tea, Mr. Hale rose and stood beside the fireplace, elbow resting on the mantel, hand supporting his head. He stared into the flames, sighing deeply now and then. Mrs. Hale stepped out to consult with Dixon about winter clothing for the poor. Margaret prepared her mother’s knitting, dreading the length of the evening, longing for bed, where she might quietly review the events of the day.

“Margaret!” he said suddenly, in a voice broken by urgency, making her start. “Is that tapestry work urgent? Can you leave it and come into my study? I need to speak to you about something very serious—something that concerns us all.”

“Very serious,” repeated Margaret, her mind racing. Mr. Lennox had not spoken privately with her father after her refusal—so this could not be about him. Still, guilt pricked her. She was no longer just a girl, but a woman capable of marriage. And what if her father disapproved of her having refused him?

But soon she realized this was not about a recent event. It was deeper, older. Her father motioned her to sit beside him. He stirred the fire, snuffed the candles, sighed again, and finally spoke—his words forced out like a burden:

“Margaret… I am going to leave Helstone.”

“Leave Helstone, papa? Why?” she whispered.

He did not answer at first. His hands fidgeted with papers on the table, opening his mouth several times, closing it again. Margaret watched, helpless, as the tension tightened around him. She could not bear it.

“But why, dear papa? Please tell me!”

He looked up, his eyes full of sorrow, and said slowly, with effort: “Because I can no longer serve as a minister in the Church of England.”

She had expected news of a new appointment—perhaps a grand rectory far from Helstone, something her mother had long desired. But this—this was worse than any change of residence. This was a severance of faith, of identity.

“What do you mean?” she breathed. “Why not?”

“It is not about Frederick,” he said quickly. “Not about anything he has done. The bishop would not concern himself with that. It is about me. About what I have come to believe.”

He paused, gathering strength. “Margaret, I will tell you now. I will answer your questions—but after tonight, let us never speak of this again. I can face the consequences. But speaking of it… it breaks me.”

“Doubts, papa?” she whispered. “Doubts about religion?”

“No,” he said firmly. “Not doubts about religion. Not harm to that. But… about whether I have the right to hold this living. For years I’ve wrestled with it. I’ve tried to silence my conscience with the authority of the Church. But I cannot go on. I love the Church—I love it dearly—but I cannot stay where I must betray my own truth.”

He broke off, unable to continue. Margaret sat frozen. This was not mere change—it was exile. A fall from grace, perhaps.

“I’ve been reading today,” he said, trying to steady himself, “about the two thousand ministers ejected from their churches. I tried to take courage from their example. But it does no good. I feel it—deeply.”

“Oh, papa,” she cried, tears rising. “Have you thought of this carefully? It seems impossible—horrible.”

The foundation of her world trembled. Her father—her anchor—was choosing a path that shattered everything she knew.

Seeing her distress, he gathered himself. He walked to the bookshelf, took down a volume he had read often lately, and placed it in her hands.

“Listen,” he said, putting an arm around her waist. She clutched his hand, unable to lift her face. She could not focus on the words, so overwhelmed was she by emotion.

“This is from a clergyman named Oldfield—minister of Carsington, over a hundred years ago. He wrote this while facing the same choice. His trials are over. He fought the good fight.”

He read aloud:

*When thou canst no longer continue in thy work without dishonour to God, discredit to religion, foregoing thy integrity, wounding conscience, spoiling thy peace, and hazarding the loss of thy salvation; in a word, when the conditions upon which thou must continue (if thou wilt continue) in thy employments are sinful, and unwarranted by the word of God, thou mayest, yea, thou must believe that God will turn thy very silence, suspension, deprivation, and laying aside, to His glory, and the advancement of the Gospel’s interest. When God will not use thee in one kind, yet He will in another. A soul that desires to serve and honour Him shall never want opportunity to do it; nor must thou so limit the Holy One of Israel, as to think He hath but one way in which He can glorify Himself by thee. He can do it by thy silence as well as by thy preaching; the laying aside as well as thy continuance in thy work. It is not pretence of doing God the greatest service, or performing the weightiest duty, that will excuse the least sin, though that sin capacitated or gave us the opportunity for doing that duty. Thou wilt have little thanks, O my soul! if, when thou art charged with corrupting God’s worship, falsifying thy vows, thou pretendest a necessity for it in order to a continuance in the ministry.*

As he read, he found strength. He believed he could stand firm. But when he finished, he heard her quiet sob. His courage faltered.

“Margaret,” he murmured, drawing her closer, “think of the early martyrs. Think of those who suffered for truth.”

“But father,” she said, lifting her tear-streaked face, “the martyrs died for truth. You—oh, dear, dear papa—”

“I suffer for conscience,” he said, his voice firm despite the trembling in his frame. “I must do what my conscience demands. I have carried shame for too long—shame that would have moved any stronger man to act. Your mother’s wish—granted at last, though in mockery—has brought this moment. And I am thankful for it. A month ago, the bishop offered me another living. To accept it, I would have had to make a fresh declaration of conformity. I tried. I tried to simply refuse the preferment and stay here—strangling my conscience, as I had already strained it. God forgive me.”

He walked the room, muttering words of self-reproach. Margaret listened, grateful for the few she heard.

At last he stopped. “We must leave Helstone.”

“Yes,” she whispered. “But when?”

“I’ve written to the bishop—though I may have told you already, I forget things now. I’ve informed him of my resignation. He was kind. He argued, pleaded. But it was useless. They are the same arguments I’ve used on myself. I must resign. I must go to him in person to say farewell. That will be hard. But worse—far worse—will be leaving my people. A curate, Mr. Brown, has been appointed. He arrives tomorrow. Next Sunday, I preach my farewell sermon.”

It was sudden. And yet—perhaps it was better. Delay would only prolong the pain. To hear it all at once, laid bare, was like being stunned into numbness.

“What does mamma say?” she asked, her voice thick.

To her surprise, her father began pacing again. At length, he stopped.

“Margaret,” he said, “I am a coward. I cannot bear to hurt her. I know her married life has not been all she hoped. This will break her. I have not had the strength to tell her. But she must know. Now.”

Margaret felt a wave of shock. Her mother knew nothing. And yet, everything was already in motion.

“Yes,” she said, “she must know. But—she will be shattered.”

“Where are we going?” she asked, trying to grasp the future.

“To Milton-Northern,” he said flatly.

“The manufacturing town in Darkshire?”

“Yes.”

“Why there, papa?”

“Because I can earn bread there. Because no one knows me. No one will remind me of Helstone.”

“Bread?” she echoed. “But you and mamma have money—”

She stopped herself, seeing the shadow deepen on his face.

He understood. He saw the worry in her eyes, mirrored his own despair. He changed course.

“You shall know everything, Margaret. But help me tell your mother. I cannot do it. The thought of her pain makes me sick. If I tell you, perhaps you can break it to her tomorrow.”

She hesitated. She disliked it more than anything she had ever faced. But then she lifted her head.

“It is painful,” she said, “but it must be done. I will do it as well as I can. You have many hard things to do.”

He nodded, pressing her hand. She nearly wept again.

“Now,” she said, trying to shift her thoughts, “tell me our plans. You and mamma have income beyond the living, don’t you? Aunt Shaw has money.”

“Yes. We have about a hundred and twenty pounds a year. Seventy of that has gone to Frederick since he left. I don’t know if he still needs it. He serves with the Spanish army. He must have some pay.”

“Frederick must not suffer,” she said firmly. “In a foreign land, unjustly treated by his own country. A hundred pounds remains. Could we not live on that in some quiet part of England? I think we could.”

“No,” he said. “That won’t do. I must work. I must keep busy to avoid sinking into gloom. And a country parish would only remind me of Helstone—of what I’ve lost. And a hundred pounds wouldn’t stretch far enough to give your mother the comforts she deserves. We must go to Milton. It is settled.”

He added, almost apologetically, “I decide better when alone—when not influenced by those I love. I cannot endure objections. They make me weak.”

She kept silent. Where they went mattered less than the fact that they were leaving everything behind.

“A few months ago,” he continued, “when my doubt became unbearable, I wrote to Mr. Bell. You remember him?”

“No,” she said. “But I know who he is—Frederick’s godfather, your old tutor at Oxford?”

“Yes. He is a Fellow at Plymouth College. He is from Milton-Northern—or at least, he owns property there. It has grown in value since the town expanded. I wrote to him. I didn’t tell you why. But I trusted him. He has lived a peaceful life. He hasn’t given me strength—but he has been kind. And through him, we are going to Milton.”

“How?” she asked.

“He has tenants, houses, mills. Though he dislikes the place—too noisy for his taste—he must maintain connections. He says there is a position open for a private tutor.”

“A private tutor?” she said, incredulous. “What do manufacturers want with classics? With literature? With the education of gentlemen?”

“Oh,” he said, “some of them are intelligent men, aware of their own limits—more so than many at Oxford. Some want to learn. Others want their children better educated than they were. There is an opening. Mr. Bell has recommended me to Mr. Thornton—a tenant of his, a man of sense, from his letters. In Milton, I shall have a busy life, even if not a happy one. And the people, the scenes—nothing like Helstone. That is why I choose it.”

Margaret understood. It was not just about work. It was about difference. About escape. Though she despised the North—its factories, its grim towns, its harsh people—there was one thing it offered: distance. A place where Helstone could not haunt them.

“When do we go?” she asked, after a pause.

“I don’t know exactly. I wanted to talk to you first. Your mother knows nothing yet. But I think—in a fortnight. After I send my resignation, I have no right to remain.”

“In a fortnight!” she gasped.

“No—not precisely. Nothing is fixed,” he said, seeing the sorrow in her eyes. “But it should be decided soon.”

She regained composure. “Yes, papa. It should be. Only—mamma doesn’t know. That is the hardest part.”

“Poor Maria,” he murmured. “Poor, poor Maria. If I were not married—if I were alone—how easy it would be. But I cannot tell her.”

“No,” she said softly. “I will tell her. Give me until tomorrow evening. Oh, papa,” she cried, “say it’s a dream. Say it’s not real. You cannot mean to leave the Church—to abandon Helstone—to be torn from us—by some illusion? You do not really mean it?”

He remained still. Then he looked at her, his gaze steady, his voice slow and hoarse:

“I do mean it, Margaret. Do not deceive yourself. This is not a dream. It is my resolve. My fixed intention.”

He held her gaze for a long moment. She returned it, pleading, searching—until she could no longer deny the truth.

Then she rose and moved toward the door.

He called her back.

He stood by the fireplace, bent and weary. But as she approached, he straightened, placed his hands on her head, and said solemnly:

“The blessing of God be upon thee, my child.”

“And may He restore you to His Church,” she replied, from the depths of her heart.

She feared her words might be wrong, irreverent. But then she threw her arms around him. He held her close, murmuring to himself: “The martyrs bore greater pain. I will not shrink.”

They were startled by Mrs. Hale’s voice calling for her daughter.

They pulled apart, each knowing what lay ahead.

“Go, Margaret,” he said quickly. “I shall be out all tomorrow. Before night, you will have told your mother.”

“Yes,” she said, and left the room—dazed, stunned, carrying the weight of a world undone.

“I ask Thee for a thoughtful love, Through constant watching wise,
To meet the glad with joyful smiles, And to wipe the weeping eyes;
And a heart at leisure from itself To soothe and sympathise.” —ANON.

Margaret listened attentively as her mother spoke of small plans to ease the burdens of the poorest in their parish. She could not help but listen, even though each new idea pierced her heart like a fresh wound. By the time winter’s frost settled in, they would be far from Helstone. Old Simon’s rheumatism was worsening, his sight failing—no one would read to him now, no one would bring him broth or warm flannel. If someone did come, it would be a stranger, and the old man would sit by his window, waiting in vain for the familiar face he once knew so well. Mary Domville’s little crippled boy would crawl to the door every day, peering through the trees, hoping to see her return. These people would never understand why she had left them—and there were many others like them. “Your father has always spent his income here, supporting the parish,” Mrs. Hale said. “I may be stepping beyond what is strictly due, but the winter looks harsh, and our poor must be cared for.”

“Oh, mamma,” Margaret replied eagerly, her voice trembling with emotion, “let us do all we can! We may not be here long.” She didn’t see the practical concerns behind her mother’s words—only the urgency of helping those who might never again know her kindness.

“Do you feel unwell, my darling?” Mrs. Hale asked, alarmed by the pallor in her daughter’s face. “You look tired. It’s this damp, soft air—it’s not good for you.”

“No, mamma,” Margaret answered gently, pressing her lips into a faint smile as she kissed her mother’s cheek. “It’s not that. The air is delicious—so fresh, so pure after the smoke and noise of Harley Street. I’m just tired. It must be nearly bedtime.”

“It is half-past nine,” Mrs. Hale said. “You should go straight to bed. Ask Dixon for some gruel. I’ll come up later to check on you. I fear you’ve caught something—perhaps from the stagnant ponds near the woods.”

“Oh, mamma,” Margaret murmured, still smiling, “I’m perfectly well. Don’t worry. I’m simply worn out.”

She climbed the stairs slowly, accepting the gruel only to calm her mother’s fears. Lying in bed, she felt weak and heavy. When Mrs. Hale came in to say goodnight, Margaret watched her leave, then waited until the click of the locked door echoed in the silence. At once, she sprang from the bed, threw on her dressing gown, and began pacing the room. The creak of a floorboard reminded her to be quiet. She slipped onto the deep window seat, where earlier that morning she had seen the church tower glowing under bright sunlight—a promise of warmth and clarity. Now, only sixteen hours later, the sky was dark and cold, the towers stark against an endless blue void.

She sat there, unable to cry, but gripped by a dull ache that seemed to drain every spark of joy from her soul. Mr. Henry Lennox’s visit—the proposal—felt like a dream, distant and unreal. But the truth was raw and undeniable: her father had allowed doubt to take root, and now stood outside the Church, rejected by the community he once served. All the changes that followed flowed from that single, devastating choice.

Outside, the church towers loomed like silent sentinels. She stared into the vastness of space, feeling a terrible emptiness. Where was God? Was He hidden beyond those infinite depths, unreachable, indifferent? The thought filled her with despair. If the heavens stretched endlessly, how could human cries ever reach Him? What use was prayer if the cosmos swallowed sorrow whole?

Then she heard footsteps—soft, hesitant. Her father stood beside her, unseen until he touched her shoulder.

“Margaret,” he whispered, “I heard you were awake. I couldn’t stay away. Will you pray with me? Just the Lord’s Prayer. It will help both of us.”

They knelt together by the window seat. He looked upward. She bowed her head, ashamed. God was near, listening. Her father might have lost faith—but hadn’t she, just moments before, questioned everything herself? She said nothing. After he left, she crept back to bed like a child afraid of being punished.

That night, dreams tormented her. Mr. Lennox climbed a tree too high to reach—her bonnet hung from a branch. He fell, and she rushed to save him, only to be held back by an invisible hand. He died. Yet in another scene, they were in the drawing-room at Harley Street, talking as if nothing had changed. But she knew—he was dead.

A restless, painful night. No rest for the coming day. She woke suddenly, unrefreshed, the weight of reality heavier than any dream. It wasn’t just grief—it was the horror of her father’s departure, not just from place, but from belief. How far had he wandered? Had doubts led him into darkness? She longed to ask, but feared the answer more than anything.

The next morning, the crisp air lifted her mother’s spirits. At breakfast, Mrs. Hale chattered about village matters, unaware of the silence between her husband and daughter. Before the dishes were cleared, Mr. Hale rose, leaning heavily on the table.

“I won't be home until evening,” he said. “I’m going to Bracy Common. I’ll ask Farmer Dobson for dinner. I’ll return by seven.”

He didn’t look at either of them, but Margaret understood. By seven, the truth would be told. Her father had delayed, but she could not endure the suspense. Better to face it now. As she stood by the window, planning how to begin, her mother hurried upstairs to prepare for school.

“Mother,” Margaret said softly, slipping her arm around her mother’s waist, “let’s walk through the garden—just once.”

They stepped outside through the open window. Mrs. Hale began to speak, but Margaret didn’t hear. Her eye caught a bee entering a deep-throated flower. She waited—when the bee emerged, that would be the signal.

It flew out.

“Mamma!” Margaret burst out. “Papa is leaving Helstone! He’s quitting the Church. We’re moving to Milton-Northern.”

The words were spoken quickly, sharply—three truths laid bare.

“What makes you say that?” Mrs. Hale asked, stunned. “Who’s been telling you such nonsense?”

“Papa himself,” Margaret said, wanting to soften the blow but finding no words. They reached a bench. Mrs. Hale sank down and began to weep.

“I don’t understand,” she said, shaking her head. “Either you’re mistaken—or I’m missing something.”

“No, mother,” Margaret insisted. “He wrote to the bishop. He says his doubts make it impossible for him to remain a priest. He’s resigning. He’s spoken to Mr. Bell—Frederick’s godfather—and it’s decided: we’re going to Milton-Northern.”

Mrs. Hale looked up, her face pale. The truth had struck her.

“I don’t believe it,” she whispered. “He would have told me first.”

Margaret felt a pang of guilt. Her father had kept this from her mother—not out of cruelty, but out of tenderness, perhaps even cowardice. Still, it was wrong. She sat beside her, cradling her mother’s head in her arms, her own cheeks brushing gently against hers.

“Dear, dear mamma,” she murmured, “we were afraid of hurting you. Papa suffers deeply. You’re not strong, and the wait must have been agony.”

“When did he tell you?” Mrs. Hale asked.

“Yesterday,” Margaret replied, sensing the jealousy beneath the question. “Poor papa.” She tried to shift her mother’s thoughts toward pity.

“What does he mean by ‘doubts’?” Mrs. Hale asked. “Does he think differently? Does he believe better than the Church?”

Margaret shook her head. Tears welled up. That question touched the deepest part of her pain.

“Can’t the bishop help him?” Mrs. Hale asked, almost impatiently.

“I’m afraid not,” Margaret said quietly. “I didn’t ask. I couldn’t bear to hear what he might say. It’s already decided. He’s leaving in two weeks. I’m not sure if he’s sent in his resignation.”

“In two weeks!” Mrs. Hale gasped. “This is outrageous. Unfeeling. He leaves without consulting me. If he’d told me sooner, I could have helped him.”

Margaret winced. She couldn’t defend her father’s silence—but she couldn’t let her mother condemn him so harshly.

“I almost hoped you’d be glad to leave Helstone,” she said gently. “You’ve never been well here.”

“The smoky air of a factory town?” Mrs. Hale scoffed. “All chimneys and dirt? How could that be better than this pure, sweet air? Living among mill workers? Though, of course, if your father leaves the Church, we’ll be cut off from society everywhere. It’s a disgrace! Poor Sir John—he’d be horrified. Every day after dinner, when I was young at Beresford Court, he’d toast—‘Church and King, and down with the Rump!’”

Margaret was relieved her mother had shifted focus—from her father’s silence to the social fall-out. But this, too, hurt her deeply. It was not just faith that was breaking—it was identity.

“We have little society here, mamma,” she said. “The Gormans are our nearest neighbors—we hardly see them. And they’ve been in trade just as long as these Milton people.”

“Yes,” Mrs. Hale snapped, “but the Gormans built carriages for gentlemen. They had connections. These factory folk—what kind of people wear cotton who can afford linen?”

“Well, mamma,” Margaret said, “I’m not defending the cotton-spinners. But we’ll likely have little to do with them.”

“Why on earth did your father choose Milton-Northern?”

“Partly because it’s so different from Helstone,” Margaret sighed. “And partly because Mr. Bell thinks there’s a chance for a private tutor.”

“A tutor in Milton?” Mrs. Hale scoffed. “Why not Oxford? Why not teach gentlemen?”

“You forget, mamma,” Margaret said softly. “He’s leaving the Church because of his beliefs. His doubts wouldn’t serve him well at Oxford.”

Mrs. Hale fell silent, tears tracing slow paths down her cheeks. Then she whispered:

“And the furniture? How shall we move everything? I’ve never moved before. Only two weeks!”

Margaret felt a surge of relief. Her mother’s distress had narrowed to something manageable. She could help. She made plans, offered promises, guided her mother through decisions, filling in gaps while awaiting further news from her father.

All day, Margaret stayed close to her mother, absorbing every shift in her mood. By evening, she grew anxious for her father’s return—afraid he’d find no comfort waiting for him after a long, weary day. She thought of the secret suffering he must have endured. Her mother, however, only repeated, coldly, that he should have told her earlier—that she could have advised him.

When she heard his step in the hall, Margaret froze. She dared not approach. She saw her mother’s lips twitch, her color change—she, too, knew he was home. The door opened. He stood uncertain, pale and weary, his eyes shadowed with fear. A look of utter helplessness on a man’s face—something almost unbearable.

His wife rushed to him, throwing herself into his arms.

“Oh, Richard, Richard—you should have told me sooner!”

Margaret fled, rushing upstairs to collapse on her bed, burying her face in the pillows to choke back sobs that finally broke free after a day of rigid control.

How long she lay there, she didn’t know. The housemaid entered silently, arranged the room, then slipped out, whispering to Dixon that Miss Hale was crying as if her heart would break. She’d surely make herself ill, the girl warned.

At the sound, Margaret jerked upright, startled. She saw the familiar room, the silhouette of Dixon holding a candle behind her, cautious not to startle her.

“Oh, Dixon! I didn’t hear you come in!” she said, regaining composure. “Is it very late?”

“I can’t tell,” Dixon replied, voice sharp with grievance. “Since your mamma told me the news, I’ve lost track of time. I don’t know what will become of us. When Charlotte said you were sobbing, I thought, *No wonder*. And master—turning Dissenter at his age? If he hasn’t done well in the Church, he hasn’t done badly. I had a cousin who became a Methodist preacher at fifty, after being a tailor all his life. But he’d never made a pair of trousers fit—so no surprise. But master? I said to missus, ‘What would poor Sir John have said? He never liked your marrying Mr. Hale, but if he’d known it would come to this, he’d have sworn worse than ever!’”

Dixon had long commented freely on Mr. Hale’s actions to her mistress. She never noticed Margaret’s sudden tension—the flash of her eyes, the flare of her nostrils.

“Dixon,” Margaret said, voice low and steady, like thunder gathering in the distance. “You forget whom you speak to.”

She stood tall now, facing the maid with unwavering gaze. “I am Mr. Hale’s daughter. Go. You’ve made a grave mistake—one I know your own sense will regret.”

Dixon hesitated, torn between anger and shame. She had never seen such authority in Margaret before. “Miss Margaret has a touch of the old gentleman,” she muttered to herself. “And poor Master Frederick. I wonder where they get it?”

She lowered her voice, half apologetic, half offended: “May I unfasten your gown, miss? Do your hair?”

“No,” Margaret said firmly. “Not tonight. Thank you.” She lit the way for Dixon to leave, then bolted the door behind her.

From that moment, Dixon obeyed and admired her. She said it was because Margaret reminded her of Frederick. But the truth was simpler: she liked being ruled by strength.

Margaret needed that strength now. She had no time for words—only action. In the days that followed, Dixon retreated into silence, showing her disapproval by saying nothing. So Margaret worked harder, directing the packing, organizing the removal, making decisions no one else could. A fortnight was too short to plan such a move. As Dixon muttered, “Anyone but a gentleman—indeed, almost any other gentleman—” she caught Margaret’s stern glance and coughed the rest away, meekly accepting the horehound drop offered to soothe her chest.

Mrs. Hale, overwhelmed by the sudden flood of decisions, fell ill. Margaret felt a strange relief when her mother took to her bed—now she could lead.

Mr. Hale returned each evening more broken than before, visiting each parishioner in farewell. Margaret, inexperienced in practical matters, didn’t know where to turn. The cook and Charlotte worked tirelessly. Margaret’s clear mind guided them, but the bigger question remained: where would they go?

In a week, they must leave. Straight to Milton? Or somewhere else?

One evening, Margaret gathered her courage and asked her father.

“Papa,” she said, “this uncertainty is killing you both. We need to know what to plan for. Have you found a house in Milton?”

“No,” he replied, weary. “We’ll need lodgings first. Look for a house later.”

“And pack the furniture so it can wait at the station?”

“I suppose so. Do what you think best. Remember—we’ll have less money.”

Margaret knew they had never had much to spare. This burden was crushing. Four months ago, her biggest decisions were which dress to wear or who to invite to dinner. Life had been smooth, predictable. Only Captain Lennox’s offer had shaken her world. Now, every day brought a new crisis—each decision vital, each choice shaping their future.

But she would face it. Not with certainty, but with resolve. For her father. For her mother. For the life they were leaving behind—and the one they would build ahead.

“Unwatch’d the garden bough shall sway,
The tender blossom flutter down,
Unloved that beech will gather brown,
The maple burn itself away;
Unloved, the sun-flower, shining fair,
Ray round with flames her disk of seed,
And many a rose-carnation feed
With summer spice the humming air;
* * * * *
Till from the garden and the wild
A fresh association blow
And year by year the landscape grow
Familiar to the stranger’s child;
As year by year the labourer tills
His wonted glebe, or lops the glades;
And year by year our memory fades
From all the circle of the hills.”
—Tennyson

The final day had arrived. The house was a scene of quiet chaos—packing cases piled high, their contents carefully wrapped and labeled, waiting to be carted off to the railway station. Even the once-pristine lawn beside the house bore the marks of the move: scattered straw drifted through open windows, clinging to the grass like a forgotten shawl. Inside, the rooms echoed strangely, stripped of their usual warmth and life. Sunlight streamed in unfiltered through bare windows, sharp and unwelcoming, as if the house itself were already preparing to forget its past.

Mrs. Hale’s dressing-room remained untouched until the very end. There, she and Dixon worked slowly, folding garments with trembling hands, pausing now and then to hold up some small treasure—a child’s tiny boot, a faded ribbon, a drawing scrawled in crayon—each one a silent plea from the past. They spoke little, but their silence was thick with emotion. Progress was slow. The memories weighed heavier than the clothes.

Below, Margaret stood calm and composed, overseeing the men who had come to help the cook and Charlotte. She moved with quiet authority, offering directions without hesitation. The two women, weeping between tasks, marveled at her stillness. “How can she keep so steady?” they whispered. “She must not care much for Helstone—she’s been in London so long.” But Margaret, pale and quiet, watched everything—the way the light fell across the floor, the dust motes dancing in the air, the careful placement of each box. Her heart, however, was breaking beneath a weight too deep for words. To let herself feel would be to collapse—and who then would carry on? Her father was absorbed in the vestry, sorting papers and registers with the clerk. When he returned, his own books needed packing—only he could do it properly. And Margaret? She was not one to break down before servants, even those who had served her family for years.

At last, the packers retired to the kitchen for tea. Margaret left her post, moving stiffly through the hall, past the empty drawing-room, into the dimming evening. November twilight draped the garden in a soft, misty veil, blurring the edges of trees and hedges, casting everything in a faint lilac hue. The sun had not yet set, but dusk was gathering fast.

A robin sang—a single, clear note—perhaps the same bird her father had once called his winter companion, the one for whom he had built a shelter by the study window. The leaves, more brilliant than ever in the fading light, shimmered amber and gold as they drifted down, caught in the low slant of the sun. One after another, they fell, like embers from a dying fire.

Margaret walked along the path beneath the pear-tree wall—the same walk where, only a fortnight ago, she had strolled beside Henry Lennox. Here, near the thyme bed, he had begun to speak of things she could no longer think about. Her eyes had lingered on a late-blooming rose as he talked, and she had noticed the delicate feathery leaves of the carrots mid-sentence. Now, the world felt changed beyond recognition. Where was he now? In London, perhaps, dining with old friends in Harley Street, or laughing with younger companions. At this very moment, while she wandered through the damp, quiet garden, he might be closing his law books after a hard day’s work, stepping out into the Temple Gardens for a breath of fresh air—listening to the distant roar of thousands of workers, unseen but ever-present, catching glimpses of city lights rising from the river below. He had often told her of these walks, spoken of them with such warmth and energy that she had found herself drawn to the idea of that bustling life.

But here, there was silence. The robin had vanished. Only the occasional sound of a cottage door shutting in the distance broke the stillness—like a tired man returning home. Beyond the garden, in the forest, a stealthy rustling crept through the crisp leaves. A poacher, perhaps. Margaret had seen them often during autumn nights, sitting up in her room with the candle extinguished, watching their silent leaps over the fence, their swift steps across the moonlit lawn, vanishing into the dark. Once, she had admired their freedom, wished them luck. Tonight, though, she felt fear—though she did not know why.

She heard Charlotte closing the windows, locking the house for the night, unaware that anyone remained outside. Then—a heavy crack! A branch, rotten or broken, fell nearby. Margaret darted forward, swift as wind, to the window, knocking urgently with trembling hands. “Let me in! Let I’m only me, Charlotte!”

Her heart pounded until she was safely inside, the windows bolted, the familiar walls enclosing her once more. She sank onto a packing case, cold and weary. The room was bleak—no fire, no comfort, only the flickering light of Charlotte’s long-unsnuffed candle.

Charlotte looked surprised. Margaret, sensing the glance, rose quickly. “I thought you might shut me out entirely,” she said, half-smiling. “Then you’d never have heard me in the kitchen. And the doors to the lane and churchyard are locked long since.”

“Oh, miss,” Charlotte replied, “I’d have found you soon enough. The men need your direction. And I’ve laid tea in master’s study—it’s the most comfortable room, so to speak.”

“Thank you, Charlotte,” Margaret said softly. “You’re kind. I’ll miss you. Do write to me if I can ever help you—advice, or anything. I’ll always welcome a letter from Helstone. I’ll send my address as soon as I know it.”

The study was ready. A warm fire crackled, candles stood unlit on the table. Margaret sat on the rug, pulling her knees to her chest, clasping her hands tightly around them. Her head drooped slightly, her posture one of quiet exhaustion. Yet when she heard her father’s footsteps on the gravel outside, she straightened up, brushing back her hair, wiping away the tears she hadn’t known had come.

He entered, quieter than usual, more subdued. He refused food, though she tried to draw him into conversation—offering topics she knew would interest him, each attempt costing her strength. “Have you walked far today?” she asked gently.

“As far as Fordham Beeches,” he replied. “I saw Widow Maltby. She’s deeply grieved she couldn’t say goodbye. Little Susan has watched down the lane for days—waiting, hoping.” He paused. “Nay, Margaret, what is it? What troubles you?”

The image of the child—patient, hopeful, disappointed every day—was too much. Margaret broke down, sobbing as if her heart would split. Mr. Hale stood helpless, pacing the room, his face etched with distress. “I cannot bear it,” he murmured, almost to himself. “I cannot bear to see others suffer. I think I could endure my own pain with patience. Oh, is there no going back?”

“No, father,” Margaret said, lifting her eyes to meet his, her voice firm though low. “It is worse to believe you in error than to know you a hypocrite.” She lowered her voice at the last words, as if the very idea of hypocrisy applied to her father was an affront to reverence. “Besides,” she continued, “it’s only fatigue tonight. Don’t think I am suffering because of what you’ve done, dear papa. We can’t talk about it now—I believe we both feel it too deeply.”

Tears came again, despite her efforts. “I should go and bring tea to mamma,” she said, rising. “She had hers early, when I was busy. I’m sure she’ll want another now.”

The next morning, railroad time carried them away from Helstone—its beloved parsonage, half-hidden by China roses and pyracanthus, glowing in the morning sun. Each window sparkled, each room remembered. Before they could settle into the carriage sent from Southampton, they were gone—never to return.

A pang pierced Margaret’s heart as she strained to catch a glimpse of the old church tower at the bend in the road, where it might rise above the forest. But her father remembered the view first. Without a word, she yielded the window to him. She leaned back, closed her eyes—and the tears came, hanging for a moment on her lashes before sliding down her cheeks, unnoticed, onto her dress.

They would spend the night in a quiet London hotel. Mrs. Hale had wept nearly all day, quietly, steadily. Dixon expressed her sorrow through irritation, constantly adjusting her petticoats to avoid touching Mr. Hale, whom she blamed for their misfortune.
