Through the streets they passed—familiar houses, shops, faces. Margaret saw people she knew, though she could not stop. The morning had felt endless, yet they arrived in London during the busiest hour of a November afternoon. It was long since Mrs. Hale had been in the city. Now, she stirred with childlike wonder, pointing at shops, exclaiming at carriages. “Oh, there’s Harrison’s—where I bought my wedding things! Look at those windows—larger than Crawford’s in Southampton!” Then, suddenly: “Wait—no, it isn’t—yes, it is—Margaret, we just passed Mr. Henry Lennox! Where can he be going, among all these shops?”

Margaret started forward, then stopped herself, half-smiling at her own impulse. They were a hundred yards behind now—but he seemed a ghost from Helstone, tied to a bright morning, a significant day. She wanted to see him—without being seen, without speaking. Just to know he was there.

That evening, spent in a high room of a hotel with nothing to do, stretched endlessly. Mr. Hale went out to visit his bookseller, to call on friends. Everyone they saw—on the street, in the hotel—seemed bound for somewhere, expected or expecting someone. They alone felt adrift, strangers in a city of motion.

Yet within a mile, Margaret knew houses where they would be welcomed—if they came with joy, or even peace. If they came in sorrow, seeking sympathy for a complex grief, they would be shadows, burdens. London life was too swift, too full, to allow even an hour of the quiet mourning Job’s friends offered—“they sat with him on the ground seven days and seven nights, and none spake a word unto him; for they saw that his grief was very great.”

“Mist clogs the sunshine,
Smoky dwarf houses
Hem me round everywhere.”

The next afternoon, some twenty miles from Milton-Northern, the train pulled into the narrow branch line that led to Heston. The town stretched out in a long, scattered street running parallel to the sea—a place with a character all its own, distinct from the genteel seaside resorts of southern England and even more so from those on the continent. It had a certain purposefulness, a Scottish word might say—*purpose-like*. Carts here were built with more iron, less wood and leather; their harnesses sleek and functional. Even the people, though come for pleasure, moved with a quiet urgency, as if every moment counted. The colours were duller—gray, enduring, not bright or decorative. There were no smock-frocks among the country folk; they had fallen out of use, impractical near machinery, too slow for a world that valued speed.

In southern towns, Margaret had often seen shopkeepers lounging at their doors, watching the passersby, enjoying the air. Here, if there was a lull in customers, they busied themselves inside—rolling and unrolling ribbons, polishing glass, tidying shelves. It was as if idleness was a sin.

These contrasts struck her deeply as she and her mother stepped out the next morning to search for lodgings. Their two nights at hotels had cost more than Mr. Hale had expected, and so they were eager to settle quickly. They took the first clean, cheerful rooms available—simple but comfortable, with a view of the sea.

For the first time in days, Margaret felt truly at ease. A soft dreaminess settled over her, making rest feel like luxury. The distant sea lapped the shore with a steady rhythm. Closer, the cries of donkey-boys echoed through the streets. Scenes passed before her eyes like fleeting pictures—she didn’t care to understand them fully, only to let them drift by. She walked down to the beach, breathing in the warm, salt-scented air, even at the end of November. The sea stretched endlessly, misty and pale, meeting a sky tinged with tender hues. A white sail glinted silver in a weak sunbeam. In that stillness, she could almost imagine living forever in this peaceful reverie—where the present was everything, and past and future alike faded away.

But the future could not be ignored, however harsh it might appear.

One evening, plans were made: the next day, Margaret and her father would travel to Milton-Northern to find a house. Mr. Hale had received letters from Mr. Bell and one or two from Mr. Thornton. He wished to speak directly with the latter to clarify his position and prospects in the town. Margaret understood they needed to move, yet she hesitated. The idea of a manufacturing town filled her with unease. She believed her mother was benefiting from the fresh air of Heston and would have gladly delayed the journey.

But the journey came.

Long before reaching Milton, a heavy, leaden cloud hung over the horizon. It stood out sharply against the pale gray-blue of the winter sky. Frost had already begun to touch the fields around Heston. As they neared the town, the air carried a faint tinge of smoke—not strong, perhaps, but unmistakable. It wasn’t so much the smell of fire as the absence of green things, the loss of grass and herbs beneath the breath of industry.

They were swept through endless, straight streets lined with small brick houses, identical and stark. Here and there rose a large, rectangular factory—many-windowed, towering over the homes like a hen among her chicks—spewing black, unparliamentary smoke into the sky. This, Margaret realized, explained the cloud she had thought might herald rain.

From station to hotel, the roads were choked. Heavy wagons and carts blocked the narrow thoroughfares. Every vehicle seemed burdened with cotton—raw in sacks, woven in bales of calico. People crowded the sidewalks, dressed neatly enough in fabric, but slovenly in posture and manner—different from the threadbare elegance of London’s working class.

“This is New Street,” Mr. Hale said. “I believe it’s the main artery of Milton. Bell has told me about it. Thirty years ago, when this lane was widened into a great road, his property soared in value. Mr. Thornton’s mill must be nearby—he’s Bell’s tenant. I suspect he began with the warehouse.”

“Where’s our hotel, Papa?”

“Near the end of this street, I think. Shall we have lunch before or after we look at the houses listed in the *Milton Times*?”

“Oh, let’s finish the work first.”

“Very well. I’ll just check for any notes from Mr. Thornton—he said he’d send word about the houses. Then we’ll go. We’ll keep the cab. It’s safer than getting lost and missing the train.”

There were no messages waiting.

They began their search. Thirty pounds a year was all they could afford. In Hampshire, such a sum might buy a spacious home with a garden. Here, even basic accommodations—two sitting rooms, four bedrooms—seemed impossible. They visited each house on their list, rejecting every one.

At last, they looked at each other, dismayed.

“We must return to the second choice,” Mr. Hale said. “The one in Crampton, isn’t it? That suburb? Three sitting rooms—remember how we laughed at that compared to three bedrooms?”

“I’ve worked it out,” Margaret replied. “The front room downstairs will be your study and our dining room—poor Papa! You know we decided Mamma should have the cheeriest sitting room possible. And the front room upstairs—despite the dreadful blue-and-pink paper and that heavy cornice—has a lovely view. It overlooks the plain, with a bend of river or canal below. I’ll take the little bedroom behind, in that projection above the kitchen. You and Mamma can share the room behind the drawing-room. And that closet in the attic—perfect for your dressing room.”

“But what about Dixon and the maid?”

“Wait—let me think. I’m overwhelmed by my own genius for planning. Dixon gets the back sitting room—I think she’ll like that. She complained so much about the stairs in Heston. And the girl can have the sloping attic over your room and Mamma’s. Won’t that do?”

“I suppose so. But the papers… such taste! And those enormous cornices!”

“Never mind, Papa. Surely you can persuade the landlord to re-paper the drawing-room and your bedroom—Mamma will spend most time there. Your bookshelves will hide much of the pattern in the dining room.”

“So you think it’s best? Then I’ll go at once to see Mr. Donkin—the landlord mentioned in the ad. I’ll take you back to the hotel. You can order lunch and rest. By the time it’s ready, I’ll be back. I hope I can get new paper.”

Margaret hoped so too, though she said nothing. She had never understood the love for ornament, however poor, over simplicity—something she believed to be the true foundation of elegance.

Her father guided her into the hotel, left her at the foot of the stairs, and hurried off to Canute Street.

Just as Margaret reached for the door of their room, a waiter approached.

“I beg your pardon, ma’am. The gentleman left so quickly, I couldn’t tell him. Mr. Thornton called right after you left. I told him you’d be back in an hour, and he returned five minutes ago. He said he’d wait for Mr. Hale. He’s in your room now.”

“Thank you. My father will return soon. You can inform him then.”

She opened the door and entered with her usual calm dignity. No awkwardness stirred within her—she was too accustomed to society for that. This was a man visiting on business. Since he had shown kindness, she meant to treat him with full courtesy.

Mr. Thornton, however, was taken aback. He had expected a quiet, middle-aged clergyman. Instead, a young woman stepped forward—tall, composed, radiant in her plainness. Her bonnet was of fine straw, trimmed simply with white ribbon. A large Indian shawl draped her shoulders like royal robes. Her expression was open, direct—unfazed by his presence. He did not know who she was, but saw at once that she regarded him with no alarm, no flush of surprise. Her face remained serene, pale ivory under the light.

He had heard Mr. Hale had a daughter—but imagined her a child.

“Mr. Thornton, I believe?” she said, pausing briefly while he struggled for words. “Please sit. My father brought me to the door just now, but he wasn’t told you were here. He’s gone on errands. He’ll return shortly. I’m sorry you’ve had to call twice.”

Though used to command himself, Thornton found himself yielding to her quiet authority. Moments earlier, he had been annoyed at the delay. Now, he sat at her request, calm.

“Do you know where your father went? Perhaps I could find him.”

“He’s gone to Mr. Donkin in Canute Street—the landlord of the house we’re considering in Crampton.”

Thornton knew the house. He had seen the advertisement, and inspected it at Mr. Bell’s request—and also out of personal interest in the case of a clergyman who had resigned his living under such circumstances as Mr. Hale’s. He had thought the house suitable. But now, seeing Margaret—her grace, her stillness, her way of holding herself—he felt ashamed of ever thinking it adequate for such a family. The house had flaws, yes, but now they seemed vulgar beside her.

She could not help her beauty. Yet her features—her short curled upper lip, the firm, upturned chin, the way she carried her head—gave strangers the impression of pride. She was tired, and wished only to rest, but she maintained her composure. She spoke courteously, though she longed for him to leave. He answered curtly, never expanding on a topic. She had removed her shawl and hung it over the chair. She sat facing him, facing the light. Her full beauty met his gaze—her white throat rising from a lithe figure, her lips moving gently, never breaking the cool serenity of her face. Her eyes, soft and shadowed, met his with quiet freedom.

Almost, he told himself, he didn’t like her. He resented her indifference, misreading it as contempt. He felt insulted, even angry—almost tempted to rise and walk out. He saw himself in her eyes as a rough man, lacking refinement. Her calmness he took as scorn.

Just as she ran out of things to say—conversation reduced to brief exchanges—her father entered.

With his gentle, courteous manner, Mr. Hale apologized and restored the balance. He and Thornton discussed their mutual friend, Mr. Bell. Margaret, relieved her role was over, moved to the window to observe the unfamiliar street.

She became so absorbed she barely heard her father.

“Margaret! The landlord insists on keeping that dreadful paper. I fear we must let it stay.”

“Oh dear!” she sighed. “I’m sorry.” She considered hiding it with sketches, but gave up—afraid it would only make matters worse.

Meanwhile, Mr. Hale, with his old-fashioned hospitality, invited Thornton to stay for lunch. It would have been inconvenient, but he felt he should accept—if only Margaret had encouraged it. She did not. He was glad, yet irritated.

She bowed low and gravely when he left. He felt more self-conscious than he ever had in his life.

“Well, Margaret,” said Mr. Hale, “to luncheon as fast as we can. Have you ordered?”

“No, Papa. That man was here when I arrived. I haven’t had a chance.”

“Then we’ll take whatever’s available. He must have waited a long time.”

“It seemed endless,” she said. “I was nearly speechless when you came in. He never expanded on anything—just short, abrupt answers.”

“Very to the point, I’d say. He’s clear-headed. He mentioned—did you hear—that Crampton is on gravelly soil, the healthiest suburb near Milton.”

Back in Heston, they recounted the day to Mrs. Hale, who questioned them eagerly between sips of tea.

“And what is Mr. Thornton like?” she asked.

“Ask Margaret,” said her husband.

“Oh, I hardly know,” Margaret replied lazily, too weary to describe. Then she roused herself. “He’s tall, broad-shouldered—about thirty, I’d say.”

“Thirty,” confirmed her father. “And his face—neither handsome nor plain. Nothing remarkable. Not quite a gentleman—but that wasn’t to be expected.”

“Not vulgar,” added Mr. Hale, slightly defensive. “Not common.”

“Oh, no,” said Margaret. “But with such strength and resolve, no face could be vulgar. I wouldn’t want to bargain with him—he looks inflexible. A man made for his place, Mamma—strong, sagacious, as becomes a great manufacturer.”

“Don’t call them tradesmen, Margaret,” said her father. “They’re very different.”

“Are they? I use the word for anyone who sells something tangible. But if you prefer, I wonI use it. Still—oh, Mamma—prepare yourself for our drawing-room paper. Pink and blue roses with yellow leaves! And that monstrous cornice!”

When they finally moved into their new house in Milton, the offensive papers were gone. The landlord accepted their thanks calmly. He let them believe he had changed his mind. But in truth, he had done nothing for a Reverend Mr. Hale unknown in Milton. Only one sharp word from Mr. Thornton—the wealthy manufacturer—had been enough.

“And it’s hame, hame, hame,
Hame fain wad I be.”

The soft, pale papering of the rooms was all that could make the house at Milton seem tolerable. But even that was not enough—there was something else missing, something that could never be replaced. The thick, yellow November fogs had settled in, swallowing the view of the valley and the sweeping curve of the river, leaving only a dull, smothered world outside the windows. When Mrs. Hale arrived, she found no comfort in her new home. Margaret and Dixon had been unpacking for two days, but inside, everything still felt chaotic and incomplete. Outside, the fog crept up to the very glass, swirling through every open door in choking waves of damp, unclean mist.

“Oh, Margaret!” Mrs. Hale exclaimed, her voice trembling with dismay. “Are we really to live here?”

Margaret’s heart echoed the sorrow in her mother’s tone. She struggled to reply, finally managing, “Oh, the fogs in London are often worse.”

“But then,” her mother whispered, “you knew London. You knew your friends were near. Here—here we are alone. Oh, Dixon, what a place this is!”

“Indeed, ma'am,” Dixon said, her face drawn with concern, “I’m sure it’ll be your death before long—and then I know who’ll—” She stopped herself abruptly. “Miss Hale, that’s far too heavy for you to lift.”

“No, thank you, Dixon,” Margaret replied, her voice cold and distant. “The best thing we can do now is get Mother’s room ready so she can rest. I’ll go fetch her some coffee.”

Mr. Hale, too, was disheartened. He sought Margaret out, seeking solace. “Margaret,” he sighed, standing by the window, “I truly believe this place is unhealthy. Suppose either your mother or you fall ill? I wish I had taken a post in the countryside—Wales, perhaps. This is unbearable.”

There was no comfort to give. They were here. They had no choice. The move, the fortnight spent at Heston—it had drained nearly all their savings. There was no turning back. That night, as Margaret lay awake, the weight of their situation pressed upon her like the smoky air that clung to her bedroom. Her room was narrow, tucked into a long projection at the rear of the house. The window looked directly across to another similar wall, just ten feet away. Through the fog, it loomed like a silent barrier between her and hope.

Inside, chaos reigned. All efforts had gone toward making her mother comfortable. Margaret sat on a box, its direction card from Helstone catching her eye—Helstone, beautiful, beloved Helstone! A wave of grief washed over her. Then, suddenly, she remembered a letter from Edith, half-read earlier in the day’s rush.

She opened it again. Edith wrote of their arrival in Corfu, of their voyage along the Mediterranean, of music and dancing on deck, of life unfolding in vibrant colour. She described her house with its trellised balcony, the white cliffs, the deep blue sea. She wrote fluently, if not vividly—enough detail to let Margaret see it all in her mind.

Captain Lennox and another newly married officer shared a villa high above the sea. Their days, even so late in the year, were filled with boating, picnics, outdoor pleasures—life lived freely, joyfully. Edith’s existence seemed as clear and boundless as the sky above her. Her husband attended drills; she, the most musical wife among them, copied popular English tunes for the bandmaster—a duty she treated with cheerful seriousness.

She hoped, if the regiment stayed another year, that Margaret might come visit. And she asked if Margaret remembered the day twelve months ago when she wrote—how it rained all day in Harley Street, how she refused to wear her new gown to a dinner party, afraid of getting it wet, and how it was at that very dinner they first met Captain Lennox.

Yes, Margaret remembered well. Edith and Mrs. Shaw had gone to dinner. Margaret had joined later. The memory flooded back—the grandeur of the house, the elegance of the furniture, the ease of the guests. It contrasted sharply with the present. That old life had closed behind them like a smooth, quiet sea, leaving no trace. The dinners, the visits, the dances—they continued, untouched, though Edith and Aunt Shaw were gone. And Margaret? She was barely missed.

She doubted anyone from that world thought of her—except Henry Lennox. He would try to forget her, she knew. He always claimed he could push away painful thoughts with ease. But the truth was, she had never loved him as a lover. If she had, and if their lives had changed as they had, she could not doubt that Mr. Lennox would have resented the shift in her father’s position. It would have wounded his pride.

Yet, though the thought stung, she could bear it. She believed in her father’s integrity. His mistakes, though serious, were rooted in good intent. Still, the world’s judgment—its rough, blunt dismissal of her father as fallen—would have galled Mr. Lennox deeply. As she imagined what might have been, she found herself grateful for what was.

They were at rock bottom. They could not sink lower. Edith’s astonishment, Aunt Shaw’s shock—when their letters came, she would meet them bravely.

She rose slowly, began undressing, allowing herself the luxury of time, though it was late. She fell asleep, hoping for light—some sign of warmth or hope. But if she had known how long the darkness would last, her heart would have broken.

The season was cruel to both body and spirit. Her mother caught a severe cold. Dixon, too, was clearly unwell, though Margaret dared not offer help—she would only insult her dignity. No girl could be found to assist. All young women worked in the factories. Those who applied were scolded by Dixon, who scorned the idea that such girls could ever serve in a gentleman’s household.

So they kept a charwoman almost constantly. Margaret longed to send for Charlotte—but the distance was too great, and Charlotte was more skilled than they could afford now.

Mr. Hale found pupils, recommended by Mr. Bell or Mr. Thornton. Most were boys just past school age. In Milton, it was believed a boy must be trained early to become a good tradesman. Sending him to university, especially Oxford or Cambridge, risked losing him to academic dreams. So sons were placed in mills, offices, warehouses at fourteen or fifteen, their education cut short to focus entirely on commerce.

Still, there were exceptions. Some parents saw their own shortcomings and tried to correct them. A few men, already in middle age, acknowledged their ignorance and began learning late in life.

Mr. Thornton was perhaps the oldest of Mr. Hale’s students—and certainly his favourite. Mr. Hale often quoted his opinions with such respect that it became a joke in the household: how much real teaching could fit into an hour when so much time was spent in conversation?

Margaret encouraged this lightness, knowing her mother watched Mr. Thornton’s friendship with unease. Once, her father’s attention belonged solely to his books and parishioners. Now, he eagerly awaited each meeting with Mr. Thornton. To her mother, it felt like neglect.

Mr. Hale’s praise had its usual effect: admiration turned to mild rebellion. After twenty years in a quiet country parsonage, the energy and power of Milton dazzled him. The machinery, the men—it all spoke of greatness, and he accepted it without questioning its cost.

But Margaret saw differently. She did not walk among the machines or the crowds. Instead, she encountered those who bore the brunt of progress—the ones trampled underfoot in the march of industry. The question, she thought, was not whether progress was made, but whether care had been taken to lessen the suffering of those left behind. Had they been gently moved aside, or crushed beneath the wheels?

It fell to Margaret to find a servant to help Dixon. At first, Dixon had promised to find exactly the right girl—one who would do the heavy work. But Dixon’s idea of a good servant was shaped by memories of tidy schoolgirls from Helstone, proud to be allowed into the parsonage, respectful almost to fear.

Dixon enjoyed that reverence, just as Louis XIV enjoyed the flattery of his courtiers. But in Milton, the girls who applied were blunt, independent, even bold. They questioned Dixon back, doubting the family’s means—how could they live in a house worth thirty pounds a year and yet keep two servants, one so haughty?

Mr. Hale was no longer seen as Vicar of Helstone, but as a man with a certain income. Margaret grew weary of Dixon’s endless reports of these applicants’ rudeness. She was repelled by their manners, offended by their directness, their curiosity about a family’s wealth. Yet, the more she felt insulted, the less she spoke. She preferred to handle the search herself, sparing her mother the pain of repeated disappointments.

So she went out, visiting butchers and grocers, searching for the perfect girl. Each week, her hopes sank lower. In a town like Milton, where factory work offered better pay and greater freedom, few wanted to be domestic servants.

It was hard for Margaret to walk alone in such a busy place. Her aunt’s strict rules had always required a footman when going beyond Harley Street. Though she had rebelled silently against those limits, enjoying the freedom of the forest walks—running through the trees, pausing to watch birds or listen to the wind—now she had to adjust to the rigid pace of city streets.

But the real challenge was not the pace—it was the people. The side of town where Crampton stood was crowded with factory workers. From the mills nearby poured streams of men and women twice or thrice a day.

At first, Margaret was startled by their boldness. They laughed loudly, joked openly, especially at anyone who seemed above them. Their voices rang out, unbound by polite restraint. The girls, with their rough but friendly ways, would comment on her dress, even touch her shawl to feel the fabric. Once, a girl asked her about a particular article she admired.

There was such simple trust in their questions, such belief in her kindness, that Margaret found herself answering, even smiling back.

She didn’t mind the girls. But the men—those she feared and resented. They didn’t comment on her clothes, but on her face, in the same open, fearless way.

She, who had always considered any remark on her appearance an impertinence, now had to endure undisguised admiration. But their bluntness revealed innocence—not malice. Their words were honest, not meant to wound.

One day, after passing several men who called her “my sweetheart,” one lingered and said, “Your bonny face, my lass, makes the day look brighter.”

Another day, as she smiled to herself, a poorly-dressed, middle-aged worker said, “You may well smile, my lass; many a one would smile to have such a bonny face.”

He looked so worn that she smiled back, glad her looks could bring him joy. He seemed to understand, and from then on, whenever they met, a silent understanding passed between them. They never spoke again—only that one compliment—but Margaret found herself looking at him with more interest than anyone else in Milton.

On Sundays, she sometimes saw him walking with a girl—his daughter, thin and pale, clearly unwell.

One spring morning, Margaret and her father had walked to the fields beyond the town. She gathered wildflowers—dog-violets, lesser celandines—her heart heavy with longing for the south. Her father left her to attend business in Milton. On her way home, she met the man and his daughter.

The girl stared at the flowers. Without thinking, Margaret offered them.

Her eyes lit up. Her father spoke for her. “Thank yo, Miss. Bessy’ll think a deal o’ them flowers; that hoo will. And I shall think a deal o’ yor kindness. Yo’re not of this country, I reckon?”

“No,” Margaret said softly. “I come from the South—from Hampshire.”

“That’s beyond London, I reckon? And I come fro’ Burnleyways, forty miles to th’ North. And yet, yo see, North and South has both met and made kind o’ friends in this big smoky place.”

She slowed her pace to walk beside them, the girl’s steps weak and slow. Margaret spoke to the girl, her voice full of gentle pity.

“I’m afraid you are not very strong.”

“No,” the girl said quietly, “nor never will be.”

“Spring is coming,” Margaret said, trying to offer hope.

“Spring nor summer will do me good,” the girl replied.

Margaret looked at the man, expecting him to contradict her. But he said, “I’m afeared hoo speaks the truth. I’m afeared hoo’s too far gone in a waste.”

“I shall have a spring where I am boun’ to, and flowers, and amaranths, and shining robes besides.”

“Poor lass, poor lass,” the father murmured. “I’m none so sure o’ that; but it’s a comfort to thee, poor lass, poor lass. Poor father! it’ll be soon.”

Margaret was shocked—but not repelled. She felt drawn to them.

“Where do you live? We must be neighbours—we meet so often.”

“We put up at nine Frances Street, second turn to th’ left after yo’ve past th’ Goulden Dragon.”

“And your name? I must not forget that.”

“I’m none ashamed o’ my name. It’s Nicholas Higgins. Hoo’s called Bessy Higgins. Whatten yo’ asking for?”

Margaret hesitated. At Helstone, it would have been understood that she would call upon a neighbour whose name and address she’d asked for.

“I thought—I meant to come and see you.”

She felt shy, unsure. Was it an impertinence?

“I’m none so fond of having strange folk in my house,” the man said.

Then, seeing her flush, he relented. “Yo’re a foreigner, as one may say, and maybe don’t know many folk here. And yo’ve given my wench here flowers out of yo’r own hand—yo may come if yo like.”

Margaret was amused and nettled. She wasn’t sure she wanted to accept permission given as a favour.

But when they reached the turn into Frances Street, the girl stopped. “Yo’ll not forget yo’re to come and see us.”

“Aye, aye,” the father snapped. “Hoo’ll come. Hoo’s a bit set up now, because hoo thinks I might ha’ spoken more civilly; but hoo’ll think better on it, and come. I can read her proud bonny face like a book. Come along, Bess; there’s the mill bell ringing.”

Margaret returned home, wondering at her new acquaintances, smiling at the man’s insight into her thoughts.

From that day on, Milton began to feel less bleak. It was not the sun, nor time, nor habit. It was the human connection she had found—a spark of warmth in the smoke and fog.

“Let China’s earth, enriched with coloured stains,
Pencil’d with gold, and streaked with azure veins,
The grateful flavour of the Indian leaf,
Or Mocha’s sunburnt berry glad receive.”

The morning after her encounter with Higgins and his daughter, Mr. Hale ascended to the small drawing-room at an unseasonable hour. He moved slowly from one object to another—examining a framed print, adjusting a book on the shelf, lingering over a vase—as if in quiet contemplation. Yet Margaret, watching him from her seat near the window, knew this was no idle inspection. It was a nervous habit, a way of delaying the moment when he would speak what he both longed and dreaded to say.

At last, he turned to her, his voice gentle but firm: “My dear, I’ve invited Mr. Thornton to tea this evening.”

Mrs. Hale sat back in her armchair, eyes closed, her face drawn with the familiar ache that had settled upon her lately like a shadow. But at her husband’s words, she stirred abruptly, her voice rising in irritation. “Mr. Thornton? And tonight? What possible reason could he have for coming here? And Dixon is washing my finest muslins and laces—there’s no soft water, not with these dreadful east winds that seem determined to blow all winter through Milton.”

“The wind is turning,” said Mr. Hale, glancing out the window where smoke drifted steadily from the east. Though he still struggled with the compass points, he made them fit his mood as best he could.

“Don’t tell me!” Mrs. Hale shuddered, pulling her shawl tighter around her shoulders. “But whether it’s east or west, I suppose this man comes.”

“Oh, Mama,” Margaret replied, stepping forward, “you’ve never seen Mr. Thornton. He’s the kind of man who thrives on opposition—on storms, on enemies, on hardship. The worse the weather, the more certain we are to see him. But don’t worry—I’ll help Dixon. I’m becoming quite skilled at starching. And he won’t need any grand entertainment beyond talking with Papa. Papa, I confess I’m eager to meet your Pythias—your Damon, as you once called him. I’ve only seen him once before, and we were so awkward, so unsure of what to say, that our conversation didn’t go far.”

“I’m not sure you’d ever find him agreeable, Margaret,” Mr. Hale said thoughtfully. “He’s no man of fashion. Not a lady’s man.”

Margaret smiled, lifting her chin slightly. “I’ve never cared much for ladies’ men, Papa. But Mr. Thornton isn’t here as a suitor—he’s here as your friend, someone who respects you deeply.”

“The only person in Milton,” Mrs. Hale added dryly, “who does.”

“So we shall welcome him warmly,” said Margaret. “And we’ll serve cocoa-nut cakes—Dixon will be flattered to make them. And I’ll iron your caps, Mama, if you’ll let me.”

All morning, Margaret found herself wishing Mr. Thornton were miles away. She had planned other things: writing to Edith, reading Dante, visiting the Higginses. Instead, she stood at the ironing board, listening to Dixon’s endless complaints about the hardness of the water, the stiffness of the linen, the injustice of life. She forced herself to listen, to nod, to respond with sympathy—anything to keep the woman from venturing into deeper sorrows, lest they stir Mrs. Hale’s pain anew.

She had to remind herself again and again of her father’s regard for Mr. Thornton, to hold back the growing weariness that pressed against her temples, threatening one of the headaches that had begun to plague her. When at last she sat down, she told her mother, half-jokingly, “I’m no longer Peggy the laundry-maid. I’m Margaret Hale—the lady.”

She meant it as a jest—but the words struck too close to home. Her mother looked up sharply, her expression serious. “Yes,” she said, “if anyone had told me, when I was Miss Beresford, one of the finest young women in the countryside, that my child would spend half a day bent over a kitchen stove, scrubbing clothes like a servant, just to prepare for receiving a tradesman—and that this tradesman would be the only one in Milton who truly appreciated your father…”

“Oh, Mama!” Margaret rose quickly, her voice tight with emotion. “Don’t punish me for such careless talk. I don’t mind ironing—or any work—for you and Papa. I am still a lady, born and bred, even if I must scour floors or wash dishes. I’m tired now—just for a little while—but in half an hour, I’ll be ready to do it all again. And as for Mr. Thornton being in trade—well, he can’t help that. Poor fellow. I doubt his education prepared him for anything else.”

She turned and walked slowly to her room, needing silence, needing space. For now, she could endure no more.

Meanwhile, in Mr. Thornton’s house, a scene unfolded that mirrored yet contrasted with the Hales’. In a large, handsomely furnished dining-room, a woman of strong build and sharp features sat mending a fine tablecloth. She was past middle age, her presence commanding, her movements deliberate. Her face, though not soft, carried a dignity that drew attention—passersby often paused to watch her pass, rigid and purposeful, on her way to some fixed destination.

She wore stout black silk, every thread intact, every seam perfect. She held the cloth aloft, examining it under the light, searching for thin spots that required careful stitching. No books littered the room—only six volumes of Matthew Henry’s Bible commentaries, arranged neatly on the sideboard between a tea-urn and a lamp. From another room came the sound of piano practice—a rapid, uneven performance, each third note missing or blurred, the final chords often false, yet played with unwavering conviction.

A step echoed in the hall—decisive, heavy, unmistakably masculine.

“John! Is that you?” she called.

Her son entered, closing the door behind him. “What brings you home so early? I thought you were going to tea with Mr. Bell’s friend—Mr. Hale?”

“That’s right,” John said. “But I came home to dress.”

“Dress?” she scoffed. “When I was a girl, young men dressed once a day. Why should you dress to take tea with an old parson?”

“Mr. Hale is a gentleman,” John replied calmly. “His wife and daughter are ladies.”

“Wife and daughter?” she frowned. “Do they teach? What do they do? You’ve never mentioned them.”

“No, Mother. I’ve never met Mrs. Hale. I’ve only seen Miss Hale for a brief moment.”

“Then take care you don’t fall for a penniless girl, John.”

“I’m not easily caught,” he said, “as you well know. But I won’t have Miss Hale spoken of in that tone—it offends me. I’ve never known any young woman try to catch me, nor do I believe any has ever wasted the effort.”

Mrs. Thornton refused to yield. Pride, perhaps, kept her from conceding. “Well, I only warn you. Perhaps our Milton girls are too proud to fish for husbands—but this Miss Hale comes from the aristocratic counties, where rich husbands are counted prizes.”

John’s brow furrowed. “Mother,” he said, a short laugh escaping him, “you’ll make me confess. The only time I saw Miss Hale, she treated me with a haughty politeness that tasted of contempt. She held herself apart from me as if I were a lowly vassal, unwashed and unclean. Be at ease—she doesn’t interest me.”

“No?” she retorted. “I am not at ease. What right has she—a clergyman’s daughter, no less—to look down on you? If I were you, I’d dress for none of them—such a saucy set!”

As he turned to leave, he said, “Mr. Hale is good, gentle, learned. He is not saucy. As for Mrs. Hale—I’ll tell you what she’s like tonight, if you care to hear.”

He shut the door behind him.

“Despise my son? Treat him as a vassal?” Mrs. Thornton muttered, her voice low but fierce. “Humph! I’d like to know where she could find another like him. Boy and man, he’s the noblest, strongest heart I’ve ever known. I may be his mother—but I can see clearly. I know what Fanny was. I know what John is. Despise him? I hate her.”

“We are the trees whom shaking fastens more.” — GEORGE HERBERT.

Mr. Thornton left his home without entering the dining-room again. He was running late and hurried toward Crampton, eager not to seem disrespectful to his new friend by any delay. The church clock struck half-past seven as he stood at the door, waiting for Dixon’s slow approach—always twice as sluggish when she had to answer the bell, a task that seemed beneath her dignity. Once inside, he was welcomed warmly by Mr. Hale, who led him to his wife. Her pale face and shawl-wrapped figure spoke silently of weariness, explaining the quiet reserve in her greeting.

Margaret was lighting the lamp when he entered, for twilight had settled in. The flame cast a gentle glow across the dim room, where the night sky remained visible through the windows—an old country habit, untroubled by modern enclosures. This space contrasted sharply with the heavy, masculine dining-room he had just left: one filled only with furniture suited to meals, dominated by his mother’s presence, and devoid of warmth or life beyond eating and drinking. Here, though, the air was softer. No mirrors gleamed, no gilded frames reflected light like water in a painting; instead, rich, earthy tones painted the walls, softened by the familiar Helstone chintz curtains and chair covers. A davenport stood open near the window opposite the door; beside it, a tall white vase held drooping ivy, pale birch, and copper-hued beech leaves. Baskets of needlework were scattered about, and books—chosen not for their bindings but for their contents—lay on a table as if recently set down. Near the door, a tea table was laid out with care: a crisp white cloth, cocoa-nut cakes, and a basket piled high with oranges and bright red apples resting on green leaves.

To Mr. Thornton, these details spoke of a household shaped by thoughtful habits—especially those of Margaret. She stood by the tea table in a muslin dress, touched here and there with pink, her attention fixed on the cups rather than the conversation. Her hands, smooth and ivory-white, moved quietly among the china, delicate and precise. On one slender arm, a bracelet slipped down over her wrist. Mr. Thornton watched its movement—not the words being spoken, but how she pushed it up impatiently until it tightened against her skin, then how it slowly fell again. It fascinated him. He almost whispered, “There it goes again,” so clearly did he see the rhythm of it.

Once tea was prepared, there was little left to do. He found himself wishing the ritual could last longer, so he might watch her more closely. When she handed him his cup, her expression was proud, almost defiant—a slave unwilling to serve, yet dutiful. But her eyes caught the moment he needed another refill, and he felt a sudden urge to ask her to do for him what she did for her father: the man took her tiny fingers gently in his large hand and used them like sugar tongs. Margaret’s eyes lifted to her father’s, full of laughter and affection, unaware they were being watched.

She still ached from her illness—her pallor and silence made it plain—but she refused to let the evening falter. If there was a pause, she would step forward, even if only to pour tea, so that no one would think her father’s guest neglected. Yet conversation flowed steadily, and once the tea things were cleared away, she retreated to a corner near her mother, picking up her work. Now she could let her mind wander freely, safe from sudden summons.

Mr. Thornton and Mr. Hale were deep in discussion, continuing a topic begun at their last meeting. Margaret was drawn back into the present by a low remark from her mother. Looking up from her embroidery, she noticed the striking contrast between the two men—their appearances revealing such different natures.

Her father was slight, which made him seem taller than he truly was, especially beside Mr. Thornton’s broad, powerful frame. His features were soft, lines wavering like ripples on water, showing every shift of feeling. His eyelids were long and arched, lending his gaze a dreamy, almost feminine beauty. His brows were finely curved, raised far above his eyes by the size of the lids themselves.

Mr. Thornton, in contrast, had firm, straight brows that hovered low over deep-set, clear eyes—intent, observant, capable of seeing right through a thing. His face bore few lines, but those that existed were sharp and carved, like stone, mostly around his mouth, which was often slightly compressed. His teeth were perfect, white, and strong—so much so that when he smiled, the transformation was startling. One instant, he looked stern, resolute, ready to face any challenge; the next, a flash of honest joy lit his eyes, radiant and childlike. Margaret liked that smile—it was the first thing she had admired in this new acquaintance. And the differences in their looks seemed to explain why they so clearly respected each other.

She rearranged her mother’s knitting, then returned to her thoughts—unnoticed by Mr. Thornton, who was utterly absorbed in describing the steam-hammer. He spoke of its immense power and delicate precision, likening it to the genies of the Arabian Nights—capable of stretching from earth to sky one moment, then shrinking into a child’s palm the next.

“And this imagination, this practical realization of grand ideas,” he said, “came from one man’s mind in our town. That very man has the strength to climb higher, step by step, toward greater wonders. And I’ll wager we have many others who, if he were gone, could take his place and carry on the fight that forces all physical power to bow to science.”

“That reminds me,” said Mr. Hale, quoting, “‘I’ve a hundred captains in England,’ he said, ‘As good as ever was he.’”

Margaret looked up, startled. How had they jumped from machinery to Chevy Chace?

“It is no boast,” replied Mr. Thornton. “It’s fact. I’m proud to belong to a district whose needs give rise to such bold thinking. I’d rather struggle here—fail, suffer, even—toil under pressure, than live a dull, comfortable life in the South, where society moves slowly, weighed down by tradition and ease. One may be clogged with honey and unable to fly.”

“You’re mistaken,” said Margaret, stirred by his criticism of the South. A flush rose in her cheeks, tears pricked her eyes. “You don’t know the South. If there’s less excitement, less progress, perhaps—though I suppose I shouldn’t say that—there is also less suffering. I see men here whose faces tell of crushing sorrow, of hatred born from injustice. In the South, yes, we have poor people too, but their faces don’t carry that bitter sense of wrong. You don’t know the South, Mr. Thornton,” she concluded, collapsing into silence, ashamed of having spoken so much.

“And may I say you do not know the North?” he asked, his tone unexpectedly gentle, sensing he had wounded her.

She stayed silent, longing for the quiet hills and green fields of Hampshire, the memories so vivid they threatened to make her voice tremble.

“At least,” Mrs. Hale said, “you must admit Milton is far dirtier and smokier than any town in the South.”

“I fear I must concede its cleanliness,” Mr. Thornton replied, flashing his quick smile. “But Parliament commands us to burn our smoke. So, like good children, we shall obey—eventually.”

“But I thought you said you’d altered your chimneys to consume the smoke?” asked Mr. Hale.

“I did it before Parliament interfered. It cost money at first, but now it saves coal. I doubt I’d have done it otherwise. I’d have waited to be reported and fined—gone through all the trouble of yielding only when forced. But laws enforced by informers and fines become useless because of their ugliness. I suspect no chimney in Milton has been reported in five years, even though some still spew a third of their coal as ‘unparliamentary’ smoke.”

“I can only say it’s impossible to keep muslin blinds clean here for more than a week,” said Mrs. Hale. “At Helstone, we’ve kept ours up for a month, and they’re still fresh.”

“And your hands, Margaret—how many times did you wash them this morning before twelve?”

“Three times, mamma.”

“You seem to have a strong objection to Acts of Parliament and legislation affecting your way of managing things here,” said Mr. Hale.

“Yes, I do—and many others do too. And rightly so. The cotton trade’s machinery—no, not the iron kind, but the system—is so new that it’s no surprise it doesn’t run smoothly everywhere. Seventy years ago, what was it? And now, what is it not? Raw materials came together. Men of equal standing suddenly became masters and workers, thanks to foresight and opportunity. Some saw the future hidden in Arkwright’s crude model. That rapid growth gave early manufacturers vast power—not just over workers, but over buyers, over markets worldwide. Take, for instance, an ad from fifty years ago in a Milton paper: ‘So-and-so will close his warehouse at noon. All customers must come before.’ Imagine someone dictating *when* he would sell. Today, if a loyal customer came at midnight, I’d rise and stand hat in hand to receive his order.”

Margaret’s lip twitched, but she couldn’t look away. She was caught.

“I mention this to show the immense power manufacturers held at the start of the century. They were dizzy with it. Success in business didn’t mean balance in character. Often, justice and humility were buried under waves of wealth. Tales of wild extravagance during gala days are common. There’s no denying the tyranny they exercised over workers. As the saying goes, ‘Set a beggar on horseback, and he’ll ride to the devil.’ Some of these early factory owners rode to hell in style—crushing people under their horses’ hooves without remorse. But then came a reaction. More factories, more masters, more workers. Power balanced. Now, the battle is fairly waged between us. We won’t submit to an umpire, much less to a meddler who knows nothing of the facts—even if that meddler is called the High Court of Parliament.”

“Is it necessary to call it a battle between classes?” asked Mr. Hale. “I see from your use of the word that it reflects your true view.”

“It is true. And I believe it’s as necessary as wisdom fighting ignorance, or virtue battling improvidence. One great strength of our system is that a working man can rise to masterhood through his own effort. Anyone who controls himself, lives soberly, and does his duty can join our ranks—not always as a master, but as overseer, clerk, bookkeeper—on the side of order.”

“So you consider all who fail to rise, for whatever reason, as your enemies?” Margaret asked, her voice clear and cold.

“As their own enemies,” he answered quickly, stung by the pride in her tone. But instantly, his honesty made him feel his reply was weak. He owed her a better explanation, even if she disapproved. Yet how could he separate her interpretation from his meaning? The clearest way would be to speak of his own life—but was that too personal for strangers?

Still, truth demanded it. With a flicker of embarrassment coloring his dark cheek, he began:

“I speak from experience. Sixteen years ago, my father died in terrible circumstances. I was pulled from school and forced to grow up overnight. I had a mother unlike most—strong, determined. We moved to a small town where life was cheaper, and I found work in a draper’s shop—a fine place, actually, to learn about goods. Each week, our income was fifteen shillings. Three people lived on that. My mother managed so well that I saved three shillings every week. That was the beginning—that taught me self-denial. Now, when I can afford my mother comforts her age demands, I silently thank her for the discipline she gave me.

When I reflect that my success came not from luck, talent, or merit—but from habits that taught me to scorn indulgence unless earned—I believe the suffering Miss Hale sees in Milton’s people is simply the natural consequence of pleasure enjoyed dishonestly in earlier years. I don’t hate self-indulgent people. I despise them—for their weakness of character.”

“But you had the foundation of a good education,” said Mr. Hale. “Your enthusiasm for Homer shows you’re not reading it as a stranger. You’ve read it before.”

“That’s true—I stumbled through it at school. I was considered a decent classicist then, though I’ve lost most of it since. But I ask you—what good was that knowledge for the life I faced? None. Utterly none. On education, any man who can read and write starts level with me in useful knowledge.”

“Well, I disagree. Perhaps I’m a bit of a pedant. Did the heroism of Homeric life inspire you?”

“Not at all!” Mr. Thornton laughed. “I was too busy surviving. The living pressed close, neck to neck, in the fight for bread. Only now, with my mother safe and at peace, can I enjoy those old stories fully.”

“I suppose,” said Mr. Hale, “my comment came from the professional belief that nothing beats leather.”

When Mr. Thornton rose to leave, he extended his hand to shake with Mr. and Mrs. Hale. Then, turning to Margaret, he reached out as if to bid her farewell in the same way. It was a customary gesture in their world—but Margaret wasn’t expecting it. She merely bowed. The moment she saw his hand hesitate and pull back, she regretted not knowing his intent.

He walked away, unaware of her disappointment. Standing tall, he muttered to himself as he stepped from the house:

“A more proud, disagreeable girl I never saw. Even her beauty is blotted out by her scornful manner.”

“There’s iron, they say, in all our blood,
And a grain or two perhaps is good;
But his, he makes me harshly feel,
Has got a little too much of steel.”

“Margaret!” Mr. Hale called as he returned from showing their guest downstairs. “I couldn’t help watching your face with some concern when Mr. Thornton confessed to having once been a shop-boy. I knew the story already from Mr. Bell, so I was prepared for it—but I half expected you to rise and leave the room.”

“Oh, papa! You don’t mean to say you thought me so shallow?” Margaret replied, her voice warm with surprise. “Honestly, I liked that part of his story more than anything else he said. Everything else repelled me—so cold, so hard. But when he spoke of himself, he did so simply, without pretence, without the kind of showiness that often masks vulgarity in people of trade. And the way he spoke of his mother—so tender, so respectful—that made me feel less inclined to walk out than when he was talking about Milton as if no other place existed, or calmly condemning others for wastefulness while never considering it his duty to lift them up, to give them the same guidance his own mother gave him. No—he telling us he’d once worked in a shop was, in fact, the most honest and human thing he said.”

Her mother looked at her sharply. “I’m astonished, Margaret,” she said. “You who always criticized people for being ‘shoppy’ at Helstone! I don’t think, Mr. Hale, that you were quite right in introducing such a man into our home without warning us of his past. I confess I was afraid to show how deeply some of his words disturbed me. When he mentioned his father dying in ‘miserable circumstances’—well, it might have been in the workhouse.”

“It may well have been worse,” her husband replied quietly. “I learned much of what came before from Mr. Bell before we arrived here. Since he’s shared part of it, I’ll tell you the rest. His father gambled recklessly, failed, and then took his own life—unable to bear the shame. His former friends vanished when the truth came out: debts owed through reckless speculation, money borrowed from others to recoup losses. No one stepped forward to help the widow and her children. There was another child, a girl, too young to earn. Yet no hand reached out—not immediately. Mrs. Thornton, I suspect, isn’t the sort to wait for kindness that comes late. So they left Milton. I knew he had gone into a shop, and that his earnings, along with a small portion of property secured for his mother, kept them alive for years. Mr. Bell said they lived on water-porridge for years—how they managed, he didn’t know. But long after creditors had given up hope of recovering old Mr. Thornton’s debts—perhaps they never truly hoped after the suicide—this young man returned to Milton. He quietly visited each creditor, paid the first instalment, and continued until every debt was settled. No fanfare, no gathering of men—just silence and diligence. One creditor, an old, gruff man, even took him in as a partner, which helped greatly.”

“That is remarkable,” Margaret murmured. “It’s a pity such a nature should be warped by his position as a manufacturer in Milton.”

“How warped?” asked her father.

“Oh, papa, by measuring everything by wealth. When he talked of machinery, he saw only tools to expand trade and increase profit. The poor around him—he sees them not as suffering, but as flawed, lacking self-control, outside his sympathy because they lack the iron will he believes gives him success.”

“Not flawed,” corrected her father. “He said improvident and self-indulgent.”

Margaret was collecting her mother’s sewing things, preparing for bed. As she turned to go, she hesitated. She wanted to speak—to acknowledge something she believed would please her father—but doing so fully meant admitting a discomfort. Still, she spoke. “Papa, I do think Mr. Thornton is a remarkable man. But personally—I don’t like him at all.”

“And I do!” laughed her father. “Personally, as you say. I don’t make him a hero. But goodnight, child. Your mother looks worn tonight, Margaret.”

She had noticed her mother’s fatigue for days. Her father’s words sent her upstairs with a quiet dread settling in her chest. Life in Milton was so different from Helstone—no longer open air and green fields, but smog-filled streets and constant domestic strain. The very air felt heavy, lifeless. The burdens on the women in the household were new, sordid, relentless. It was no wonder her mother seemed weakened.

There were other signs too. Secret meetings between Mrs. Hale and Dixon, during which the maid would emerge tearful and agitated. Once, Margaret slipped into her mother’s room soon after Dixon left and found her on her knees—praying, she could hear, for strength and patience to endure great pain. A prayer for endurance. Not for healing.

Margaret longed to restore the closeness they once shared—the bond broken by her years with Aunt Shaw. She tried gentle touches, soft words, but though her mother responded warmly, there was a hidden sorrow, a guarded secret. And Margaret suspected it concerned her health.

That night, she lay awake, planning ways to ease the toll Milton was taking. A servant to assist Dixon permanently—she would search for one herself, even if it meant visiting every registry office, speaking to anyone, however unlikely. For several days, this became her mission.

One afternoon, she met Bessy Higgins on the street and stopped.

“Well, Bessy, how are you? Better now the wind has shifted?”

“Better and not better,” Bessy replied, her voice low. “I’m better for not coughing myself apart at night. But I’m tired—so weary of Milton. Longing to escape to the land of Beulah. And when I think I’m farther off than ever, my heart sinks—and I’m worse.”

Margaret walked beside her, silent for a moment. Then softly: “Bessy… do you wish to die?”

She shrank from the thought herself—life was precious, especially to one young and strong. But Bessy remained still for a time, then whispered: “If you’d lived as I have, grown tired as I am, thought sometimes, *maybe this goes on fifty, sixty years*—and seen each year spin round, mocking you with its endless hours—oh, wench, you’d be glad enough when the doctor said he feared you’d never see another winter.”

“Why, Bessy, what kind of life have you led?”

“Nought worse than many others’, I reckon. Only I’ve fought against it. Others just accepted.”

“But what was it? I’m new here—I don’t understand the ways of Milton as you do.”

“If you’d come to our house when you promised, I could’ve told you. But father says you’re just like the rest—out of sight, out of mind.”

“I don’t know who the rest are,” Margaret said gently. “I’ve been busy. And I must admit—I forgot my promise.”

“You offered it. We asked for nothing.”

“I did forget—for a time. But I’ll remember again. May I come with you now?”

Bessy studied her face—searching for sincerity. The sharpness in her eyes softened into longing. “I’ve none to care for me. If you care—you may come.”

They walked in silence. At the entrance to a grimy courtyard, Bessy said, “Don’t be startled if Father’s home. He speaks rough at first. But he liked you—he thought a lot of your coming. And because he liked you, he was put out.”

“I won’t be daunted,” Margaret said.

But Nicholas was not there. Instead, a younger, taller girl stood over the wash-tub, banging furniture, making noise. Margaret winced—sympathizing with Bessy, who sank into a chair, exhausted.

She asked the sister for water. While the girl rushed off—knocking over fire-irons, tripping over chairs—Margaret unfastened Bessy’s bonnet strings, easing her labored breathing.

“Do you think a life like this is worth living?” Bessy gasped.

Margaret said nothing. She held the cup to her lips.

Bessy drank deeply, then fell back, eyes closed. Margaret heard her murmur: “They shall hunger no more, neither thirst any more. Neither shall the sun light on them, nor any heat.”

Margaret leaned down. “Don’t be impatient, Bessy. Whatever your life has been—remember who gave it to you. Who shaped it.”

A voice behind her—Nicholas had entered unseen. “Now, I’ll not have my wench preached to. She’s bad enough with her dreams and visions—cities with golden gates and jewels. But if it amuses her, let it be. But I won’t have more nonsense poured into her.”

“But surely,” Margaret turned, “you believe God gave her life, and determined its course?”

“I believe what I see,” Nicholas said bluntly. “No more. That’s my faith. I don’t believe all I hear—no, not by a long stretch. I did hear a young woman offer to visit us. My daughter thought a lot of it—flushed up many times when she heard a stranger’s step. But now she’s come—and she’s welcome, so long as she keeps from preaching about things she knows nothing of.”

Bessy had watched Margaret closely. Now she sat up slightly, laying a trembling hand on her arm. “Don’t be angry with him. Many think like him—many here. If you could hear them talk, you wouldn’t be shocked. He’s a good man—my father—but oh!” she cried, sinking back, “what he says sometimes makes me long to die more than ever. I want to know so much—and yet I’m tossed about with wonder.”

“Poor wench—poor old wench,” Nicholas muttered. “I hate to vex thee. But a man must speak truth. When I see the world going wrong—busy with things it doesn’t understand, leaving undone what lies right before it—I say: stop this talk about religion. Work on what you see. That’s my creed. Simple. Close at hand. Easy to follow.”

But Bessy clung harder to Margaret. “Don’t judge him—don’t think ill of him. He’s a good man. I sometimes think I’ll be moped with sorrow in the City of God if he’s not there.”

Color flared in her cheeks, fire in her eyes. “But you will be there, father! You shall! Oh, my heart!”

She pressed a hand to her chest and went pale as death.

Margaret cradled her, laid her head upon her bosom, brushed hair from her temples, bathed them with water. Nicholas understood her gestures instantly—love taught him quick recognition. Even the younger sister moved slowly, gently, at Margaret’s quiet “hush.”

The spasm passed. Bessy stirred. “I’ll go to bed—it’s best. But,” she grasped Margaret’s gown, “you’ll come again—won’t you? Just say it!”

“I will come tomorrow,” Margaret said.

Bessy leaned back into her father’s arms as he lifted her toward the stairs. But as Margaret rose to leave, Nicholas struggled to speak. “I could wish there were a God—if only to ask Him to bless thee.”

She walked home, heavy-hearted, late for tea. At Helstone, such lateness would have drawn a scolding. But now, even these small things seemed to matter less. She almost missed the familiar complaints.

“Have you found a servant, dear?” her mother asked.

“No, mamma. Anne Buckley wouldn’t do.”

“Suppose I try,” said Mr. Hale. “Everyone else has had a turn at this difficulty. Let me try. I may be the Cinderella who fits the slipper.”

Margaret could barely smile—her thoughts still haunted by the Higginses.

“What would you do, papa? How would you begin?”

“I’d ask a good housekeeper to recommend someone known to her or her staff.”

“Very good. But we must first catch our house-mother.”

“You’ve caught her. Or rather, she’s walking into the trap. You’ll catch her tomorrow—if you’re clever.”

“What do you mean, Mr. Hale?” his wife asked, intrigued.

“My paragon pupil—Margaret calls him that—has told me his mother intends to call on us tomorrow.”

“Mrs. Thornton!” exclaimed Mrs. Hale.

“The mother he spoke of?” asked Margaret.

“Mrs. Thornton—the only mother he has, I believe,” Mr. Hale said quietly.

“I’d like to meet her,” said Mrs. Hale. “She must be an extraordinary woman.”

“Perhaps she has a relation who might suit us,” Margaret added. “She seemed so careful, so economical. Anyone from her family might be ideal.”

“My dear,” Mr. Hale said, alarmed. “Please don’t go down that path. I suspect Mrs. Thornton is as proud and aloof in her way as our little Margaret is in hers. She likely ignores entirely the poverty and hardship her son speaks of. I’m certain she wouldn’t want strangers to know about it.”

“Take note,” Margaret said, “that’s not my kind of pride, if I have any at all—which I deny, though you’re always accusing me of it.”

“I don’t know for sure,” her father replied, “but from little hints he’s dropped, I suspect it’s hers.”

They cared too little to ask how he spoke of her. Margaret only wondered if she must stay in to receive the visitor—knowing it would delay her visit to Bessy until late. And then she remembered: her mother must not be left alone to entertain such a distinguished guest.

CHAPTER XII. MORNING CALLS. “Well—I suppose we must.” FRIENDS IN COUNCIL.

Mr. Thornton had found it no easy task to persuade his mother into making a social call. She was not one for visiting, and when she did, it was with all the solemnity of a ceremonial duty. Her son had provided her with a carriage, but she refused to keep horses for it—only hiring them for rare occasions such as morning or evening calls. Just three days earlier, she had used horses to visit all her acquaintances in Milton, leaving them to reciprocate in kind if they wished. Yet now, the Hales lived too far away for walking, and so she had repeatedly questioned her son whether his desire for her to call on them was strong enough to justify the expense of hiring a cab.

“I’d be glad if it weren’t,” she said, “for I see no reason to make friends with every schoolteacher and master in town. Next thing you’ll want me calling on Fanny’s dancing-master’s wife!”

“And so I would, mother,” he replied firmly, “if Mr. Mason and his wife were strangers in a strange place, like the Hales.”

“Oh, don’t speak so quickly,” she retorted. “I’m going tomorrow. I only wanted you to understand exactly what I meant.”

“If you’re going tomorrow,” he said, “I shall order the horses.”

“Nonsense, John,” she snapped. “One would think you were made of money.”

“Not quite yet,” he answered. “But about the horses, I’m determined. The last time you went out in a cab, you came home with a headache from the jolting.”

“I never complained,” she insisted.

“No,” he said, with quiet pride, “my mother is not given to complaints. But that makes me all the more careful. As for Fanny—well, a little discomfort might do her good.”

“She isn’t built like you are, John,” she said softly. “She couldn’t bear it.”

Mrs. Thornton fell silent. Her words touched upon a subject she deeply resented—her daughter’s weakness. She had an unconscious disdain for frailty, and Fanny embodied it in ways that contrasted sharply with her own strength and resolve. Mrs. Thornton did not reason much; her sharp instincts and firm will guided her without need for prolonged debate. She sensed instinctively that nothing could make Fanny endure hardship patiently or face adversity bravely. Though this realization stung her, it also stirred a gentle, almost maternal tenderness toward her daughter—much like how mothers often treat their sickly children. A casual observer might have thought Mrs. Thornton loved Fanny more than John. But such a judgment would have been profoundly wrong. It was precisely the fearless honesty between mother and son—the way they spoke plainly, even painfully, to each other—that revealed the deep trust between them. The hesitant affection she showed Fanny, the shame she felt over her daughter’s lack of strength in qualities she valued so highly—these betrayed not love, but a lack of confidence in where her heart could truly rest.

She never called her son by anything but “John.” Terms like “love” or “dear” were reserved for Fanny. Yet every day, in silence, she gave thanks for him. And among women, she walked with quiet pride because of him.

“Fanny dear,” she said, turning to her daughter, “I’ve ordered horses for the carriage today—we’re going to call on the Hales. Shouldn’t you go and visit Nurse? It’s in the same direction, and she always enjoys seeing you. You could go there while I’m at Mrs. Hale’s.”

“Oh, mamma,” Fanny sighed, “it’s such a long way, and I’m so tired.”

“Tired of what?” Mrs. Thornton asked, her brow tightening slightly.

“I don’t know—the weather, perhaps. It’s so draining. Couldn’t you bring Nurse here instead? The carriage could fetch her, and she could stay the rest of the day. I know she’d like that.”

Mrs. Thornton said nothing. She set down her work and seemed to consider. After a moment, she murmured, “It would be a long walk for her to return at night.”

“Oh!” Fanny exclaimed, “I’ll send her home in a cab. I never thought of her walking.”

At that moment, Mr. Thornton entered the room just before heading to the mill. “Mother,” he said, “I need hardly say that if there’s any small comfort you can offer Mrs. Hale as an invalid, I’m certain you’ll do so.”

“If I can discover what it is,” she replied, “I will. But I’ve never been ill myself, so I don’t know much about what invalids desire.”

“Well,” he said, “here’s Fanny—she’s rarely without some complaint. Perhaps she can suggest something. Won’t you, Fan?”

“I don’t always have an ailment,” Fanny muttered pettishly. “And I’m not going with you. I have a headache today. I shan’t go out.”

Mr. Thornton looked annoyed. His mother kept her eyes fixed on her stitching, working steadily. “Fanny,” he said firmly, “I wish you to go. It will do you good, not harm. You will oblige me by going—without my saying another word.”

He left abruptly. Had he stayed a moment longer, Fanny would have burst into tears at his tone—even though he had said, “You will oblige me.” As it was, she merely grumbled. “John always speaks as if I pretend to be ill. I never do! Who are these Hales, anyway, that he’s making such a fuss over?”

“Fanny,” Mrs. Thornton said sternly, “don’t speak so of your brother. He has reasons, or he wouldn’t insist. Now hurry and get ready.”

But the brief exchange between son and daughter did little to warm Mrs. Thornton’s feelings toward “these Hales.” A quiet jealousy stirred within her, echoing Fanny’s question: *Who are they, that he insists we pay them such attention?* The thought lingered long after Fanny forgot it entirely, absorbed in admiring her new bonnet in the mirror.

Mrs. Thornton was shy by nature. Only recently had she found time to enter society—and even then, she did not enjoy it. She took pleasure in hosting dinners and critiquing others’ meals, but meeting strangers was altogether different. She felt ill at ease. When she stepped into the Hales’ modest drawing-room, she appeared more stern and severe than usual.

Margaret was busy embroidering a delicate piece of cambric for Edith’s expected baby—a task Mrs. Thornton dismissed inwardly as “flimsy, useless work.” She preferred Mrs. Hale’s double knitting; that was practical. The room was cluttered with trinkets that would take hours to dust—time, to someone of limited means, was money. These thoughts passed through her mind as she spoke in her formal manner to Mrs. Hale, uttering the usual polite phrases most people recite without thinking.

Mrs. Hale, however, was more engaged than usual. She was captivated by the old lace adorning Mrs. Thornton’s dress—lace, she later told Dixon, “of that ancient English point which hasn’t been made in seventy years and cannot be bought. It must be an heirloom. That shows she has ancestors.” So the woman who wore such a relic deserved more than the usual half-hearted effort to be pleasant.

Soon, Margaret, struggling to find conversation with Fanny, noticed her mother and Mrs. Thornton diving into the endless topic of servants.

“I suppose you’re not musical,” Fanny remarked, noticing no piano in the room.

“I enjoy listening to good music,” Mrs. Hale replied, “but I don’t play well myself. Papa and mamma aren’t much interested either, so we sold our old piano when we moved here.”

“I can’t imagine living without one,” Fanny said. “It seems almost essential.”

“Fifteen shillings a week,” Margaret thought to herself, “and three saved from it. But she must have been very young. She probably doesn’t remember. Still, she must know of those times.”

Margaret’s voice grew colder when she next spoke. “You have good concerts here, I believe.”

“Oh, yes!” Fanny replied. “Delightful! Too crowded—that’s the worst. The directors admit anyone. But one hears the newest music there. I always give a large order to Johnson’s the day after.”

“Do you like new music simply because it’s new?” Margaret asked.

“Oh, one knows it’s fashionable in London,” Fanny said. “Otherwise, the singers wouldn’t bring it down here. You’ve been to London, of course.”

“Yes,” Margaret said quietly, “I’ve lived there for several years.”

“Oh! London and the Alhambra are the two places I long to see!”

“London and the Alhambra?”

“Yes!” Fanny whispered. “Ever since I read the Tales of the Alhambra. Don’t you know them?”

“I don’t think I do,” Margaret said. “But surely, it’s a simple journey to London.”

“Yes,” Fanny said, lowering her voice, “but somehow—mamma has never been to London herself, and she doesn’t understand my longing. She’s very proud of Milton—though I feel it’s dirty and smoky. I believe she admires it all the more for those very qualities.”

“If it has been Mrs. Thornton’s home for years,” Margaret said clearly, “I can well understand her attachment to it.”

“What are you saying about me, Miss Hale?” Mrs. Thornton asked sharply.

Margaret, caught off guard, hesitated. But Fanny stepped in. “Oh, mamma! We were only trying to explain why you’re so fond of Milton.”

“Thank you,” Mrs. Thornton said coldly. “I do not feel that my natural affection for the place where I was born and raised—where I’ve lived for many years—requires explanation.”

Margaret was irritated. While Fanny had spoken innocently, it did seem as though they had been prying into Mrs. Thornton’s feelings. But she also resented the lady’s offended tone.

After a pause, Mrs. Thornton continued: “Do you know anything of Milton, Miss Hale? Have you seen our factories? Our magnificent warehouses?”

“No,” Margaret said honestly. “I haven’t seen anything of the sort yet.”

Then she felt compelled to add truthfully, “I dare say papa would have taken me sooner if I’d cared. But I truly don’t find pleasure in touring manufactories.”

“They are curious places,” Mrs. Hale said gently, “but so noisy and dirty. I once went in a lilac silk to watch candles being made—and ruined my gown completely.”

“Very likely,” Mrs. Thornton said curtly. “I only thought, since you are newcomers to a town that has risen to prominence through its industrial character, you might wish to see where this progress takes place—places unique in the kingdom, I’m told. If Miss Hale ever decides to show interest in Milton’s manufactures, I can arrange access to print-works, reed-making, or even the simpler spinning operations in my son’s mill. Every advancement in machinery, I believe, can be seen there in its fullest form.”

“I’m so glad you don’t like mills and manufactories,” Fanny whispered as she rose to leave with her mother, who stood up with dignified rustle.

“I think I should like to know all about them,” Margaret replied calmly, “if I were you.”

As they drove away, Mrs. Thornton said, “Fanny, we’ll be civil to the Hales—but don’t form one of your hasty friendships with the daughter. She won’t do you any good. The mother looks unwell, but she seems a decent, quiet woman.”

“I don’t want to be friends with Miss Hale, mamma,” Fanny said, pouting. “I only tried to talk to her and amuse her.”

“Well,” Mrs. Thornton said, “at least John will be satisfied now.”

“That doubt and trouble, fear and pain,
And anguish, all, are shadows vain,
That death itself shall not remain;
That weary deserts we may tread,
A dreary labyrinth may thread.
Thro’ dark ways underground be led;
Yet, if we will one Guide obey,
The dreariest path, the darkest way
Shall issue out in heavenly day;
And we, on divers shores now cast,
Shall meet, our perilous voyage past,
All in our Father’s house at last!”

Margaret hurried up the stairs as soon as the visitors had left, snatching her bonnet and shawl before rushing out to see how Betsy Higgins was faring. She meant to sit with her as long as she could before dinner, her heart drawn toward the girl by a quiet, growing tenderness. As she walked through the narrow, crowded lanes of Milton, the city seemed different—less alien, more alive—now that she had come to care for someone who lived among its soot-stained walls and echoing streets.

Mary Higgins, Betsy’s younger sister, had done what she could to make the house presentable for the visit. The central floor had been roughly scrubbed, though the corners beneath chairs and along the walls remained dark and grimy. Though the sun beat down fiercely outside, a large fire blazed in the grate, filling the room with an oppressive heat. Margaret did not understand why so much coal was burned—it seemed wasteful, even cruel—but Mary meant it as a gesture of welcome. Margaret thought perhaps it was needed for Betsy’s comfort, though she suspected it was more about hospitality than necessity.

Betsy lay on a short sofa under the window, weaker than the day before. Her strength had ebbed away, and she had grown tired from straining to see each passerby, hoping it might be Margaret. Now that Margaret was there, Betsy relaxed, her eyes softening as she studied the young woman’s face, her fingers brushing gently over the fine fabric of her dress with a childlike wonder.

“I never knew why folks in the Bible cared so much for soft clothes,” she murmured. “But it must feel good to wear such things. It’s not like common cloth. Most fine folk’s clothes blind me with their colors, but yours… they rest my eyes. Where did you get this gown?”

“London,” Margaret replied, smiling.

“London? You’ve been there?” Betsy asked, wide-eyed.

“Yes,” Margaret said. “I lived there for years. But my real home was in the country—in a forest.”

“Tell me about it,” Betsy whispered. “I love hearing of trees and open places.”

She leaned back, closed her eyes, and folded her hands across her chest, as if preparing to receive every word. Margaret had never spoken of Helstone since leaving it—only mentioned it in passing. Yet in dreams, the place came vividly to life: the deep shade of ancient oaks, the rustling leaves that made a constant whispering sound even when no wind stirred, the moss-covered ground soft as velvet, and the hidden brook that trickled just beyond sight, feeding the lush greenery.

“There are ferns too,” Margaret continued, “whole fields of them—some in shadow, others lit by golden sunlight, like waves on the sea.”

“I’ve never seen the sea,” Betsy murmured. “But go on.”

“And high above the trees, there are wide commons—open hills where you can see for miles.”

“I’m glad of that,” Betsy said softly. “Down here I always feel smothered. When I walk outside, I want to climb up high, to breathe deeply and see far. In Milton, I feel trapped. And that noise—the constant hum in the mill—it makes my head ache. Sometimes I think if I could just hear silence again, just once… I’d be healed.”

“You’ll find peace,” Margaret said gently. “Even in heaven, there is rest beyond anything we know.”

Betsy shifted uneasily. “I wish my father wouldn’t talk to me the way he does. He means well—I told you that yesterday, and I’ll tell you again. But when I’m feverish, half awake, his words come back to me—so strong, so terrible. I think, if this is all there is—if I’ve only been born to work myself to death in this stifling place, listening to those machines until I scream for them to stop, breathing cotton fluff until I thirst for clean air, with my mother gone and no chance to say how much I loved her—then I’d go mad. I’d kill you, I swear I would.”

She collapsed back, exhausted by the intensity of her emotion. Margaret knelt beside her, her voice calm and steady. “Bessy—we have a Father in Heaven.”

“I know,” Betsy moaned, turning her head from side to side. “I know. I’m wicked. I spoke wickedly. Don’t be afraid of me. I wouldn’t hurt a hair on your head. And…”—her eyes opened, meeting Margaret’s with sudden clarity—“I believe more than you do in what comes after. I’ve read Revelation till I know it by heart. When I’m清醒, I don’t doubt the glory that awaits us.”

“Then let’s not speak of the fears that come when you’re unwell,” Margaret said. “Tell me instead—what did you do when you were strong?”

“I was strong when Mother died,” Betsy said quietly. “But I’ve never been truly well since. I began working in the carding-room soon after, and the fluff got into my lungs. It poisoned me.”

“Fluff?” Margaret asked, puzzled.

“Tiny bits that fly off the cotton during carding,” Betsy explained. “They fill the air like white dust. They say it wraps around the lungs, tightens them. Many workers end up coughing blood, wasting away—poisoned by the very air they breathe.”

“But can’t it be stopped?” Margaret asked.

“I don’t know. Some factories have great wheels at one end to draw the dust away. But they cost hundreds of pounds—no profit, so few masters install them. And some men don’t like them either—they say the fresh air makes them hungry, after years of swallowing fluff. So they refuse to work where the wheels are. Between master and man, the wheels never get built. I wish ours had one.”

“Didn’t your father know?” Margaret asked.
