“Yes. He was sorry. But our factory was steady, our people reliable. He feared moving me to another place. Back then, many called me a fine girl. I didn’t want to seem weak or delicate. And Mary needed schooling—Mother insisted—and Father liked books and lectures. All of it cost money. So I kept working, until I’ll never shake the hum from my ears or the fluff from my throat in this world.”

“How old are you?” Margaret asked.

“Nineteen, next July.”

“And I am nineteen too,” Margaret said softly. For a moment, she couldn’t speak—overcome by the contrast between their lives, her own sheltered youth against Betsy’s suffering and early decline.

“About Mary,” Betsy said, changing the subject. “I wanted to ask you to be kind to her. She’s seventeen, the youngest of us. I don’t want her to go to the mill. But I don’t know what else she’s fit for.”

“She could hardly take a servant’s place,” Margaret said, glancing unconsciously at the untidy corners of the room. “We have a faithful old servant—almost a friend—who needs help, but she’s particular. I couldn’t ask her to take someone who might cause irritation.”

“No,” Betsy agreed. “I see you’re right. Our Mary’s a good girl, but who taught her how to run a house? No mother, and me too sick to guide her properly. I scolded her for doing things wrong—when I didn’t know how to do them myself. Still, I wish she could have lived with you.”

“Even if she can’t come here,” Margaret said, “I’ll always be a friend to her—for your sake, Bessy. I must go now, but I’ll return as soon as I can. Even if it’s not tomorrow, or the next day, or even a week or two later—don’t think I’ve forgotten you. I may be busy.”

“I’ll know you won’t forget me,” Betsy said, squeezing her hand. “But remember—within a week or two, I may be dead and buried.”

“I’ll come again as soon as I can,” Margaret promised, holding her hand tightly. “And if you grow worse, tell me.”

“Ay,” Betsy whispered, returning the pressure.

From that day onward, Mrs. Hale grew increasingly frail. The anniversary of Edith’s marriage approached, and looking back over the year’s trials, Margaret marveled at how they had endured. If she had known then what lay ahead, she would have fled in terror. Yet each day passed with quiet resilience, small joys sparkling through the sorrow like drops of light in darkness.

A year ago, when she first came to Helstone and first sensed her mother’s irritability, she would have dreaded a long illness in a strange, noisy town, with comforts diminished. But now, despite the hardship, something new had taken root in her mother’s spirit—a deeper patience. Though physically suffering, she was gentle, quiet, almost peaceful, in a way she had never been when her troubles were merely imagined.

Mr. Hale, meanwhile, was caught in the trap of denial, a common flaw among men of his nature. He resented his daughter’s concern more than ever.

“Indeed, Margaret, you’re growing fanciful!” he declared. “God knows I’d be the first to act if your mother were truly ill. We always knew when she had headaches at Helstone—she turned pale. But now she has a healthy color, just as she did when I first met her.”

“But Papa,” Margaret hesitated, “I think that flush is pain.”

“Nonsense! You’re imagining things. You’re the one who’s not well. Send for the doctor tomorrow—for yourself. Then, if it will ease your mind, he can see your mother.”

“Thank you, dear Papa,” Margaret said, her voice soft. “It would make me happier.”

She went to him to kiss him, but he gently pushed her away—just enough to show discomfort, as if her presence carried unpleasant thoughts he wished to dismiss. He paced the room, restless.

“Poor Maria,” he muttered, half to himself. “I wish one could do right without hurting others. I’ll hate this town—and myself—if she…”

He paused, then turned to her. “Does your mother often talk of Helstone?”

“No, Papa,” Margaret said, her voice heavy.

“Then she’s not grieving for it, eh? That’s a relief. I always found comfort in knowing your mother was honest—she never hid her troubles from me. She would never lie about her health. So don’t bring these morbid ideas into our home. Come, give me a kiss and go to bed.”

She kissed him and retired slowly, undressing with languid movements. But long after she had lain down, she heard him pacing—back and forth, like a caged animal—his steps echoing in the silence, a rhythm of anxiety that refused to cease.

“I was used to sleep at nights as sweetly as a child—
Now if the wind blew rough, it made me start,
And think of my poor boy tossing about
Upon the roaring seas. And then I seemed
To feel that it was hard to take him from me
For such a little fault.”

At this time, Margaret found solace in the growing closeness between herself and her mother—a tenderness more profound than she had ever known since childhood. Her mother now turned to her not merely as a daughter, but as a confidante, a trusted friend. It was the role Margaret had always longed for, one she had once envied Dixon for holding so easily. Now, she gave herself fully to the duty, responding with care and patience to every appeal for comfort—no matter how small or trivial the concern might seem. She listened attentively, even when the worry was no greater than a pin beneath an elephant’s foot, yet still lifted carefully at the keeper’s command.

Unknowingly, Margaret was drawing near to a deeper understanding—one that would change her forever.

One evening, Mr. Hale absent from home, her mother began speaking of Frederick, the brother Margaret had never truly known. It was the subject she had longed to ask about, yet feared to broach—the only topic where her natural openness faltered under the weight of timidity.

“Oh, Margaret,” her mother said, her voice soft and strained, “it was so windy last night! The wind howled down the chimney into our room. I couldn’t sleep. I never can when the storm is fierce like that. I fell into a habit of wakefulness when poor Frederick was at sea—and now, even if I don’t awaken suddenly, I dream of him caught in a tempestuous sea. Great glass-green walls of water rise on either side of his ship, higher than its masts, curling over with cruel white foam—like some monstrous serpent coiled around the vessel. It’s an old dream, but it returns every time the wind rises, until I’m grateful to wake up, sitting bolt upright in bed, trembling.”

She paused, her breath shallow. “Poor Frederick. He’s on land now, so the wind can do him no harm. Though I did wonder—might it shake down one of those tall chimneys?”

“Where is Frederick now, mamma?” Margaret asked gently. “Our letters are sent to Messrs. Barbour in Cadiz. But where is he himself?”

Her mother hesitated. “I can’t remember the exact name of the place—but he is not called Hale. You must remember that, Margaret. Look for the initials F.D. in every corner of the letters. He has taken the name Dickenson. I wanted him to be Beresford—he had a claim to it, you know—but your father thought it better he didn’t. If he bore my name, he might be recognised.”

Margaret sat quietly, her heart beating softly against her ribs. “Mamma,” she said, “I was at Aunt Shaw’s when all this happened. I was too young, I suppose, to be told plainly. But now—I should like to know, if I may—if it does not cause you pain.”

“Pain?” Mrs. Hale replied, her cheeks flushing faintly. “No. Yet it is painful to think I may never see my darling boy again. Or perhaps—he did right, Margaret. They may say what they will, but I have his own letters. I’ll believe him, even if he is my son, before any court-martial on earth.”

She reached toward a small, ornate cabinet by the wall. “Go to my little japan cabinet, dear. In the second left-hand drawer, you’ll find a packet of letters.”

Margaret fetched them. The envelopes were yellowed, stained with salt and sea air, carrying the faint, lingering scent of ocean winds and distant shores. She returned them to her mother, who untied the silken string with trembling fingers. As she examined the dates, she handed the bundle to Margaret, murmuring anxious comments almost before her daughter could read.

“You see, Margaret—how from the very beginning he disliked Captain Reid. He was second lieutenant aboard the *Orion*, the ship Frederick sailed on first. Poor little fellow—how well he looked in his midshipman’s uniform, dirk in hand, slicing open newspapers as though it were a paper knife!”

She paused, her voice tightening. “But this Mr. Reid—then just a lieutenant—seemed to take a dislike to Frederick from the start. Then—ah, these are the letters written aboard the *Russell*. When Frederick was assigned to her, he discovered his old enemy, Captain Reid, was in command. He meant to endure everything patiently. Look—read this letter. Where does he say—wait—‘my father may rely upon me, that I will bear with all proper patience everything that one officer and gentleman can take from another. But from my former knowledge of my present captain, I confess I look forward with apprehension to a long course of tyranny on board the *Russell*.’”

Margaret slowly read the words, the ink faded and nearly illegible. It was likely an account exaggerated by emotion—written hotly after a confrontation. But the truth of it stirred deep within her.

The letter described how, during drills, Captain Reid ordered sailors aloft in the main-topsail rigging to race down, threatening the slowest with the cat-o’-nine-tails. One man, farthest along the spar, knowing he could not pass his companions, yet terrified of the punishment, leapt desperately for a lower rope. He missed. He fell—senseless—onto the deck. He lived only a few hours. The crew’s fury boiled over. Young Hale wrote the letter in the midst of that chaos.

“But we didn’t receive this letter until long after we heard of the mutiny,” Mrs. Hale continued, her voice low. “Poor Fred! I dare say it was a comfort to him to write it—even if he had no way to send it. And then—oh, Margaret—we saw a report in the papers—before Fred’s letter arrived—of a terrible mutiny aboard the *Russell*. The mutineers seized control, and the ship vanished, believed to have become a pirate vessel. Captain Reid was set adrift in a boat with several men—officers or others—whose names were listed, because they were rescued by a West Indian steamer.”

Mrs. Hale stopped, her breath catching. “Oh, Margaret—how your father and I sickened when we read that list. There was no name of Frederick Hale. We thought it must be a mistake. Poor Fred was such a fine boy—only perhaps too passionate. We hoped ‘Carr’—the name on the list—was a misprint for Hale. Newspapers are so careless.”

She took a shuddering breath. “That same day, your father walked to Southampton to get the papers. I couldn’t stay home. I went to meet him. He came late—much later than I expected. I waited under the hedge, near the Oldham beech-tree. When he finally appeared, his arms hung limp, his head bowed, walking as if each step weighed a lifetime.”

She closed her eyes. “I see him now.”

“Don’t go on, mamma,” Margaret whispered, leaning close, pressing her cheek gently against her mother’s hand. “I understand.”

“No,” Mrs. Hale said, her voice breaking. “You cannot. No one can who didn’t see him then. I could barely stand. Everything spun around me. When I reached him, he didn’t speak. Didn’t seem surprised to see me there, three miles from home. But he took my arm, stroked my hand—as if trying to calm me through some terrible blow. And when I trembled so violently I couldn’t speak, he pulled me into his arms, bent his head over mine, and began to cry—not aloud, but in a choked, groaning sound—until I stood frozen with fear, begging him to tell me what he had seen.”

Then, with his hand jerking as if controlled by unseen hands, he handed her a newspaper. “Read it,” he said. “It calls our Frederick a ‘traitor of the blackest dye,’ a ‘base, ungrateful disgrace to his profession.’ Oh, Margaret—I cannot recall the hateful words they used. I took the paper—read it—then tore it into tiny pieces. I tore it—oh, I believe I tore it with my teeth. I didn’t cry. I couldn’t. My face burned like fire, my eyes felt as if they would burst. I saw your father watching me, grave and silent. I said it was a lie—and it was.”

Months later, Frederick’s letter arrived. And now, Margaret read it—seeing clearly the provocation. He hadn’t rebelled for himself. He had spoken out against injustice, against cruelty, against a captain who ruled with arbitrary cruelty. And most of the crew stood with him.

“I think, Margaret,” Mrs. Hale said after a long silence, her voice weak and trembling, “I am glad of it. I am prouder of Frederick standing up against injustice than if he had simply been a good officer.”

“I am sure I am,” Margaret replied firmly. “Loyalty and obedience to wisdom and justice are noble. But nobler still is defiance of power when it is wielded unjustly and cruelly—not for oneself, but for others who cannot defend themselves.”

Yet still, her mother sighed. “For all that, I wish I could see Frederick once more—just once. He was my first baby, Margaret.”

She spoke wistfully, almost as if apologising for the longing, as though wanting to see her son diminished the love she bore for Margaret. But such a thought never entered Margaret’s mind. Instead, she wondered: *Could this wish be fulfilled?*

“It’s been six or seven years,” she said quietly. “Would they still prosecute him, mother? If he came back, and faced trial—what would happen? Surely, he could bring evidence of his great provocation.”

“It would do no good,” Mrs. Hale whispered. “Some of the sailors who followed Frederick were captured. A court-martial was held aboard the *Amicia*. I believed every word they said—they matched Frederick’s story exactly. But it mattered nothing.”

For the first time, tears welled in her eyes. “They were hanged at the yard-arm,” she said solemnly. “And the worst part—the court declared they had allowed themselves to be led astray from their duty… by their superior officers.”

Silence filled the room. Long, heavy silence.

“And Frederick was in South America for many years, was he not?”

“Yes. And now he is in Spain—at Cadiz, or somewhere near. If he comes to England, he will be hanged. I shall never see his face again—for if he comes to England, he will be hanged.”

There was no comfort to offer. Mrs. Hale turned her face to the wall, motionless, consumed by grief. Margaret could say nothing. She withdrew her hand, sensing her mother needed solitude. When Mr. Hale returned, Margaret slipped away, her heart heavy with sorrow, seeing no light ahead—no hope on the horizon.

“Thought fights with thought; out springs a spark of truth
From the collision of the sword and shield.”

“Margaret,” her father said the next day, “we must return Mrs. Thornton’s call. Your mother isn’t feeling well, and she thinks she can’t walk so far. But you and I will go this afternoon.”

As they walked, Mr. Hale began to speak of his wife’s health, his voice tinged with a quiet worry that Margaret was glad to see had finally stirred him.

“Did you consult the doctor, Margaret? Did you send for him?”

“No, Papa,” she replied. “You mentioned he’d come to see me. But I was well then. Still, if I could find a good doctor, I’d go today and ask him to visit Mama. I’m certain she’s more unwell than she lets on.”

She spoke plainly, driven by the urgency she’d felt before, when her father had dismissed her concerns with such firmness. Now, however, he answered in a tone heavy with doubt.

“Do you think there’s something serious? Is she truly ill? Has Dixon said anything? Oh, Margaret! I can’t shake the idea that coming to Milton has harmed her. My poor Maria!”

“Oh, Papa, don’t say such things!” Margaret exclaimed, alarmed. “She’s not well—that’s all. Many people feel unwell for a time, but with proper care, they grow stronger than ever.”

“But has Dixon said anything about her?”

“No. You know how Dixon loves turning small matters into mysteries. She’s been unusually vague about Mama’s condition, which has only made me anxious—though perhaps without reason. After all, you did say the other day I was getting fanciful.”

“I hope I am,” he said softly. “But don’t mind what I said then. I’d rather you were fanciful about your mother’s health. Don’t hesitate to tell me your thoughts. I want to hear them—even if I sounded annoyed. We’ll ask Mrs. Thornton if she knows of a good doctor. We won’t waste money on anyone but someone truly skilled. Wait—this is the street we’re looking for.”

The street looked unlikely to hold a house grand enough for Mrs. Thornton. Her son’s presence had never given any clue as to where he lived, but Margaret had unconsciously imagined that such a tall, dignified woman would live in a home matching her stature. Yet Marlborough Street stretched on with rows of modest houses, some broken by blank walls—nothing from their vantage point suggested grandeur.

“He told me he lived in Marlborough Street,” Mr. Hale said, puzzled.

“Perhaps it’s one of the savings he still practices—living in a small house,” Margaret mused. “But there are people around. Let me ask.”

She approached a passerby, who pointed out the factory lodge door at the end of the long wall they’d noticed. The lodge gate was no different from a garden entrance. On one side stood massive iron gates for wagons and lorries. The keeper admitted them into a vast rectangular yard. On one side were offices for business; opposite, a towering mill, its windows countless, humming with machinery and the deep groan of steam engines—a sound so constant it seemed to threaten to drown out human voices.

At the end of the yard, facing the street, stood a stone house—blackened by smoke, yet kept immaculate: steps, windows, paint all gleaming. It had clearly been built fifty or sixty years ago—the long narrow windows, the stone trim, the double flights of stairs flanking the front door—all spoke of age. Margaret wondered why those who could afford such a house didn’t choose a quieter life in the country or suburbs, away from the ceaseless din of industry. Her ears strained to catch her father’s words as they waited on the steps.

Inside, the drawing-room felt untouched. No one sat there. It seemed abandoned since the moment the furniture had been wrapped up, as though expecting an earthquake. The walls were pink and gold; the carpet bore floral patterns, but its center was covered by a plain linen rug. Lace curtains hung at the windows; every chair and sofa wore a delicate veil of netting. Alabaster figures stood under glass domes. In the center, beneath a chandelier also wrapped in cloth, stood a circular table, its polished surface lined with neatly bound books arranged like spokes of a wheel. Everything reflected light; nothing absorbed it. The room shimmered with a cold, glittering sterility, so starkly clean it felt uncomfortable. Margaret noticed not the effort to keep it spotless, but the absence of warmth—the labor here was not for comfort, but for preservation, for beauty untouched by use.

They had time to observe, to whisper, before Mrs. Thornton entered, rustling in black silk, as always. Her lace and muslin matched the purity of the room. Margaret explained why her mother couldn’t join them, careful not to deepen her father’s fears. But her explanation was clumsy, leaving Mrs. Thornton with the impression that Mrs. Hale’s illness was merely a temporary, high-society fancy—one that might have been ignored had the occasion warranted it. Remembering the horses hired for her own visit, and how Fanny had been sent ahead by Mr. Thornton to show respect, Mrs. Thornton stiffened slightly, offering neither sympathy nor belief.

“How is Mr. Thornton?” asked Mr. Hale. “I was worried by his hurried note yesterday.”

“My son rarely falls ill. When he does, he doesn’t complain. He told me he couldn’t spare time to read with you last night, sir. He regretted it—I’m sure he values your company.”

“It’s a pleasure to me as well,” Mr. Hale replied. “Seeing his delight in classical literature makes me feel young again.”

“I suppose classics are useful for those with leisure,” Mrs. Thornton said. “But I opposed my son returning to them. His life demands full attention. Classics may suit men who idle in colleges or country estates—but Milton men should focus entirely on the work of today. That’s my opinion.”

“But surely,” Margaret interjected, “if the mind focuses too long on one thing, it becomes rigid, unable to take in new ideas.”

“I don’t understand what you mean by a mind becoming stiff,” Mrs. Thornton replied sharply. “And I don’t admire those who jump from interest to interest, forgetting everything the next day. A Milton manufacturer must have one great purpose—and all his life should serve it.”

“And that purpose is—?” Mr. Hale asked.

Her cheeks flushed, her eyes brightened.

“To hold a respected, honorable place among the merchants of the land—the men of the town. My son has earned that. Go anywhere—England, Europe—the name John Thornton of Milton is known and respected. Of course, it’s unknown in fashionable circles,” she added with disdain. “Idle gentlemen and ladies don’t concern themselves with a factory owner unless he enters politics or marries nobility.”

Both Mr. Hale and Margaret felt a quiet discomfort. They had never heard of this famous name—until Mr. Bell had written, recommending Mr. Thornton as a valuable ally in Milton. This proud woman’s world was alien to theirs—neither Harley Street society nor Hampshire gentry. Margaret’s expression, though she tried to hide it, revealed her disconnection. Mrs. Thornton noticed.

“You think you’ve never heard of my wonderful son, Miss Hale. You think I’m an old woman whose pride is rooted in Milton, whose crow is the whitest ever seen.”

“No,” Margaret said, with quiet strength. “It may be true I hadn’t heard of Mr. Thornton before coming here. But since I’ve arrived, I’ve heard enough to respect and admire him—and to believe your pride is well placed.”

“Who told you this?” Mrs. Thornton asked, softened a little, yet wary that another’s words might not do him justice.

Margaret hesitated. She disliked being questioned so directly. Mr. Hale stepped in.

“It was what Mr. Thornton himself said,” he offered. “Wasn’t it, Margaret?”

Mrs. Thornton straightened.

“My son doesn’t boast of his own deeds. May I ask again, Miss Hale—whose words shaped your opinion of him? A mother is naturally eager to hear praise of her child.”

“It was more what Mr. Thornton chose not to say,” Margaret replied. “What he withheld, compared to what Mr. Bell told us about his past—that made us appreciate him more. And it gave me reason to see why you’re proud.”

“Mr. Bell!” Mrs. Thornton scoffed. “What does he know of John? He lives in a sleepy college, doing nothing. But I thank you, Miss Hale. Many young ladies would shy away from giving an old woman such pleasure.”

“Why?” Margaret asked, staring at her.

“Because,” Mrs. Thornton said with a grim smile, “they might fear their conscience telling them they’re making me an advocate—should they have designs on my son.”

Margaret laughed—out loud, brightly. It startled Mrs. Thornton, who found the laughter almost offensive, as if the words behind it must be utterly absurd.

She stopped herself immediately, seeing the irritation.

“I beg your pardon, madam. But I truly thank you for clearing me of any plans on Mr. Thornton’s heart.”

“Young ladies have done so before,” Mrs. Thornton said stiffly.

“I hope Miss Thornton is well,” Mr. Hale said, wishing to shift the topic.

“She is as well as she ever is. Not strong,” Mrs. Thornton replied curtly.

“And Mr. Thornton? I trust I may expect to see him on Thursday?”

“I cannot guarantee my son’s schedule. There’s unrest in town—a threat of a strike. If it comes, his judgment will be sought. But I expect he can come. At the very least, he’ll let you know if he can’t.”

“A strike?” Margaret asked. “For what?”

“For the mastery of other people’s property,” Mrs. Thornton snapped. “That’s always their goal. If my son’s workers strike, I’ll call them ungrateful hounds. But I suspect they will.”

“They want higher wages, I suppose?” Mr. Hale asked.

“That’s the surface. But the truth is, they want to be masters, to turn the masters into slaves. They try this every five or six years. This time, they’ll learn their mistake. If they walk out, they may not get back in so easily. I believe the masters have plans ready to teach them not to strike again soon.”

“Does it make the town dangerous?” Margaret asked.

“Of course. But surely you’re not afraid? Milton isn’t for cowards. I’ve walked through crowds of angry men swearing they’d kill Makinson if he showed his face. He knew nothing. Someone had to warn him—or he’d have died. And I had to go. Once inside, I couldn’t leave. So I climbed to the roof, where stones were piled to drop on the crowd if they forced the doors. I would have thrown them myself—if I hadn’t fainted from the heat. If you live here, you must learn courage, Miss Hale.”

“I would try,” Margaret said, pale. “I don’t know if I’m brave until I’m tested. But I fear I might be a coward.”

“Southern people often fear what we Darkshire folk call living and struggling. But after ten years among people who harbor grudges and wait to repay them, you’ll know whether you’re brave or not.”

That evening, Mr. Thornton came to call. He was shown into the drawing-room, where Mr. Hale read aloud to his wife and daughter.

“I’ve come partly to deliver a note from my mother, and partly to apologize for missing our appointment yesterday. The note contains the address you requested—Dr. Donaldson.”

“Thank you,” Margaret said quickly, taking the note. She didn’t want her mother to know they’d been seeking a doctor. She was pleased when Mr. Thornton handed it over without comment.

Mr. Hale turned to the strike.

“Yes,” Mr. Thornton said, his face hardening into an expression like his mother’s worst. “The fools will strike. Let them. It suits us. We gave them a chance. They think trade is as strong as last year. We see the storm coming and are pulling in our sails. But because we don’t explain, they refuse to believe we’re acting wisely. They demand reasons for every decision. Henderson tried a trick at Ashley—he wanted a strike. So when the men asked for a five percent raise, he said he’d consider it and reply on pay day, knowing full well what he’d decide. But they guessed the truth, heard about declining trade, and withdrew their claim. Now he’s stuck working. But today, the Milton masters have decided—we won’t raise wages. We may even lower them. So we stand waiting for their next move.”

“And what will that be?” Mr. Hale asked.

“I expect a general strike. Soon, Miss Hale, you’ll see Milton without smoke.”

“But why,” Margaret asked, “couldn’t you explain your reasons? I don’t know the right words, but I think you understand.”

“Do you give your servants reasons for your spending or saving?” Mr. Thornton replied. “We, the owners of capital, have the right to decide how to use it.”

“A human right,” Margaret murmured, so low he barely heard.

“I beg your pardon?” he said.

“I’d rather not repeat it,” she said. “It was a personal feeling—I don’t think you’d agree.”

“Won’t you try me?” he asked, suddenly intense.

She was irritated by his persistence but didn:’t wish to dwell on it.

“I said you had a human right. I meant—there seems no reason but religious ones why you shouldn’t do as you please with your own.”

“I know we differ in religion,” he said quietly, “but don’t you grant me some beliefs, even if they’re not yours?”

He spoke softly, as if addressing only her. She didn’t want the conversation to become private.

“I don’t need to consider your particular views,” she said evenly. “All I meant is, while no human law stops employers from wasting their wealth, Scripture suggests stewards have a duty not to squander it. But I know little about strikes, wages, capital, or labor—so I’d better not talk to a political economist like you.”

“On the contrary,” he said eagerly, “I’d be glad to explain anything that seems strange. Especially now, when everyone will be talking.”

“Thank you,” she said coldly. “But I’ll first ask my father for guidance. I don’t want to misunderstand this new world.”

“You think it strange. Why?”

“I don’t know—perhaps because I see two groups dependent on each other, yet treating each other as enemies. I’ve never lived where people constantly tear each other down.”

“Who have you heard speaking against the masters?” he asked. “I don’t ask who criticized the workers—I see you still misunderstand me. But who has slandered the masters?”

Margaret blushed, then smiled.

“I don’t enjoy being questioned. I won’t answer. Besides, it’s irrelevant. I’ll only say I’ve heard someone—perhaps a worker—say it’s in the employers’ interest to keep their workers poor, so they stay dependent.”

“I dare say it was Higgins,” Mrs. Hale remarked. Mr. Thornton didn’t seem to notice, but he caught it.

“And I heard it’s better for the masters to have ignorant workers—not hedge-lawyers,” Margaret added, addressing her father more than him.

“Who is Captain Lennox?” Mr. Thornton thought to himself, a strange displeasure rising. He didn’t reply.

Her father took up the thread.

“You’ve never cared much for schools, Margaret. You wouldn’t have known how much education is being provided in Milton.”

“No,” she said gently. “I admit I don’t care enough. But the ignorance I meant wasn’t about reading or writing. It was about wisdom—the kind that guides people. I don’t know what that is. But my informant said the masters prefer their hands to be large children—living only in the moment, obeying blindly.”

“In short,” Mr. Thornton said, offended, “your informant found a willing listener for every slander he chose to spread.”

Margaret didn’t reply. She resented the personal attack.

Mr. Hale spoke next.

“I confess, though I haven’t spoken with many workers as Margaret has, I’m struck by the hostility between employer and employee—on the surface, at least. Even your own words suggest it.”

Mr. Thornton paused. Margaret had just left the room. He was annoyed by the tension between them. But the frustration cooled his temper, lending dignity to his reply.

“My theory is simple: my interests are the same as my workers’. And theirs are mine. Miss Hale dislikes the word ‘hands,’ so I won’t use it—though it’s the natural term, born long before me. Someday—in some perfect future—this unity might exist. Just as I can imagine a republic as the most ideal form of government.”

“We’ll read Plato’s Republic once we finish Homer,” Mr. Hale said.

“Trust in that veiled hand, which leads
None by the path that he would go;
And always be for change prepared,
For the world’s law is ebb and flow.”

The afternoon sun slanted through the windows of the Hale home as Dr. Donaldson arrived to visit Mrs. Hale for the first time since her illness had taken hold. The air in the house was thick with unspoken tension, a silence broken only by the soft tread of servants and the distant hum of industry from the town beyond. Margaret stood just outside the drawing-room door, her heart pounding like a drum against her ribs. She had hoped that their old closeness might have bridged the gap between them, but instead, she found herself shut out—excluded while Dixon entered without hesitation.

Margaret was not one to love lightly. When she did, it was with fierce devotion, and with an instinctive protectiveness that could border on jealousy. She retreated into her mother’s bedroom, a small chamber tucked behind the drawing-room, and began to pace its narrow length. Her hands clenched at her sides, her breath held tight in her chest. Every now and then, she paused—listening. Was that a moan? A whisper? Her ears strained. Yes—she was certain. A faint cry, swallowed by the walls, but real enough to freeze her blood.

Then silence. For a moment, nothing. Then the shuffle of chairs, a murmur of voices, the formalities of departure. The door creaked open. Margaret stepped forward, composed herself, and moved down the hall.

“My father is away,” she said, her voice steady despite the tremor beneath. “He has a pupil to attend to this hour. May I ask you to come into his study downstairs?”

She watched as Dixon hesitated, her face hardening with duty. But Margaret carried herself with a quiet authority—a daughter asserting her place in her own home. It was not pride, but necessity. And it worked. Dixon stepped aside, surprised, almost defeated. Margaret felt a flicker of triumph, though she knew it was fleeting. The moment passed, and she descended the stairs, the weight of what was to come pressing down upon her.

In the study, she faced the doctor. His expression was grave, guarded. He looked at her not as a patient’s relative, but as someone who demanded truth.

“What is wrong with my mother?” she asked, her voice firm, cutting through any pretense. “Please, sir—tell me plainly.”

Dr. Donaldson hesitated. He glanced toward the doorway, then back to her. “Your mother has a devoted servant,” he began gently. “One who has been more than a nurse—almost a friend.”

“I am her daughter,” Margaret interrupted softly, yet with conviction.

“And she specifically requested that you not be told,” he continued.

“I cannot obey such a request,” she replied. “I am not strong enough to pretend ignorance. And I know you did not swear to keep it secret. You are too wise to make such a promise.”

He smiled faintly, sadly. “You are right. I did not promise. And truth be told, it will not remain hidden much longer.”

He paused. Margaret paled, her lips tightening. No movement crossed her face, but her eyes—dark and wide—held him fast. He saw the strength there: the kind of resolve only those who have known deep loss can possess.

Then, quietly, he spoke two sentences. Two lines that shattered the fragile peace of the room.

Margaret went still. Her breath caught. Her pupils dilated, darkening like pools of ink. The color drained from her cheeks, leaving her skin ashen. She did not cry. She did not flinch. But when she finally spoke, her voice was low, trembling only slightly.

“I thank you, sir,” she whispered. “That fear has haunted me for weeks. It is agony—real, living agony. My poor mother…”

Tears came then, hot and swift, but only a few. She blinked them away, already turning her mind to what must be done.

“Will she suffer?” she asked.

He shook his head. “We cannot say. It depends on her constitution, on many things. But modern medicine offers us ways to ease the pain. We may not cure it—but we can comfort.”

“My father,” she murmured, her voice breaking. “What shall I tell him?”

“You must not tell him yet,” he advised. “Let him learn gradually. Let him see the signs—not the truth all at once. Give him time. And when you do speak, do so gently. Your father… I have seen him. I respect him deeply. Though I may disagree with his choices, I honor his sacrifice.”

He hesitated, then added, “This once, I will come as a friend. Not just a doctor. You must allow me to be that. In times like these, knowing each other matters more than years of polite calls.”

Margaret could not answer. She reached out and took his hand. Her grip was strong—so strong it left a mark. He smiled, touched, as he stepped into his carriage.

“That’s a fine girl,” he mused, watching the imprint on his palm. “Who’d have thought such a delicate hand could hold so much power? Bones well set—like a queen’s. Looked up at me at first, daring me to speak the truth. Then leaned forward, eager to hear every word. Poor thing. I’ll watch over her. These noble spirits can endure so much. She won’t break. Not her. She’d win my heart if I were young again. But I’m too old. Ah—here we are at the Archers’.”

He stepped out, ready to serve another family, but never forgetting the quiet strength he had just witnessed.

Back in the house, Margaret returned to her father’s study, needing a moment to gather herself before facing her mother. She sank into a chair, her hands clasped tightly.

“Oh, God,” she breathed. “This is terrible. How can I bear it? Such a dreadful sickness—no hope. Mamma, I wish I had never left you. If only I had stayed at Helstone. Poor mamma—how much has she suffered alone? Oh, please, God, let her pain be light. How can I face seeing her suffer? How can I endure Papa’s grief? Not yet. Not all at once. He would die. But I cannot lose a single moment with her.”

She rose and hurried upstairs. The room was quiet. Dixon was gone. Mrs. Hale sat in a chair, wrapped in a soft shawl, her cap neatly placed. Her face bore a faint flush, the exhaustion after the examination giving her a peaceful, almost serene look.

Margaret stared.

“Why, Margaret,” her mother said, her voice gentle. “You look strange. What is wrong?”

A sudden understanding crossed Mrs. Hale’s face. “You’ve been speaking to Dr. Donaldson—have you? You shouldn’t have asked him questions.”

Margaret didn’t reply. She knelt beside the chair, took her mother’s hand, and refused to let go—even when Mrs. Hale tried to pull away.

She kissed her fingers, her tears falling onto the skin, warm and wet.

“It was wrong of you,” Mrs. Hale said softly. “You knew I didn’t want you to know.”

“I made him tell me,” Margaret whispered. “It was I who broke your trust. Blame me.”

Her mother sighed, weary. Then, slowly, she allowed her hand to rest in Margaret’s grasp.

“Do you know what you’re asking?” she murmured.

“Yes,” Margaret said firmly. “I know more than you think. Let me be your nurse. Please. Let me try. No one will ever care as much as I will. It would mean so much to me—to you.”

Mrs. Hale closed her eyes. “My dear child… I don’t know if you understand what lies ahead.”

“I do,” Margaret insisted. “And I will learn everything Dixon knows. Let me be near you—first. I need to be close. I used to fear you’d forget me when I was away at Aunt Shaw’s. I’d lie awake, crying, thinking you’d grown tired of me.”

Mrs. Hale smiled faintly. “And I feared you’d find our life here—our little make-shifts—beneath you. That you’d miss the grandeur of Harley Street.”

“Oh, mamma,” Margaret laughed through her tears. “I loved it here. The wardrobe shelf turned into a supper tray! The tea-chests stuffed into ottomans! Those were treasures. I called them contrivances, but they were joy.”

Mrs. Hale looked at her, her eyes glistening. “I shall never see Helstone again,” she whispered.

Margaret could not speak.

“I wanted to leave it,” her mother continued. “Every place seemed better. And now I shall die far from it. I am being punished.”

“No!” Margaret said sharply. “He said you might live for years. We’ll bring you back. We will.”

“No,” Mrs. Hale said, her voice fading. “Never. That is my penance. But—Frederick…”

At the name, she gasped—a sharp, piercing cry tore from her throat. Her body convulsed. “Frederick! Come to me! I am dying! Little first-born child—come to me!”

She fell into violent sobs, her limbs trembling, her breath ragged. Margaret sprang up, terrified.

“Dixon!” she called.

Dixon arrived, cross and impatient. “You’ve overexcited her,” she accused.

Margaret said nothing. She obeyed every instruction—calm, silent, efficient. And in doing so, she softened the old servant’s anger.

They laid Mrs. Hale in bed. Margaret sat beside her until sleep claimed her, then until Dixon motioned her out. With a sour face, the woman handed her a cup of coffee, standing over her like a sentinel.

“You shouldn’t have been curious, Miss,” she said. “Now you’ll tell your father—and I’ll have a household full of trouble.”

“No,” Margaret said, her voice quiet. “I won’t tell him. He couldn’t bear it. Not like I can.”

And then, to prove it, she burst into tears.

“Ah,” Dixon muttered. “I knew it. Now you’ll wake her again. Miss Margaret, I’ve kept this secret for weeks. I don’t claim to love her like you do—but I loved her more than anyone else—except Master Frederick. Since the day I saw her dressed in white crape and poppies, when I pricked my finger and she tore up her handkerchief to bandage it—I’ve never loved anyone like her. I never dreamed I’d see her like this.”

She paused, wiping her eyes. “Many call you pretty. Even here, where smoke blinds the eye, the owls can see it. But you’ll never be like your mother. Not even if you live to a hundred.”

“Mamma is still beautiful,” Margaret whispered.

“Don’t start again,” Dixon warned. “Or I’ll give way myself. You’ll never stand master’s return, questioning like this. Go out. Walk. Clear your head. Many’s the time I’ve longed to walk off the thought of it all.”

“Oh, Dixon,” Margaret said softly. “How often I’ve been harsh to you—without knowing the burden you carried.”

“Bless you, child,” Dixon said, her voice thick. “I like to see you show spirit. That’s the Beresford blood. The last Sir John but two shot his steward dead for telling him he’d squeezed the tenants dry. And he did—until there was no more money than skin off a flint.”

“Well,” Margaret said, smiling faintly, “I won’t shoot you. And I’ll try not to be cross again.”

“You never have,” Dixon replied. “If I’ve said it, it’s been to myself—just to pass the time. And when you fire up, you’re the very image of Master Frederick. I could put you in a passion just to see that stormy look come over your face. But go now, Miss. I’ll watch over Missus. And your father—he has his books. They’ll keep him company enough.”

“I’ll go,” Margaret said.

She lingered for a moment, then suddenly kissed Dixon on the cheek and fled.

“Bless her,” Dixon whispered, watching her go. “She’s sweet as a nut. There are three people I love—Missus, Master Frederick, and her. Just them three. The rest—hanged. Master was born to marry Missus. If I thought he truly loved her, I might grow fond of him. But he should’ve made more of her. Always reading, thinking—never noticing. See what it’s brought him to. Many men who never read or think become rectors, deans. He might have, too—if he’d just paid attention. But now—there she goes.”

She looked out the window, watching the front door close.

“Poor young lady,” she murmured. “Her clothes look shabby compared to when she came to Helstone a year ago. Then she hadn’t a darned stocking or cleaned glove in her whole wardrobe. And now—!”

“There are briars besetting every path,
Which call for patient care;
There is a cross in every lot,
And an earnest need for prayer.”

Margaret stepped out into the street with reluctance, her spirits heavy and her steps slow. But as she walked, the mere act of moving through Milton’s narrow lanes began to stir something within her—the fresh air, the distant hum of life, the rhythm of the city waking up around her. Before long, her pace lightened, her cheeks flushed with color, and her thoughts lifted from their inward burden. She began to notice the world again: men with hands deep in pockets, strolling idly; groups of girls laughing loudly, their voices sharp and defiant; others standing near the doors of gin-shops, smoking and muttering about passersby as if they owned the street.

She had planned to walk through the fields beyond the town, but the thought of that long journey now seemed daunting. Instead, she turned toward Bessy Higgins’s home. It wouldn’t be the quiet escape she’d hoped for, but perhaps it would be kinder—more meaningful.

Nicholas Higgins sat by the hearth, pipe in hand, when she entered. Bessy was rocking gently in her chair on the other side of the room. Nicholas paused mid-puff, stood, and pushed his chair aside for Margaret to sit. He leaned against the mantel, arms crossed, watching her as she asked after Bessy’s health.

“Hoo’s down i’ th’ spirits,” he said, “but better in body. Hoo doesn’t like this strike. Hoo likes peace more than anything.”

“This is th’ third strike I’ve seen,” Bessy sighed, as if that explained everything.

“Well,” Nicholas replied, “third time pays for all. See if we don’t bring the masters to their knees this time. See if they don’t come crawling back, begging us to return at our own price. We’ve missed our chance before, I’ll grant you—but not this time. This time we’ve laid our plans deep.”

Margaret looked at them both, curious. “But why do you strike?” she asked. “Is it simply refusing to work until you get your wages? That’s what I understand it to mean. And I admit—I know nothing about strikes. Where I come from, such things never happen.”

“I wish I were there,” Bessy murmured, eyes distant.

“But it’s not for me to grow tired of strikes,” Nicholas said. “This will be the last one I see. Before it ends, I shall be gone—to the Great City, the Holy Jerusalem.”

“He sees only the next world,” Nicholas added, “so he can’t bear this one. Me? I’m bound to make the best of this life. A bird in the hand is worth two in the bush. So that’s how we differ on the strike.”

Margaret hesitated. “But if people struck where I come from—if they stopped working—what would happen? The fields wouldn’t be sown, the hay wouldn’t be gathered, the harvest lost.”

“Well?” Nicholas challenged, puffing on his pipe.

“If the farmers couldn’t hire laborers,” she continued, “they’d have no crops to sell. Then where would the money come from to pay wages next year?”

He smoked on, silent. At last, he said, “I know little of your ways down South. I’ve heard they’re a people crushed, spiritless, too broken by hardship to even know when they’re being wronged. But not here. Here, we know when we’re being cheated. We’ve got blood in our veins. We won’t stand for it. We take our hands from the looms and say, ‘You may break us, but you won’t dominate us, my masters!’ And this time—by God—they won’t!”

“I wish I lived down South,” Bessy whispered.

“There’s much to endure there,” Margaret said softly.

“There’s sorrow everywhere,” she added. “Hard labor, little food, no rest. But at least it’s outdoors.”

“And away from the endless noise, the stifling heat,” Bessy agreed.

“It’s cold or wet sometimes,” Margaret said. “A young person can bear it. But an old man—rheumatism sets in, his body breaks down before its time. Yet he must keep working—or go to the workhouse.”

Bessy nodded slowly. “I thought you admired the South so much.”

“I do,” Margaret admitted, smiling faintly. “But I meant only that every place has good and bad. Since you’ve suffered here, I thought it fair you should know the burdens of the South too.”

“And you say they never strike there?” Nicholas asked sharply.

“No,” Margaret answered. “I think they have too much sense.”

“And I think,” he replied, tossing the ashes from his pipe with such force it shattered, “it’s not because they’re wise—but because they lack courage.”

“Oh, father!” Bessy protested. “What have you gained by striking? Remember the first strike—when mother died? We all had to suffer. You worse than any. And yet many kept going back week after week, taking the same wage, until all were back at work. Some became beggars for life.”

“Aye,” Nicholas said quietly. “That strike was poorly led. Men who were fools or not true men took charge. But this time—it’ll be different.”

Margaret waited, then said, “But still—you haven’t told me what you’re striking *for*.”

“Why,” he said, “there are five or six masters who’ve been paying us the same wages for two years—wages that kept us alive and let them grow rich. Now they come to us and say we must accept less. We won’t. We’ll starve them first—and see who works for them then. They’ll have killed the goose that laid their golden eggs.”

“So you plan to die,” Margaret said, “just to punish them?”

“No,” he replied. “I don’t plan to die. But I’m ready to die at my post rather than give in. That’s called honor in a soldier. Why not in a poor weaver?”

“But a soldier dies for his country—for others.”

He laughed, a dry, bitter sound. “My lass, you’re young. But don’t you think I can feed three people—Bessy, Mary, and myself—on sixteen shillings a week? Do you think I strike for myself? No. I fight for others—as much as any soldier. Only while he dies for strangers, I fight for John Boucher next door, whose wife is sick and has eight children too young for the factory. I fight for justice. Why should we earn less now than two years ago?”

“Perhaps trade is hard,” Margaret suggested. “Maybe they can’t afford to pay the same.”

“Trade?” Nicholas scoffed. “That’s just their excuse. It’s about wages. They control trade—manipulate it like a puppet show to scare us into submission. Their job is to squeeze us, to swell their profits. Ours is to stand firm—not just for ourselves, but for everyone around us. We help build their wealth. Shouldn’t we have a say in how it’s spent? We’ve saved money now. We’re resolved to stand together. Not one of us will return unless the Union says it’s fair. So hooray for the strike! Let Thornton, Slickson, Hamper, and their kind look to it!”

“Thornton?” Margaret echoed. “Mr. Thornton of Marlborough Street?”

“Aye,” Nicholas said. “Thornton of Marlborough Mill. He’s one of them.”

“What sort of master is he?”

“Have you ever seen a bulldog?” Nicholas asked. “Put one on hind legs, dress him in coat and breeches—and you’ve got John Thornton.”

Margaret smiled. “No,” she said, “I deny it. Mr. Thornton is plain enough, but he’s not like a bulldog—with its broad nose and snarling lip.”

“Not in looks,” he conceded. “But let him get hold of an idea—and he’ll cling to it like a bulldog. You could pull him off with a pitchfork before he’d let go. He’s worth fighting with. As for Slickson—he’ll lure his men back with sweet words, then cheat them once they’re under his thumb. He’s slippery as an eel. Like a cat—sleek, cunning, fierce. No honest fight with him. But Thornton—he’s stubborn as a door-nail. Obstinate, every inch. The old bulldog!”

Margaret turned to Bessy. “Poor Bessy,” she said. “You sigh over all this. You don’t like the struggle, do you?”

“No,” Bessy said heavily. “I’m weary of it. I wished I’d heard different talk in my final days—no more clashing, clanging, and arguing about work, wages, masters, and hands. Just peace.”

“Poor girl,” Nicholas said. “Your days are far from ending. You look better already for a change. And I’ll be here to keep things lively.”

“Tobacco smoke chokes me,” Bessy muttered.

“Then I’ll never smoke in this house again,” Nicholas replied tenderly. “But why didn’t you tell me sooner, you foolish wench?”

She said nothing for a moment. Then, so quietly that only Margaret heard: “I reckon he’ll want all the comfort he can get—from his pipe, or drink—before it’s done.”

Nicholas went outside, clearly to finish his pipe. Bessy spoke passionately. “Am I not a fool? Am I not, Miss? I knew I should have kept him home—away from those who tempt a man to drink during a strike. And there—my tongue quarreled with his pipe—and now he’ll go off, I know he will, whenever he wants to smoke. And no one knows where it might end. I wish I’d let myself be choked first.”

“Does your father drink?” Margaret asked gently.

“No—not really,” Bessy replied, voice still wild. “But what would you do? There are days—like everyone else—when you just ache for a change, a spark. I’ve bought bread from another baker just because I couldn’t face the same sight, the same sound, the same taste, day after day. I’ve longed to be a man, to go out and roam—even if only to find new work. And men—father, all men—feel that stronger than I do. What can they do? It’s no fault if they go to the gin-shop to make their blood flow faster, to see colors, reflections, things they never see otherwise. Father’s never been a drunkard—but maybe he’s gotten worse now and then. Still,” her voice softened, “at times like this, when hope starts high but the weight grows heavy—where’s the comfort? He gets angry, mad—all of them do. Then he’s exhausted from anger, and might do things he’d regret. Bless your sweet face, but you don’t know what a strike truly is.”

“Come, Bessy,” Margaret said softly. “I won’t say you exaggerate—I don’t know enough. But perhaps, since you’re unwell, you’re seeing only the dark side. There may be brighter things too.”

“You speak easily,” Bessy said, “who’ve lived in green places all your life, never known want or worry—or evil, for that matter.”

“Take care,” Margaret said, her cheeks flushing, her eyes brightening. “How you judge, Bessy. I go home to my mother—who is so ill, so terribly ill, that death is the only escape from her suffering. Yet I must speak cheerfully to my father, who knows nothing of her real condition. The only one who could comfort her—whose presence would mean more than anything—is falsely accused. If he came to see her, he’d risk his life. I tell you this—only you, Bessy. Don’t speak of it. No one else in Milton—perhaps no one in England—knows. Do I not have care? Anxiety? Though I wear fine clothes and eat well, do I not suffer?”

Bessy bowed her head. “I ask your pardon. Sometimes, when I think of my life—and the little joy I’ve known—I’ve believed I was one of those doomed to die by a falling star from heaven—‘And the name of the star is called Wormwood; and the third part of the waters became wormwood; and men died of the waters, because they were made bitter.’ One bears pain better if it feels destined. Otherwise, it seems pointless.”

“Nay, Bessy,” Margaret said. “God does not delight in pain. Don’t dwell on prophecy. Read the clearer parts of Scripture.”

“I dare say it’s wiser,” Bessy replied, “but where would I hear such grand promises—words so far removed from this dreary world, this town above all—except in Revelation? Many times I’ve repeated the seventh chapter just for the sound. It’s like music—like an organ. No, I cannot abandon Revelation. It gives me more comfort than any other book.”

“Let me come and read some of my favorite chapters,” Margaret offered.

“Aye,” Bessy said eagerly. “Come. Father won’t hear you. He says my talk is useless—has nothing to do with today’s matters. That’s his business.”

“Where is your sister?”

“Gone fustian cutting. I didn’t want her to go—but we must live. The Union can’t support us much.”

“I must go now,” Margaret said. “But you’ve done me good, Bessy.”

“I done you good?” Bessy asked, surprised.

“Yes,” Margaret said. “I came here sad, thinking my grief was the only one in the world. But hearing of your years of struggle—your endurance—makes me stronger.”

“Bless you,” Bessy whispered. “I thought all good came from gentlefolk. I’ll grow proud if I believe I’ve helped you.”

“You won’t,” Margaret said. “If you think about it, you’ll only puzzle yourself. That’s one comfort.”

“You’re unlike anyone I’ve ever seen,” Bessy murmured. “I don’t know what to make of you.”

“Nor I of myself,” Margaret said. “Good-bye.”

Bessy stopped rocking, staring after her.

“I wonder if there are many like her down South,” she whispered. “She’s like a breath of country air—freshens me up. Who’d have thought that face—bright and strong as the angel I dream of—could carry such sorrow? I wonder how she’ll sin. All of us must sin. I think a great deal of her. But father does the same. Even Mary. It’s rare that she notices much.”

“My heart revolts within me, and two voices make themselves audible within my bosom.” — WALLenstein.

When Margaret returned home, she found two letters waiting on the table: one addressed to her mother, the other—bearing foreign postmarks, thin and silvery, rustling as she lifted it—clearly from Aunt Shaw. She picked up the latter, her eyes scanning the handwriting, when her father entered abruptly.

“So your mother is tired and has gone to bed early!” he said, his voice tight with concern. “I’m afraid such a stormy day was not ideal for the doctor’s visit. What did he say? Dixon told me he spoke with you.”

Margaret hesitated. Her father’s expression deepened into something more serious, more anxious.

“He doesn’t think her seriously ill?” he asked, pressing forward.

“No,” she replied quietly. “Not at present. He says she needs care. He was very kind, and promised to call again to see how the medicine works.”

“Only care?” Her father frowned. “He didn’t suggest a change of air? Didn’t he mention that this smoky town might be harming her?”

“No,” Margaret said firmly. “Not a word. I think he was genuinely concerned.”

“Doctors always look worried,” he said dismissively. “It’s part of their manner.”

But Margaret saw through the pretense. In the way he paced, in the tension of his hands, she sensed the fear he refused to name—the quiet dread that had taken root beneath his calm façade. He could not let go of the subject. Over and over, he returned to it, each time trying to dismiss any hint of danger, even as he clung to hope like a lifeline. It made Margaret’s heart ache.

“This letter is from Aunt Shaw, Papa,” she said gently. “She’s arrived in Naples, but finds it too hot. So she’s moved to Sorrento. But she doesn’t seem to enjoy Italy.”

He ignored her words. “Did the doctor say anything about diet?” he asked sharply.

“It was to be nourishing, digestible,” she answered. “Mother’s appetite seems fairly good.”

“Yes,” he murmured, “and that makes it all the stranger he should have mentioned food at all.”

“I asked him, Papa,” she said.

A silence followed. Then she added, with a slight smile, “Aunt Shaw says she sent me some coral ornaments. But she’s afraid the Milton dissenters won’t appreciate them. She still thinks of Dissenters as Quakers, doesn’t she?”

Her father gave a small nod. “If ever you notice your mother wanting anything—anything at all—be sure to tell me. I’m so afraid she hides what she truly desires. And please see after that girl Mrs. Thornton recommended. If we had a proper house-servant, Dixon could attend to her full-time. I’d guarantee it—we’d soon have her back on her feet. A little rest, a little peace—she’ll recover. Won’t she, Margaret?”

“I hope so,” Margaret said—but the sadness in her voice did not escape him.

He reached out and pinched her cheek. “Come now,” he said, forcing cheerfulness. “You’re looking pale. I may need to rouge you up. Take care of yourself, child, or you’ll be next needing the doctor.”

Yet he couldn’t settle. All evening, he crept back and forth on tiptoe, checking the bedroom door, listening for signs of movement. Margaret watched him, pained by his unease, by the way he tried to suppress the terror gnawing at his soul. Finally, he returned, somewhat relieved.

“She’s awake now,” he said softly. “She smiled when she saw me. Just her old smile. She says she feels refreshed, ready for tea. Where’s the note for her? She wants to read it. I’ll read it aloud while you prepare the tea.”

The note turned out to be an invitation from Mrs. Thornton—formal and precise—for Mr., Mrs., and Miss Hale to dine at the Thorntons’ on the twenty-first. Margaret was startled. After everything she’d learned that day, she expected refusal. Yet her mother had already decided.

Even before Margaret knew the contents, her mother had seized upon the idea with fervor. The dinner would break the monotony of her invalid life. When Margaret voiced hesitation, her mother insisted.

“Nay, Margaret,” she said firmly. “If she wishes it, then we must go. She wouldn’t ask unless she felt stronger—truly stronger than we believe, eh, Margaret?”

Mr. Hale looked at her anxiously. “Eh, Margaret?” he repeated, his hands trembling slightly.

It was cruel to deny him comfort. And yet, his fierce denial of fear somehow stirred a flicker of hope in her own heart.

“I do think she’s better since last night,” Margaret said. “Her eyes are brighter. Her color looks clearer.”

“God bless you,” her father whispered, almost reverently. “But is it true? Yesterday was so oppressive—everyone felt unwell. It was unfortunate Dr. Donaldson came then.”

And so he left, returning to his duties—now heavier, for he had pledged to deliver lectures at a nearby Lyceum. He chose Ecclesiastical Architecture, not because it suited the audience, but because it aligned with his own scholarly tastes. The institution, struggling financially, welcomed the free course from a man of learning like Mr. Hale, regardless of subject matter.

That evening, at the Thornton household, conversation turned to the guest list.

“Well, mother,” Mr. Thornton asked, “who has accepted your invitation for the twenty-first?”

Fanny fetched the notes. “The Slicksons accept. The Collingbrooks accept. The Stephenses accept. The Browns decline. The Hales—father and daughter come. Mother is too ill. Macphersons come. Mr. Horsfall. Mr. Young. I thought of asking the Porters, since the Browns can’t attend.”

“Very good,” said Mr. Thornton. “But I must say—I’m truly concerned about Mrs. Hale’s health, based on what Dr. Donaldson told me.”

“It’s odd they’d accept if she’s very ill,” Fanny remarked.

“I didn’t say *very* ill,” her brother corrected sharply. “Just far from well. They may not realize it themselves.”

Then, suddenly, he remembered—Dr. Donaldson had spoken plainly to Margaret. She likely knew the truth.

“Perhaps they’re fully aware,” he mused aloud. “Of the advantage it would bring Mr. Hale—to meet people like the Stephenses and Collingbrooks.”

“That motive wouldn’t influence them,” he insisted. “No. I think I understand why they’re coming.”

“John!” Fanny laughed lightly, nervously. “How you claim to understand these Hales, yet refuse to admit we might know anything about them. Are they really so different from anyone else?”

She meant no harm. But the question stung. He clenched his jaw, refusing to respond.

“They don’t seem unusual to me,” Mrs. Thornton observed. “Mr. Hale appears decent enough—perhaps a bit too simple for trade. That’s why it’s fortunate he was first a clergyman, then a teacher. His wife is a fine lady in her sickness, and the daughter—ah, the daughter is the one who puzzles me. She carries herself with airs, though I cannot fathom why. She seems to think herself above her company. And yet—they’re not wealthy. From what I’ve heard, they never were.”

“And she’s not accomplished,” Fanny added. “She can’t play the piano.”

“Go on, Fanny,” her mother said, amused. “What else does she lack to meet your standards?”

“Nay, John,” Mrs. Thornton said, turning to her son. “Fanny’s remark did no harm. I myself heard Miss Hale say she couldn’t play. If you’d leave us alone, we might learn to like her—and see her merits.”

“I’m certain I never could!” Fanny murmured, shielded by her mother’s presence.

Mr. Thornton heard, but chose silence. He walked the length of the dining room, wishing candles would be lit so he could retreat to reading or writing—anything to end the talk. But he never questioned his mother’s habits, born of lifelong thrift.

“Mother,” he stopped, speaking plainly, “I wish you would like Miss Hale.”

She looked surprised. “Why? You’re not thinking of marrying her? A girl without a penny?”

“She would never have me,” he said, laughing shortly.

“No, I suppose not,” his mother agreed. “She laughed in my face when I praised her for speaking plainly about Mr. Bell’s remarks in your favor. I liked her honesty—it showed she had no designs on you. But then, the next moment, she annoyed me terribly by acting as if—well, never mind. But you’re right—she holds herself too high to consider you. The impudent girl! I’d like to know where she’d find a better match!”

“Then you’ll believe me,” he said steadily, “when I say I speak of her not out of personal interest. I am convinced—just as you are—that she’s in trouble. Perhaps lacking proper care. I only ask you to be ready to help her, should she need it. Now, Fanny,” he continued, “I trust you have enough delicacy to understand that suggesting I have ulterior motives would wound her more than me. She would take it harder.”

“I cannot forgive her pride,” Mrs. Thornton declared. “I’ll befriend her—if you ask it, John. I’d befriend Jezebel herself if you asked. But this girl—who turns up her nose at us all—turns up her nose at *you*—”

“Nay, mother,” he said quietly, “I’ve never put myself within reach of her scorn. I mean never to.”

“Scorn, indeed!” she snorted—a sound full of meaning. “Don’t speak of her if I must be kind to her. When I’m near her, I don’t know whether I like or dislike her. But when I think of her—when I hear you talk—then I hate her. I can see she treats you with contempt, as if you’d confessed it.”

“And if she does,” he said slowly, “I am not a boy to be cowed by a proud glance. I laugh at it.”

“Of course! And at her too—with her lofty notions and haughty ways!”

“Then why do you speak of her so often?” Fanny asked, weary. “I’m sick of the subject.”

“Well,” her brother replied, a trace of bitterness in his tone, “let’s find something more pleasant. How about a strike? That might cheer us.”

“Have the workers actually walked out?” Mrs. Thornton asked, her interest sharpened.

“Hamper’s men are out,” he said. “Mine are finishing their week—afraid of prosecution. I’d have punished every one who left early. But the cost of legal fees would exceed their worth—ungrateful scoundrels!”

“To be sure,” she agreed. “But I’d show them who’s master. I’d hire whoever I pleased.”

“You can,” he said. “And you will—if this goes on. It’ll cost money, and there’s risk. But I’d rather do it than give in.”

“If there’s extra expense,” she sighed, “I regret we’re hosting a dinner now.”

“So do I,” he admitted—not for the cost, but because he’d have much on his mind. “But we must invite Mr. Horsfall—he won’t stay long. The others? We owe them dinners. It’s all one burden.”

He resumed pacing, breathing deeply, as if fighting off a troubling thought. Fanny peppered her mother with trivial questions—about the weather, the servants, the price of milk—each met with curt replies. She was relieved when, at ten o’clock, the servants filed in for prayers. Mrs. Thornton read a chapter from the Old Testament, then led the family in prayer. When it ended, she wished her son goodnight—with that steady gaze of hers, devoid of warmth, yet carrying a depth of affection he could not ignore.

He continued walking. His plans—carefully laid, painfully earned—had been thrown into chaos by the looming strike. Hours of foresight, months of preparation, undone by blind folly. These men, who believed themselves capable of directing the masters’ affairs, would destroy themselves even more than him. Hamper had said just that day: if ruined by the strike, he’d start anew, comforted by knowing those who caused it were worse off—because he had both head and hands, while they had only hands. But for Mr. Thornton, revenge brought no satisfaction. He valued the position he’d built with blood, sweat, and relentless effort. To see it threatened by ignorance—by arrogance—was agony.

He paced, grinding his teeth. At last, the clock struck two. Candles flickered. He lit his own, muttering under his breath:

“Once and for all, they shall know whom they have to deal with. I’ll give them two weeks—no more. If they don’t see their madness by then, I’ll bring in Irish labor. I suspect it’s Slickson’s doing—confound him and his schemes! He thought he was overstocked. So he pretended to yield when the delegation came—only to embolden them. That’s where it began.”

“As angels in some brighter dreams
Call to the soul when man doth sleep,
So some strange thoughts transcend our wonted themes,
And into glory peep.”

Mrs. Hale found herself curiously captivated by the idea of the Thornton dinner party. It stirred her imagination like a child anticipating a grand feast, eager for every detail to be laid bare before the moment arrived. The quiet, confined life of an invalid often brings forth such childlike wonder—both she and children alike live within narrow walls, believing their world to be the only one that matters. Mrs. Hale had once known vanity as a young woman; perhaps she had felt its sting keenly when she became the wife of a poor clergyman. Those feelings had been buried beneath years of modesty, but they were not gone. Now, she delighted in picturing Margaret dressed for such an occasion, musing over what gown she might wear, with a nervous excitement that amused her daughter.

“Then you’re certain you’ll wear your white silk?” Mrs. Hale asked, her voice trembling slightly. “Are you sure it still fits? It’s nearly a year since Edith was married!”

“Oh, yes, Mama,” Margaret replied cheerfully. “Mrs. Murray made it, so it should fit perfectly. Maybe it’s a hair shorter or longer-waisted, depending on whether I’ve gained or lost weight. But I don’t believe I’ve changed at all.”

“Perhaps Dixon should see it,” suggested Mrs. Hale. “It might have yellowed from sitting unused.”

“If you wish,” Margaret said gently. “But if it comes to the worst, I have a lovely pink gauze—Aunt Shaw gave it to me just a few months before Edith’s wedding. That couldn’t have faded.”

“No,” Mrs. Hale murmured, “but it may have lost its color.”

“Well,” Margaret laughed, “then there’s always the green silk. I feel as though I’m drowning in choices.”

“I only wish I knew what would be best,” Mrs. Hale confessed, wringing her hands.

Margaret’s tone softened instantly. “Shall I go try them on one by one, Mama? Then you could decide which you prefer?”

“Yes,” Mrs. Hale said hesitantly. “That might be best.”

So Margaret left the room, already half tempted to playfully exaggerate her transformation—making her white silk billow like a cloud, or stepping backward like a queen before her mother. But seeing how seriously her mother took the matter, she quickly composed herself. She couldn’t understand why the world—her world—should care so much about a dress. Yet that very afternoon, when she mentioned her invitation to Bessy Higgins—prompted by Mrs. Thornton’s promise to inquire about a servant—Bessy reacted with sudden intensity.

“Dear!” she exclaimed. “You’re going to dine at Mr. Thornton’s at Marlborough Mills?”

“Yes, Bessy. Why are you so surprised?”

“Oh, I dunno,” Bessy replied, her eyes wide. “But they entertain all the finest folk in Milton.”

“And you don’t think we’re among the finest here, then, Bessy?”

A flush crept across Bessy’s cheeks. “Well,” she admitted, “they reckon money counts for a lot around here. And I suppose you haven’t got much.”

“No,” Margaret agreed quietly. “That’s true. But we’re educated people. We’ve lived among educated people. Is it so extraordinary that someone like Mr. Thornton—once a draper’s assistant—would come to my father to learn, and now invite us to his home? I don’t mean to fault him. Few men who began where he did could have risen so far.”

“But can you return the favor?” Bessy pressed. “Can you host a dinner in your little house? His place is three times the size.”

“I think we could manage,” Margaret said steadily. “Maybe not in such a large room or with so many guests. But we’ve never thought about it in terms of competition.”

“I never imagined you’d be dining with the Thorntons,” Bessy repeated, astonished. “Why, even the mayor dines there—the Members of Parliament, everyone important.”

“I believe I could hold my head high beside the mayor of Milton,” Margaret said with quiet confidence.

“But those ladies—they dress so finely!” Bessy added, glancing nervously at Margaret’s simple print dress, which her Milton eyes valued at sevenpence a yard.

Margaret smiled, her face lighting up. “Thank you, Bessy, for caring so much about how I’ll look among all those elegant people. But I have several fine dresses—just last week, I’d have said they were far too grand for anything I’d ever need here. But since I’m going to Mr. Thornton’s, and might meet the mayor, I’ll certainly wear my very best.”

“What will you wear?” Bessy asked, relieved.

“White silk,” Margaret answered. “The same gown I wore to my cousin’s wedding a year ago.”

“That’ll do!” Bessy declared, sinking back into her chair. “I wouldn’t want you looked down upon.”

“Oh, I’ll be fine enough,” Margaret teased. “If that keeps me from being overlooked in Milton.”

“I wish I could see you dressed,” Bessy sighed. “I don’t suppose you’re what folks call pretty—you’re not red and white enough for that. But do you know, I dreamed of you long before I ever saw you?”

“Nonsense, Bessy!”

“Ay, but I did,” she insisted. “Your very face—looking out with those clear, steady eyes from the darkness, your hair blown back from your brow, spreading like rays around your forehead, just as it is now—smooth and straight—and you always came to give me strength, drawing it from your deep, comforting gaze. You were clothed in shining garments—just like the one you’re going to wear. So, you see—it was you!”

“No, Bessy,” Margaret said softly. “It was only a dream.”

“And why shouldn’t I dream in my affliction, like others have?” Bessy retorted. “Didn’t many in the Bible? And see visions too! Even my father believes in dreams. I tell you again—I saw you clearly, coming swiftly toward me, your hair flying back from the motion, just like it grows, standing slightly away—exactly like this—and wearing that white shining dress. Let me come and see you in it. I want to touch you, as if you were truly the one from my dream.”

“My dear Bessy,” Margaret said gently, “it’s just your fancy.”

“Fancy or no,” Bessy insisted, “you’ve come, just as I knew you would when I saw your movement in my dream. And when you’re near me, I feel easier, comforted—just as a fire warms you on a cold day. You said it was the twenty-first. Please God, I’ll come to see you.”

“Oh, Bessy,” Margaret whispered, “you may come, of course—but don’t speak like this. It makes me sorrowful.”

“Then I’ll keep it to myself,” Bessy said, biting her lip. “Even if it kills me. But it’s true, all the same.”

Margaret remained silent. After a long pause, she said, “Let’s talk about it another time, if you believe it. But not now. Tell me—has your father returned?”

“Ay,” Bessy replied heavily, her earlier brightness gone. “He and many others—Hamper’s men, and more besides. The women are as fierce as the men this time. Food is high—and they must feed their children, I suppose. If the Thorntons sent them meals—same money spent on potatoes and flour could quiet many a crying baby, and calm a mother’s heart for a while!”

“Don’t say such things,” Margaret pleaded. “You make me feel guilty for going to this dinner.”

“No,” Bessy said firmly. “Some are predestined to sumptuous feasts and fine linen—perhaps you are one. Others toil all their lives, and even dogs are less pitiful today than Lazarus was. But if you ask me to cool your tongue with the tip of my finger, I’ll cross the great gulf to reach you—for the sake of what you’ve done for me.”

“Bessy,” Margaret said, alarmed, “you’re feverish. I can feel it in your hand, and in your words. There won’t be enough division in that terrible day to separate us by wealth—some were beggars, some rich. We’ll not be judged by that, but by our faithfulness to Christ.”

Margaret rose and fetched water. Dipping her handkerchief, she placed the cool cloth on Bessy’s forehead and began warming her icy feet. Bessy closed her eyes, allowing herself to be soothed. At last, she murmured,

“You’d have been driven mad, just like me, if you’d had one after another coming in, asking for Father, staying to tell me their stories. Some spoke of deadly hatred—what they said about the masters made my blood run cold. But most—especially the women—kept crying, tears streaming down their faces, unheeded, about the price of meat, and how their children couldn’t sleep at night from hunger.”

“And do they think the strike will help?” Margaret asked.

“They say so,” Bessy replied. “They claim trade has been good for years, and the masters have made huge profits. How much, Father doesn’t know—but the Union does. And naturally, they want their share now that food is rising. The Union says they’d fail in duty if they didn’t demand their portion. But the masters have the upper hand somehow—and I fear they’ll keep it forever. It’s like Armageddon—fighting and grinning, until even as they fight, they’re dragged down into the pit.”

At that moment, Nicholas Higgins entered. He caught the last of her words.

“Ay!” he declared. “I’ll fight on too—and I’ll win this time. It won’t take long for them to give in. They’ve got orders under contract—soon they’ll realize it’s better to give us five percent than lose the profit, not to mention the fine for breaking the contract. Hah! My masters—I know who’ll win.”

Margaret sensed from his manner that he had been drinking—not from what he said, but from the fervor of his speech. Bessy’s evident anxiety confirmed it. She turned to Margaret.

“The twenty-first—that’s Thursday next. I may come to see you dressed for Thornton’s, I reckon. What time is your dinner?”

Before Margaret could reply, Higgins burst in:

“Thornton’s! Are you going to dine at Thornton’s? Tell him to raise a toast to the success of his orders. By the twenty-first, I’ll bet he’ll be scratching his head wondering how to finish them on time. Tell him seven hundred of us will march into Marlborough Mills the morning after he gives the five percent—and we’ll help him through his contract in no time. You’ll have them all there. My master, Hamper—he’s old-fashioned. Never speaks ill, never swears. I’d think he’d die if he spoke civil to me. But his bark’s worse than his bite. You can tell him one of his men said so, if you like. Eh! You’ll have a fine gathering of mill owners at Thornton’s! I’d love to speak to them after dinner, when they’re tired and can’t run away. I’d tell them my mind. I’d speak up against the cruel way they treat us!”

“Good-bye,” Margaret said quickly. “Good-bye, Bessy. I’ll expect you on the twenty-first, if you’re well.”

Dr. Donaldson’s treatments had brought significant relief to Mrs. Hale at first. Both she and Margaret began to hope he might have been mistaken—that recovery might yet be possible. Mr. Hale, though he’d never fully grasped the severity of their fears, now showed visible relief, proving how deeply the ordeal had shaken him. Only Dixon remained grim, whispering warnings to Margaret. But she refused to listen—she chose to hope.

This small light inside their home was needed, for outside, even to their untrained eyes, the air was thick with unrest. Mr. Hale had friends among the working men, and they shared their tales of hardship and endurance with him. They would never have spoken so openly to someone in power—but he was a stranger, confused by the system, and each wanted him to judge their suffering. He brought these grievances to Mr. Thornton, hoping the factory owner could explain their causes. Thornton always responded with economic logic: trade had its cycles—prosperity waxed and waned. In decline, some masters and workers inevitably fell, crushed by circumstance. He spoke as if this were inevitable, unavoidable—neither employer nor worker had a right to complain. The master, unable to continue, would feel failure, wounded pride, trampled by rivals, humbled into begging for work. The worker, passed by in progress, would long to rest—but could not, haunted by the cries of children left behind. He envied the bird that fed its young with its own blood.

Margaret recoiled from this cold reasoning—as if commerce were everything, humanity nothing. Yet Thornton had shown kindness—coming that evening to offer her, privately, every comfort his wealth and foresight had accumulated, knowing from Dr. Donaldson that Mrs. Hale might need them. His presence, after such words—his knowledge of the fate she tried to deny—set her teeth on edge. How could he, who understood her secret pain, speak so harshly of human suffering?
