His pitying eyes, his grave voice—how reconcile them with his rigid doctrines? The contradiction tore at her soul, especially with Bessy’s tales fresh in her mind.

Nicholas Higgins, however, spoke differently. Appointed a committee-man, he claimed to know secrets the public did not. On the eve of Mrs. Thornton’s dinner, Margaret found him arguing fiercely with Boucher, a neighbor whose name she recognized—sometimes a source of compassion, sometimes rage. When she entered, Higgins was clearly agitated. Boucher stood by the mantel, hands gripping it, staring wildly into the fire, his despair both touching and infuriating. Bessy rocked violently, a sign of distress Margaret now recognized. Her sister Mary tied on her bonnet clumsily, blubbering as she fled, desperate to escape.

Margaret paused at the door, then silently slipped to a seat near Bessy. Higgins nodded curtly. Mary rushed out, sobbing. Boucher paid no attention.

“It’s no use, Higgins,” he said. “She can’t live long like this. She’s sinking—not from lack of food, but from seeing the children starve. Five shillings a week suits you—two mouths, one earning her own. But it’s crushing us. And I swear—if she dies before we get the five percent, I’ll throw the money back in the master’s face and curse the whole cruel world. I’ll hate you, and the entire Union. I’ll chase you through heaven with my hatred—if you’re leading me astray. You said on Wednesday last week—now it’s Tuesday of the second week—that within two weeks the masters would come begging us to return, at our wages. Time’s nearly up—and our little Jack lies in bed, too weak to cry, just sobbing now and then from hunger. Our little Jack, I tell you! She’s never looked up since birth, and loves him as if he were her life—for I know he’s cost me that price. Our little Jack, who woke me each morning with his sweet lips pressing my rough face, seeking a smooth spot to kiss—now he lies starving.” His sobs choked him. Higgins looked up, eyes full of tears, toward Margaret.

“Hold up, man,” he said. “Your little Jack won’t starve. I’ve got money—we’ll buy him milk and a four-pounder right now. What’s mine is yours. Just don’t lose heart.” He fumbled in a tea-pot for coins. “I swear we’ll win. One more week, and you’ll see the masters begging us to return. The Union—I’ll see to it you have enough for the children and your wife. Don’t turn faint-hearted, and go to the tyrants for work.”

Boucher turned slowly—his face pale, gaunt, streaked with tears, utterly hopeless. Its stillness forced Margaret to weep.

“You know well,” he said, “that a worse tyrant than any master says, ‘Starve to death, let them all starve, before you return to the Union.’ You know it, Nicholas, even though you’re one of them. You may be kind individually—but banded together, you have no more mercy than a wolf ravenous with hunger.”

Higgins gripped the door handle, then turned sharply.

“So help me God!” he cried. “If I’m not doing right, if I’m wrong when I think I’m right, it’s their sin who left me in ignorance. I’ve thought till my brain ached, John. I say again—there’s no help but faith in the Union. They’ll win. See if they don’t!”

Neither Margaret nor Bessy had spoken. Their silence was filled only by the breathless sighs they exchanged.

“I never thought to hear Father call on God again,” Bessy whispered. “But you heard him say, ‘So help me God!’”

“Yes,” Margaret said softly. “Let me bring you what money I can spare—let me bring food for that man’s children. Don’t let them know it comes from anyone but your father. It will be little.”

Bessy lay back, ignoring her words. She didn’t cry—only trembled.

“My heart’s drained dry of tears,” she said. “Boucher’s told me of his fears lately. He’s weak, I know—but he’s a man. I’ve been angry with him and his wife before, but now I see—people aren’t wise, yet God lets them live. Gives them someone to love, someone who loves them back, as good as Solomon. And when sorrow comes to them, it hurts as much as it did Solomon. I can’t understand. Perhaps it’s better that Boucher has the Union to care for him. But I’d like to see the men who built the Union—face them one by one with Boucher. I reckon, if they heard him, they’d tell him—go back, get what you can, even if it’s less than ordered.”

Margaret sat in silence. How could she leave this scene and return to comfort, forgetting that voice—so full of agony, saying more than words could bear? She took out her purse. Not much was hers, but she placed it in Bessy’s hands without speaking.

“Thank you,” Bessy said. “Many get no more, and are not as bad off. But Father won’t let them starve now he knows. Boucher’s been pulled down by his children—his wife sick, everything pawned this past year. Don’t think we’d let them starve, even if we’re hard-pressed. If neighbors don’t care for neighbors, who will? Besides, Father’s certain the masters must give in soon. But thank you—thank you for yourself, as much as for Boucher. It warms my heart.”

Bessy seemed quieter today, but terribly weary. As she finished, she looked so faint that Margaret grew alarmed.

“Old and young, boy, let ’em all eat, I have it;
Let ’em have ten tire of teeth a-piece, I care not.”

Margaret returned home burdened with the weight of what she had seen and heard in the narrow streets of Milton. Her thoughts were heavy, her spirits low, as she struggled to summon the cheerfulness required by her mother’s expectations. Mrs. Hale, confined to the house and eager for news from the outside world, looked to Margaret’s return as a source of daily gossip. The slightest excursion was expected to yield stories, laughter, and updates on the lives of others.

“And can your factory friend come on Thursday to see you dressed?” Mrs. Hale asked, her voice light but her eyes sharp with curiosity.

“She was so ill I never thought of asking her,” Margaret replied, her voice low and strained.

“Dear! Everyone seems to be unwell these days,” Mrs. Hale murmured, a flicker of resentment crossing her face—common among invalids who feel overlooked when others suffer more visibly. “But it must be dreadful to be sick in one of those cramped back lanes.” Her tone softened, touched by compassion, and the old Helstone sensibilities stirred within her.

“It’s bad enough here,” Margaret said gently. “What could we do for her? Mr. Thornton sent me some of his old port wine while I was out. Would that help?”

“No, mamma,” Margaret insisted. “I don’t think they’re very poor—at least, not by their own account. And Bessy has consumption. She won’t need wine. Perhaps I might take her some preserve—made from our Helstone fruit. But there’s another family I’d like to help—Oh, mamma, how can I go off to smart parties in my finery, after what I’ve seen today?” she burst out, breaking through the restraint she had imposed upon herself.

She told her mother everything—the squalor of the cottage, the feverish woman, the children huddled in silence, the desperate hopelessness that clung to the air like smoke.

Mrs. Hale was deeply disturbed. She could not sit still. She ordered Margaret to pack a basket immediately—right then—in the drawing-room. It must go without delay. When Margaret hesitated, explaining that Higgins had already provided for them and that money had been left with Bessy, Mrs. Hale turned sharply.

“You’re unfeeling!” she declared. “You don’t understand how much this means.”

She would not rest until the basket was dispatched. Then, a moment later, she paused, troubled.

“But perhaps we’ve done wrong,” she whispered. “Mr. Thornton said only last time he visited that helping the turn-outs only prolongs their struggle. And this Boucher man—he was one of them, wasn’t he?”

The question was passed to Mr. Hale when he came upstairs, fresh from tutoring Mr. Thornton. He had just finished a lesson, which, as usual, had led into a long conversation about philosophy, theology, and the nature of duty.

Margaret cared little about such consequences. In her present state, she could only feel sorrow and urgency. Mr. Hale listened carefully, trying to weigh both sides. He recalled Mr. Thornton’s arguments—clear, decisive, rooted in economic logic—and yet found himself unable to condemn his wife and daughter.

They had done right, he decided. Not just in this case, but in spirit. Yet, as a general rule, Mr. Thornton was correct: if the strike continued, the masters would eventually bring in laborers from afar—or invent machines that would render human hands obsolete. So, to aid the strikers now might only delay the inevitable, and prolong their suffering.

Still, he resolved to visit Boucher himself the next morning.

He did so. Boucher was not at home, but his wife spoke with him at length. He promised to secure an infirmary order for her. Seeing the provisions left by Mrs. Hale—plentiful, even generous—and watching the children, who seemed to run the household in their father’s absence, Mr. Hale returned with a surprisingly hopeful report.

“It’s better than I feared,” he said, though he admitted he still felt uncertain. “These homes are different from ours at Helstone. They have furniture our labourers wouldn’t dream of owning, food we’d consider luxuries. Yet now, with no wages, they have nothing but the pawnshop to turn to. One must learn a new standard here.”

Bessy, too, seemed slightly improved. Still weak, she appeared to have forgotten her earlier wish to see Margaret dressed—perhaps it had been a fever dream.

Margaret could not help comparing this evening’s preparation—her dressing for a party she didn’t want to attend—with the joyful, carefree rituals of the past. With Edith, under the sunlit skies of Helstone, they had laughed over ribbons and gowns, full of life and anticipation. Now, the act of dressing brought no joy, only a sense of obligation. The only comfort was knowing her mother would be pleased.

When Dixon opened the drawing-room door, she exclaimed, “Miss Hale looks well, ma’am—doesn’t she? Mrs. Shaw’s coral couldn’t have come in better. It gives just the right touch of colour. Otherwise, Miss Margaret, you’d have been too pale.”

Margaret’s thick black hair was wound tightly into massive coils around her head, like a crown, and fastened behind with two large coral pins shaped like arrows. Her white silk sleeves were looped up with coral strings, and across her throat lay a heavy strand of coral beads.

“Oh, Margaret!” Mrs. Hale sighed. “How I’d love to go with you to one of the old Barrington assemblies—just as Lady Beresford used to take me.”

Margaret kissed her mother, touched by the sentiment, though she could barely smile. “I’d rather stay home with you—much rather, mamma.”

“Nonsense, darling!” Mrs. Hale teased. “Be sure you notice the dinner well. I’ll enjoy hearing how they manage things in Milton. Especially the second course. Look what they have instead of game!”

She would have been astonished, had she seen the table—laden with silver, crystal, and dishes piled high with delicacies. To Margaret, accustomed to London refinement, it seemed excessive. Half the amount would have sufficed, and the effect would have been more elegant. But this was Mrs. Thornton’s way—generous, almost ostentatious. She believed every guest should have access to every dainty, whether they ate it or not.

Her son shared this belief. Though he lived frugally, denying himself even a sixpence, he took pride in the grandeur of his hospitality. This dinner, though reluctantly arranged, was to be magnificent. He had not known any society except one built on elaborate meals, and even now, he welcomed the spectacle.

Margaret and her father arrived first. Mr. Hale was punctual, as always. The drawing-room was empty save for Mrs. Thornton and Fanny. Every cover was removed. The room blazed with yellow damask and a brilliant floral carpet. Ornaments crowded every corner, creating a visual overload. Outside, the great mill loomed, casting its shadow early across the summer evening.

“My son was detained by business,” Mrs. Thornton said. “He will be here directly. May I ask you to take a seat?”

Mr. Hale stood by the window. “Doesn’t the proximity to the mill trouble you sometimes?” he asked.

“Never,” she replied, stiffening. “I am not so refined as to forget where my son’s wealth comes from. Besides, there is no other factory like it in Milton. One room alone measures two hundred and twenty square yards.”

“The noise and smoke—the constant comings and goings of workers—might be bothersome,” Mr. Hale suggested.

“I agree,” said Fanny. “There’s always steam and oil—and the noise is deafening.”

“I’ve heard music called worse,” Mr. Hale replied. “The engine-room is at the street end. We hardly hear it, except in summer. As for the hum of the workers—it’s like bees. It doesn’t disturb me. I associate it with my son. I know it belongs to him. His mind directs it.”

She paused. “Now, there are no sounds from the mill. The hands have turned out—ungrateful creatures. You may have heard. But my son’s business tonight concerns the steps he’s taking to teach them their place.”

Her face darkened with anger. It did not fade when Mr. Thornton entered. He carried the weight of worry, though he greeted his guests warmly. He shook hands with Margaret—though neither noticed it was their first meeting. He inquired after Mrs. Hale, heard Mr. Hale’s optimistic account, and glanced at Margaret, seeking signs of dissent. None appeared.

And then, as he looked, he was struck anew by her beauty. He had never seen her thus—so composed, so dignified in her attire. She seemed made for such elegance. Her presence was calm, her expression serene. She talked quietly with Fanny, whose restless fingers fidgeted with her dress, her eyes darting about aimlessly. Margaret sat still, her head slightly forward, her gaze steady, her lips parted in gentle interest. Light caught the raven hair atop her head, tracing a smooth line down to her ivory shoulder. Her arms rested softly, motionless, yet graceful.

Mr. Thornton sighed—deeply, silently. Then he turned away, forcing himself into conversation with Mr. Hale.

More guests arrived. Fanny left Margaret’s side to assist her mother. Mr. Thornton noticed that no one spoke to her. He felt uneasy—but he did not approach her. He did not look at her. Yet he knew exactly what she was doing, or not doing, better than anyone else in the room.

Margaret, unaware of her own presence, watched the others with quiet amusement. She didn’t care whether she was noticed.

Someone led her downstairs to dinner. She didn’t catch the name. He didn’t speak much.

At the table, the men debated fiercely. Mr. Horsfall, the visitor, asked questions about the trade. The Milton men answered with pride. A dispute arose. It was referred to Mr. Thornton, who had spoken little before. But when he spoke, his reasoning was clear, firm, undeniable. Even his opponents yielded.

Margaret listened, drawn in. She saw him differently now—not as the proud, impatient man who had once visited their home, but as a leader, confident and respected. Among his peers, he needed no defense. He was acknowledged. And that gave his manner a quiet strength she had never seen before.

He rarely spoke to the ladies. To Margaret, he said almost nothing.

Yet she found herself enjoying the dinner. She understood more now—technical terms, local interests. She followed the debate closely, silently aligning herself with the Milton men. Their conversation was earnest, alive, unlike the tired chatter of London soirées.

She wondered why no one mentioned the strike. Then she realized—these men saw it as inevitable. The workers were fools, playing into the hands of paid agitators. If they chose to destroy themselves, they must bear the consequences. Some noted that Thornton looked weary. Of course—he’d lose from the strike. But it could happen to anyone. And Thornton was strong—iron, they called him. The men had misjudged their man.

They chuckled inwardly at the idea of the workers’ defeat.

After dinner, the ladies chattered about dresses and dinners. Margaret found it dull. She was glad when the gentlemen returned. She liked their confidence, their belief in progress, their defiance of limits. They spoke of future triumphs—over matter, over time—grand visions that none would live to see.

Then Mr. Thornton stood beside her, speaking quietly.

“I could see you were on our side during the discussion—were you not, Miss Hale?”

“Certainly,” she replied. “Though I know so little. I was surprised to hear Mr. Horsfall speak of someone named Morison—someone who thinks quite differently. Is he a gentleman?”

“I’m not the one to judge,” Mr. Thornton said. “But I’d say he’s no true man. I don’t know him—only from Mr. Horsvalid’s description.”

“I suspect my ‘gentleman’ includes your ‘true man.’”

“And more,” he replied. “I differ. A man is higher, fuller, than a gentleman.”

“What do you mean?” she asked.

“I believe ‘gentleman’ describes a person in relation to others. But ‘man’—that speaks of one’s relationship to himself, to life, to eternity. A castaway on a desert island, a prisoner in a dungeon, even a saint on Patmos—they are best described as *men*. I grow weary of the word ‘gentlemanly.’ It’s often misused, exaggerated. While the simple words ‘man’ and ‘manly’ are ignored. I find it a kind of cant.”

Margaret thought. But before she could reply, he was pulled away by the manufacturers.

She heard their voices—short, firm answers, like distant cannon fire. They were discussing the strike.

“That has been done,” Mr. Thornton said.

A murmur rose. “All arrangements made.”

Mr. Slickson grasped his arm. “But there are risks—difficulties.”

“I take the risk,” Mr. Thornton replied. “You need not join unless you choose.”

More objections. “I’m not afraid of incendiarism. We’re open enemies. I can protect myself—and those who come to me for work. They know my resolve.”

Mr. Horsfall stepped aside, asking him about the strike—then, as Margaret guessed, about her.

“A Milton lady?” he asked.

“No,” came the cold reply. “From the south—Hampshire, I believe.”

Meanwhile, Mrs. Slickson questioned Fanny.

“Who is that beautiful girl? A sister of Mr. Horsfall’s?”

“Oh dear, no. That’s Mr. Hale—her father—talking to Mr. Stephens. He gives lessons. My brother John goes twice a week. That’s why mamma invited them—hoping to make connections. We have prospectuses, if you’d like one.”

“Mr. Thornton!” Mrs. Slickson exclaimed. “Does he really find time to study with a tutor—amidst all his business—and this terrible strike?”

Fanny hesitated. Was she proud? Ashamed? She wasn’t sure. She flushed, unsure of how to feel.

The guests began to disperse.

"On earth is known to none
The smile that is not sister to a tear."

Margaret and her father walked home together under the quiet glow of a summer night. The air was cool and fresh, the streets clean and still, and Margaret, in her white silk dress—kilted up to her knee like Leezie Lindsay’s green satin gown from the old ballad—moved with lightness, almost as if she were dancing through the evening. Her spirits rose with every step, buoyed by the crisp breeze and the sense of freedom after an evening spent among strangers and new ideas.

“I think Mr. Thornton isn’t quite at ease about the strike,” she said, glancing toward her father. “He seemed anxious tonight.”

“Indeed, I wouldn’t expect him to be,” replied Mr. Hale. “But he spoke with his usual composure when others offered their opinions just before we left.”

“He did so after dinner too,” Margaret added. “Nothing seems to ruffle his manner, yet there’s something strained in his face. It strikes me as worried.”

“I should be troubled if I were in his place,” Mr. Hale said. “He must know how deeply his workers resent him—how they see him as a ‘hard man,’ not because he’s unjust, but because he’s unyielding. He stands fast on his rights, as though they were absolute, without regard for what we all are before God. I’m glad you noticed his anxiety. When I remember Boucher’s wild words and agitated state, I can scarcely bear to think how calmly Mr. Thornton responded.”

“In truth,” Margaret said, “I’m not convinced Boucher was truly desperate. At the moment, yes—he was clearly distressed. But these unions have ways of providing relief, even when it seems hopeless. And from what you told me, he’s the kind of man who wears his emotions openly—passionate, impulsive. That doesn’t mean he’s beyond help.”

“Oh, Margaret!” Mr. Hale sighed, half amused, half exasperated. “You’re defending him now?”

“No, not exactly,” she replied. “But I want to be fair. Mr. Thornton may be proud to the point of hiding his feelings. That’s the sort of man I might admire—strong, self-contained, capable. And yet… I don’t feel certain those feelings exist beneath the surface. He has great strength of character, and intelligence beyond what one might expect given his background.”

“It’s not so little,” Mr. Hale countered gently. “He’s lived a practical life since youth, exercising judgment, restraint, responsibility. That shapes the mind. He lacks only the wisdom drawn from history—the deeper understanding of human nature and society. But he knows he lacks it. He sees it. And that awareness matters.”

“You’re biased against him, papa,” Margaret said softly.

“And why shouldn’t I be?” he asked, smiling faintly. “He’s your first encounter with a manufacturer—a man of trade. He’s your first olive. Let me make a face while I swallow it.”

She laughed. “I know he’s good for his kind. And perhaps, in time, I’ll come to like the kind. I already find myself intrigued by what the men discussed tonight—though I understood barely half. I was sorry when Miss Thornton came to take me away, saying I’d be uncomfortable as the only woman among so many gentlemen. I hadn’t thought of it—I was too busy listening. And the ladies? Oh, papa—they were so dull! Yet it was clever. It reminded me of our childhood game where we had to weave certain nouns into sentences.”

“What do you mean?” Mr. Hale asked.

“They used words that signaled wealth—housekeepers, under-gardeners, glasshouses, lace, diamonds—and each lady crafted her speech so that these items appeared naturally, as if by accident. It was like a subtle display of status.”

“You’ll be proud of your own servant when you finally have one,” he said, chuckling. “If Mrs. Thornton’s stories are true.”

“I shall,” Margaret agreed. “And I felt like a hypocrite tonight, sitting here in my fine silk, hands idle, while I remembered all the honest work done by the servants today. They took me for a fine lady, I’m sure.”

“Even I mistook you,” Mr. Hale said quietly, a soft smile playing on his lips.

But the smile vanished instantly when Dixon opened the door. Her face was pale, her eyes wide with fear.

“Oh, master! Oh, Miss Margaret! Thank God you’ve come! Dr. Donaldson is here. The servant next door ran for him—the charwoman’s gone home. She’s better now, but oh, sir! I thought she’d die an hour ago!”

Mr. Hale clutched Margaret’s arm to keep himself upright. His face twisted with dread. He looked at his daughter—her expression filled with sorrow, but not the raw terror that gripped him. She knew more than he did, yet she listened with that same terrible stillness, as though awaiting the inevitable.

“Oh! I should not have left her—wicked daughter that I am!” she whispered, supporting her father’s trembling steps up the stairs.

Dr. Donaldson met them on the landing.

“She’s better now,” he murmured. “The opiate has taken effect. The spasms were severe—no wonder your maid was frightened—but she’ll rally this time.”

“This time?” Mr. Hale gasped. “Let me go to her!”

Half an hour ago, he had been a man in his prime. Now, he was frail, dim-eyed, staggering as if aged decades in minutes. Dr. Donald:don guided him into the bedroom, where Margaret followed closely.

There lay her mother—sleeping, yes, but with a face that bore no sign of peace. Death had marked her. Though she breathed, it was clear he would return soon.

Mr. Hale stared at her silently for a long time. Then, without warning, he began to tremble violently. He turned away from the doctor’s concerned gaze and fumbled blindly for the door, unable to see it despite the flickering candles. He stumbled into the drawing room, groping for a chair. Dr. Donaldson brought one and helped him sit.

“Speak to him, Miss Hale,” he urged. “We must rouse him.”

“Papa!” Margaret cried, her voice broken with grief. “Papa! Speak to me!”

A fleeting look of recognition passed over his face. He made a desperate effort.

“Margaret—did you know this was coming? Oh, it was cruel of you!”

“No, sir,” Dr. Donaldson answered firmly. “Miss Hale acted under my instructions. There may have been a mistake, but it wasn’t cruel. Your wife will be different tomorrow, I trust. She had spasms, as I expected, though I didn’t tell her. She’s taken the opiate I brought. She’ll sleep deeply. By morning, the look that frightens you will be gone.”

“But not the disease?”

Dr. Donaldson glanced at Margaret. Her head bowed, her face lifted—not pleading, but accepting. He saw the truth in her silence.

“No,” he said quietly. “Not the disease. We cannot cure it. All we can do is slow its course, ease the pain. Be strong, sir. Be a Christian. Have faith in the immortality of the soul—nothing earthly can touch that.”

The only reply was choked words: “You’ve never been married, Doctor. You don’t know what it is.” And then deep, heavy sobs—like thunder rolling through the stillness of the night.

Margaret knelt beside him, brushing his hair, whispering comfort, her tears falling onto his hand. No one counted the hours. Time lost meaning.

It was Mr. Hale who broke the silence first.

“What must we do?” he asked, his voice weak. “Tell us both. Margaret is my staff—my right hand.”

Dr. Donaldson gave clear, calm advice. No immediate danger—perhaps peace for days. But no hope of lasting recovery. He advised Mr. Hale to rest, to leave one watcher. He promised to return at dawn.

With a firm handshake, he left.

They exchanged few words. Exhaustion had stripped them of speech. Mr. Hale insisted on staying awake through the night. Margaret could only persuade him to lie on the drawing-room sofa.

Dixon refused to sleep. She sat, staring, blinking, slumping, jerking herself upright again—until finally, defeated, she fell into a snoring stupor.

Margaret had removed her dress and thrown it aside in irritation. She wore only her dressing gown now. She felt alive in a way she never had before—every nerve alert, every sense heightened. She watched. She listened. Every sound, every breath, every shadow stirred her.

For more than two hours, she heard her father move restlessly in the next room. He came to the door of her mother’s chamber again and again, pausing to listen. Each time, Margaret would open the door, unaware of his presence, and explain how things stood—answering questions formed only by his lips.

At last, he too fell asleep.

And the house grew still.

Margaret sat behind the curtain, thinking.

The world outside seemed distant—far away in time and space. Less than thirty-six hours ago, she had cared deeply for Bessy Higgins and her father, grieved for Boucher. Now, those concerns felt like memories from another life. Everything outside the house seemed disconnected from her mother—and therefore unreal.

Even Harley Street felt clearer now. She remembered vividly how she had traced her mother’s features in Aunt Shaw’s face, how letters had stirred her longing for home. Helstone itself receded into the past. The gray, monotonous winter and spring now seemed more real, more precious, than any joy she had known lately.

She wished she could reach out and pull back that vanished time—beg it to return, to give her back what she had failed to cherish while it lasted.

Life, she thought, was a hollow show—fleeting, insubstantial. From some high tower above the world, a bell tolled endlessly: *All are shadows—All are passing—All is past.*

When morning came, cool and gray like so many others, she looked upon the sleeping faces—her father, Dixon, her mother—and wondered if the terrible night had been a dream.

It, too, was a shadow.

It, too, was past.

Mrs. Hale woke with no memory of her illness. She was surprised by Dr. Donaldson’s early visit, puzzled by the anxious looks of her husband and daughter. She agreed to stay in bed, saying she was tired. But the next day, she insisted on getting up. Dr. Donaldson allowed it.

She was restless, uncomfortable in every position. By nightfall, she grew feverish.

Mr. Hale was listless, unable to decide anything.

“What can we do to spare her such a night again?” Margaret asked on the third day.

“It’s partly the reaction from the opiates I’ve had to use,” Dr. Donaldson explained. “More painful for you to watch than for her to endure. But a water-bed might help. Mrs. Thornton has one. I’ll call there this afternoon.”

He paused, noticing Margaret’s pale face, worn by vigilance.

“I’m not sure I can go,” he said. “I have a long round. But you could walk to Marlborough Street—ask Mrs. Thornton if she’d lend it.”

“Of course,” Margaret said. “I’ll go while mamma sleeps. I’m sure Mrs. Thornton will help.”

And indeed, Dr. Donaldson was right. Mrs. Hale seemed to recover quickly. That afternoon, she looked brighter than Margaret had dared hope.

After dinner, Margaret left her seated in the easy chair, her hand resting in her husband’s. He looked more worn than she—yet he smiled now, slowly, faintly. A few days earlier, Margaret had believed she’d never see that smile again.

The walk to Marlborough Street was two miles. The August sun beat down fiercely at three o’clock. Margaret walked slowly, absorbed in her thoughts. She had grown accustomed to navigating the crowded streets of Milton. For the first mile and a half, nothing seemed unusual.

Then, she noticed a strange swelling among the crowd ahead—people not moving forward, but talking, listening, buzzing with agitation. They parted for her, but she was too wrapped in her purpose to notice fully.

Only when she reached Marlborough Street did the full weight of it sink in: a restless tension hung in the air—moral and physical. From every narrow lane came a low, distant roar, like thousands of angry voices.

Every poor dwelling emptied into the street. People stood in doorways, windows, even the middle of lanes—all fixed on one point.

Marlborough Street was the center of their gaze—eyes blazing with anger, threats, fear, or pleading.

As Margaret approached the small side entrance of Marlborough Mill Yard, she waited for the porter’s answer to her ring.

She looked around—and heard the first distant roll of the storm.

She saw the first wave of the crowd surge forward, crash, and retreat at the far end of the street.

It had been silent moments before—now, ominously still.

All these signs struck her, but did not penetrate her heart.

She did not understand their meaning.

She was too consumed by another truth—the sharp, unbearable knowledge that soon, she would be motherless.

She was trying to grasp it—to prepare herself, so that when the moment came, she might comfort her father.

The porter opened the gate cautiously, barely wide enough for her.

“It’s you, ma’am?” he said, breathing heavily, widening the gap slightly. “Th’ folk are all coming up here, I reckon.”

“I don’t know,” Margaret replied. “Something’s happening—something unusual. But this street is empty.”

She crossed the yard and climbed the steps to the front door.

No steam engine roared. No machinery clicked. No voices clashed.

Only far off—the ominous, gathering thunder.

“But work grew scarce, while bread grew dear,
And wages lessened, too;
For Irish hordes were bidders here,
Our half-paid work to do.”

Margaret was ushered into the drawing-room. It had returned to its usual state—curtains drawn back, furniture restored, but the air heavy with tension. The windows stood half-open against the stifling heat, and the Venetian blinds cast a dull, grey light across the room, reflecting the dusty pavement below. The shadows fell oddly, distorted by the angle of the sun, and mingled with the greenish glow from above to give even Margaret’s own face, as she caught her reflection in the mirror, a ghostly pallor. She sat quietly, waiting. No one came. From time to time, the wind seemed to carry the distant roar of voices closer—yet there was no wind. Then silence would fall, deep and absolute, as if the world itself held its breath.

At last, Fanny entered.

“Mamma will come directly, Miss Hale,” she said, breathless. “She asked me to apologize. You may know my brother has brought in workers from Ireland—Irish labourers—and it’s stirred up the Milton folk terribly. As if he didn’t have the right to hire where he could! These people refused to work for him, and now they’ve frightened the poor Irish so badly with their threats that we dare not let them out. You can see them crowded in the top room of the mill—they’re to sleep there, for safety. And mamma is looking after their food. John is speaking to them—some of the women are begging to go back. Ah! Here’s mamma!”

Mrs. Thornton entered, her face set in a mask of sternness that made Margaret feel instantly that this was not the best moment to make her request. Still, she had promised to ask for anything needed in her mother’s illness. Mrs. Thornton’s brow furrowed, her lips tightened, as Margaret spoke gently of her mother’s restlessness and Dr. Donaldson’s suggestion that a water-bed might bring relief. She paused. Mrs. Thornton did not answer at once. Then she sprang up, her voice sharp with alarm:

“They’re at the gates! Call John, Fanny—call him from the mill! They’re at the gates! They’ll break them down! Call John, I say!”

And at that very moment, the sound she had been listening for—the tramp of countless feet—rose just beyond the wall. A rising din of angry voices surged behind the wooden barrier. The gate trembled under the weight of unseen hands, as if the crowd outside were using their bodies as battering rams. They stepped back only to charge again, united and relentless, until the great iron gates quivered like reeds in a gale.

The women gathered at the windows, transfixed by the scene unfolding before them. Mrs. Thornton, the servants, Margaret—all stood watching. Fanny had fled upstairs, screaming as if hunted, and collapsed in hysterics on the sofa. Mrs. Thornton waited for her son, still inside the mill. He emerged, glanced upward at the pale faces clustered at the window, and offered a brief smile of defiance before locking the factory door. Then he called to one of the maids to open his own door, which Fanny had bolted behind her in panic. Mrs. Thornton went herself. The sound of his firm, commanding voice seemed to ignite the fury outside. Until then, the mob had been silent, breathing hard in their effort to break through. But now, hearing him speak, they unleashed a roar so fierce and unnatural that even Mrs. Thornton turned pale.

He entered the room, slightly flushed, but his eyes gleamed with the fire of danger. There was a proud, defiant look upon his face that made him seem noble, though not conventionally handsome. Margaret, who had always feared she might fail in courage, found herself forgetting herself entirely. In this moment of real terror, she felt nothing but a fierce, painful empathy for those around her.

Mr. Thornton stepped forward without hesitation.

“I’m sorry, Miss Hale, that you’ve come at such a time—when, I fear, you may be caught up in whatever danger we face. Mother, perhaps you’d better retreat to the back rooms? I’m not certain whether they’ve reached the stable yard from Pinner’s Lane—but if not, you’ll be safer there than here. Go, Jane!” he added, turning to the housekeeper.

She obeyed, followed by the others.

“I stay here,” said his mother firmly. “Where you are, there I remain.” And indeed, retreat offered no safety—the crowd had surrounded the outbuildings at the rear, their voices echoing threats from every side. The servants fled to the garrets, shrieking. Mr. Thornton smiled scornfully as he heard them. He glanced at Margaret, standing alone near the factory window. Her eyes sparkled, her cheeks flushed. Sensing his gaze, she turned and asked a question that had long been on her mind:

“Where are the Irish workers? Are they in the factory?”

“Yes,” he replied. “I left them huddled in a small room at the top of a back staircase. I told them to flee down the stairs if they heard any attack on the mill doors. But it’s not them they want—it’s me.”

“When will the soldiers arrive?” his mother asked, her voice low but steady.

He pulled out his watch, calm as ever. After a brief calculation, he said:

“If Williams left immediately and didn’t have to dodge through them, it should be twenty minutes yet.”

“Twenty minutes!” she repeated, her voice finally betraying her fear.

“Shut the windows at once, mother,” he ordered. “The gates won’t hold another shock. Shut that one, Miss Hale.”

Margaret closed her window, then hurried to help Mrs. Thornton’s trembling hands.

For several minutes, the street outside fell silent. Mrs. Thornton watched her son’s face, seeking meaning in the sudden quiet. His expression was rigid—contemptuous, defiant. No trace of hope or fear showed.

Fanny lifted her head.

“Are they gone?” she whispered.

“Gone?” he replied. “Listen.”

They all listened. Then came the sound—a single, deep breath, the groan of wood straining, the wrench of iron, the thunderous crash of the massive gates falling. Fanny staggered upright, took a few steps toward her mother, then collapsed into her arms, unconscious.

Mrs. Thornton lifted her with strength born more of will than muscle, and carried her away.

“Thank God,” Mr. Thornton murmured as he watched her leave. “Had you not better go upstairs, Miss Hale?”

Margaret’s lips formed a “No,” but he couldn’t hear her. Beneath the house wall, the tramp of hundreds of feet resumed, accompanied by a deep, growling murmur—more terrifying than their earlier cries, filled with a savage satisfaction.

“Never mind,” he said, trying to reassure her. “I’m sorry you’ve been caught in this alarm, but it won’t last long. The soldiers will be here soon.”

“Oh, God!” Margaret suddenly exclaimed. “There’s Boucher. I recognize his face—he’s livid with rage, fighting to get to the front. Look! Look!”

“Who is Boucher?” Mr. Thornton asked coolly, stepping close to the window to identify the man.

As soon as they saw him, the crowd erupted in a yell—unhuman, animalistic, like a beast denied its prey. Even Mr. Thornton recoiled, stunned by the depth of hatred he had provoked.

“Let them scream,” he said. “In five minutes—. I only hope my poor Irishmen aren’t terrified out of their minds by such a fiendish noise. Hold on for five minutes, Miss Hale.”

“Don’t worry about me,” she said quickly. “But what will happen in five minutes? Can’t you do something to calm these people? It’s awful to see them.”

“The soldiers will arrive soon. That will restore reason.”

“To reason?” she asked sharply. “What kind of reason?”

“The only kind that works when men turn into beasts. By heaven—they’ve turned to the mill door!”

“Mr. Thornton,” she said, trembling with passion, “go down at once—if you’re not a coward. Face them like a man. Protect these poor strangers you’ve brought here. Speak to your workers like human beings. Speak kindly. Don’t let the soldiers come in and cut down people driven mad. I see one who is already losing control. If you have any courage, any nobility, go out and speak to them—man to man!”

He turned to look at her as she spoke. A shadow passed over his face. He clenched his teeth.

“I will go. Perhaps you’ll accompany me downstairs and bar the door behind me. My mother and sister will need protection.”

“Oh, Mr. Thornton—I don’t know—only—”

But he was already moving. Downstairs he went, unbarred the front door. All she could do was follow swiftly, fasten the door behind him, and climb back up the stairs, heart sick, head dizzy. She returned to the farthest window. He stood on the steps below—she could see that by the direction of a thousand furious eyes. But she could neither see nor hear much beyond the savage, rolling murmur of anger.

She threw open the window. Many in the crowd were boys—cruel, reckless, thoughtless. Others were men, gaunt as wolves, starving for blood. She understood. Like Boucher, they had children at home—starving, helpless—counting on higher wages, enraged to discover Irish laborers had been brought in to steal their livelihood. She saw it all in Boucher’s face: desperate, broken, livid with fury. If Mr. Thornton would only speak—just let them hear his voice—it might be better than this blind rage against stone silence.

Perhaps he was speaking now. A momentary hush fell over the crowd—like animals pausing to listen. She tore off her bonnet, leaned forward. But she could only see. If he had spoken, the moment had passed. The fury rose again, fiercer than before.

He stood with arms folded, motionless as a statue, face pale with suppressed emotion. They were trying to intimidate him—to force him to flinch. Each urged the other forward, ready for violence. Margaret knew instinctively that the next moment would be chaos. One touch, and the explosion would come—among hundreds of inflamed men and reckless boys, even Mr. Thornton’s life would not be safe.

Then she saw it—boys in the background bending to remove their heavy wooden clogs, the nearest thing to weapons they had. The spark struck the powder. With a cry unheard by others, she rushed from the room, down the stairs. She lifted the great iron bar with strength born of desperation, threw open the door—and stood before the raging sea of men, her eyes blazing with reproach.

The clogs froze in mid-air. Faces, moments ago grim and determined, now looked uncertain, confused—asking what this meant. She stood between them and their enemy. She could not speak, but stretched her arms out, waiting for breath.

“Oh, don’t use violence! He is one man, and you are many,” she whispered. But the words died in her throat—no tone, only a hoarse gasp.

Mr. Thornton stepped slightly aside, as if offended by anything between him and danger.

“Go!” she said again—this time, her voice a cry. “The soldiers are coming. Go peacefully. You’ll have relief for your grievances—whatever they are.”

“Will those Irish scum be sent back?” shouted one from the crowd, voice thick with threat.

“Never, by your command!” Mr. Thornton declared.

Instantly, the storm broke. Hoots and roars filled the air. But Margaret did not hear. Her eyes were fixed on the boys with their clogs. She saw their movement—knew their intent. Another moment, and Mr. Thornton might be struck down—the man she had urged to face this danger.

She threw her arms around him, using her body as a shield.

He shook her off.

“Go away,” he said, voice deep and firm. “This is no place for you.”

“It is,” she insisted. “You didn’t see what I saw.”

If she hoped her gender would protect her—if she believed that by turning away, they might pause and vanish—she was wrong. Their fury had gone too far. Only the reckless boys led the riot—those who loved cruelty for its own sake, regardless of bloodshed.

A clog flew through the air. Margaret watched it—missed its mark. She turned sick, but did not move. She buried her face in Mr. Thornton’s arm.

Then she raised her head.

“For God’s sake! Don’t destroy your cause with violence! You don’t know what you’re doing.” She forced her voice to be clear.

A sharp pebble struck her forehead, grazing her cheek. A blinding flash of light exploded before her eyes. She slumped, lifeless, onto Mr. Thornton’s shoulder.

He unfolded his arms, held her briefly in one hand.

“You do well,” he said, voice low and charged. “You come to defend the innocent stranger. You fall—hundreds—on one man. And when a woman stands before you, pleading for reason, your cowardice turns on her! You do well!”

They fell silent. They stared—wide-eyed, mouth agape—at the thin line of dark blood trickling down her temple. Those closest to the gate backed away, ashamed. A ripple moved through the crowd—a retreat.

One voice called out:

“The stone was meant for you—but you were sheltered by a woman!”

Mr. Thornton trembled with rage. The blood loss brought Margaret back—dimly, vaguely aware. He laid her gently on the doorstep, her head resting against the frame.

“Can you rest here?” he asked.

Without waiting, he descended the steps, stepped into the middle of the crowd.

“Now kill me,” he said, “if it is your brutal will. There is no woman to shield me now. Beat me to death—you will never move me from what I have decided—not you!”

He stood among them, arms folded, exactly as he had been on the steps.

But the crowd began to move backward—unreasonably, blindly, as if the tide had turned. Or perhaps it was the thought of the approaching soldiers, and the sight of that pale, still face, eyes closed, tears forming beneath her lashes, blood dripping slowly from her wound.

Even Boucher hesitated, scowled, and finally turned away, muttering curses.

As soon as the flight began—inevitable from its nature—Mr. Thornton darted up the steps to Margaret.

She tried to rise.

“It’s nothing,” she said, forcing a weak smile. “Just a graze. I was stunned. Oh, I’m so glad they’re gone!” And she wept openly.

He could not comfort her. His anger burned hotter now that the danger had passed. The clank of soldiers’ boots echoed in the distance—five minutes too late to impress the mob with authority.

While he thought, Margaret clung to the doorpost. Then a film covered her eyes. He caught her just in time.

“Mother—mother!” he called. “They’re gone. Miss Hale is hurt!”

He carried her into the dining room, laid her softly on the sofa. Looking at her pale face, the truth struck him with sudden force.

“Oh, my Margaret—my Margaret! No one can tell what you are to me! Dead—cold as you lie there—you are the only woman I’ve ever loved! Oh, Margaret—Margaret!”

He knelt beside her, whispering, almost moaning. Then he started, ashamed, as his mother entered. She saw only her son, paler, sterner than usual.

“Miss Hale is hurt, mother. A stone grazed her temple. She’s lost quite a bit of blood.”

“She looks terribly injured—almost dead,” Mrs. Thornton said, alarmed.

“It’s only a fainting fit. She spoke to me just now.” But as he said it, his blood seemed to rush inward, and he trembled.

“Go call Jane—she’ll find what I need. And you—go to the Irish workers. They’re shouting like mad with fright.”

He left, each step weighted as if dragging anchors. He called Jane, called his sister. She must have every care, every tenderness. Yet every pulse in his body throbbed with memory—how she had come down, placed herself in danger—could it be to save him?

At the time, he had pushed her aside, spoken harshly. He had seen only unnecessary risk.

Now, among the frightened Irish, he struggled to understand their words, to calm them. They refused to stay. They demanded to be sent back.

So he had to think, to argue, to reason.

Mrs. Thornton bathed Margaret’s temples with eau de Cologne. When the spirit touched the wound—hidden until then—Margaret opened her eyes. But she did not recognize where she was, or who they were. Her lips quivered, her eyes darkened, and she slipped back into unconsciousness.

“She’s suffered a terrible blow,” Mrs. Thornton said. “Is there anyone who can fetch a doctor?”

“Not me, ma’am,” Jane said, shrinking. “Them rabble may still be around. I don’t think the cut is as deep as it looks.”

“I won’t take the chance. She was hurt in our house. If you’re afraid, Jane, I’m not. I’ll go.”

“Please, ma'am, send one of the police. There are plenty here—soldiers too.”

“And yet you’re afraid to go! I won’t waste their time on our errands. They’ll have enough to do catching some of the mob. You’ll not be afraid to stay here,” she asked coldly, “and keep bathing Miss Hale’s forehead? I’ll be back in ten minutes.”

“Couldn’t Hannah go, ma'am?”

“Why Hannah? Why not you? No, Jane. If you won’t go, I will.”

Mrs. Thornton went first to the room where Fanny lay on the bed.

“Oh, mamma! You frightened me!” Fanny cried. “I thought it was a man who’d gotten into the house.”

“Nonsense! The men are gone. Soldiers are everywhere, searching now that it’s too late. Miss Hale lies on the dining-room sofa, badly hurt. I’m going for the doctor.”

“Oh, don’t, mamma! They’ll murder you!” Fanny clung to her gown.

Mrs. Thornton wrenched it away.

“Find someone else. But that girl must not bleed to death.”

“Bleed? Oh, how horrible! How did she get hurt?”

“I don’t know—I haven’t time. Go down to her, Fanny. Try to be useful. Jane is with her. I trust it looks worse than it is. Jane refuses to leave—cowardly woman! And I won’t allow further refusals from my servants. I’m going myself.”

“Oh, dear, dear!” Fanny sobbed, preparing to descend rather than be left alone with thoughts of wounds and blood in the house.

“Oh, Jane!” she whispered, creeping into the dining room. “What happened? How white she looks! How did she get hurt? Did they throw stones into the drawing-room?”

Margaret did look pale, though her senses were returning. But the daze of the fainting spell kept her weak, helpless. She felt movement around her, the cooling scent of cologne, a longing for the bathing to continue. But when they paused to talk, she could not open her eyes, could not speak—just as a person in death-like trance cannot move or cry out to stop the preparations for burial, even while fully aware of everything happening around them.

Jane paused in her task.

“She’d have been safe enough, miss, if she’d stayed in the drawing-room or come up with us. We were in the front garret, safe from harm.”

“Where was she then?” Fanny asked, inching closer.

“Just before the front door—with master!” Jane said, significantly.

“With John! With my brother! How did she get there?”

“Nay, miss, that’s not for me to say,” Jane replied, lifting her chin slightly. “Sarah did——”

“Sarah what?” Fanny pressed.

Jane resumed her work, as if reluctant to repeat what Sarah had said.

“Sarah what?” Fanny demanded sharply. “Don’t speak in halves or I can’t understand.”

“Well, miss, since you insist—Sarah, you see, was in the best position to see, being at the right-hand window. And she says, and said at the time, that she saw Miss Hale with her arms around master’s neck, hugging him before all the people.”

“Which when his mother saw, she in her mind
Was troubled sore, he wist well what to ween.”

Margaret had not been gone more than five minutes when Mr. Thornton entered the dining room, his face flushed and agitated. “I couldn’t come sooner—the superintendent held me up—Where is she?” He scanned the room, then turned sharply toward his mother, who was quietly restoring order to the furniture, her movements deliberate but unhurried.

“Where is Miss Hale?” he asked again, his voice tight with urgency.

“Gone home,” she replied, a little curtly.

“Gone home!” he echoed, as if the idea were absurd.

“Yes. She was much better. I don’t think it was anything serious—just a fainting fit, nothing more. Some people are so sensitive.”

He paced restlessly. “I’m sorry she left. She couldn’t have been strong enough for it.”

“She said she was. And Mr. Lowe confirmed it. I sent for him myself.”

“Thank you, mother.” He paused, half-extending his hand as if to offer a grateful squeeze. But she didn’t notice.

“What did you do about the Irish workers?” he asked.

“I sent them to the Dragon Inn for a good meal—poor souls. Then I managed to catch Father Grady just in time. I’ve invited him to speak to them, to keep them from all leaving at once.”

“How did Miss Hale get home? She certainly couldn’t walk.”

“She took a cab. Everything was arranged properly—even paid for. Let’s talk of something else. She’s caused enough trouble already.”

“I don’t know where I’d be without her,” he said, low and firm.

“You’re becoming so helpless that you need a girl to defend you?” Mrs. Thornton retorted, her tone edged with disdain.

He flushed. “Not many girls would have taken blows meant for me—blows thrown with real goodwill, too.”

“A girl in love will go far,” she said shortly.

“Mother!” He stepped forward, stopped himself, and fought to contain his rising emotion. She felt the tension in his stance—the way his breath came fast, his eyes gleamed, his body tensed. It unsettled her. She wasn’t sure what stirred in him—anger? pride? joy?—but its force was undeniable. Strong feelings, especially when unshared, made her uneasy.

She walked to the sideboard, opened a drawer, and pulled out a duster. There was a smudge of eau de Cologne on the sofa arm. She began to wipe it away, though she lingered longer than necessary, keeping her back turned.

“You’ve taken measures about the rioters, I suppose? No more violence expected? Where were the police? Always absent when needed!”

“On the contrary,” he said. “I saw three or four when the gates gave way—battling and pushing like madmen. More arrived just as the yard cleared. I could have arrested some then, if I’d been quicker. But there’ll be no problem—plenty of witnesses.”

“But won’t they return tonight?”

“I’m going to arrange a guard. I’ve set a meeting with Captain Hanbury in half an hour at the station.”

“You must have tea first.”

“Tea? Yes, I suppose so. It’s half-past six. I may be out for a while. Don of sitting up for me, mother.”

“You expect me to go to bed before I see you safe?” she countered.

“Well… perhaps not.” He hesitated. “But if I have time, I’ll stop by Crampton after seeing the police and speaking with Hamper and Clarkson.”

Their eyes met. They stared at each other, silent, intense. Then she asked, “Why are you going to Crampton?”

“To check on Miss Hale.”

“I’ll send Williams. He’ll deliver the water-bed she requested. He can ask how she is.”

“I must go myself.”

“Not just to inquire about her?”

“No. To thank her—for standing between me and the mob.”

“What made you go down there at all? You were putting your head into a lion’s mouth!”

He glanced at her sharply, realizing she didn’t know what had passed between him and Margaret in the drawing-room. Instead of explaining, he asked: “Will you be afraid to stay alone until I can secure the police? Or shall we send Williams now? They could be here by the time we finish tea. Time is short—I must leave in fifteen minutes.”

Mrs. Thornton left the room. Her servants noticed her orders were unusually hesitant, uncertain. Mr. Thornton remained, pretending to focus on business matters at the police station—but in truth, his mind was elsewhere. The world beyond seemed blurred, distant. Only one image burned clear: the feel of her arms around his neck, the softness of her grip, the flush of color in her cheeks as he recalled it. He could not forget.

Tea passed in silence, broken only by Fanny’s endless recounting of her own fright—how she’d trembled, fainted, felt sick, nearly swooned.

“There—that’s enough,” said her brother, rising abruptly. “The reality was enough for me.”

As he turned to leave, his mother caught his arm.

“You’ll come back here before going to the Hales’,” she said, her voice low, strained.

“I know what I know,” Fanny muttered to herself. “Why? Will it be too late to disturb them?”

“John,” she said, “come back to me this evening. It will be late for Mrs. Hale. But that’s not why. Tomorrow… you will—Come back tonight, John!”

It was rare for her to plead—too proud for such things. Yet she had never failed. “I’ll return straight here after my errands. You’ll make sure to ask after them—after her?”

Mrs. Thornton was not usually a companionable woman, nor a good listener when her son was away. But upon his return, she listened closely, her eyes sharp, eager for every detail—what steps he’d taken, whom he’d trusted, how he’d secured the premises.

He spoke plainly. Punishment was due. Those who had rioted must suffer. That was justice. That was protection. That was the will of the master—clean, decisive, like a blade.

“Mother,” he said suddenly, “you know what I have to say to Miss Hale tomorrow?”

The question startled her. She had forgotten Margaret.

“Yes,” she said slowly. “I do. You can hardly do otherwise.”

“Do otherwise? I don’t understand.”

“I mean,” she continued, “that after allowing her emotions to overcome her, you are bound in honour—”

“Bound in honour?” he scoffed. “Honour has nothing to do with it. ‘Her feelings overcame her?’ What feelings?”

“Nay, John, no need to be angry. Did she not rush down and cling to you to save you?”

“She did,” he said, quiet now. “But mother…” He stopped, stepping directly before her. “I dare not hope. I’ve never been weak before—but I cannot believe such a woman cares for me.”

“Don’t be foolish, John. Such a woman? She might be a duke’s daughter, the way you speak. And what proof would you want of her care? I believe she struggled against her own class pride—yet she saw clearly at last. It’s much for me to admit,” she said, smiling slowly, tears glistening in her eyes, “for after tonight, I stand second. I wanted you to myself—just a few hours longer—that’s why I begged you not to go till tomorrow.”

“Dearest mother!” he whispered.

(Still, love is selfish—and instantly, he reverted to his own fears, his hopes, his longing.)

“But I know she doesn’t care for me. I’ll put myself at her feet—I must. If there’s even one chance in a million, I’ll do it.”

“Don’t fear,” she said, crushing down her own wounded pride, the sting of jealousy that betrayed how deeply she loved him. “Don’t be afraid. As far as love goes, she may be worthy of you. It must have taken much to overcome her pride. Don’t fear, John,” she added, kissing him gently as she bid him goodnight.

And she left the room, slowly, with dignity.

But once inside her own chamber, she locked the door and sank into a chair, weeping—tears she had never known before.

* * *

Margaret entered the room where her parents sat in quiet conversation. She looked pale, drained. She approached them slowly, unable at first to speak.

“Mrs. Thornton will send the water-bed, mamma.”

“Dear, how tired you look! Is it very hot, Margaret?”

“Very hot. And the streets are rough with the strike.”

A flicker of colour returned to her cheeks—bright, vivid—but vanished just as quickly.

“There’s been a message from Bessy Higgins,” said Mrs. Hale. “She wants you to visit her. But you look too worn.”

“Yes,” Margaret said. “I am tired. I cannot go.”

She was silent, trembling, as she prepared tea. She was relieved her father was occupied with her mother, so he wouldn’t notice her condition. Even after her mother retired, he stayed close, reading aloud until she fell asleep.

Then she was alone.

Now I will think of it—now I will remember it all. I could not before—I dared not.

She sat rigid in her chair, hands clasped, lips pressed tight, eyes fixed as if seeing a vision.

She drew a deep breath.

“I, who hate scenes—I, who have mocked others for showing emotion—who have called them weak—went down and threw myself into the chaos like a romantic fool! Did I do any good? Perhaps they would have left anyway.”

But her judgment corrected itself instantly.

“No—perhaps not. I did some good. But what possessed me to defend that man as if he were a child? Ah!” she exclaimed, clenching her fists. “No wonder they thought I was in love with him—after disgracing myself so. Me—in love—with him?”

Her cheeks flamed red. She covered her face with her hands.

When she uncovered them, her palms were wet with hot tears.

“Oh, how low I’ve fallen—so they should say that of me! I could not have been so brave for anyone else—because he is so utterly indifferent to me—if not positively hateful. That made me all the more determined to ensure fairness. And I could see what fair play was. It was not fair,” she said fiercely, “that he should stand there—safe—waiting for soldiers to trap those poor, frantic men—without trying to reason with them. And it was worse than unfair for them to attack him as they threatened. I would do it again—let them say what they like. If I prevented one blow, one cruel act that might have happened, I did a woman’s work. Let them insult my pride—my maiden pride—as they will—I walk pure before God!”

She raised her eyes. A calm, noble peace settled over her face—still as carved marble.

Dixon entered. “If you please, Miss Margaret, here’s the water-bed from Mrs. Thornton’s. Too late for tonight, I’m afraid—missus is nearly asleep—but it’ll do nicely for tomorrow.”

“Very well,” Margaret said. “Please send our best thanks.”

Dixon paused. “He says he’s to ask particularly how you are. I think he means missus—but he insists his last words were, ‘Ask how Miss Hale is.’”

“Me?” Margaret said, straightening. “I am quite well. Tell him I am perfectly well.”

But her face was as white as her handkerchief. Her head throbbed with pain.

Mr. Hale came in. He had left his sleeping wife and wanted, as Margaret saw, to be cheered by her presence. With gentle patience, she endured her pain, saying nothing, weaving small talk—everything except the riot. She never mentioned it once.

It turned her sick just to think of it.

“Goodnight, Margaret. I have every hope of a peaceful night myself. You look very pale from your watch. I’ll call Dixon if your mother needs anything. Go to bed and sleep soundly—you need it, poor child.”

“Goodnight, papa.”

She let her colour fade, the false smile slip, her eyes grow dull with pain. She released her will, exhausted.

Till morning, she might feel ill, weary. She lay down and did not stir.

To move a finger would have been impossible—no strength, no will. She was so drained, so stunned, that she thought she never slept at all. Her thoughts raced between waking and dreaming, clinging to their own torment.

She could not be alone—prostrate, powerless. Faces crowded around her—not angry, not threatening—but watching. And with that gaze came shame—deep, burning shame—that she should be the center of attention, the object of such regard.

It was so acute, she longed to bury herself in the earth. Yet she could not escape the unblinking stare of many eyes.

“Your beauty was the first that won the place
And scal’d the walls of my undaunted heart,
Which, captive now, pines in a caitive case,
Unkindly met with rigour for desert:—
Yet not the less your servant shall abide,
In spite of rude repulse or silent pride.”

The morning light found Margaret rising slowly, weary but relieved that the night had passed without alarm. Though she felt no true rest, her mother had stirred only once, and all within the house remained quiet. A faint breeze stirred the still air, and though there were no trees near to show its play among the leaves, Margaret could imagine it—the soft rustle through thickets, the whispering dance of branches in distant woods. The very thought brought a quiet joy to her spirit, like a hidden song beneath the surface of silence.

She sat at her sewing in her mother’s room, her thoughts fixed on the day ahead. Once her mother woke fully from her afternoon slumber, she would help her dress. After dinner, she meant to visit Bessy Higgins, whose condition weighed heavily upon her. And then—she resolved firmly—she would banish every thought of the Thorntons. There was no need to dwell on them until they stood before her again in flesh and blood.

But the effort to forget only made their presence more vivid. From time to time, a sudden warmth flushed her pale cheeks, as if sunlight broke through storm clouds over the sea. She could not escape them.

Dixon entered softly, tiptoeing across the floor. “Mr. Thornton, Miss Margaret. He is in the drawing-room.”

Margaret dropped her needle. “Did he ask for me? Isn’t Papa home yet?”

“He asked for you, miss. Master is out.”

“Very well,” she said quietly, though she hesitated long before moving.

Mr. Thornton stood by the window, his back turned, gazing into the street. But he was not truly watching the world outside. His heart beat hard and fast, each pulse echoing with dread and longing. He could not forget the feel of her arms around his neck—how, in the moment, it had seemed impulsive, almost reckless. Now, the memory burned through him: the way she had clung to him, defending him, shielding him, as if her own life depended on it. It melted every resolve, every wall of self-control, like wax before fire.

He feared what might come next. He dreaded stepping forward, arms open, pleading silently for her to come to him—as she had done the day before, unknowingly, unheeded. Yet never again would he let such a moment pass unclaimed.

His breath came quick and shallow. Strong as he was, he trembled. What words would he speak? How would she receive them? Would she turn toward him, her eyes bright with recognition, her body drawn to his like something long lost and found? Or would she recoil, rejecting him with fierce disdain—shattering his future with one word?

A sudden awareness startled him. Someone had entered the room.

He turned.

She had come so quietly, so gently, that he hadn’t heard her footsteps. The noise of the street had been louder in his distracted mind than her soft tread in her muslin dress. She stood beside the table, hands clasped, not offering to sit. Her eyelids were lowered, half-shielding her eyes. Her lips were parted just enough to reveal the white line between them. Her breathing was slow and deep, lifting her chin and flaring her nostrils slightly. That was the only movement on her face.

Her skin, usually warm and glowing, was pale today—so pale that the dark shadow of her hair, drawn down to cover the mark of the blow, seemed to deepen the contrast. Yet even in her stillness, her head was held high, tilted back ever so slightly, in the old proud posture. Her arms hung motionless at her sides.

She looked like a woman falsely accused—a prisoner of honor, too proud to defend herself against a charge she loathed.

Mr. Thornton stepped forward quickly, then stopped himself. He moved instead to the door—left open—and shut it with quiet firmness. Then he returned, standing opposite her, letting the full impression of her presence settle upon him before speaking.

“Miss Hale,” he began, voice low and steady, “I was very ungrateful yesterday—”

“You had nothing to be grateful for,” she replied, lifting her eyes to meet his directly.

“You mean,” he said, “that you believe I ought to thank you for what you did.”

She could not help it. A flush spread across her face, warming her cheeks and burning into her eyes. Yet she did not look away. Her gaze remained calm, resolute.

“It was only instinct,” she said, her voice clear despite the emotion beneath. “Any woman would have done the same. We all feel the sanctity of our sex as a sacred duty when danger threatens. In truth, I should rather apologize—for saying things that sent you into harm’s way.”

“It was not your words,” he insisted. “It was the truth they carried—sharp as they were. But you cannot push me aside with that excuse. You cannot deny me this gratitude. My—” He paused, forcing himself to control the rush of feeling. He would not speak hastily, not now. He would choose each word carefully. And he did. He stopped mid-sentence.

“I do not try to escape anything,” she said. “I simply say—you owe me no thanks. And I may add, any expression of it will pain me, because I do not feel I deserve it. Still—if it eases your conscience, speak on.”

“I do not want relief from obligation,” he said sharply, stung by her composure. “Whether real or imagined—I care not. I believe I owe my very life to you. Yes—smile if you must, call it exaggeration—but I believe it. Because knowing that… knowing that whenever I rejoice in life, I can say to myself—‘All this joy, all this pride in doing my work, all this sense of being alive—I owe it to her!’—it makes the joy deeper, the pride fiercer, the feeling of existence sharper. I hardly know whether it is pleasure or pain to think it. But it is true. And I will not be silenced.”

He stepped forward, determined. “I love you—more than any man has ever loved a woman.”

He took her hand in his. She did not pull away.

He waited, breathless, listening for her response.

Then, in a voice cold and trembling, she spoke: “Your way of speaking shocks me. It is blasphemous. I cannot help it—it is my first reaction. Perhaps it would not be so, if I understood what you mean. I don’t wish to hurt you. And we must be gentle—mamma is asleep. But your manner… it offends me.”

“How?” he demanded.

“Offends me!” she repeated, regaining her dignity. “Yes. I do feel offended—and rightly. You seem to think my actions yesterday”—another deep blush, but now her eyes sparked with anger rather than shame—“were a personal act between us. That you may come here to thank me, as if it were some private favor. But a gentleman—yes, a gentleman—would understand that any woman worthy of the name would step forward to protect a man threatened by violence. It is not personal. It is honor.”

“And the gentleman thus saved is denied even the right to thank her?” he snapped. “I am a man. I claim the right to express my feelings.”

“And I yielded to that right,” she replied, proudly. “I merely said your insistence caused me pain. But you seem to think—no, you *assume*—that I acted not just from instinct, but from… from something else. Something directed at you—*you*.” Her voice broke. Tears, long suppressed, rose to her eyes and choked her. “But I would have done the same for any man—any poor desperate man in that crowd. For none of them would I have shown less sympathy, less courage.”

“You may speak on,” he said, his tone hardening. “I know of your misplaced sympathies. But now I believe it was only your natural sense of justice—that same sense that can see oppression, even in one who holds power. Yes—I, though a master, can be oppressed. And you saw it. You acted nobly. I know you despise me. Let me say it—because you do not understand me.”

“I do not care to understand,” she answered, gripping the edge of the table to steady herself. She thought him cruel—and he was. And she was weak with anger.

“You are unfair,” he said.

She pressed her lips together. She refused to answer.

And yet—he could have thrown himself at her feet, kissed the hem of her dress. He would have done it, if she had allowed it.

She did not speak. She did not move.

Tears of wounded pride fell hot and fast.

He waited. Longed for her to say something—anything—to give him a reason to reply.

But she remained silent.

He picked up his hat.

“One last word,” he said. “You look as if being loved by me taints you. But you cannot avoid it. Nor can I cleanse you of it. I would not, even if I could. I have never loved any woman before. My life has been too full, my mind too absorbed. Now I love. And I will love. But do not fear too much expression from me.”

“I am not afraid,” she said, lifting her head straight. “No one has ever dared to be impertinent to me—and no one ever shall. But Mr. Thornton,” she added, softening her tone, “you’ve been kind to my father. Please—don’t let us make each other angry. Not again.”

He ignored her words. Instead, he smoothed the nap of his hat with his sleeve for a moment. Then, without taking her offered hand, and ignoring her solemn look of regret, he turned abruptly and left.

As the door closed behind him, Margaret caught one glimpse of his face.

And in that glance, she thought she saw tears—washed clean, but present.

That sight changed everything.

Her pride, once so sharp, softened into something tender—and painful. She felt shame—not for herself, but for having caused such humiliation to another.

“But how could I help it?” she whispered to herself. “I never liked him. I was civil. But I never hid my indifference. I didn’t think about him—or myself. So my manners showed the truth. All that happened yesterday—he might misread it. But that is his fault, not mine. I would do it again, if needed. Even if it brings me this shame and sorrow.”

“Revenge may have her own;
Roused discipline aloud proclaims their cause,
And injured navies urge their broken laws.”

Margaret found herself wondering if all proposals were as sudden, as jarring in their arrival, as the two she had received. A quiet comparison rose unbidden in her mind between Mr. Lennox and Mr. Thornton. With Henry Lennox, she had felt a pang of regret—not for his feelings, but for the moment when they had slipped past friendship into something deeper. He had seemed to step over that line briefly, then recoil from it almost as sharply as she did, though for different reasons. But with Mr. Thornton—there had been no such soft transition. Their relationship had been nothing but conflict: sharp disagreements, clashing opinions, and a constant sense that he dismissed her thoughts not as ideas, but as mere noise against his iron will. She had grown weary of arguing, of trying to make him see her point of view when he turned away from it with contempt. And now—here he was, speaking of love, not with tenderness, but with a fierce, wild passion that left her breathless.

At first, she thought his offer stemmed only from pity—a compassion born of seeing her exposed, vulnerable, perhaps misunderstood by others. But even before he left the room, and certainly within minutes, a clear truth emerged: he loved her. Not just now, but always. That realization struck her like a force beyond reason, an overwhelming current pulling her toward a future she could not imagine. She recoiled, shuddered—fearing this powerful emotion that threatened to upend everything she had built.

She fled into silence, seeking refuge from the echo of his voice, the weight of his gaze. Yet escape was impossible. His presence lingered in her mind, haunting her thoughts like a ghost refusing to depart. She resented him for having so thoroughly penetrated her inner world—for daring to declare that he would love her still, even if she rejected him utterly. How could he assume such power? How dared he threaten her with enduring affection?

She wished she had spoken more firmly—more decisively. Now, sharp words flooded her mind, too late to speak. The memory of the encounter clung to her like a nightmare: vivid, unshakable, refusing to fade even as she tried to smile through it. It remained, crouched in the corner of her soul, watching, waiting—knowing she could not confess it to another without trembling.

What did he mean? Did he truly believe she couldn’t frighten him away? She would test him. Such boldness was unworthy of a man. Was he relying on yesterday’s events? If need be, she would do the same tomorrow—through poverty, illness, or sacrifice—but never again would she let him dictate her choices. She acted because it was right, simple, true. To save where she could. *Fais ce que dois, advienne que pourra.*

For a long time, she stood motionless, caught in the aftermath of his final words and the intensity of his eyes—eyes that had held hers until she looked down, humbled. Then, driven by a sudden impulse, she opened the window to let in fresh air, chasing the oppressive stillness. She opened the door, longing to break free from the silence, to lose herself in action or company. But the house was hushed—still as a tomb under the midday sun, where an invalid slept fitfully, deprived of rest even in daylight.

She could not stay alone. What should she do?

Then it came to her: Bessy Higgins. The message sent the night before flashed back—she must go.

When Margaret arrived, Bessy lay flat on the settle, drawn close to the fire despite the heat. Her face was pale, her breathing shallow. Margaret knew she needed better posture, so she gently raised her head and adjusted the pillows, easing her discomfort without a word.

“I thought I’d never see you again,” Bessy whispered, gazing at her with wide, pleading eyes.

“I’m afraid you’re worse,” Margaret said softly. “I couldn’t come yesterday—my mother was ill.”

Bessy smiled faintly. “You might think I went beyond my place, sending Mary for you. But the shouting, the voices—they tore me apart. When father left, I just wanted to hear your voice, reading something peaceful… something that would carry me into God’s stillness, like a baby lulled to sleep.”

“Shall I read you a chapter?” asked Margaret.

“Yes. At first, I won’t listen—words will seem far off. But when you come to the comforting ones… those will reach me, like a hand touching my heart.”

Margaret began reading. Bessy shifted uneasily, torn between focus and pain. Then suddenly, she broke in.

“Don’t read anymore. It’s useless. I’m blaspheming in my mind, thinking angry things I can’t change. You heard about the riot at Marlborough Mills yesterday? Thornton’s mill?”

“No,” said Margaret, coloring slightly. “Was your father there?”

“No. He’d give his hand to keep it from happening. That’s what’s breaking him. He’s crushed—broken-hearted.”

“But why?” Margaret asked, confused.

“Because he’s a committee-man for the strike. The Union chose him—he’s known as steady, deep-thinking, loyal to the bone. They made plans together: stand firm, follow the majority, obey the law. No violence. If people saw them endure hunger and hardship with quiet dignity, they’d gain sympathy. But if they took up sticks, fought—then all was lost. They tried to talk to the agitators, warn them. But the Committee swore every member would lie down and die rather than strike a blow. They believed that would win public support. And they were right in their demand—just wages. They didn’t want justice tangled with crime, like medicine mixed in jelly. The jelly’s bigger, but the taste stays.”

She paused, exhausted. “I’ve told you much. Now you understand what it means for father to see all his work undone—by someone like Boucher. He disobeyed orders, sparked the riot, ruined everything. As bad as Judas. Oh, father gave him hell last night! He said he’d tell the police where to find the ringleader—give him up to the mill-owners, let them do what they liked. He wanted the world to know the real leaders weren’t men like Boucher, but decent men—good workers, good citizens—who only wanted fair pay, who wouldn’t work even if they starved, but who would never harm property or life.”

Her voice dropped. “They say Boucher threw a stone at Thornton’s sister—killed her.”

“That’s not true,” Margaret said quickly. “It wasn’t Boucher.”

She flushed red, then white.

“You were there?” Bessy asked weakly, her breath labored.

“Yes. Never mind. Go on. It wasn’t Boucher.”

“What did he say to your father?”

“He didn’t speak. He was shaking with rage, so badly I couldn’t look. I heard his breath—fast, ragged. Once, I thought he was crying. When father said he’d turn Boucher over to the police, he screamed—and hit father across the face with a closed fist. Then he ran out like lightning.”

Father staggered from the blow, though Boucher was weak with fury. He sat down, covered his eyes, then rose and moved toward the door.

I don’t know where I found strength—but I threw myself onto the settle and grabbed him. ‘Father! Father!’ I cried. ‘You’ll never betray that poor man. I’ll hold you till you promise you won’t.’”

He pushed me away—though I was faint, nearly unconscious. His face was gray, streaked with blood. Seeing it made me sick.

I don’t remember if I slept or fainted. Then Mary came in. I told her to bring you.
