Now, don’t talk. Just read. I’ve poured it out—I feel lighter. But I need something beautiful, something far away, to wash the bitterness from my mouth. Not a sermon—tell me a story. One with pictures. Read about the New Heaven and the New Earth. Maybe I’ll forget this.”

Margaret read in a low, soothing voice. Though Bessy’s eyes were closed, tears gathered on her lashes. Eventually, she fell asleep—fitfully, muttering prayers. Margaret covered her gently and left, uneasy but knowing she was needed at home.

Mrs. Hale sat in the drawing-room when Margaret returned. It was one of her better days, and she praised the water-bed, comparing it fondly to the beds at Sir John Beresford’s estate. She insisted it was superior to anything she’d known since youth, though Mr. Hale suggested youth itself might have lent more comfort to old beds. His wife scoffed.

“No, indeed, Mr. Hale. It was those beds at Sir John’s. Margaret, you’re young—you move about during the day. Are your beds comfortable? Do they give you perfect rest? Or do you toss, unable to find ease, waking tired as when you went to bed?”

Margaret laughed. “To be honest, mamma, I never think about my bed. I’m so sleepy at night that any surface does. So I’m not a reliable judge. But I never tried Sir John’s beds—I never went to Oxenham.”

“Oh, yes—poor Fred,” Mrs. Hale sighed. “I took him once, when he was a baby. I only went once after marriage—to Aunt Shaw’s wedding. And little Fred was the baby then. Dixon didn’t like changing from lady’s maid to nurse, and I feared taking her near her old home might make her leave. But Fred fell ill at Oxenham—teething—and with Anna’s wedding looming and my own weakness, Dixon took full care of him. And she grew so attached—so proud when he turned from everyone to cling to her—that I think she never considered leaving again.”

She paused, her voice thickening. “Poor Fred. Everyone loved him. He was born with the gift of winning hearts. It makes me despise Captain Reid—knowing he disliked my own son. A proof, surely, of a cruel heart.”

Mr. Hale quietly left the room.

“Ah, your poor father, Margaret,” she whispered. “He can’t bear to hear Fred mentioned.”

“I love to hear about him, mamma,” Margaret said softly. “Tell me everything. Tell me what he was like as a baby.”

“Well, Margaret, you mustn’t be hurt—but he was much prettier than you were. I remember seeing you in Dixon’s arms—‘Dear, what an ugly little thing!’ I said. And she replied, ‘It’s not every child who’s like Master Fred, bless him!’”

She smiled sadly. “I could have held Fred every moment. His cot was beside my bed. And now—now I don’t know where my boy is. Sometimes I fear I shall never see him again.”

Margaret knelt beside her mother’s sofa, holding her hand, kissing it gently, trying to soothe her.

Mrs. Hale wept openly. Then she sat upright, staring at her daughter with desperate earnestness.

“Margaret,” she said, voice trembling, “if I recover—if God gives me a chance—it must be through seeing Frederick once more. It will awaken the last spark of health in me.”

She paused, gathering strength.

“And if I am to die—if I am among those meant to pass within weeks—I must see him. I cannot pray until I have this. I charge you, Margaret, as you hope for comfort in your final hours, bring him to me. Only five minutes. There’s no danger in five minutes. Oh, Margaret—let me see him before I die!”

Margaret did not question the logic. In moments like these, reason fades. The plea of a dying mother strikes deeper than any argument. This wish was natural, noble—right for both mother and son. She felt compelled to act.

She stood before her mother, calm and steady.

“Mamma,” she said, “I will write tonight. I am certain Frederick will come. Be at ease. You shall see him, as much as any earthly promise can guarantee.”

“You’ll write tonight? Oh, Margaret—the post leaves at five. Will you send it then? I have so few hours—sometimes I feel I won’t recover, though your father tries to convince me otherwise. Write immediately—don’t miss a single post. By that very post, I may miss him.”

“But papa is out,” Margaret said.

“Papa is out! And what then? Would he deny me this last wish? Why, I wouldn’t be here, dying, if he hadn’t taken me from Helstone to this unhealthy, smoky place.”

“Oh, mamma!” Margaret breathed.

“Yes. He knows it. He’s said so many times. He’d do anything for me. You don’t mean he’d refuse this—this prayer? And Margaret, the longing to see Frederick stands between me and God. I cannot pray until I have this one thing. Don’t delay—write now!”

Margaret hesitated. But her mother trembled, feverish with urgency.

“You’re not writing!” she finally gasped. “Bring me pens and paper—I’ll try myself.”

Margaret gently stopped her. “Wait for papa. Let us ask him how best to proceed.”

“You promised—only a quarter of an hour ago. You said he would come.”

“And so he shall, mamma. Don’t cry. I’ll write now—you’ll see me write. It will go by this post. If papa wishes, he can add a note later—only a day’s delay. Please, don’t cry so. It breaks my heart.”

Mrs. Hale sobbed uncontrollably, conjuring visions of her son kneeling beside her grave, weeping over her cold body while she remained unaware. The grief overwhelmed her, leaving her spent and silent.

At last, she calmed. Her eyes fixed on Margaret as she wrote swiftly, urgently, sealing the letter quickly, afraid her mother might insist on reading it. Then, at her mother’s request, she carried it herself to the post office.

On her way home, her father caught up with her.

“And where have you been, my pretty maid?” he asked.

“To the post office—with a letter. A letter to Frederick. Oh, papa, perhaps I’ve done wrong. Mamma was seized with such longing—she said seeing him would make her well. Then she said she must see him before she dies. I couldn’t resist. Did I do wrong?”

Mr. Hale was silent for a moment.

“You should have waited,” he said at last.

“I tried to persuade her—” Margaret began, then stopped.

“I don’t know,” he said slowly. “She ought to see him, if she wants it so deeply. I believe it would do her more good than all the doctor’s medicines—perhaps heal her entirely. But the danger to him—ah, Margaret, it is great.”

“All these years since the mutiny?” she asked.

“Yes. The government takes no mercy. Especially in the navy. A commander must be seen as backed by absolute authority. Any insult must be avenged—no matter how long ago. They spare no expense—ships scour the seas, hunting down offenders. Time doesn’t erase the offense. It remains fresh on the Admiralty books until it is wiped out by blood.”

“Oh, papa—what have I done?” Margaret whispered. “And yet it seemed so right. I’m sure Frederick would run the risk.”

“So he would,” Mr. Hale said. “So he should. Nay, Margaret, I’m glad it’s done. I wouldn’t have dared. I’m thankful it’s settled. I might have hesitated too long—too late to help.”

He paused. “Dear Margaret, you’ve done what is right. The outcome is beyond our control.”

It was comforting—but beneath his words, Margaret sensed his true anxiety. The relentless pursuit of mutineers, the possibility that her brother might be hunted down and killed—these thoughts chilled her to the bone. Had she lured him home to seal his fate? She saw her father’s worry deepen, hidden behind his reassurances.

She took his arm and walked home in silence, weighed down by sorrow and dread.

“I have found that holy place of rest
Still changeless.”

When Mr. Thornton left the house that morning, he was consumed by a storm of emotion so fierce it nearly overpowered him. He felt as though he had been struck blind—not by the sun or by dust, but by something far more devastating: the sight of Margaret Hale, so graceful and composed, yet standing before him like a stranger who had shattered his world. His head throbbed with pain; his pulse beat erratically, as if his body refused to keep pace with the chaos inside. The noise of the city, the glare of the daylight, the constant rumble of carriages and foot traffic—all of it grated on his nerves. He cursed himself for being so weak, for allowing such a woman to wound him so deeply. Yet even as he spoke the words “I hate her,” a sharp, undeniable surge of love pierced through his anger like lightning, leaving him breathless.

His only solace was in clinging to his torment—the stubborn certainty that no matter what she said or did, he would not change. She could scorn him, reject him, treat him with cold indifference; none of it would alter his heart. He loved her. He always would. And he would defy her, and the pain, and the shame.

He paused for a moment, gathering himself, trying to fix this resolve in his mind. An omnibus passed by, heading out into the countryside. The conductor, thinking he wanted a ride, stopped near the curb. There was no point in explaining—so Thornton climbed aboard without a word. As the vehicle rolled forward, he watched the city fade behind him—row after row of houses, then elegant villas with neat gardens, until at last they reached open fields and hedgerows. Soon they arrived at a quiet country town, where all the passengers disembarked. Thornton stepped down too, not because he had a destination, but because everyone else did.

He walked into the fields, moving briskly, as if motion might clear the fog from his mind. Now, in the stillness, the memory came back in full force—the absurdity of his behavior, the way he had thrown caution aside, done exactly what he had sworn never to do, and reaped the consequences he had long predicted. Was he bewitched by those eyes? By the softness of her lips, pressed against his shoulder just yesterday? He couldn’t shake the memory of her arms around him—even if it were only once.

He saw her now in fragments: bold one moment, timid the next; tender, then proud as a queen. He recalled every encounter, each glance, each word, searching for meaning, for a clue to her true nature. He saw her in different clothes, in different moods—each version beautiful, each one unattainable. Even that morning, how fiercely she had looked at him, her eyes blazing with defiance when she thought he believed her affection was born of shared danger. But it wasn’t. She didn’t care for him. Not really.

If he was a fool in the morning, he was no wiser by afternoon. All the journey had done was deepen his conviction: there was no one like Margaret Hale. She would never love him. But neither would anyone—no matter how powerful or persuasive—ever make him stop loving her.

He returned to the market square and boarded another omnibus, heading back to Milton. It was late when he stepped off near his warehouse. The familiar surroundings brought back routine and duty. He had much to do—more than usual, thanks to the unrest of the previous day. He needed to meet with the magistrates, finalize arrangements for the new Irish workers, ensure they were isolated from the discontented locals. And finally, he had to face his mother.

Mrs. Thornton had spent the entire day waiting in the dining room, her hands folded tightly in her lap, her eyes fixed on the door. Every sound—a footstep, a knock, a carriage passing—sent her heart racing. She would snatch up her embroidery, begin stitching again, though her hands trembled and her vision blurred. Then the door would open—and it would be only the servant, or a tradesman, or some minor visitor. Her face would slacken, the rigid mask of composure slipping away, replaced by a look of quiet despair.

She tried to push aside thoughts of the future—the changes that would come with her son’s marriage. Instead, she focused on practicalities: the linen, the household goods. She sorted through baskets filled with tablecloths and napkins, some marked with her initials, G.H.T., others with John’s. Some of the older ones were Dutch damask, fine as silk, things she had cherished since her wedding. She stared at them long, remembering the pride she’d once taken in them. Then, with sudden determination, she unpicked the old initials. She searched for the Turkey-red thread to sew in new ones—but it was gone. She didn’t have the strength to order more. So she stood, staring blankly ahead, seeing visions instead: visions of her son, always at the center, always hers.

Still, he did not come. She assumed he was with Miss Hale. Already, she felt displaced—her place in his heart shrinking. A sharp pang, half physical, half emotional, gripped her chest. She sank into her chair, unable to breathe. But within seconds, she rose again, straighter than ever, a grim smile playing on her lips. She was ready—ready to greet the triumphant news, to pretend joy, while hiding the sorrow she felt deep within.

There was little room in her thoughts for Margaret as a person. She was merely John’s wife-to-be. To become mistress of the house, to inherit all the comforts and honors that came with it—that was the prize. Wealth, status, obedience, friends—all would follow naturally, like jewels adorning a king’s robe. To be chosen by John was enough to elevate any woman above the rest. And Miss Hale—well, she was not entirely unsuitable. If she had been a local girl, Mrs. Thornton might even have liked her. She had spirit, character, a certain fire. True, she was ignorant, prejudiced—typical of southern upbringing. But that could be managed.

A strange comparison formed in her mind—between Fanny and Margaret. For the first time, she spoke harshly to her daughter, scolding her bitterly. Then, as if to atone, she picked up Henry’s Commentaries and forced herself to read, abandoning the linen inspection she usually took pride in.

Then—his step.

She heard him before she registered the sentence she was reading. Her senses sharpened, interpreting every movement: the clatter of his boots on the hall floor, the pause at the hat stand, the slow approach toward the door. Why did he hesitate?

Her head remained bent over the book. She did not look up.

He stood beside her, close enough to feel her presence. He waited for her to finish the paragraph, as if it mattered more than anything else in the world.

With effort, she raised her eyes.

“Well, John?”

He knew what that simple question meant. He had prepared himself. He wanted to jest, to deflect, but the bitterness in his heart could have produced mockery—yet he owed his mother better.

He moved behind her, so she could not see his face, and gently tilted her chin upward. He kissed her gray, stern forehead, whispering, “No one loves me—no one cares for me but you, mother.”

Then he turned away, leaning against the mantelpiece, tears welling in his eyes.

She rose—then staggered. For the first time in her life, the strong woman faltered.

She placed her hands on his shoulders—she was tall—and forced him to look at her.

“Mother’s love is given by God, John,” she said, voice steady. “It holds fast forever. A girl’s love is like smoke—it vanishes with the wind. And she would not have you, my own lad, would she?”

She clenched her teeth, showing them like a dog baring its fangs.

He shook his head slowly.

“I am not fit for her, mother. I knew I was not.”

She ground out words between her teeth. He could not hear them clearly, but the fire in her eyes spoke volumes—curse, perhaps, though not in words. Still, her heart lifted. He was hers again.

“Mother!” he said quickly, “I cannot bear to hear a word against her. Spare me—spare me! I am weak, my heart is broken. I love her—I love her more than ever.”

“And I hate her,” she replied, low and fierce. “I tried not to hate her when she stood between us. I told myself she would make you happy—and I would give my blood to see that. But now… I hate her for your suffering. Yes, John, don’t hide it from me. I am your mother. Your pain is mine. And if you won no hatred for her, I will.”

“Then you make me love her more,” he said softly. “She is unjustly treated by you. I must balance the scales. But why speak of love or hate? She does not care for me. That is enough. Let us never mention her name again. That is the only kindness you can give me.”

“With all my heart,” she answered. “I wish she—and all of her kind—were sent back to where they came from.”

He stood silent, gazing into the fire. She watched him, her dry eyes suddenly filling with unexpected tears. But when he spoke again, she was as calm as ever.

“Warrants are out for three men—conspiracy charges,” he said. “Yesterday’s riot helped end the strike.”

And so, Margaret’s name was never spoken again between them.

They fell back into their usual conversation—facts, business, matters of the day. Their voices were quiet, their tones detached. A stranger might have walked in and thought them strangers—so distant, so cold in their manner. Yet beneath the surface, something had shifted—something irrevocable.

“For never any thing can be amiss
When simpleness and duty tender it.”
—*A Midsummer Night’s Dream*

Mr. Thornton moved through the day with a sharpness and purpose that seemed to erase the shadows of the previous one. The market showed a modest demand for finished goods, and he seized the opportunity, pressing hard in negotiations, his mind clear and decisive. He arrived punctually at the magistrates’ meeting, where his keen judgment and foresight quickly guided the deliberations. Though older men—those long established in the town, whose wealth was rooted in land rather than trade—held positions of influence, they looked to him for direction. He was entrusted with coordinating the police and overseeing essential measures. Yet he took no pride in their quiet respect, as though it were nothing more than the faint breeze that barely stirred the smoke rising from the mill chimneys.

He did not notice the admiration others offered; nor would he have welcomed it, had he known. Such recognition might have been a burden on his path forward. As it was, he focused only on action, on accomplishing what needed doing. It was his mother who gathered the whispers—how Mr. This or Mr. That spoke highly of her son, how things would have gone awry without him. But he dismissed such talk as irrelevant, sweeping through his duties with unwavering efficiency.

By midday, the weight of yesterday’s humiliation had lifted, replaced by a renewed sense of strength. His thoughts ran free, but they turned inevitably to her—not the moment of rejection, but the memory of the day before: her closeness, the warmth of her presence, the way her heart beat against his. He walked through the crowded streets, lost in thought, unseeing of the people around him, longing for that fleeting moment to return.

“Why, Mr. Thornton!” came a voice behind him. “You’re cutting me very coolly, I must say. And how is Mrs. Thornton? Brave weather this! We doctors don’t like it, I can tell you.”

“I beg your pardon, Dr. Donaldson,” he replied, startled. “I didn’t see you. My mother is well, thank you. A fine day, and good for the harvest, I hope. If the wheat comes in well, we’ll have brisk trade next year—whatever troubles may befall you physicians.”

“Ay, ay,” said the doctor with a smile. “Each man for himself. Your bad weather, your bad times—they’re my good ones. When trade falters, health suffers among the Milton folk more than you know.”

“Not mine,” Mr. Thornton answered firmly. “I’m made of iron. News of the worst debt I’ve ever faced never made my pulse falter. This strike—though it affects me more than anyone—doesn’t touch my appetite. You’ll need to look elsewhere for a patient, Doctor.”

“By the way,” the doctor added, “you’ve recommended me a good one—poor lady! I won’t go on in this heartless manner. But I fear Mrs. Hale—the woman in Crampton, you know—hasn’t many weeks left. I never held out much hope, as I told you. But I saw her today, and I think worse now than ever.”

Mr. Thornton paused. For a brief second, his famed composure wavered.

“Can I do anything?” he asked, his voice softer than usual. “You know—I’d want to help. Is there anything she needs? Comforts? Dainties?”

“No,” the doctor replied, shaking his head. “She craves fruit—she has a constant fever. Jargonelle pears will do, and there are plenty in the market.”

“I’ll let you know if there’s anything else,” Mr. Thornton said, already turning away. “I rely on you.”

“Oh, never fear,” the doctor chuckled. “I won’t spare your purse—I know it’s deep enough. I wish you’d give me a carte-blanche for all my patients.”

But Mr. Thornton was not a man given to broad charity. Few believed him capable of deep feeling. Yet he went straight to the first fruit shop in Milton, selecting the finest bunch of purple grapes, the richest peaches, the freshest vine leaves. They were packed into a basket, and the shopkeeper waited for instructions.

“Where shall we send them, sir?”

“No,” Mr. Thornton said. “Give the basket to me. I’ll take it.”

He carried it with both hands, weaving through the busy streets, drawing curious glances from young ladies who found it odd to see him so occupied—like a porter, or an errand-boy. But he did not care. In his mind, he repeated: *I will not be stopped by her. I like to bring this to the poor mother. It is right. She shall not turn me away. What a joke it would be if I refused kindness because of a proud girl. I do this for Mr. Hale. I do this in defiance.*

He moved swiftly, arriving at Crampton in short order. He climbed the stairs two at a time, entering the drawing-room before Dixon could announce him—his face flushed, his eyes bright with genuine concern.

Mrs. Hale lay on the sofa, feverish and pale. Mr. Hale read aloud. Margaret sat beside her, sewing quietly on a low stool. Her heart quickened at his sudden appearance—but he ignored her, even Mr. Hale. He stepped directly to Mrs. Hale, holding out the basket.

“I met Dr. Donaldason, ma'am,” he said, his voice low and gentle—a tone rare from a man so strong and direct. “He said fruit would help you. So I took the liberty—great liberty—of bringing some that seemed fine.”

Mrs. Hale trembled with delight. Mr. Hale, quieter but deeply grateful, nodded. “Fetch a plate, Margaret—anything.”

Margaret rose slowly, standing near the table, afraid to move, afraid to draw attention. She hoped he hadn’t noticed her—though she knew, somehow, that he felt her presence, even if he didn’t look at her.

“I must go,” he said. “I cannot stay. If you’ll forgive this liberty—my rough ways—too abrupt, I fear—but I’ll be more gentle next time. May I bring you more fruit if I find something tempting? Good afternoon, Mr. Hale. Good-bye, ma'am.”

And he was gone—without a word, without a glance toward her.

Margaret stood silent, then reached for a plate. With careful fingers, she lifted the fruit, handling each piece with tenderness. It was kind of him to come—after everything.

“Oh!” murmured Mrs. Hale, weakly. “It is so delicious! How thoughtful of him! Margaret love, taste these grapes! Wasn’t it kind?”

“Yes,” Margaret replied quietly.

“Margaret!” Mrs. Hale said, slightly cross. “You won’t like anything Mr. Thornton does. I never saw anyone so prejudiced.”

Mr. Hale, peeling a peach, cut off a small piece for himself. “If I had any prejudices,” he said, “this fruit would melt them. I’ve not tasted such since I was a boy. Not even in Hampshire. And boys, I suppose, find all fruit delicious. Do you remember the matted currant bushes at the corner of the west wall at home, Margaret?”

She did. Every stain on the old stone, every patch of lichen like a map, the tiny crane’s-bill growing in the cracks—it all flooded back. The past two days had strained her spirit, and now, hearing her father’s words, she rose abruptly, dropped her sewing, and fled into her own room.

Before she could fully grieve, she heard footsteps. Dixon stood at her drawer, rummaging.

“Bless me, miss! How you startled me! Is Missus worse? Is anything wrong?”

“No,” Margaret whispered, tears in her eyes. “Only I’m foolish and need water. What are you looking for? My muslins are in that drawer.”

Dixon kept searching. The scent of lavender drifted into the air.

At last, she pulled something out—what, Margaret couldn’t see. Then she turned.

“I don’t like telling you this,” Dixon said, “because you’ve got enough to bear. I meant to wait till night, maybe. But I couldn’t keep it.”

“What is it? Please, Dixon—tell me.”

“That young woman you visit—Higgins, I mean—died this morning. Her sister’s here, asking for something strange. The girl wanted to be buried in something of yours. That’s why I was looking for a nightcap—not too fine to give.”

“Oh!” Margaret gasped, her tears fresh. “Poor Bessy! I never thought I’d lose her.”

“But there’s more,” Dixon said. “The girl downstairs wants to know—would you like to see her?”

“She’s dead!” Margaret said, turning pale. “I’ve never seen a dead person. No—I’d rather not.”

“I wouldn’t have asked if you hadn’t come in,” Dixon replied. “I told her you wouldn’t.”

“I’ll go down,” Margaret said quickly, afraid that Dixon’s blunt manner might hurt the girl.

She took the cap and went to the kitchen. Mary Higgins sat there, face swollen with grief. She broke into sobs when she saw Margaret.

“Oh, ma'am,” she cried. “She loved you! She loved you! She did indeed!”

For a long while, nothing more could be said. Then, under Margaret’s sympathy and Dixon’s sharp prompting, a few facts emerged.

Nicholas Higgins had left early that morning, leaving Bessy seemingly well. But within an hour, she worsened. A neighbor rushed to call Mary, who had just arrived moments before she passed.

“It were a day or two ago,” Mary said, choking, “she axed to be buried in somewhat o’ yourn. She never tired o’ talking o’ yo’. Said yo’ were the prettiest thing she ever clapped eyes on. She loved yo’ dearly. Her last words—‘Give her my affectionate respects—and keep father fro’ drink.’ Yo’ll come and see her, ma'am. She would ha’ thought it a great compliment.”

Margaret hesitated. “Yes… perhaps I may. Yes, I will. I’ll come before tea. But where’s your father, Mary?”

Mary shook her head and began to rise.

“Miss Hale,” Dixon said quietly, “what’s the use of you going? I’d never say a word against it—if it helped the girl. And I wouldn’t mind going myself. These folks think it’s a sign of respect. Here—let me go instead. Miss Hale is busy. She can’t come, or else she would.”

Mary looked up, pleading. Dixon’s coming might be a gesture, but it wasn’t the same. The sister had once felt jealousy over Bessy’s closeness to the young lady.

“No, Dixon,” Margaret said firmly. “I will go. Mary, you shall see me this afternoon.”

And to silence her own fear, she left the room—resolute, determined not to retreat.

“Through cross to crown!—And though thy spirit’s life
Trials untold assail with giant strength,
Good cheer! good cheer! Soon ends the bitter strife,
And thou shalt reign in peace with Christ at length.”

“Ay sooth, we feel too strong in weal, to need Thee on that road;
But woe being come, the soul is dumb, that crieth not on ‘God.’”

That afternoon, Margaret walked swiftly toward the Higgins household. Mary stood near the door, her face pale and uncertain. Margaret smiled gently, seeking to calm her friend’s anxiety. They passed through the quiet house, up the narrow stairs, and into the stillness of the room where Bessie lay. There, Margaret found a strange peace. The face, once worn by pain and restless thoughts, now bore a soft, quiet smile—the mark of rest eternal. Tears gathered in Margaret’s eyes, but within her heart, a deep serenity settled. This was death—not as she had feared, but as something gentle, more peaceful than life itself. Verses from Scripture rose in her mind: “They rest from their labours.” “The weary are at rest.” “He giveth his beloved sleep.”

Slowly, she turned away from the bed. Mary knelt in silence behind, her sobs barely heard. Together, they descended the stairs without a word.

Nicholas Higgins stood in the centre of the room, one hand resting on the kitchen table. His eyes were wide, stunned by the news he had just received—his daughter dead. He had been told while walking through the court, his mind overwhelmed by voices and chaos. Now, he stared blankly, dry-eyed, trying to grasp the truth. She had been ill for so long that he had convinced himself she would recover. That she would “pull through.” But now, she was gone.

Margaret felt out of place—too close to this moment of grief, too familiar with death, when he, her father, had only just begun to feel it. For an instant, she hesitated on the stair, then tried to move past him, quietly, respectfully, leaving him to his sorrow.

Mary sank onto the nearest chair, pulled her apron over her head, and wept.

The sound seemed to awaken Nicholas. Suddenly, he seized Margaret’s arm, gripping it tightly as if afraid she might vanish. His voice came thick and strained, choked by emotion.

“Were you with her? Did you see her die?”

“No,” Margaret replied, standing still, patient and composed, now that she was noticed.

A long silence followed. He held her arm, lost in thought.

“All men must die,” he said at last, his voice heavy with a strange solemnity. It struck Margaret that he might have drunk—just enough to blur his thoughts, not enough to make him drunk. “But she was younger than me.”

He kept staring ahead, not looking at her, yet holding her fast. Then, abruptly, he looked up, his gaze wild, searching.

“Are you sure she’s dead? Not in a faint? Not dreaming? She’s been like that before—often.”

“She is dead,” Margaret answered firmly. She did not fear him, even as his grip tightened and confusion flickered across his face.

“She is dead,” she repeated.

His eyes lingered on hers, then slowly dimmed. At last, he released her. With a sudden, violent cry, he threw himself half across the table, shaking the furniture, sobbing uncontrollably. Mary trembled, stepping forward.

“Get away! Get away!” he shouted, striking wildly at her. “What do I care for you?”

Margaret took Mary’s hand, holding it softly. He tore at his hair, beat his head against the wooden wall, until he collapsed, spent and hollow. Still, neither girl moved. Mary shook with silent terror.

After what felt like hours, Nicholas lifted himself. His eyes were swollen, bloodshot. He ignored them at first, then scowled when he saw them watching. He gave them one harsh glance, said nothing, and moved toward the door.

“Oh, father, father!” Mary cried, throwing herself at his arm. “Not tonight! Any night but tonight. Help me—he’s going out to drink again! Father, I won’t leave you. You may strike me, but I won’t go. She told me—she said, ‘Keep your father from drink!’”

Margaret stood in the doorway, silent but firm. He turned to her, defiant.

“It’s my own house. Stand out of the way, girl, or I’ll throw you aside!”

He had already pushed Mary away violently. He looked ready to strike Margaret—but she did not flinch. Her eyes remained steady, calm, unshaken. He glared back, furious, but something in her presence held him.

“What are you staring at me like that for?” he asked finally, shaken by her quiet strength. “You think you can stop me from going where I please—because she loved you? In my own house, where I never asked you in—you’re wrong. It’s hard on a man to be denied the only comfort left.”

Margaret sensed he acknowledged her power. What could she do now?

He sat down near the door, half-defeated, half-resisting. He meant to leave as soon as she stepped aside—but he no longer dared use violence. Margaret placed her hand gently on his arm.

“Come with me,” she said. “Come and see her.”

Her voice was low, grave—but without fear or doubt. He rose slowly, reluctantly. He paused, stubborn, testing her patience. She waited—calm, unwavering. He enjoyed making her wait. But eventually, he moved toward the stairs.

They stood together beside the body.

“Her last words to Mary were, ‘Keep my father from drink.’”

“It can’t hurt her now,” he muttered. “Nothing can hurt her now.” Then, raising his voice in a broken cry, “We may quarrel, fall out—we may make peace—we may fight till we’re skin and bone—and none of it will touch her. She’s had her share. Hard work first, sickness at last—she lived like a dog. And to die without ever knowing joy! No, girl, whatever she said, she knows nothing now. And I must have a drink—to steady myself.”

“No,” Margaret said, her tone softening. “You shall not. If her life was as you say, at least she didn’t fear death as others do. You should have heard her speak of the life to come—the life hidden with God. That is where she has gone.”

He shook his head, glanced sideways at her. His face was pale, gaunt—pained.

“You’ve been through much. Where have you been all day? Not at work?”

“Not at work,” he said, giving a short, grim laugh. “Not at what you call work. I was at the Committee, sickened by fools who wouldn’t listen. I was called to Boucher’s wife early this morning—she’s bedridden, raving about her husband, as if I were supposed to control him—as if he were fit to be ruled by me. That fool—ruined our plans. I’ve walked miles to find men who wouldn’t see me. The law is against us now. And I was heartbroken—worse than tired. And if I’d met a friend offering a drink—I didn’t know she was dying here. Bess, lass—you’d believe me, wouldn’t you?” He turned to the corpse, his voice pleading.

“I’m sure,” Margaret said, “you didn’t know. It was sudden. But now you do. You see her. You hear her last words. You won’t go?”

No answer. Where could he turn for comfort?

“Come home with me,” she said at last, trembling slightly, yet bold. “At least you’ll have food. I’m certain you need it.”

“Your father—he’s a parson?” he asked suddenly.

“He was,” she replied shortly.

“I’ll go take tea with him, since you ask. I’ve always wanted to talk to a parson. Doesn’t matter if he’s preaching or not.”

Margaret hesitated. Her father, unprepared—her mother ill—it seemed impossible. But if she refused now, he would surely go to the gin-shop. If she could bring him to their house, that would be a victory. The rest, she decided, would depend on chance.

“Goodbye, dear girl. We’ve parted at last. But you’ve been a blessing to your father since you were born. Bless your white lips—there’s a smile on them now. And I’m glad to see it, even if I’m alone forever.”

He bent down and kissed Mary tenderly, covered her face, then followed Margaret. She hurried downstairs to tell Mary of the plan—to keep him from the gin-shop. She urged Mary to come too, feeling guilty at leaving her alone. But Mary said neighbours would stay with her. “It’s all right. But father—”

He was already there. He had composed himself, ashamed of his earlier breakdown, even adopting a bitter mockery, like thorns crackling under a pot.

“I’m going to take tea with her father,” he declared.

Yet he pulled his cap low over his brow, avoiding eye contact as they walked. He feared the pitying looks of neighbours.

As they neared her street, he looked down at his clothes, hands, shoes.

“I should’ve cleaned up first.”

Margaret assured him he could wash at the pump, and he wouldn’t be allowed to slip away.

While he followed the servant through the passage, careful not to mark the oilcloth with dirt, Margaret ran upstairs. She met Dixon on the landing.

“How is mamma? Where is papa?”

Mrs. Hale was tired, resting in her room. She had wanted to sleep, but Dixon had persuaded her to lie on the sofa instead, with tea brought to her. Better than lying restless in bed.

So far, so good. But where was Mr. Hale? In the drawing-room. Margaret rushed in, breathless.

“Papa—Mr. Higgins is coming. He’s been drinking—very little, but enough. I brought him home to keep him from the gin-shop. He’s waiting in the study.”

Mr. Hale was startled. A drunken weaver—coming to his quiet home, while his wife was ill? He looked dismayed. Yet Margaret pleaded earnestly.

“Oh, papa—he’s not really drunk. Just upset. And he’s not like that usually. He’s a decent man—once you get past the shock.”

Mr. Hale took her face in his hands, kissed her forehead.

“It’s all right, dear. I’ll go see him. You attend to your mother. But if you can come in later, I’d be glad.”

“Oh, yes—thank you.” As he turned to leave, she caught him.

“Papa—don’t judge him by what he says. He doesn’t believe in our ways. He’s… well, he’s not religious.”

“Oh dear,” Mr. Hale murmured to himself. “A drunken infidel weaver.” But aloud, he only said, “If your mother sleeps, come directly.”

Margaret went to her mother’s room. Mrs. Hale stirred.

“When did you write to Frederick, Margaret? Yesterday or the day before?”

“Yesterday, mamma.”

“And the letter went?”

“Yes. I carried it myself.”

“Oh, Margaret, I’m so afraid he’ll come! If he’s recognized! If he’s taken! If he’s executed after all these years of hiding! I keep dreaming he’s caught, tried…”

“Oh, mamma, don’t worry. There’s some risk—but we’ll minimize it. And here, no one knows us. Dixon will guard the door—won’t you, Dixon?”

“They’ll be clever if they get past me!” Dixon grinned fiercely.

“And he needn’t go out—only at dusk.”

“Poor fellow,” echoed Mrs. Hale. “But I almost wish you hadn’t written. Could we stop him now?”

“I’m afraid not, mamma,” Margaret said, remembering how urgently she had begged him to come.

“I hate things done in such haste,” Mrs. Hale sighed.

Margaret was silent.

“Now, ma'am,” Dixon said firmly, “seeing Master Frederick is exactly what you’ve been longing for. And I’m glad Miss Margaret wrote straightaway. I’ve thought of doing it myself. We’ll keep him safe. Only Martha might be a problem—she’d help him if needed. But I’ll send her to visit her mother—she’s been wanting to go. Her mother had a stroke. So trust me. Take your tea and rest.”

Mrs. Hale trusted Dixon more than Margaret. Her fears eased. Margaret poured tea in silence, struggling to think of something pleasant. But her mind clung to the danger—Frederick’s peril. Her mother chatted with Dixon, forgetting the threat. She was one who scattered fears like sparks—until they caught fire. Margaret was relieved when she could go downstairs.

In the study, she found her father and Higgins engaged in quiet conversation. Mr. Hale, courteous and kind, had drawn out the best in the rough man. He treated Nicholas with respect—calling him “Mr. Higgins,” placing a chair, standing until he sat. Nicholas, now clean, tidy, and composed, seemed transformed. He had washed his face, smoothed his hair, polished his clogs with a candle stub. He spoke earnestly, with a lowered voice, despite his thick accent.

Margaret watched, surprised and pleased. Her father listened intently. He smiled as she entered, offered her his chair, then sat again quickly, bowing slightly in apology.

Higgins nodded at her. She quietly arranged her knitting, preparing to listen.

“As I was saying, sir,” Nicholas began, “if you’d grown up here, you’d likely not believe much. I beg your pardon for my words—but I mean belief as thinking on promises made by people you never saw, about lives you’ve never known. You say they’re true. I say—where’s the proof? I see wise men, learned men around me—men who’ve had time to ponder. Their lives are open to me. They don’t believe in the Bible—not truly. They may say they do, but their first thought each morning isn’t, ‘How do I hold on to eternal life?’ It’s, ‘How do I fill my purse today? Where shall I go? What bargains can I make?’ The purse, the gold—those are real. Eternal life is talk. Fit for—well, forgive me, sir—you’re a parson out of work, I believe. I won’t speak disrespectfully. But let me ask—suppose salvation, life to come, were truly real—wouldn’t they preach it as they preach political economy? They’re eager to convert us to that. But this? That would be a greater conversion—if it were true.”

“But the masters aren’t concerned with your religion. They only deal with you in trade—so they think. And all they want to fix is trade.”

“I’m glad,” Nicholas said, with a curious wink, “you said ‘so they think.’ Otherwise, I’d call you a hypocrite. Though you’re a parson, or rather because you are. If you said religion wasn’t urgent—didn’t matter above all else—then I’d think you a knave. But I’d rather think you a fool than a knave. No offence, sir.”

“None at all. You think me mistaken. I think you more dangerously mistaken. I won’t convince you in a day. But let us know each other. Speak freely. Truth will prevail. I believe in God because I believe in truth. Mr. Higgins—I trust, whatever else you’ve given up—you believe”—his voice dropped reverently—“you believe in Him.”

Nicholas stiffened, stood rigid. Margaret jumped to her feet—thinking he might collapse. Mr. Hale looked alarmed.

“Man!” Nicholas burst out. “I could knock you down for tempting me. What right have you to question me now? Think of her—lying there, after her life. And then deny me the one comfort—that there is a God, and He gave her peace. I don’t believe she’ll live again,” he said, sinking down, speaking to the fire. “I don’t believe in any other life. Only this one—where she suffered so much. I can’t bear to think it was all chance—could have changed with a breath. Many times I’ve thought I didn’t believe in God—but I never said it plainly. I’ve laughed at those who did—but I’ve looked around afterward, wondering if He heard me. But today, when I’m left alone—I won’t listen to your doubts. There’s only one thing steady in this world—and I’ll cling to it, reason or no reason. It’s easy for happy folk—”

Margaret touched his arm gently. She had not spoken before.

“Nicholas—we don’t argue. You misunderstand my father. We don’t argue—we believe. And so do you. That’s the only comfort in times like this.”

He turned, grasped her hand.

“Ay! It is. It is.” He wiped his eyes. “But you know—she lies dead at home. I’m dazed with sorrow. I don’t know what I’m saying. It’s like the smart things I’ve heard—clever speeches—rising up now that my heart’s broken. The strike failed, you know—did you hear? I was coming home to ask her for comfort—like a beggar. And I was told she was dead—just dead. That was all. But it was enough.”

“Some wishes crossed my mind and dimly cheered it,
And one or two poor melancholy pleasures,
Each in the pale unwarming light of hope,
Silvering its flimsy wing, flew silent by—
Moths in the moonbeam!”

The morning after brought Margaret a letter from Edith. It was affectionate, rambling, and utterly without order—just as Edith herself might have written it. Yet the warmth of it stirred something deep within Margaret, whose own heart was tender to kindness and sincerity. She had known Edith’s way of speaking all her life; it was so familiar that she hardly noticed its quirks. The letter read:

“Oh, Margaret! It’s worth the journey from England just to see my little boy! He’s a perfect little fellow—especially when he wears his caps, and most especially the one you sent him. You good, dainty-fingered, persevering little lady! You’ve made all the mothers here green with envy, so now I want to show him off to someone new—to hear fresh admiration, perhaps. That may be all I’m after—but maybe not. Possibly there’s a little cousinly love mixed in too. Still, I *do* want you so much to come here, Margaret! I’m sure it would do Aunt Hale the world of good. Everyone around here is young and strong, our skies are always blue, the sun never stops shining, and the band plays lovely music from dawn till dusk. And, returning to the main tune of my song—my baby always smiles.

I’m constantly asking you to draw him for me, Margaret. Doesn’t matter what he’s doing—whatever he’s up to is the prettiest, most graceful thing imaginable. I think I love him better than my husband, who’s growing stout and grumpy—what he calls ‘busy.’ No, wait—that’s not fair. He’s just come in with news of a delightful picnic hosted by the officers of the Hazard, anchored in the bay below. Because he brought such cheerful news, I take back everything I said. Didn’t someone burn their hand for saying something they later regretted? Well, I can’t burn mine—it would hurt, and the scar would be ugly. So I retract all I said as fast as I can. Cosmo is just as wonderful as the baby, not at all stout, and no more grumpy than any decent husband should be. Only sometimes—he *is* very busy. I can say that without wifely duty—where was I? Oh yes—I had something important to tell you. Here it is: Dearest Margaret, you must come and visit me. It would help Aunt Hale immensely, as I said before. Get the doctor to prescribe it. Tell him it’s the smoke of Milton that’s harming her. I truly believe it is. Three months—no less—of this glorious climate—sunshine every day, grapes as plentiful as blackberries—would restore her completely.

I don’t ask my uncle”—(here the handwriting became neater, more careful. Mr. Hale sat quietly in the corner, like a child caught misbehaving, having given up his living.)—“because I suppose he disapproves of war, soldiers, and bands. At least, I know many Dissenters belong to the Peace Society, and I fear he wouldn’t enjoy it. But if he *would* come, dear, please say that Cosmo and I will do our best to make him happy. I’ll hide Cosmo’s red coat and sword, and make the band play solemn, serious tunes. Or, if they insist on pomp and vanity, I’ll have them play it in double slow time. Dear Margaret, if he’d care to join you and Aunt Hale, we’ll try to make it pleasant—even though I confess I’m a little wary of anyone who has acted out of conscience. You never did anything like that, I hope. Tell Aunt Hale not to bring warm clothes—though I suspect it will be late in the year before you arrive. But you have no idea how hot it gets here! I tried wearing my great beauty Indian shawl at a picnic. I held myself together with proverbs—‘Pride must abide,’ and other wholesome bits of wisdom. But it was no use. I felt like mamma’s little dog Tiny wrapped in an elephant’s costume—smothered, hidden, killed by my finery. So I turned it into a splendid carpet for us all to sit upon.

Now, here’s my boy, Margaret—if you don’t pack your things the moment you get this letter and come straight away to see him, I shall think you’re descended from King Herod!”

Margaret longed for just one day of Edith’s life—the ease, the laughter, the untroubled days under endless blue skies. If only a wish could carry her there—just for a single day. She craved the strength such a change would give her, even if only for a few hours to feel young again, to breathe freely among joy and light. Not yet twenty, and already she had borne burdens that made her feel ancient. That was her first thought after reading the letter.

Then she read it again, forgetting her own weariness, laughing aloud at the familiar tone, the vivid pictures, the sheer delight of Edith’s voice. She was still smiling when Mrs. Hale entered the drawing-room, leaning on Dixon’s arm. Margaret rushed forward to adjust the pillows. Her mother looked weaker than usual.

“What were you laughing at, Margaret?” she asked once she had settled onto the sofa.

“A letter from Edith. Shall I read it to you, mamma?”

She read it aloud, and for a while, her mother seemed drawn into it, guessing names for the baby, speculating on why each might have been chosen. Just as the conversation reached its peak, Mr. Thornton arrived, bearing another basket of fruit for Mrs. Hale.

He could not—would not—deny himself the chance to see Margaret. It was not for any deeper purpose, but simply for the pleasure of seeing her. It was a stubborn act of will, rare for a man usually so composed and self-possessed. He stepped into the room, glancing briefly at Margaret, then immediately turned away. After a stiff, formal bow, he never once looked at her again. He stayed only long enough to present the peaches, to speak a few kind words, and then, as he left, his cold, offended eyes met hers in a quiet farewell.

Margaret sat down, silent and pale.

“Do you know, Margaret,” Mrs. Hale said gently, “I really begin to like Mr. Thornton.”

Margaret said nothing at first. Then, forcing the words out, she replied, “Do you?”

“Yes. I think he’s becoming quite polished in his manners.”

Her voice was calmer now. “He is very kind and attentive—there’s no denying that.”

“I wonder Mrs. Thornton never comes. She must know I’m ill—she knows about the water-bed.”

“She probably hears from her son.”

“But still, I’d like to see her. You have so few friends here, Margaret.”

Margaret understood the unspoken plea beneath her mother’s words—a quiet longing for some woman to take care of her daughter, should she soon be left alone. But she could not respond.

“Do you think,” Mrs. Hale asked after a pause, “you could go and invite Mrs. Thornton to visit me? Just once. I don’t want to be a burden.”

“I’ll do anything you wish, mamma—but if Frederick comes—”

“Oh, yes, of course. We must keep our doors shut. We must let no one in. I scarcely know whether I dare hope for him to return. Sometimes I’d rather he didn’t. I have such terrible dreams about him.”

“Oh, mamma! We’ll take care. I’ll lock the door with my own hands before he could come to harm. Trust me—trust the care of him to me. I’ll watch over him like a lioness guarding her cubs.”

“When can we hear from him?”

“Not for a week, certainly—perhaps longer.”

“We must send Martha away in good time. It would never do to have her here when he arrives and then rush her off.”

“Dixon will remind us. I was thinking—we could get Mary Higgins to help while he’s here. She’s not very diligent, but she’s honest, and she’d try her best. She sleeps at home and never comes upstairs, so she wouldn’t know who’s in the house.”

“As you like. As Dixon likes. But, Margaret, don’t use those dreadful Milton words. ‘Slack of work’—it’s a provincialism. What will Aunt Shaw say when she returns?”

“Oh, mamma! Don’t make Aunt Shaw into a monster,” Margaret laughed. “Edith picked up all sorts of military slang from Captain Lennox, and Aunt Shaw never minded.”

“But yours is factory slang.”

“And if I live in a factory town, I must speak its language when I need to. Why, mamma, I could astonish you with words you’ve never heard. I don’t think you know what a *knobstick* is.”

“No, child. I only know it sounds vulgar—and I don’t want to hear you use it.”

“Very well, dearest mother, I won’t. But I’ll have to explain whole sentences instead.”

“I don’t like this Milton,” Mrs. Hale murmured. “Edith’s right—this smoke is what’s made me so ill.”

Margaret started at her mother’s words. Her father had just entered the room, and she was desperate to prevent any reinforcement of the impression she’d seen flicker across his face—the idea that Milton’s air had harmed her mother. She couldn’t tell if he’d heard her mother’s remark, but she began speaking quickly of other things, unaware that Mr. Thornton was following behind him.

“Mamma says I’ve picked up a lot of vulgarity since we came to Milton.”

The word “vulgarity” referred only to local speech, born from their recent conversation. But Mr. Thornton’s brow darkened. Margaret suddenly realized how her words might be taken—how they could seem coarse or disrespectful to him. In a natural impulse to avoid causing pain, she forced herself to continue, addressing him directly.

“Now, Mr. Thornton, though ‘knobstick’ doesn’t sound very pleasant, isn’t it expressive? Could I do without it when describing the thing it means? If using local words is vulgar, then I was very vulgar in the Forest—wasn’t I, mamma?”

It was unusual for Margaret to dominate a conversation, but she was so anxious to correct any misunderstanding that she didn’t notice until later how deeply she blushed. Mr. Thornton seemed barely to grasp the point of her words. He passed by her with a stiff, formal gesture, turning instead to speak to Mrs. Hale.

That sight reminded Margaret of her mother’s request—her quiet plea for Mrs. Thornton to visit. She sat in silence, burning with shame and frustration at her inability to remain calm and composed in his presence. Her thoughts kept returning to him—not with affection, but with a quiet sorrow that she had wounded him so deeply. And yet, she found herself striving gently, patiently, to rebuild the old balance between them—the position of mutual respect and uneasy friendship he had once held in her regard, and in that of her family.

There was a quiet humility in her manner toward him now, as if silently apologizing for the harsh words spoken in the aftermath of the riot. But he resented those words fiercely. They echoed in his mind, sharp and clear. He took pride in the justice that drove him to offer kindness to her parents, even when it pained him. He exulted in his own strength—the power he showed in forcing himself to face her whenever it might bring comfort to her father or mother.

He told himself he disliked seeing her, that her presence was a torment. But he was wrong. It was a sharp, secret pleasure to be near her, to feel her presence, even if he refused to look at her. He was not a man who examined his own motives closely—and so he remained mistaken, as I have said.

“The saddest birds a season find to sing.” SOUTHWELL.

“Never to fold the robe o’er secret pain, Never, weighed down by memory’s clouds again, To bow thy head! Thou art gone home!” MRS. HEMANS.

The next morning, Mrs. Thornton arrived at the Hale household. She found Mrs. Hale far worse than before—her condition having taken one of those sudden, unmistakable turns toward death that struck fear into even the closest of her family. In just twelve hours, her face had grown pale and sunken, as though life itself were slipping away with each breath. Mrs. Thornton, who had not seen her in weeks, was unprepared for such a sight. Though she had come only because her son had begged it as a personal favor—despite her deep-seated resentment toward the Hales—she was now confronted with something far greater than pride or prejudice.

She had always doubted the sincerity of Mrs. Hale’s illness, believing it no more than a passing whim, a temporary break from the rigid routine she expected of herself. She had told her son plainly that she regretted their ever coming near this place, that he should never have become acquainted with them, and that Latin and Greek—those dead tongues—had been invented for nothing but waste and folly. Her son had listened in silence, but when she finished her tirade, he simply repeated his request: she was to visit Mrs. Hale at the appointed hour, as it would be most convenient for the invalid.

Reluctantly, Mrs. Thornton agreed—though with every ounce of ill grace she could muster. Yet, even as she resented the act, she secretly admired her son for insisting on it. He seemed so determined to keep up the connection with the Hales, so steadfast in his kindness, that she began to view him with a new respect. And yet, she still saw Margaret Hale as an intruder, someone whose presence disturbed the order of things, someone whom she could not bring herself to like.

But none of these feelings remained when she stepped into Mrs. Hale’s room. There lay the woman—once vibrant, now still and fragile—like a mother, younger than herself, lying in a bed from which there was no return. The room was dimmed, quiet, broken only by faint whispers and long silences. Life had become monotonous, yet unbearable in its very stillness. Mrs. Thornton, strong and full of vitality, entered, and Mrs. Hale, though clearly aware of her presence, did not stir at first. Tears glistened on her lashes before she finally opened her eyes.

With a trembling hand, she reached out across the bedclothes, searching for contact—until her fingers brushed against Mrs. Thornton’s firm, large ones. Then, barely above a whisper, she spoke.

“Margaret—you have a daughter… my sister is in Italy. My child will be without a mother… in a strange land… if I die… will you…”

Her voice trailed off, her eyes fixed on Mrs. Thornton’s face with a desperate hopefulness. For a moment, Mrs. Thornton’s expression did not change—cold, rigid, unreadable. But then, something stirred within her. Not thoughts of her own children, nor even her son—but a sudden memory, sparked by the arrangement of the room: a little girl, long dead in infancy, buried beneath years of grief and silence. A flash of tenderness, unexpected and sharp, pierced through her icy reserve.

“You wish me to be a friend to Miss Hale,” said Mrs. Thornton, her voice measured, unyielding, though her heart trembled beneath the surface.

Mrs. Hale held her hand tighter, unable to speak. Mrs. Thornton sighed.

“I will be a true friend—if circumstances require it. Not a tender one. That I cannot be.” (She almost added “to her,” but stopped, seeing the anguish in the dying woman’s face.)

“It is not my nature to show affection, even where I feel it. Nor do I give advice lightly. Still, at your request—and if it brings you comfort—I promise.”

There was silence. Mrs. Thornton, too honest to make promises she couldn’t keep, knew that helping Margaret Hale—whom she disliked more than ever—would be difficult, perhaps impossible. Yet, duty called.

“I promise,” she said, gravely, “that in any difficulty Miss Hale may face, if she comes to me for help, I will assist her with all my power—as if she were my own daughter. I also promise that if I ever see her doing what I believe to be wrong—wrong not touching me or mine, so that my motives might be questioned—I will tell her plainly, honestly, as I would wish my own daughter to be told.”

A pause followed. Mrs. Hale felt the limits of the promise—its conditions, its coldness—but still, it was much. She was weak, dizzy, fading fast.

“I thank you,” she whispered. “I pray God to bless you. I shall never see you again in this world. But my last words are: I thank you for your promise to my child.”

“Not kindness,” Mrs. Thornton corrected, stiffly, refusing to soften even in farewell. But once the words were spoken, she was glad they hadn't been heard. She pressed the frail hand once more, rose, and left the house without speaking to a soul.

While Mrs. Thornton was with Mrs. Hale, Margaret and Dixon huddled together in quiet urgency, planning how to keep Frederick’s arrival a secret from everyone outside the house. A letter from him might arrive any day; he would follow swiftly after. They decided Martha must leave immediately for her holiday. Dixon would guard the front door strictly, allowing only essential visitors into Mr. Hale’s downstairs room—using Mrs. Hale’s illness as justification. If Mary Higgins was needed in the kitchen, she was to see and hear as little as possible. Frederick, if necessary, would be referred to as “Mr. Dickinson.” But Mary’s dullness and lack of curiosity made her the safest ally of all.

They resolved that Martha would leave that afternoon. Margaret wished she had gone the day before, fearing it might seem odd to give a servant time off while her mistress lay so ill.

Poor Margaret! All afternoon, she played the role of a dutiful daughter, drawing strength from her own dwindling reserves to support her father. Mr. Hale clung to hope between the fits of his wife’s suffering, believing each respite marked the beginning of recovery. But when the attacks returned—each more violent than the last—they shattered his courage anew. That afternoon, he sat in the drawing-room, unable to endure the solitude of his study. His head rested on folded arms upon the table, motionless.

Margaret watched him, her heart breaking. She wanted to comfort him, but dared not speak unless he asked. Martha had gone. Dixon stayed with Mrs. Hale during her sleep. The house was silent, darkening slowly, with no one to fetch candles.

Margaret sat by the window, staring at the street lamps, but seeing nothing—only hearing her father’s heavy sighs. She hesitated to go downstairs for lights, afraid that leaving him alone might let his grief spill over unchecked. But just as she thought of checking the kitchen fire—no one else could do it—she heard the doorbell ring, sharply muffled, jingling through the house despite its quiet tone.

She jumped up, passed her father—still unmoving—then turned back, kissed him gently. He did not react.

She went down quietly through the darkness. Dixon would have chained the door, but Margaret didn’t think of danger. A tall man stood between her and the lighted street. He turned at the latch.

“Is this Mr. Hale’s?” he asked, his voice clear, full, and oddly delicate.

Margaret trembled. At first, she said nothing. Then, in a breath:

“Frederick!” she gasped, reaching out both hands to pull him inside.

“Oh, Margaret!” he said, holding her shoulders after their embrace, as if even in the dark he could read her face. “My mother—is she alive?”

“Yes, she is alive, dear brother—alive, though as ill as she can be. Alive!”

“Thank God,” he breathed.

“Papa is crushed by this sorrow.”

“You expect me?”

“No—we’ve had no letter.”

“Then I came before it. But my mother knows I’m coming.”

“Oh—we all knew you’d come. But wait—come in. Give me your hand. What’s this? Oh—your carpet bag. Dixon has shut the shutters. This is papa’s study. I’ll take you to a chair. Just a moment—let me tell him.”

She fumbled for the taper and matches. When the small flame flickered to life, she felt suddenly shy. She saw only that her brother’s face was darker than usual, and caught the glint of long blue eyes, suddenly amused, as if both were silently inspecting each other. Their glance held a moment of understanding—no words needed. Margaret already loved him, and now sensed she would like him as a companion too. Her heart lifted. The sorrow remained, but it no longer weighed so heavily.

She ran upstairs, her spirits soaring. Her father lay prostrate, helpless. But now she had a weapon to rouse him—her brother’s presence.

“Papa,” she said, throwing her arms around his neck, lifting his head gently until it rested in hers. “Guess who’s here?”

He looked at her. A flicker of recognition passed through his eyes—then vanished, dismissed as fantasy.

He buried his face again in his arms. “I don’t know. Don’t tell me it’s Frederick—not Frederick. I can’t bear it. I’m too weak. And his mother is dying!”

He wept, childlike, broken. Margaret froze, disappointed. Then she spoke again—softer, gentler.

“Papa, it is Frederick. Think of mamma—how glad she’ll be! And for her sake, how glad we ought to be! For his sake, too—our poor, poor boy!”

Her father didn’t move, but he seemed to listen.

“Where is he?” he whispered at last.

“In your study. Alone. I lit the taper and came to tell you. He’s alone, and wondering why—”

“I’ll go to him,” he interrupted, rising slowly, leaning on her arm.

Margaret led him to the study door, but her nerves overwhelmed her. She couldn’t bear to witness the meeting. She turned away, ran upstairs, and wept freely—the first time in days. The strain had been immense. But Frederick was here. He was safe. Among them again.

She wiped her tears, opened her bedroom door. No sound. Almost afraid she had dreamed. She went downstairs, listened at the study door—heard voices. Enough. She went to the kitchen, stoked the fire, lit the house, prepared food for the traveler. How fortunate that her mother slept—she knew it from the candle-lighter slipped through her bedroom keyhole. The reunion could unfold quietly, the shock absorbed before the dying woman woke.

When all was ready, Margaret entered the study, tray in hand, like a servant. She was proud to serve him. But Frederick sprang up, took the tray from her. It was a sign—of relief, of care, of everything to come.

Together, they set the table. Few words, but hands touched, eyes met—silent language of blood and kinship. The fire had died. Margaret tried to relight it, though the evenings were chilly. Yet noise must be kept distant from Mrs. Hale’s room.

“Dixon says lighting fires is a gift, not an art,” she murmured.

“Poeta nascitur, non fit,” Mr. Hale quoted faintly. Margaret smiled—glad to hear a familiar phrase again.

“Dear old Dixon! We’ll kiss her properly,” Frederick laughed. “She used to kiss me, check my face, then start again! But Margaret—what clumsy hands you have! Never saw such awkwardness. Run and wash them. I’ll manage the fire. Lighting fires is one of my natural talents.”

So Margaret went—returned—moved about, restless, joyful. The more Frederick needed, the happier she was. He understood her instinctively. It was a joy stolen from mourning, made sharper by the knowledge of the sorrow ahead.

Then came the sound of footsteps—Dixon ascending the stairs. Mr. Hale jerked upright from his chair, watching his children as if they were actors in a play not meant for him. He stood, facing the door, his face suddenly tense—fear flashing across it. Margaret shivered. It reminded her of the new dangers they faced. She gripped Frederick’s arm tightly, brow furrowed, teeth clenched. Yet it was only Dixon’s steady tread. She heard her walk to the kitchen.

Margaret rose.

“I’ll go to her. Tell her. Ask how mamma is.” Mrs. Hale was awake. She wandered at first, but tea revived her slightly. She was not talkative. Better to wait until morning to tell her of Frederick’s return. Dr. Donaldson’s visit would bring enough emotion. He could advise how to prepare her.

Margaret could not sit still. She helped Dixon with preparations for “Master Frederick.” She felt invincible. Each glimpse of Frederick talking with her father filled her with strength. She studied him—he had delicate features, softened by a swarthy complexion and quick, intense expressions. His eyes often sparkled with humor, but sometimes flashed with sudden passion—something fierce, fleeting, like the look of a wild countryman. It startled her, but not with fear. It was not anger—it was impulse, raw energy, melting back into gentleness. She trusted him instantly.

Their conversation flowed naturally. He remembered Helstone, the New Forest, their childhood. He spoke of her as if she had never changed. She could talk freely, without fear of boring him. She had feared him before—afraid he’d be unrecognizable after years abroad. But he was kind, thoughtful, sensitive to their parents’ pain. He knew when to be bright, when to be quiet. When the mood was wrong, he became a patient nurse. And when he spoke of old times, Margaret nearly wept—his memories were vivid, warm, real.

For a few hours, Mrs. Hale rallied. She held Frederick’s hand, refused to let go—even in sleep. Margaret fed him like a child, so he wouldn’t disturb her mother. Once, Mrs. Hale woke, turned her head slowly, smiled at her children.

“I am very selfish,” she whispered. “But it won’t last long.”

Frederick bent down, kissed her hand.

That peace could not last, Dr. Donaldson warned Margaret. After he left, she slipped down to Frederick, who had been ordered to stay hidden in the back parlour—now his room.

She told him what the doctor said.

“I don’t believe it,” Frederick declared. “She’s ill—very ill, perhaps dangerously. But she wouldn’t be like this if she were dying. Margaret—she needs another doctor. A London physician. Have you never thought of that?”

“Yes,” Margaret replied. “More than once. But I don’t think it would help. And we can’t afford a great surgeon from London. Dr. Donaldson is second only to the best—perhaps even equal.”

Frederick paced the room, agitated.

“I have credit in Cadiz,” he said. “But none here—because of this wretched name change. Why did Father leave Helstone? That was the blunder.”

“It wasn’t a blunder,” Margaret said grimly. “And above all, don’t let Papa hear anything like that. You don’t know how he torments himself—believing mamma would never have fallen ill if we’d stayed at Helstone. He blames himself terribly.”

Frederick stopped pacing, stood directly before her, looking at her drooping form.

“My little Margaret,” he said, caressing her. “Let us hope as long as we can. Poor little woman—look at you, all wet with tears. I’ll hope. I’ll hope against a thousand doctors. Be brave, Margaret. Hope!”

Margaret choked, her voice low.

“I must try to trust. Oh, Frederick—mamma was beginning to love me so. And I was beginning to understand her. Now death comes to tear us apart.”

“Come, come,” he said. “Let’s go upstairs. Do something—don’t waste precious time. Thinking has made me sad many times, darling. But doing? Never. My rule is a parody of ‘Get money, my son, honestly if you can; but get money.’ Mine is: ‘Do something, my sister—do good if you can; but at any rate, do something.’”

“Not excluding mischief,” Margaret whispered, smiling through her tears.

“Show not that manner, and these features all,
The serpent’s cunning, and the sinner’s fall?”

The October morning crept in with a chill, damp breath—no golden mist to veil the world in softness, no sun to burn through it like fire, but a thick, clinging fog that choked the streets of Milton, turning them into dim corridors of grey. It was not the autumn of the countryside, where leaves turned crimson and gold under clear skies; here, the season wore a shroud, and even the light struggled to pierce the gloom.

Margaret moved slowly through the house, helping Dixon prepare for the day. Her eyes were heavy, her vision blurred by tears she could not afford to shed openly. The weight of grief pressed down on her shoulders, yet she carried it without complaint, for her father and brother leaned upon her now more than ever. She was the only one who could think, plan, act—while they were lost in sorrow, she must remain steady.

When the fire crackled warmly in the grate and the kettle sang its familiar tune, Margaret paused to glance around the room before calling her father and Frederick to breakfast. She wanted everything to appear cheerful, as if life still held some measure of peace. But the effort only deepened her sadness—the contrast between the bright hearth and her inner desolation brought fresh waves of emotion. She sank to her knees beside the sofa, burying her face in the cushions so no one would hear her cry.

Then a hand touched her shoulder.

“Come, Miss Hale—come, my dear,” said Dixon gently. “You mustn’t give way. There’s no one else in this house who can manage a thing. So much must be done—funeral arrangements, who shall come, where it shall be held… Master Frederick is half out of his mind with weeping, and your father… he walks like a man who has lost his way. I know it’s hard, miss—but death comes to us all. And you’ve been blessed: never lost a friend till now.”

Perhaps. But this loss was different. It was not just the end of a life—it was the unraveling of a world. Margaret did not find comfort in Dixon’s words, but the old woman’s quiet tenderness stirred something deep within her. Out of gratitude, more than anything else, she lifted her head, wiped her eyes, and offered a fragile smile. Then she rose and went to summon her family.

Mr. Hale entered as though in a trance—his movements automatic, his gaze distant, as if he saw only ghosts. Frederick came in briskly, forcing cheerfulness, grasping Margaret’s hand, looking into her eyes—and then breaking down in sudden tears.

Breakfast passed in silence broken only by small talk, trivial remarks meant to keep their minds from wandering back to the last meal they’d shared, when every sound had seemed a possible signal from the sickroom.

Afterward, Margaret resolved to speak with her father about the funeral. He nodded weakly, agreeing to everything she suggested—even when her proposals contradicted each other. No real decision emerged from him. As she turned to leave, he called her back.

“Ask Mr. Bell,” he said, voice hollow.

“Mr. Bell?” she repeated, surprised.

“Mr. Bell of Oxford,” he added. “He was my groom’s-man.”

She understood at once. “I’ll write to him today,” she promised.

And with that, he slipped again into silence.

All morning, Margaret worked—tired, overwhelmed, yet moving forward through a sea of melancholy tasks. By evening, Dixon approached her with news.

“I’ve done it, miss,” she began. “I was afraid for your father—he might have a stroke from grief. He’s been with poor missus all day, talking to her as if she were alive. When I listened at the door, I heard him speaking to her over and over. When I stepped in, he’d grow quiet, but utterly confused. So I thought—someone ought to rouse him. Even if it shocks him at first, it may help later.”

“And what did you do?” asked Margaret.

“I told him,” Dixon replied, “that I don’t believe it’s safe for Master Frederick to stay here.”

Margaret stiffened. “Why?”

“Well, it was Tuesday when I met a Southampton man—the first I’ve seen since coming to Milton. They don’t often come up this way. It was young Leonards—son of old Mr. Leonards, the draper. A scamp, the worst kind. He drove his father nearly mad, then ran off to sea. I never liked him. He was aboard the *Orion* at the same time as Frederick—I don’t recall if he was there during the mutiny.”

“Did he recognize you?” Margaret asked, her voice tight with alarm.

“That’s the trouble,” Dixon said. “I don’t think he would’ve known me, except I called out his name. He was a stranger in a strange place—so I didn’t hesitate. ‘Miss Dixon!’ he said. ‘Who’d have thought of seeing you here? Perhaps I’m mistaken—you’re not Miss Dixon anymore?’”

“I told him he could still call me an unmarried lady,” she continued, “though I might’ve had chances for marriage if I hadn’t been so particular. He said he couldn’t look at me and doubt me. But I wouldn’t take his nonsense. So I asked after his father—knowing full well he’d been turned out of the house—as if they were the best of friends. To spite me, he started asking after Master Frederick. Said how he’d gotten into a terrible scrape—like as if our boy’s troubles could clean George Leonards’ reputation! Said he’d be hanged for mutiny if caught, that a hundred pounds reward was offered, that he’d disgraced his family…”

Dixon paused, her voice dropping. “To make me suffer, you see. Because I’ve helped old Mr. Leonards give George a good dressing-down before. So I said there were other families who had far more reason to blush—and to be thankful their sons were earning honest livings far away.”

He answered, smug as ever, “I’m in a confidential position. If you know any young man who’s led a bad life and wants to turn over a new leaf, I’d be happy to lend him my patronage.”

“Him!” Dixon spat. “He’d corrupt a saint. I felt worse than I have in years standing there talking to him. I wanted to lash out, but he kept smiling, as if I were serious. And I couldn’t tell if he cared what I said at all—while I was furious inside.”

“You didn’t tell him anything about us—about Frederick?” Margaret asked, breathless.

“No, miss. He never asked where I was staying. I wouldn’t have told him if he had. Nor did I ask what his precious job was. He was waiting for a bus, and it came. He hailed it, but before boarding, he turned back and said—‘If you can help me trap Lieutenant Hale, Miss Dixon, we’ll go partners in the reward. I know you’d like to be my partner—wouldn’t you? Don’t be shy, say yes.’ Then he jumped on, and I saw his ugly face leering at me, laughing because he’d had the last word.”

Margaret felt a cold dread settle in her chest.

“Have you told Frederick?” she asked.

“No,” said Dixon. “I was uneasy knowing Leonards was in town—but there was so much else. But when I saw your father sitting so rigid, eyes glazed, I thought—maybe it would rouse him to know Frederick might be in danger. That he’d have to go, poor fellow, before Mr. Bell arrives.”

“I’m not afraid of Mr. Bell,” Margaret murmured. “But I am afraid of Leonards. I must tell Frederick. What did he look like?”

“A bad-looking fellow,” Dixon said firmly. “Red whiskers—so red I’d be ashamed to wear them. And despite saying he had a confidential post, he was dressed in fustian like a laborer.”

Frederick had to leave. Not just now—but immediately. He had only recently found his place in the family, promising strength and support. His devotion to their mother, his grief for her absence, had made him seem almost bound to them by a shared sorrow. Now, that bond was about to break.

As Margaret sat by the fire, lost in thought, her father paced restlessly, haunted by the fear Dixon had awakened—fear he had not yet voiced.

Then Frederick entered, his usual brightness dimmed, but the raw pain of grief softened. He came to Margaret, kissed her forehead.

“How pale you are, Margaret,” he whispered. “You’ve thought of everyone—no one has thought of you. Lie down. There’s nothing left for you to do.”

“It’s the worst,” she said softly.

But she obeyed, lying on the sofa. He covered her feet with a shawl and sat beside her on the floor. They spoke quietly, sharing thoughts.

She told him of Dixon’s encounter.

Frederick exhaled sharply. “I’d just love to confront that fellow. A worse sailor never sailed—nor a worse man. Margaret—you know how it happened?”

“Yes,” she said. “Mamma told me.”

“Well, when all the decent sailors were angry with our captain, this one tried to curry favor—disgusting. And now he’s here? Oh, if he knew I were near, he’d hunt me down to settle old scores. I’d rather anyone took the hundred pounds than that scoundrel. What a pity poor old Dixon couldn’t betray me and make a fortune for herself!”

“Oh, Frederick, hush,” she said, alarmed.

Mr. Hale approached, trembling, having overheard. He took Frederick’s hand in both of his.

“My boy,” he said, “you must go. It’s terrible—but I see you must. You’ve done all you could. You’ve been a comfort to her.”

“Oh, papa, must he go?” Margaret pleaded, though she knew the answer.

“I’d rather face trial,” Frederick said. “If I could only gather my evidence. I can’t bear being at the mercy of such a villain. In other circumstances, this stolen visit would have been thrilling—just like those forbidden pleasures the Frenchwoman spoke of.”

“One of the earliest things I remember,” Margaret said, “was you stealing apples, Fred. We had plenty—trees full of them. But someone told you stolen fruit tasted sweetest, and you believed it _au pied de la lettre_. Off you went robbing. You haven’t changed much, have you?”

“Not really,” he said, smiling faintly.

“Yes—you must go,” Mr. Hale repeated, answering Margaret’s earlier question.

His mind was fixed on one thing. He could not follow the twists of his children’s conversation.

Margaret and Frederick exchanged a glance—one filled with understanding, with sorrow, with the unspoken knowledge that this moment of closeness would soon be gone.

Frederick broke the silence. “You know, Margaret, I nearly gave both you and Dixon a fright this afternoon. I was in my room. I heard the front doorbell ring, but assumed the visitor had left. I was about to step into the hall when I opened my door—and there was Dixon, descending the stairs. She frowned and kicked me back into hiding. I kept the door open and heard a message given to a man in Father’s study. Then he left. Who could it have been? One of the shopmen?”

“Likely,” Margaret said, distracted.

“There was a little man who came about two o’clock,” she added.

“But this wasn’t a little man—a big, strong fellow. And it was past four.”

“It was Mr. Thornton,” Mr. Hale said.

They were glad to shift the topic.

“Mr. Thornton?” Margaret said, surprised.

“I thought—” she began, then stopped.

“What did you think?” Frederick asked, amused.

“Oh,” she said, blushing, “I thought you meant someone of a lower class—not a gentleman. Someone on business.”

“He looked like that,” Frederick said carelessly. “I took him for a shopman. Turns out he’s a manufacturer.”
