Margaret fell silent. She remembered how, at first, she too had judged him by appearance. It was natural—yet she felt a flicker of annoyance. She wanted Frederick to understand who Mr. Thornton truly was—but she couldn’t bring herself to explain.

Mr. Hale continued. “He came to offer help. But I couldn’t see him. I told Dixon to ask if he wished to speak with you—I think I asked her to find you.”

“He’s been a very agreeable acquaintance, hasn’t he?” Frederick asked, tossing the question into the air.

“A very kind friend,” Margaret said, stepping in.

Frederick was quiet for a while. Then he spoke.

“Margaret, it hurts to think I can never thank those who’ve shown you kindness. Our lives must part ways. Unless… unless I risk a court-martial. Or unless you and Father come to Spain.”

He threw out the idea lightly, testing the waters. Then he plunged in.

“You don’t know how much I wish you would. I have a good position—chances for more. Dolores Barbour—remember I told you of her? I wish you could meet her. I’m sure you’d—love her. Father, if you knew her, you’d love her too. She’s not eighteen, but if she feels the same next year, she’ll be my wife. Mr. Barbour won’t let us call it an engagement. But if you came, you’d find friends everywhere—especially Dolores. Think of it, Father. Let Margaret stand by me.”

“No,” Mr. Hale said firmly. “No more moves for me. One move cost me my wife. No more in this life. She will be here—and I will stay until my time ends.”

“Oh, Frederick,” Margaret said, “tell us more. I never thought of this—but I’m so glad. You’ll have someone to love and care for. Tell us all.”

“In the first place,” he said, “she’s Roman Catholic. That’s the only objection I foresaw. But my father’s change of heart—don’t sigh, Margaret.”

She sighed anyway. For Frederick himself was Roman Catholic in truth, though not yet in profession. This explained why his sympathy for her distress over her father’s departure from the Church had been so muted in his letters. She had thought it mere sailor’s indifference. But the truth was deeper—he had already begun to drift from the faith of his baptism, drawn toward beliefs opposite to his father’s. How much love shaped that change, even he could not say.

Margaret dropped the subject. But then she returned to it, thinking anew.

“For her sake, Fred, you must try to clear yourself. Even if the charge of mutiny is true, there may be a way to show your disobedience was justified—because the authority was abused.”

Mr. Hale stirred, listening.

“First,” Frederick said, “who would find my witnesses? All but a few are scattered across the fleet. And those who stayed were involved or sympathetic. Second—don’t think a court-martial is justice. It’s authority weighing nine-tenths, evidence barely one-tenth. Even testimony can be swayed by power.”

“But isn’t it worth trying? To see what proof might be found? Right now, people believe you guilty without excuse. You’ve never defended yourself. We’ve never known where to look. For Dolores’ sake, you should prove your innocence. Disobeying orders was wrong—but standing by while cruelty was used would have been worse. People know what you did—but not why. Your action wasn’t crime—it was heroism. They ought to know.”

“But how?” Frederick asked. “I can’t trust the judges. I can’t send a bellman shouting my heroism through the streets. No one would read a pamphlet years later. And even if I did—what good would it do?”

“Will you consult a lawyer?” Margaret asked, her voice rising slightly.

“I must first find one I can trust. Many a barrister might twist conscience to earn a hundred pounds by handing me over.”

“Nonsense!” she said. “I know a man—honorable, skilled, and connected to Aunt Shaw. Mr. Henry Lennox. He’d do much for one of her relations.”

“I think it’s a good idea,” Mr. Hale said. “But don’t propose anything that keeps Frederick here. Not for your mother’s sake.”

“We could send him to London by night train,” Margaret said, warming to the idea. “He must go tomorrow, papa—we decided, because of Mr. Bell and Dixon’s meeting.”

“Yes,” Frederick said firmly. “I must go tomorrow.”

Mr. Hale groaned. “I can’t bear to lose you—but I’m tormented by worry as long as you stay.”

“Well then,” Margaret said, “listen to my plan. He arrives in London Friday morning. I’ll write to Mr. Lennox—give him a note. You’ll find him at his chambers in the Temple.”

“I’ll write down every name I can remember from the *Orion*. I’ll leave it with him to track them down. He’s Edith’s husband’s brother, isn’t he? I remember you mentioning him. I have money with Barbour. I can pay a long bill if there’s hope. Money, dear father—that I meant for another purpose. I’ll consider it borrowed from you and Margaret.”

“Don’t,” Margaret said. “It’s risky. But worth trying. You can sail from London as easily as Liverpool?”

“Of course,” Frederick said. “Wherever I feel water beneath a plank, I’m home. I’ll find a ship—don’t worry. I won’t stay twenty-four hours in London—away from you, and from someone else.”

Margaret smiled. It was comforting that he looked over her shoulder as she wrote to Mr. Lennox. Had she not been watched, she might have hesitated, struggled with words. But she wrote steadily, clearly.

When she finished, he took the note before she could review it, tucking it into a pocket-book. From it fell a long lock of black hair.

Frederick’s eyes lit up. “Now you’d like to see that, wouldn’t you?”

“No,” he said. “Wait till you see her. She’s too perfect to be known by fragments. No mean brick shall be a specimen of the building of my palace.”

“What! remain to be denounced—dragged, perhaps, in chains.”

The next day passed slowly, the three of them seated together in silence, as if time itself had paused in mourning. Mr. Hale spoke rarely, only when his children pressed him with questions that pulled him reluctantly into the present. Frederick’s grief, once raw and overwhelming, had settled into quiet sorrow; the first storm had passed, and now he was ashamed of having been so undone by it. Though his love for his mother remained deep and enduring, he would never speak of it again. Margaret, less outwardly emotional at first, found her pain growing heavier with each passing hour. She wept often, and even in ordinary conversation, her voice carried a softness edged with sorrow—especially when her eyes met Frederick’s, knowing how soon he must leave.

She was glad he was going—glad for her father’s sake, though her own heart ached at the thought. The fear that consumed Mr. Hale—the constant dread that his son might be recognized and seized—outweighed any comfort he took in Frederick’s presence. Since Mrs. Hale’s death, his anxiety had worsened, sharpened by isolation and the weight of silence. He jumped at every sudden noise, and could not relax unless Frederick sat out of sight from anyone entering the room.

As evening approached, he turned to Margaret. “You’ll go with Frederick to the station, won’t you? I need to know he’s safely off. Will you bring me word that he’s clear of Milton—at least?”

“Of course,” she said. “I’d like to go, if you won’t be lonely without me, papa.”

“No, no!” he insisted. “I’d never rest easy unless I knew you’d seen him depart. Go to Outwood station. It’s just as near, and fewer people about. Take a cab. Less chance of being seen. What time is your train, Fred?”

“Ten minutes past six—nearly dark,” Frederick replied. “So what will you do, Margaret?”

“Oh, I can manage,” she answered, forcing a smile. “I’m getting braver, harder. The road home is well lit, even if it’s dark. And I was out much later last week.”

She was relieved when the moment came to part—not just from the dead, but from the living, too. She hurried Frederick into the cab, eager to shorten a scene that tore at her father’s heart. He would walk with his son to see him one last time at his mother’s side.

They reached Outwood earlier than expected, thanks in part to a common error in the Railway Guide, which listed the arrival time incorrectly. They had nearly twenty minutes to spare. The booking office was closed, so they couldn’t purchase tickets. Instead, they descended the steps leading down to the level ground beneath the tracks. A wide cinder path cut diagonally across a field beside the carriage road, and there they walked back and forth, waiting.

Margaret’s hand rested in Frederick’s arm. He gently squeezed it.

“Margaret,” he said softly, “I’ve decided to consult Mr. Lennox about clearing my name. Not for myself alone—but for you. I can’t bear the thought of you left alone if anything should happen to Father. He looks so worn—so broken. I wish you’d consider the Cadiz plan. If he were taken away, what would you do? You have no close friend nearby. We’re oddly isolated.”

Margaret fought back tears at the depth of his concern, for she knew the same fate was possible, given how frail her father had become. But she steadied herself. “These past two years have brought such strange changes to my life that I’ve learned not to dwell too closely on what might come. I try to live only in the present.”

She paused. They stood still, near the stile leading into the road. The sun was setting, casting golden light across their faces. Frederick held her hand tightly, reading more sorrow in her eyes than she would ever admit aloud.

“We’ll write often,” she continued. “And I promise—I know it will ease your mind—to tell you everything that troubles me. Papa is…” She faltered slightly, a barely perceptible tremor in her hand.

Frederick felt it. He turned sharply toward the road. A horseman was approaching slowly, passing right by the stile where they stood.

Margaret bowed stiffly. The rider returned the gesture with equal formality.

“Who is that?” Frederick asked, almost before the man was out of earshot.

“That’s Mr. Thornton,” she said quietly. “You saw him before.”

“Only his back,” Frederick replied. “He’s not an appealing man. Look at that scowl.”

“Something has upset him,” she said gently. “You wouldn’t think him unkind if you’d seen him with mamma.”

“I suppose it’s time to get our ticket,” Frederick said, glancing at the sky. “If I’d known it would be this dark, I wouldn’t have sent the cab back.”

“Oh, don’t worry,” she said, brushing aside his concern. “I can catch a cab here, or return by rail. From Milton station, there are shops, people, lamps—all the way. Don’t think of me. Just take care of yourself. I’m terrified Leonards might be on the same train. Check the carriage carefully before you board.”

They returned to the station. Margaret insisted on stepping inside, under the bright gaslight, to buy the ticket. A few idle young men loitered near the station master. One caught her eye—she thought she recognized him—and returned his bold stare with a look of quiet dignity. She hurried to Frederick, who waited outside.

“Have you got your bag?” she asked, gripping his arm. “Let’s walk around the platform.”

She was flustered, suddenly afraid of being left alone. Her courage, so strong just moments ago, began to slip.

A step followed them along the platform flags. It stopped when they did. They stood in silence, watching the distant line, listening to the whistle of the approaching train. Their hearts pounded. One moment more, and the train would arrive. Another second, and he would be gone.

Margaret regretted urging him to leave by train. It seemed reckless—more chances of discovery. If he had sailed from Liverpool to Spain, he might have been out of reach in hours.

Frederick turned, facing the lamp, where the gas flared brightly in anticipation of the train. Suddenly, a man dressed as a railway porter lunged forward—coarse, brutish, clearly drunk, yet fully aware. He shoved Margaret violently aside and grabbed Frederick by the collar.

“By your leave, miss!” he snarled. “Your name’s Hale, I believe?”

In a flash—how, Margaret didn’t know—Frederick twisted, tripped the man, and sent him crashing down from the elevated platform onto the soft earth beside the tracks. He lay motionless.

“Run, run!” Margaret gasped. “The train’s here! Was it Leonards? Oh, run! I’ll carry your bag!”

She seized his arm, pushing him forward with all her strength. A carriage door opened—he leapt in. As he leaned out, he called, “God bless you, Margaret!”

Then the train roared past, leaving her standing alone.

She was sick, faint, trembling. She stumbled into the ladies’ waiting-room and sank into a seat, gasping for breath. It had been so fast—so terrifying—so close. Had the train not arrived when it did, the man would have risen and shouted for help.

She wondered if he had moved. Had he been hurt? She forced herself to go back to the platform. It was lit, but empty. She walked to the edge and looked down—no one was there. She was grateful she had checked. Otherwise, nightmares would haunt her.

Still, she shivered. The road home seemed long and dark from the station’s glare. She could not walk it alone. She would wait for the down train. But what if Leonards recognized her as Frederick’s companion?

She hesitated before stepping into the booking office, counting her change with shaking fingers, her back to the men standing near the counter.

“So Leonards has been drinking again,” one said, sounding exasperated. “He’ll need all his influence to keep his job this time.”

“Where is he?” another asked.

“I don’t know. He came in five minutes ago, babbling about a fall, swearing like mad. Wanted to borrow money to catch the next up-train to London. Made all sorts of promises—but I told him to mind his own business. He left through the front door.”

“He’s probably at the nearest vaults,” the first man muttered. “Your money would’ve gone there too, if you’d been foolish enough to lend it.”

“Catch me!” the second laughed. “I knew better what his London meant. Why, he hasn’t paid me that five shillings yet.”

They went on, unaware of the woman listening behind them.

Now, Margaret’s only hope was for the train to arrive quickly. She retreated again to the waiting-room, every sound making her jump—every loud voice echoing like Leonards’. But no one came near her until the train pulled in.

A porter helped her aboard. She avoided his face until the train was moving. Then she glanced up—and breathed a silent sigh of relief. It wasn’t Leonards.

“Sleep on, my love, in thy cold bed,
Never to be disquieted!
My last Good Night—thou wilt not wake
Till I thy fate shall overtake.”

The house was unnaturally still after the storm of fear and noise that had shaken it. Her father had seen to everything—warm food, a clean bed, all the comforts needed for her return—and then settled back into his usual chair, drifting once more into one of those quiet, sorrowful reveries that had become his habit since his wife’s passing. Dixon had sent Mary Higgins into the kitchen to supervise the preparations, and though Mary scolded with her usual vigor, she did so in hushed tones, afraid to raise her voice while death still lingered in the home.

Margaret had decided not to speak to her father about the final, terrifying moment—the sudden panic at the station, the near escape. There was no point in bringing it up; it was over. The only lingering dread was that Leonards might somehow manage to borrow enough money to follow Frederick to London, to hunt him down there. But the odds against such a plan were great, and Margaret refused to torment herself with thoughts beyond her control. Frederick would be cautious—she had made sure of that—and within days he would be safely out of England.

“I suppose we’ll hear from Mr. Bell tomorrow,” Margaret said quietly.

“Yes,” her father replied, barely lifting his eyes. “I suppose so.”

“If he can come, he’ll probably arrive by evening.”

“If he cannot,” her father murmured, “I’ll ask Mr. Thornton to accompany me to the funeral. I cannot go alone. I’d break down completely.”

“No, papa,” Margaret said quickly, almost sharply. “Don’t ask Mr. Thornton. Let me go with you.”

“You?” He looked at her, surprised. “My dear, women don’t usually attend funerals.”

“No,” she answered, “not because they’re not allowed—but because they can’t help themselves. Women like us don’t go because we’re too emotional, yet ashamed to show it. Poor women do go—they don’t care if they weep openly. But I promise you, papa—if you let me go, I won’t be a burden. Don’t bring in a stranger and leave me behind. Please, dear papa—let me go. If Mr. Bell can’t come, I will go. I won’t press it if he does.”

But Mr. Bell could not come. His gout had flared up, and his letter was full of sorrow and regret. He longed to be there, to comfort his old friend, but duty called him to Milton. His estate required his presence, and his agent insisted it was essential. He wrote that he hoped to visit soon, if they would have him, and that the only thing making this journey bearable was the thought of seeing them again, perhaps offering some small comfort.

Margaret struggled to convince her father not to invite Mr. Thornton. The idea filled her with a deep, unspoken aversion. She couldn’t explain why—only that the thought of asking him felt like a betrayal, a surrender to something she could not accept.

The night before the funeral, a formal note arrived from Mrs. Thornton: at her son’s request, their carriage would be sent to attend the service, should it not be inconvenient to the family.

Margaret threw the letter toward her father without reading it aloud.

“Oh, don’t let us have these pretenses,” she said. “Let us go alone—you and I, papa. They don longer care for us, or else he would have offered to come himself. Instead, he sends an empty carriage—it’s a mockery.”

Her father blinked, taken aback. “You’ve always been so opposed to him coming, Margaret.”

“And I still am. I don’t want him here. I especially don’t want us to ask him. But this—this is so hollow, so false. I didn’t expect it from him.”

She burst into tears, startling her father. He had never seen her so agitated. In the midst of grief, she had been calm, thoughtful, patient—always putting others first. But now she seemed restless, overwhelmed, and when he reached out to comfort her, she only wept harder.

That night passed in fitful sleep. The next morning brought another letter—from Frederick.

Mr. Lennox was away, his clerk wrote, and wouldn’t return until Tuesday at the latest—possibly Monday. After weighing the options, Frederick decided to stay in London a few more days. He had considered returning to Milton, the temptation was strong—but the memory of Mr. Bell being in his father’s home, and the sudden alarm at the station, made him decide to remain where he was. He assured Margaret he would take every precaution against being followed by Leonards.

Margaret was relieved to read this letter while her father was with her mother. Had he been present, he would have expected her to read it aloud—and that would have stirred up fears she couldn’t soothe. Not only was Frederick delayed, but the letter mentioned the close call at the station, the possibility of pursuit. The mere thought chilled her blood. How would her father react? And how many times had she regretted suggesting they consult Mr. Lennox at all?

At the time, it had seemed wise—just a small delay, a minor risk. But now, every event pointed to it being a mistake. She fought hard against the guilt, knowing it was useless to dwell on what couldn’t be changed. Yet she feared her father’s fragile state would make him fall into despair, consumed by regrets he couldn’t shake.

She gathered all her strength to hold him together.

On the day of the funeral, her father seemed lost in thought, unaware of anything but the fact that his wife’s body would soon be carried away, hidden from sight. As the undertaker arranged the black draperies around the coffin, he trembled. He looked at Margaret, his eyes pleading.

“Pray for me, Margaret,” he whispered. “I have no strength left. I cannot pray. I give her up because I must. I try to bear it—I truly do. I know it is God’s will. But why did she die? Pray for me, Margaret—that I may have faith to pray. It is a great strait, my child.”

She sat beside him in the coach, holding him gently as he leaned into her. She recited verses of comfort, passages of quiet resignation—anything she could remember that spoke of enduring faith. Her voice never wavered. And in speaking, she found strength. Her father repeated the words after her, his lips moving mechanically, his mind struggling to grasp the peace he could not feel.

As the procession moved through the streets, Dixon subtly drew Margaret’s attention to Nicholas Higgins and his daughter Bessy, standing apart but watching closely. Nicholas wore his usual rough clothes, but a strip of black cloth adorned his hat—a mourning token he had never shown before. Yet Mr. Hale saw nothing. He remained lost in silent prayer, repeating the funeral service word for word, his breath catching only once or twice at its end. Then, placing his hand on Margaret’s arm, he walked forward as if blind, trusting her to guide him through the world he could no longer see.

Dixon wept openly, covering her face with her handkerchief. She was so absorbed in her own sorrow that she didn’t notice the crowd dispersing—until someone touched her shoulder.

It was Mr. Thornton.

He had stood behind a group of people, head bowed, unseen. Now he stepped forward.

“I beg your pardon,” he said, his voice low. “Can you tell me how Mr. Hale is? And Miss Hale? I should like to know how they are.”

“Of course, sir,” Dixon replied. “They’re doing as well as can be expected. Master is terribly broken. Miss Hale bears up better than most would.”

Mr. Thornton had wished she were suffering more. He wanted to believe she was grieving deeply, so that he might step in—not as a man of comfort, but as a lover who could console her. A selfish hope, yes—but one that stung like a secret joy. He imagined her leaning on him, needing him, and the thought brought a sharp pang of longing.

But then came the memory of the station—of the young man standing beside her, their posture so intimate, so natural. That image struck him like a wound. At that hour, so far from home—how could she be so free with another? He clenched his fists to keep the pain from showing.

With effort, he forced himself to believe in her purity, to trust her innocence. But the moment he stopped trying, doubt flooded back—wild, irrational fears chasing each other through his mind like shadows.

She bore up better than likely. So she had something to look forward to—some hope bright enough to ease even this grief. Yes—he knew how she loved. He had loved her, and in doing so, had learned the depth of her heart. She would walk in sunlight if any man were worthy enough to earn her love. And yet, even in mourning, she would rest in peace, supported by sympathy.

Whose sympathy? His? Or that other man’s?

The thought turned his pale face colder, sterner. He had to know.

“I suppose I may call,” he said, his tone flat. “On Mr. Hale, I mean. Perhaps after tomorrow.”

He said it as if it mattered little. But it did. Despite the pain, he longed to see her—to stand near her, to breathe the air she touched. Though he hated her sometimes—hated the memory of that gentle closeness—he still craved her presence. He was caught in the whirlpool of passion, spiraling ever closer to the center he could not escape.

“I dare say, sir,” Dixon replied, “Master will see you. He was very sorry to turn you away the other day. Circumstances weren’t right then.”

For reasons unknown, Dixon never told Margaret about this meeting. And so, Margaret never knew that Mr. Thornton had stood at her mother’s funeral, unseen, unheard, a silent witness to her grief.

“Truth will fail thee never, never!
Though thy bark be tempest-driven,
Though each plank be rent and riven,
Truth will bear thee on for ever!”

The strength Margaret had summoned to carry her through the days since her mother’s passing was now a fragile thread, stretched thin by grief and uncertainty. She moved through the house with a quiet dignity, yet beneath it all, a storm raged unspoken. At times, even in the midst of conversation with her father, a sudden pang would strike her—a sharp, silent cry of loss—reminding her that she no longer had a mother to turn to. Her heart was heavy, and her thoughts often wandered to Frederick, whose fate remained shrouded in silence. The Sunday post had passed without word from London, and by Tuesday, still no letter arrived. Margaret stood in the dim light of the drawing-room, her hands folded tightly in her lap, her mind racing with questions she dared not voice.

Her father, too, was restless. Once content to sit quietly in his armchair for hours, he now paced the room like a caged animal, his steps echoing against the walls. He would vanish into the hall, opening and closing doors as if searching for something unseen. Margaret tried to soothe him by reading aloud, but his mind was elsewhere—unreachable. It was then she felt immense relief that she had kept to herself the encounter with Leonards. To burden him further would have been unbearable.

A knock at the door brought a sudden shift in the air. Mr. Thornton had arrived. He entered directly to Mr. Hale, took his hands firmly in his own, and held them without speaking. In that silence, a deep understanding passed between them—one that words could not express. Then, turning to Margaret, he hesitated. He had meant to maintain the distance he had cultivated lately, but seeing her—pale, composed, yet marked by sorrow—his resolve faltered. She stood apart, her posture stiff with uncertainty, her eyes lowered. He stepped forward, his voice softening as he offered a few polite words. The moment touched her deeply; her eyes filled with tears, and she turned away quickly, clutching her work as if to anchor herself.

Mr. Thornton felt his pulse quicken. For a brief moment, the memory of Outwood Lane faded. He attempted to engage Mr. Hale in conversation, and though their exchange was measured, it brought a sense of stability to the older man—something Margaret noticed with quiet gratitude.

Then Dixon appeared at the doorway, breathless and pale. “Miss Hale, you are wanted.”

Margaret’s blood ran cold. Something had happened to Fred. There was no doubt in her mind. She glanced at her father and Mr. Thornton, who were still absorbed in their talk, and whispered, “Take care papa does not come down. Mr. Thornton is with him now.”

She followed Dixon silently, her hand trembling slightly as she reached the study door. Before entering, she turned and said, “Be sure he doesn’t come.”

Inside, the inspector stood waiting. His presence was abrupt, his manner formal. But Margaret met him with an icy calm. Her expression was unreadable—no fear, no curiosity, only a quiet defiance that seemed to challenge him to proceed.

“I beg your pardon, ma'am,” he began, “but my duty requires me to ask a few questions. A man died at the Infirmary after falling at Outwood station on Thursday evening, between five and six o’clock. At first, the fall seemed minor—but internal injuries, combined with alcohol, proved fatal.”

Margaret’s eyes widened slightly, but otherwise she did not move. Her lips pressed into a firm line, her jaw set. She watched him intently, as if expecting more.

“There is reason to believe the fall was caused by a push or scuffle,” he continued. “It appears the man had been behaving rudely—half-drunk—toward a young lady walking with a gentleman. That gentleman may have pushed him. One witness saw the altercation but thought little of it. Another observed the man, later identified as Leonards, running wildly after the train, shouting curses. He collapsed shortly afterward and was taken to the Infirmary. He never regained full consciousness. His last words were about a ‘Cornish trick’ and a curse on someone who’d made him lose money.”

The inspector paused. “There is some evidence linking the woman seen at the station to you, Miss Hale. You were seen near the time of the incident, described as remarkably handsome, and associated with a gentleman. A shop assistant claimed to recognize you—your family shops at his store.”

Margaret’s voice came low, steady: “I was not there.”

The inspector bowed slightly, but said nothing. He studied her closely. No tremor, no flinch. She stood like a statue, her gaze fixed, her face impassive.

“Then, madam,” he said, “you deny being the woman accompanying the gentleman who caused the fall?”

A flash of pain crossed her features—so swift, so deep, it might have escaped any but the most perceptive observer. But the inspector, though sharp, was not trained to read such hidden anguish. He saw only repetition—the same words, unchanged.

“I was not there,” she repeated, slowly, heavily.

He closed his notebook deliberately. “I hope you won’t think me impertinent, but I may need to call on you again. If the witness insists on identifying you, I may summon you to give testimony—perhaps to prove an alibi.”

She did not blink. Not once. Her face remained still, her posture rigid.

“It is unlikely,” he added, “that I’ll need to do so. I only act in my duty.”

Margaret inclined her head slightly. She could not speak. Her lips were dry, her throat tight. But then, without warning, she stepped forward, opened the study door, and walked ahead of him to the front entrance. She held the door open, watching him leave with that same distant, glassy stare—her eyes following him until he disappeared into the street.

Only when the door clicked shut behind him did she collapse. She staggered back into the study, swaying, her breath catching. Then, as if driven by some inner force, she turned and locked the door from within.

She stood still for a moment—then fell forward, unconscious, onto the floor.

“There’s nought so finely spun But it cometh to the sun.”

Mr. Thornton sat on, long past the hour he had intended to leave. He sensed that his presence brought comfort to Mr. Hale, and was moved by the quiet pleading in his friend’s voice—those half-spoken words, “Don’t go yet,” repeated now and again like a whispered prayer. He wondered where Margaret was, but lingered not for her sake. In this moment, amid the solemnity of a man confronting the void of mortality, Thornton found himself strangely composed. His usual intensity softened; he listened with deep attention to every word Mr. Hale spoke.

“Of death, and of the heavy lull, And of the brain that has grown dull.”

It was remarkable how Thornton’s presence seemed to unlock the hidden thoughts Mr. Hale had kept even from his daughter. Perhaps it was because Margaret’s empathy was so vivid, so alive, that he feared the emotional toll of sharing such doubts. Or perhaps, in his reflective state, questions arose—doubts about faith, meaning, the uncertainty of life—that he knew she would reject outright. She would have seen them as signs of weakness, even betrayal of belief. Yet here, with Thornton, he could speak freely. The industrialist said little, but each sentence carried weight—each pause, clarity. When Mr. Hale faltered mid-sentence, describing some old pain, Thornton would complete it as if he’d lived it too. When doubt crept in, trembling and blind, Thornton did not recoil—he offered direction, pointing toward the light that might dispel the shadows. Though a man of action, driven by ambition and will, Thornton carried within him a quiet devotion, deeper than Mr. Hale had ever imagined. They never discussed these matters again, but this one conversation forged between them a bond no casual talk of piety could ever achieve. For when all are admitted, how can there be a Holy of Holies?

Meanwhile, Margaret lay still and pale upon the floor of the study, as though life had left her body. She had collapsed beneath the weight of her burden—long borne, quietly endured—until suddenly, her faith failed. She groped in darkness, seeking help that did not come. A faint contraction marked her brow, though no other sign of awareness remained. Her lips, once set in defiance, now hung slack and bluish.

“E par che de la sua labbia si mova Uno spirto soave e pien d’amore, Chi va dicendo a l’anima: sospira!”

The first stir of returning consciousness came as a tremor at her mouth—a silent effort to speak. But her eyes stayed closed. The quiver faded. Then, leaning weakly on her arms, she rose. Her comb had fallen from her hair. Instinctively, she searched for it, needing to restore order, to erase any trace of collapse. Yet again and again, she had to sit, catching her breath. Her head drooped forward, her hands folded meekly in her lap. She tried to recall the exact details that had terrified her—but memory failed. Only two truths remained clear: Frederick had been in danger of being caught in London—not merely accused of manslaughter, but of leading the mutiny—and she had lied to save him.

Yet one thought brought solace: her lie had bought time. If the inspector returned tomorrow, after she received news of Frederick’s safety, she would face shame openly. She, Margaret Hale, would stand before a crowded court and confess: “I am a dog, and I have done this thing.” But if he came before she heard from Frederick—if he returned soon, as he had threatened—then she would lie again. How? She didn’t know. The words might betray her, after this night of torment. But she would say them. For Frederick.

She was roused by Dixon’s entrance. She had just let Mr. Thornton out.

No sooner had Thornton stepped onto the street than an omnibus stopped nearby. A man descended, touching his hat. It was the police inspector.

Thornton had once secured this man’s position in the force and had heard of his progress, though they rarely met. At first, Thornton did not recognize him.

“My name is Watson—George Watson, sir, that you got——”

“Ah, yes! You’re doing well, I hear.”

“Yes, sir. I ought to thank you. But it’s a matter of business I wanted to mention. You were the magistrate who took down the deposition of the man who died at the Infirmary last night?”

“Yes,” replied Thornton. “He gave a confused account—nothing much use, according to the clerk. I’m afraid he was drunk. But there’s no doubt he died from violence. One of my mother’s servants was engaged to him. She’s devastated today. What about him?”

“Well, sir, his death seems tied to someone in the house I saw you leaving—Mr. Hale’s, I believe.”

Thornton turned sharply. “What about it?”

“It appears I’ve gathered a fairly clear chain of evidence implicating a gentleman who was walking with Miss Hale at Outwood station the night Leonards fell. The young lady denies she was there.”

“Miss Hale denies it?” Thornton repeated, voice low and changed. “When? What time?”

“About six o’clock, Thursday the twenty-sixth.”

They walked in silence for a moment.

“You see, sir,” the inspector began, “there may be a coroner’s inquest. I have a young man—fairly certain at first—but since hearing Miss Hale’s denial, he says he wouldn’t swear. Still, he insists he saw her at the station, walking with a gentleman, just before the porter saw a scuffle—though he later claimed it might’ve been nothing more than rough play. Leonards might’ve jumped off himself. The porter’s story keeps shifting. Jennings, the grocer’s boy—he wasn’t bad, but I doubt he’d stand firm under oath after hearing she denied it. Seeing you leave that house, sir, I thought I’d ask your advice—both as the magistrate who saw Leonards die, and as the man who helped me into the force.”

“You were right,” Thornton said. “Wait until you see me again.”

“She’ll expect you to call, given what I told her.”

“I only need an hour. It’s three now. Come to my warehouse at four.”

“Very well, sir.”

They parted. Thornton hurried to his warehouse, forbidding his clerks to disturb him. He locked himself in his private room and allowed himself the agony of reflection. How could he have lulled himself into calm, remembering her tearful face just hours before—pitying her, yearning toward her—when he had once felt such fierce jealousy at the thought of her near him, unknown to him, at that hour, in that place? How could such purity have stooped from her noble bearing? Yet—was it truly noble? For an instant, the thought flashed: *Was it?* He hated himself for it, but the idea stirred the old fire in him—the attraction he could not deny. And then—her lie. What terror must she have known, to bow so completely to falsehood? He almost pitied her. What would become of her? She couldn’t have foreseen the consequences. If the young man testified… Suddenly, he stood up. There would be no inquest. He would stop it. He would take responsibility. The medical evidence was uncertain—Leonards had a fatal internal illness; the fall might have accelerated death, or the cold and drink afterward. But the cause was ambiguous. If he had known she would be implicated—if he had known she would be forced to lie—he could have prevented it with one word. The decision had hung in balance the night before. Miss Hale might love another—might scorn him—but he would still serve her, silently, without her knowing. He might despise her, but the woman he once loved deserved protection. Shame would follow if she stood in court, swearing to a lie. He would spare her that.

Pale and stern, Thornton emerged through his astonished clerks. He was gone half an hour. When he returned, he looked no less grim—though his purpose had been fulfilled.

He wrote two lines on a slip of paper, sealed it, and handed it to a clerk.

“I appointed Watson—the former packer who joined the police—to call at four. I’ve just met a gentleman from Liverpool who wants to see me. See that he receives this note.”

The message read:

“There will be no inquest. Medical evidence insufficient. Take no further steps. I have not seen the coroner, but I assume responsibility.”

Watson read it and sighed in relief. “That saves me a nasty job. My witnesses weren educate—only the young woman was certain. The porter? Changed his story. Jennings? Not sure. After hearing Miss Hale deny it, none would swear. It would’ve been messy. Now I can tell them they’re free.”

That evening, he returned to Mr. Hale’s. Her father and Dixon urged Margaret to bed, but she refused—though neither understood why. Dixon knew part of the truth—but not all. Margaret had not spoken of her lie, nor of Leonards’ death. So Dixon, torn between duty and concern, pressed her to rest. Margaret did not speak unless addressed. She tried to smile in reply to her father’s worried glances, but the attempt turned into a sigh. At last, worn down, she agreed to retire.

She stood beside her father, gripping the back of his chair.

“You’ll go to bed soon, Papa, won’t you? Don’t stay up alone.”

Her father’s reply was lost in the soft chime of the doorbell.

She kissed him and rushed downstairs, moving with a speed no one would have believed possible moments before. She brushed aside Dixon.

“Don’t come. I’ll open it. I know it’s him—I must manage it myself.”

“As you please, miss,” Dixon muttered. Then, softer: “But you’re not fit for this. You’re more dead than alive.”

“Am I?” Margaret asked, turning, her eyes blazing with strange fire, cheeks flushed, lips still pale and dry.

She opened the door to the inspector and led him into the study. She placed the candle on the table and carefully snuffed it, then faced him.

“You’re late,” she said. “Well?”

She held her breath.

“I’m sorry for the trouble, ma’am. They’ve decided against an inquest. I had other duties, or I’d have come earlier.”

“So it’s over,” she said. “No further inquiry?”

“I believe I have Mr. Thornton’s note,” the inspector said, fumbling in his pocketbook.

“Mr. Thornton?” she echoed.

“Yes. He’s a magistrate. Ah—here it is.”

She couldn’t read it—no, not even close to the flame. The words swam before her. But she held it, staring as if studying it.

“I’m sure it’s a great relief, ma’am. The evidence was shaky—whether he was struck at all. And identity? That made it complicated. As I told Mr. Thornton——”

“Mr. Thornton?” she said again.

“I met him this morning, just as he was leaving your house. Being an old friend and the magistrate who saw Leonards, I thought I’d consult him.”

Margaret sighed deeply. She didn’t want to hear more. She was afraid—afraid of what she’d heard, afraid of what she might hear. She wished he would leave. She forced herself to speak.

“Thank you for coming. It’s very late. I dare say it’s past ten. Oh—here’s the note!” she said, suddenly noticing his hand extended. He was putting it away, when she said, “It’s cramped, dazzling writing. I can’t read it. Will you read it aloud?”

He did.

“Thank you,” she said. “You told Mr. Thornton I wasn’t there?”

“Oh, certainly, ma'am. I’m sorry now I acted on information that turned out so wrong. At first, the young man was sure. Now he says he doubted all along. Hopes his mistake hasn’t cost you your custom. Good night, ma'am.”

“Good night.” She rang for Dixon to show him out. As the woman returned, Margaret darted past.

“It’s all right,” she said, without looking. Before Dixon could question her, she raced upstairs, entered her room, and bolted the door.

She threw herself, still dressed, onto the bed. Too exhausted to think. Half an hour passed before fatigue and chill began to stir her senses. Then she remembered—recalled, combined, questioned.

First: the fear for Frederick was over. The strain was past.

Then: she needed to remember every word the inspector said about Mr. Thornton. When had he seen him? What had he said? What had Thornton done? What were the exact words in the note?

Until she could reconstruct the letter perfectly—even the punctuation—her mind refused to move forward.

Then came the realization: Thornton had seen her near Outwood station on that fatal night. He had been told she denied being there. To him, she was a liar.

She was a liar.

But she felt no remorse before God. Only chaos surrounded the one truth: in Thornton’s eyes, she was degraded. She didn’t care to justify herself. That mattered nothing to him. She hadn’t thought he’d suspect her motives—her brother’s presence was natural. But the lie itself was known. And he had a right to judge her.

“Oh, Frederick! Frederick!” she cried. “What have I sacrificed for you?”

Even in sleep, her mind wandered the same path—now twisted, now monstrous.

When she woke, a new idea dawned like morning light: Thornton had learned of her lie *before* speaking to the coroner. Perhaps he had intervened precisely to spare her from repeating the falsehood. But she pushed it aside—sick with childish pride. If so, she owed him no gratitude. It only proved how deeply he had already judged her guilty. He had taken action not out of kindness, but because he saw her disgrace clearly. She would have faced the whole trial—would have lied again—rather than let him know he had spared her because he already knew her shame.

Why had fate brought him into contact with the inspector? Why was he the magistrate sent to record Leonards’ final words? What had Leonards said? Had Thornton heard enough to connect it to Frederick’s past? Might he already know of the accusation through Mr. Bell? If so, he had saved the son who defied the law to reach his dying mother.

Under this thought, she could feel gratitude—though not yet. Not if his interference stemmed from contempt.

Had anyone just cause to look down on her? Thornton, above all? She had once looked down on him from her imagined heights. Now, she found herself at his feet. And the realization filled her with distress. She shrank from tracing the logic to its end—how much she valued his respect. Whenever the idea surfaced, she turned away. She would not believe it.

It was later than she thought. In her turmoil, she had forgotten to wind her watch. Mr. Hale had ordered she not be disturbed. Soon, the door creaked open. Dixon peeked in. Seeing Margaret awake, she stepped forward with a letter.

“Here’s something to cheer you, miss. A letter from Master Frederick.”

“Thank you, Dixon. How late it is.”

She spoke weakly, letting Dixon place the letter on the counterpane without reaching for it.

“You want breakfast, I’m sure. I’ll bring it in a minute. Master’s got the tray ready.”

Margaret didn’t answer. She waited until Dixon left. She must be alone to open it.

At last, she did. The first thing she saw was the date—two days earlier than she had received it. He had written when he promised. Their fear might have been unnecessary.

She read. It was hasty, but clear. He had met Henry Lennox, who initially shook his head—called his return reckless, given the accusations and powerful enemies. But after discussion, Lennox admitted there might be a chance of acquittal—if credible witnesses could be found. Otherwise, it was a dangerous gamble. He would investigate thoroughly.

“It struck me,” Frederick wrote, “that your introduction, little sister of mine, went a long way. Is it so? He asked so many questions. Seemed sharp, intelligent, busy—lots of clerks. But maybe just lawyer tricks. I’ve just caught a packet—leaving in five minutes. Might have to return to England on this. Keep my visit secret. I’ll send Father some rare old sherry—can’t buy it in England. He needs something good. My dear love to him—God bless him. P.S.—What an escape! Take care you don’t breathe a word of my having been—*not even to the Shaws*.”

“The steps of the bearers, heavy and slow,
The sobs of the mourners, deep and low.”

At the hour they had agreed upon the day before, Margaret and her father set out to visit Nicholas Higgins and his daughter. Both were struck by a quiet unease—their new clothes felt strange, as though they belonged to another life; it was the first time in weeks they had gone anywhere together with purpose, and the weight of their shared grief hung between them like an unspoken word. They walked close, drawn into each other’s presence not by words, but by the silent understanding that only sorrow can forge.

Nicholas sat in his usual corner by the fire, but without his pipe. His head rested on his hand, elbow propped on his knee. He did not rise when they entered, though Margaret saw the warmth in his eyes. “Sit ye down, sit ye down,” he said, giving the hearth a sharp shove as if to distract from himself. “Fire’s welly out.”

He looked worn—his beard unshaven for days, dark against his pale face, his jacket patched and threadbare. “We thought we’d find you here, just after dinner,” Margaret said gently.

“We’ve had our sorrows too since last we met,” Mr. Hale added.

“Ay, ay,” Nicholas replied, voice low. “Sorrows are more plentiful than dinners these days. I reckon my dinner hour stretches all over the day—you’re likely to find me any time.”

“Are you out of work?” Margaret asked.

“Ay,” he answered shortly. Then, after a pause, he raised his eyes for the first time: “I’m not wanting brass. Don’t think I am. Bess, poor lass, left a little savings under her pillow—ready to slip into my hand at the last moment—and Mary’s fustian-cutting. But I’m out of work just the same.”

“We owe Mary some money,” Mr. Hale said, before Margaret could stop him with a swift pressure on his arm.

“If she takes it,” Nicholas snapped, “I’ll turn her out o’ doors. I’ll stay inside these four walls, and she’ll stay out. That’s all.”

“But we owe her thanks,” Mr. Hale began again, “for her kindness.”

“I ne’er thanked your daughter for what she did for my poor wench,” Nicholas said. “I couldn’t find the words. Now I must begin to try—if you start making a fuss about what little Mary could do for you.”

“Is it because of the strike?” Margaret asked softly.

“The strike’s over,” he said. “It’s done for this time. I’m out of work because I didn’t ask for it. And I didn’t ask, because good words are scarce, and bad ones are plenty.”

He spoke in riddles, but Margaret sensed he wanted to be questioned further.

“And good words are—?” she prompted.

“Askin’ for work,” he said. “I reckon them’s almost the best words a man can say. ‘Gi’ me work’ means ‘and I’ll do it like a man.’ Them’s good words.”

“And bad words are refusing you when you ask?”

“Ay. Bad words is saying, ‘Aha, my fine chap! You’ve been true to your order, and I’ll be true to mine. You did the best you could for those who needed help—that’s your way of being faithful. And I’ll be true to mine. You’ve been a poor fool, one who didn’t know better than to be a loyal fool. So go and be d—d. There’s no work for you here.’ Them’s bad words. I’m not a fool. And if I were, folks ought to have taught me how to be wise after their fashion. I might have learned—if anyone had tried.”

“Would it not be worth trying,” Mr. Hale said, “to speak to your old master? Ask him to take you back? It might be a poor chance, but it’s a chance.”

Nicholas looked up sharply, then let out a dry, bitter laugh. “Measter? If it’s no offence, I’ll ask you a question or two in return.”

“You’re welcome,” Mr. Hale replied.

“I reckon you’ve some way of earning your bread. Folk don’t live in Milton just for pleasure, if they can live elsewhere.”

“You’re right,” Mr. Hale said. “I have some property, but I came here to become a private tutor.”

“To teach people,” Nicholas said. “Well, I reckon they pay you for teaching, don’t they?”

“Yes,” Mr. Hale smiled. “I teach to earn my living.”

“And those who pay you—do they tell you what to do with the money they give you? What not to do with it?”

“No,” Mr. Hale said. “Of course not.”

“They don’t say, ‘You may have a brother or a friend dear as a brother who needs this money for something both you and he believe is right—but you must promise not to give it to him. You may see a good use for your money—but we don’t think it’s good, so if you spend it that way, we’ll just stop dealing with you.’ They don’t say that, do they?”

“No,” Mr. Hale said firmly. “They wouldn’t dare.”

“Would you stand for it if they did?”

“It would be a very hard pressure indeed,” Mr. Hale admitted, “that would make me even consider submitting to such control.”

“There’s not the pressure on all the broad earth that would make me,” Nicholas said. “Now you’ve got it. You’ve hit the bull’s eye. Hampers—that’s where I worked—makes their men pledge themselves not to give a penny to help the Union or keep turn-outs from getting aid. They may make pledges,” he sneered, “but they only make liars and hypocrites. And that’s worse than making men’s hearts so hard they won’t do a kindness to those in need, or support the just cause—even if it goes against the strong hand. But I’ll never swear away my soul for all the work the king could give me. I’m a member of the Union. I believe it’s the only thing that can help working men. I’ve been on strike, I’ve known what it’s like to starve. So if I get a shilling, sixpence shall go to those who ask for it. The consequence? I don’t see where I’ll get a shilling.”

“Is that rule enforced in all the mills?” Margaret asked.

“I can’t say. It’s new at ours. I reckon they’ll find they can’t hold to it. But it’s in force now. By-and-by they’ll learn—tyrants make liars.”

There was silence. Margaret hesitated, reluctant to upset a man already so burdened. Yet the truth pressed upon her. At last, in soft tones, she spoke.

“Do you remember poor Boucher saying the Union was a tyrant? I think he called it the worst tyrant of all. And I remember—I agreed with him then.”

Nicholas remained still, head in hands, staring into the fire. She could not read his face.

“I’ll not deny,” he said slowly, “that the Union sometimes forces a man into his own good. A man leads a hard life outside the Union. But once inside, his interests are cared for better than he could manage himself. It’s the only way working men can win justice—by standing together. More members, more strength for each man. Government looks after fools and madmen. If a man threatens harm to himself or others, it puts a check on him whether he likes it or not. That’s all we do in the Union. We can’t lock folk in prison—but we can make life so heavy that a man has no choice but to come in, and be wise and helpful, despite himself. Boucher was a fool all along—and never a greater fool than at the end.”

“He hurt you?” Margaret asked.

“Aye, that he did. We had public opinion on our side—until he and his kind started rioting, breaking laws. Then it was all over with the strike.”

“So would it not have been better to leave him alone? To let him stay free, rather than force him into the Union? He did you no good. You drove him mad.”

“Margaret,” her father murmured, seeing the storm gather on Higgins’s face.

“I like her,” Nicholas said suddenly. “She speaks plainly what’s in her mind. She doesn’t understand the Union—yet. But it’s a great power. Our only power. I’ve read poetry about a plough going over a daisy—it made tears come to my eyes before I had cause to cry. But the farmer never stopped ploughing, I warrant, for pitying the flower. He had too much common sense. The Union is the plough, preparing the land for harvest. Boucher—well, he’s no daisy. He’s more like a weed spreading across the field. He’s meant to be cleared away. I’m sore vexed with him now. So maybe I don’t speak fair. I could plough over him myself—with all the pleasure in life.”

“What? What has he done? Anything new?”

“Ay, to be sure. He’s never out of mischief. First, he went raging like a madman, kicked up that riot. Then he hid—would have stayed hidden, if Thornton had pursued him as I hoped. But Thornton, having gotten his own way, didn’t care to continue the prosecution. So Boucher crept back home. Didn’t show himself for a few days. Had that grace. Then—where do you think he went? Why, to Hampers’. Damn him! He went with that weak, pleading face—turns me sick to look at it—asking for work, knowing full well the new rule: pledging themselves to give nothing to the Union, nothing to help starving turn-outs! Why, he’d have starved to death if the Union hadn’t helped him in his time of need. There he went, offering to promise anything, to betray everything—to tell all he knew about our plans. A Judas, the good-for-nothing! But I’ll say this for Hamper—thank him at my dying day—he turned Boucher away. Wouldn’t listen to a word. Though others said the traitor cried like a babe!”

“Oh!” Margaret gasped. “How shocking! How pitiful!”

“Higgins,” she said, “don’t you see—you made Boucher what he is? By forcing him into the Union against his will—without his heart in it. You made him what he is!”

Made him what he is? What was he?

From the narrow street came a hollow, measured sound—slow, deliberate, growing louder. Voices hushed. Feet moved—not forward, but circling, as if around a fixed point. Then, clear above the rest, a steady tramp of feet—men carrying a heavy load.

All were drawn toward the door, not by curiosity, but by a solemn compulsion. Six men walked down the road, three of them policemen. On their shoulders they bore a door, removed from its hinges. Upon it lay a dead body. From either side, water dripped steadily onto the stones.

The entire street poured out to witness. Some questioned the bearers, who answered reluctantly—having told the story so many times.

“We found him in the brook beyond there.”

“The brook? There’s not enough water to drown a man!”

“He was determined. Lying face down. Sick of living—whatever his reason.”

Nicholas crept to Margaret’s side, whispering in a broken voice: “It’s not John Boucher? He had no guts for that. Sure! It’s not John Boucher! Look—they’re all looking this way! Listen! I’ve a singing in my head. I can’t hear.”

They laid the door carefully on the cobbles. All could see the drowned man—eyes glassy, one half-open, staring upward. His face was swollen, discoloured, stained by the dye-water of the stream. The front of his head was bald, hair thin and long behind, each strand a channel for the water. Through all this, Margaret recognized John Boucher.

It seemed sacrilegious to gaze upon that twisted, agonized face. In a flash of instinct, she stepped forward and covered the dead man’s face with her handkerchief.

The eyes that watched her followed her as she turned away—and landed upon Nicholas Higgins, rooted to the spot.

The men conferred, then one approached Higgins, who tried to retreat into his house.

“Higgins,” the man said. “You knew him. You must go tell the wife. Do it gently, man—but quickly. We can’t leave him here long.”

“I can’t,” Higgins whispered. “Don’t ask me. I can’t face her.”

“You know her best,” the man insisted. “We’ve done our part bringing him here. You take your share.”

“I can’t,” Higgins repeated. “I’m shattered by seeing him. We weren’t friends. And now he’s dead.”

“Well, if you won’t, you won’t. Someone must. It’s a hard task—but every minute counts. If she hears it in a rougher way, it’ll break her.”

“Papa,” Margaret said softly, “you should go.”

“If I could—if I had time to think—” he stammered.

Margaret saw he was trembling. “I will go.”

“Bless you, miss,” the man said. “It’ll be a kind act. She’s been poorly, I hear. Few here know much of her.”

Margaret knocked. But inside, the noise of children—many, restless, ill-behaved—made no reply. She doubted she was heard. Every second stretched her resolve thinner. She opened the door, stepped in, closed it behind her, and, unseen, bolted it.

Mrs. Boucher sat in a rocking chair across the untidy fireplace. The house seemed untouched—no sign of cleaning for days.

Margaret spoke, barely knowing the words, her throat dry, the children’s cries drowning her voice. She tried again.

“How are you, Mrs. Boucher? I fear you’re very poorly.”

“I’ve chance of being well,” she snapped. “Left alone with these childer, and nothing to quiet them. John shouldn’t have left me. Not when I’m so weak.”

“How long since he left?”

“Four days. No one would give him work. He had to go tramping toward Greenfield. But he might have returned by now—or sent word. He might——”

“Oh, don’t blame him,” Margaret said. “He felt it deeply, I’m sure——”

“Will you hold thy din and let me hear the lady speak?” the woman shouted at a toddler, about a year old, clinging to her dress. “He’s always asking for ‘daddy’ and ‘butty.’ I’ve no butties to give him. Daddy’s gone, forgotten us all, I think. He’s his father’s darling,” she added, suddenly tender, lifting the child to her lap and kissing him fiercely.

Margaret placed a hand on her arm. Their eyes met.

“Poor little fellow,” she said slowly, “he _was_ his father’s darling.”

“He _is_ his father’s darling,” the woman corrected, rising abruptly, facing Margaret.

Silence. Then she began, voice low, growing wilder:

“He _is_ his father’s darling. Poor folk can love their children as well as rich. Why don’t you speak? Why do you stare at me with those big, pitiful eyes? Where’s John?”

Weak as she was, she shook Margaret to force an answer.

“Oh, my God!” she cried, reading the tears. She collapsed back into the chair.

Margaret took the child and placed him in her arms.

“He loved him,” she whispered.

“Ay,” the woman said, shaking her head. “He loved us all. We had someone to love once. Long ago. But while he lived, he did love us. He loved this babby best, perhaps. But he loved me—and I loved him, even though I called him five minutes ago. Are you sure he’s dead?”

She tried to rise.

“If it’s only that he’s ill and near death, they may bring him round. I’m weak myself—I’ve been ill for months.”

“But he is dead—he is drowned!”

“People are brought round after drowning. What was I thinking of, sitting still when I should be moving? Here, whisth thee, child—whisth thee! Take this, take anything to play with, but don’t cry while my heart’s breaking! Oh, where has my strength gone? Oh, John—husband!”

Margaret caught her before she fell. She sat in the rocking chair, cradling the woman in her arms, her head resting on Margaret’s shoulder.

The other children, huddled together in terror, slowly grasped the truth. When they did, they erupted into screams of despair. Johnny’s cry was loudest—though he did not know why.

The mother trembled in Margaret’s embrace.

Then—noise at the door.

“Open it. Open it quick,” Margaret said to the eldest child.

“It’s bolted. Be still. Oh, papa, send the men upstairs quietly. Perhaps she won’t hear them. She’s fainted—that’s all.”

“It’s for the best,” said a woman following the bearers. “But you’re not fit to hold her. Let me fetch a pillow. We’ll lay her down gently.”

This neighbour—new to the district—brought relief. Her kindness made Margaret feel unnecessary. It was better, perhaps, to leave.

She looked for Nicholas Higgins. He was gone.

She turned to the woman who had helped place Mrs. Boucher on the floor.

“Can you gently tell these people to leave quietly? So when she wakes, she finds only those she knows. Papa, speak to the men—get them to go. She can’t breathe with this crowd.”

Margaret knelt beside the widow, bathing her face with vinegar. Moments later, she noticed a sudden rush of air.

She looked up. Her father and the woman exchanged a smile.

“What is it?” she asked.

“Our good friend here,” Mr. Hale said, “thought of a clever way to clear the room.”

“I told them to begone,” the woman said, “each to take a child with them. And to remember—they’re orphans now, and their mother a widow. Who can do most? The children are sure of food today—and kindness.”

“Does she know how he died?”

“No,” Margaret said. “I couldn’t tell her all at once.”

“She must be told—for the inquest. See? She’s coming round. Shall I tell her? Or you? Or perhaps your father?”

“No,” Margaret said. “You. You.”

They waited in silence until she fully regained consciousness.

Then the neighbour sat on the floor, cradling Mrs. Boucher’s head.

“Neighbour,” she said, “your man is dead. Guess how he died?”

“He was drowned,” Mrs. Boucher whispered, beginning to cry.

“He was found drowned. Coming home, hopeless. He thought God could not be harder than men—perhaps not so hard. Perhaps as tender as a mother. Perhaps even tenderer. I’m not saying he did right. Nor wrong. All I say is—may neither me nor mine ever have his heart. Or we might do the same.”

“He has left me alone with all these children!” the widow moaned, less distressed by the manner of death than Margaret expected. It was consistent with her helpless nature—she felt his loss primarily as a blow to herself and her children.

“Not alone,” Mr. Hale said solemnly. “Who is with you? Who will take up your cause?”

The widow stared, startled, realizing he was there.

“Who has promised to be a father to the fatherless?” he continued.

“But I’ve six children, sir,” she said, “the eldest not eight. I don’t doubt His power—only it takes great trust.”

And she wept again.

“She’ll be better tomorrow,” the neighbour said. “Best comfort now is the feel of a child at her heart. I’m sorry they took the baby.”

“I’ll go for him,” Margaret said.

In minutes, she returned, holding Johnnie—face smeared with food, hands full of shells, crystal fragments, and the head of a plaster figure.

She placed him in his mother’s arms.

“There,” the woman said. “Now you go. They’ll cry together, comfort together—better than any adult could do. I’ll stay with her as long as needed. Come tomorrow—you can talk wisely with her. She’s not up to it today.”

As they walked slowly up the street, Margaret paused at Higgins’s door.

“Shall we go in?” her father asked.

“I was thinking of him too.”

They knocked. No answer. They tried the door—it was bolted. But they thought they heard movement within.

“Nicholas,” Margaret called.

No reply.

Then—a book dropped.

“Nicholas,” she said again. “It’s only us. Won’t you let us in?”

“No,” he said. “I spoke plainly about words when I bolted the door. Let me be today.”

Mr. Hale would have pressed, but Margaret silenced him with a finger to his lips.

“I don’t wonder,” she said. “I myself long to be alone. After a day like this, it seems the only thing that can do one good.”

“A spade! a rake! a hoe!
A pickaxe or a bill!
A hook to reap, or a scythe to mow,
A flail or what ye will—
And here’s a ready hand
To ply the needful tool,
And skilled enough, by lessons rough,
In Labour’s rugged school.”

The next day, Higgins’s door was locked when Margaret and her father called on Mrs. Boucher. A neighbour, eager to be helpful, informed them that he had indeed left home earlier that morning. But before departing, he had stopped by to see the widow—though no one could say what business had taken him away.

It was a visit that brought little comfort to Mrs. Boucher. She saw herself as a victim of cruel fate, unjustly abandoned by her husband’s suicide. There was truth in her sorrow—enough to make it hard to argue against—but her grief was tangled with self-absorption. Her thoughts turned inward, fixed upon her own suffering, and even her children seemed burdens rather than blessings, though she loved them fiercely in a way that felt raw and instinctive.

Margaret tried to connect with the children, to draw them into conversation. They were more honest mourners than their mother. Each child remembered something kind Daddy had done—how he’d let them stay up late, how he’d shared his food, how he’d tucked them in with a gentle word. One whispered, trembling, “Is yon thing upstairs really him? It doesna look like him. I’m feared on it, and I never was feared o’ daddy.”

Margaret’s heart ached. The mother had dragged the children to see their dead father, disfigured by water and time—mixing horror with grief, turning natural sorrow into something grotesque. She gently steered the children toward action: what they could do for their mother now, what their father would have wanted them to do. They responded—not with words, but with quiet effort, helping clean the messy room, each trying to carry out her suggestion.

But Mr. Hale struggled. He spoke of duty, virtue, divine mercy—abstract ideals too distant for a woman whose mind was numb with pain and resentment. She couldn’t imagine her husband’s despair; she only felt its consequences. She refused to accept that he had been driven to desperation. Instead, she blamed everyone else—the masters, especially Mr. Thornton, whose mill had been attacked; the Union, represented by Higgins; the children themselves, so many and so hungry. All of them formed an army of enemies who had forced her husband into ruin.

Margaret listened, growing weary. When they returned home, she found herself unable to lift her father’s spirits.

“It is the town life,” she said quietly. “People are stirred by constant motion, noise, confinement. These houses, shut tight and dark, wear down the soul. In the country, people live outside—even in winter. Children play in fields, breathe fresh air. There’s space to think, to rest.”

“But people must live in towns,” replied her father. “And in the country, some grow stagnant—fatalists, almost, who see no future beyond the next meal.”

“Yes,” she agreed. “Each life brings its own trials. Town dwellers struggle with impatience, overwhelmed by the rush of every moment. Country folk may fall into dullness, content with mere survival, without hope or plan. Both fail to imagine the future—townsfolk because the present is too loud, country folk because they’ve forgotten there *is* a future.”

“And so both end in the same place,” he murmured. “Yet this poor woman—what can we do for her?”

“Nothing, perhaps,” Margaret said softly. “But we cannot stop trying. Even if our efforts seem useless—we must go on. Oh, Papa… it’s such a hard world.”

“So it is,” he answered. “But we’ve been happy, even through our sorrow. Remember Frederick’s visit?”

“Yes,” she said, brightening. “It was lovely—brief, secret, forbidden.”

Then she stopped. A wave of shame washed over her. She had lied to protect herself. And she despised cowardice above all things. To lie—especially to someone like Mr. Thornton—was a betrayal of everything she believed in. She thought of him now, wondering how he might judge her. Would it have mattered less if it had been anyone else? She imagined telling Aunt Shaw, Edith, Captain Lennox, Frederick. The thought of Frederick knowing—of him seeing her weakness—hurt deeply. But worse still was the fear of facing Mr. Thornton again. Not just shame, but humiliation. She could not bear the idea of meeting his eyes, knowing he saw her as dishonest.

And yet—she longed to see him. To know where she stood. To face the truth.

Her cheeks burned as she recalled their early conversations. How proudly she had spoken of trade, condemning it for deceit—passing off inferior goods, pretending wealth one didn’t possess. She remembered his calm reply: that in the long run, dishonourable acts always failed. That success built on lies was folly. That honesty was not merely a moral ideal, but a practical necessity.

She had challenged him once—asking if buying cheap and selling dear wasn’t itself a failure of justice. She used the word *chivalric*. Her father corrected her: “Christian.” And she had sat back, feeling a flicker of superiority.

No more. Now, she would feel humbled. Disgraced. And she knew it.

When the doorbell rang, her heart leapt. Then sank. Again and again. She waited—hoping, dreading. Her father expected Mr. Thornton. He had looked forward to their promised meeting, to continuing their talk about society, religion, labor, and the meaning of justice. He had been restless all evening, certain the visitor would come.

“I quite expected to see Mr. Thornton,” he said. “Perhaps the messenger forgot a note last night. Has anything arrived today?”

“I’ll check, Papa,” Margaret said. Then—“Stay! There’s a ring!”

She sat down quickly, pretending to work. A step sounded on the stairs—only one. Dixon.

“Sarvant, sir!” Dixon announced. “He wants to see you—or Miss Hale. Or maybe Miss Hale first, then you. He’s in a queer state.”

“Let him come up,” said Mr. Hale. “We’ll both be here. He can choose.”

“Oh, very well,” Dixon sighed. “I don’t care to hear what he has to say. If you could see his shoes, you’d say the kitchen was better suited.”

“He can wipe them,” said Mr. Hale.

Dixon went to fetch him. But when Higgins reached the bottom stair, he paused. He took off his muddy boots, walked up barefoot, and entered silently.

“Sarvant, sir,” he said, smoothing his hair. “If hoo’l excuse me (looking at Margaret) for being i’ my stockings—I’ve been tramping all day. Streets aren’t clean.”

Margaret noticed his fatigue, his unusual quietness. He seemed unsure of himself.

Mr. Hale, always kind to those who hesitated, offered tea. “You’re tired, I’m sure. Margaret, dear, can’t you hasten it?”

She did—though it meant taking over from Dixon, who was still grieving for her former mistress and now irritable. But Martha, like others drawn to Margaret, willingly helped. Soon, even Dixon softened.

“Why master and you must always bring the lower classes up-stairs since we came to Milton, I cannot understand,” she muttered. “At Helstone, folk weren’t brought higher than the kitchen. I’ve told a few they might count it an honour just to be there.”

Higgins found it easier to speak alone. After Margaret left, he closed the door, stepped close to Mr. Hale.

“Master,” he began, “yo’d not guess what I’ve been doing today. Especially if yo’d heard me yesterday. I’ve been seeking work. I have.”

He paused. “I said to myself, I’d keep a civil tongue in my head, no matter what. I’d set my teeth into my tongue before speaking in haste. For that man’s sake—yo’ understand.”

“No,” said Mr. Hale, baffled. “Who is ‘that man’?”

“That chap as lies theer,” Higgins said, jerking his thumb. “Him as drowned himself. Poor chap! I never thought he’d let the water take him so still. Boucher, yo’ know.”

“Yes,” said Mr Hale. “Go on.”

“For his sake. Yet not for his sake—he’s gone, wherever he is. But for the wife’s sake. For the bits o’ childer.”

“God bless you,” Mr. Hale breathed, rising. Then, calming himself: “What do you mean? Tell me plainly.”

“I’ve told yo’,” Higgins said, surprised by his emotion. “I wouldn’t ask for work for myself. But them’s left as a charge on me. I reckon I should’ve guided Boucher better. I led him off the road—and so I must answer for it.”

Mr. Hale seized his hand, gripping it tightly. Higgins looked embarrassed, ashamed.

“Theer, theer,” he stammered. “There’s ne’er a man among us who wouldn’t do the same—ay, better. But I’ve got no work. None. I told Hamper I’d be good for his mill—better than any. But he wouldn’t have me. Nor any other. I’m a poor black feckless sheep. Childer may clem for ought I can do—unless, parson, yo’d help me?”

“Help you?” Mr. Hale asked. “How? I would do anything—but what can I do?”

“Miss there”—Margaret had returned—“has often talked o’ the South. The ways down there. I dunnot know how far it is, but I’ve been thinking—if I could get ’em down theer, where food’s cheap and wages good, and folks rich and poor, master and man, friendly like—yo’ could help me find work. I’m not forty-five. I’ve strength in me, measter.”

“What kind of work could you do?” asked Mr. Hale.

“Well, I reckon I could spade a bit—”

“And for that,” Margaret interrupted, stepping forward, “you might earn nine shillings a week—maybe ten. Food’s much the same. You might have a garden.”

“The childer could work at that,” he said.

“I’m sick o’ Milton anyways,” he added. “Milton is sick o’ me.”

“You must not go south,” Margaret said firmly. “You could not endure it. You’d be out in all weather. It would kill you with rheumatism. The work—so hard, so monotonous—would break you down. The food is different. You’re used to butcher’s meat once a day. Pay for that out of ten shillings? Keep those children? I owe it to you—to tell you plainly. You’d hate the dullness. Those who live there all their lives are used to it—they soak in stagnant waters. They labour endlessly in vast, steaming fields—never speaking, heads bent low. The work robs their minds. Their imaginations die. They don’t care to talk after work—just want food and sleep. You could never stir them into companionship—something you have in towns as naturally as air. And you, of all men, could not stand such isolation. What is peace to them would be torment to you. Don’t think of it, Nicholas. Besides—you could never afford to move your family. That’s one good thing.”

“I’ve reckoned that,” he said. “One house would do. Furniture from the other would help. Men there have families—six or seven children. God help ’em.”

He fell silent, suddenly convinced. The dream dissolved.

“God help ’em,” he repeated. “North and South—each has its own troubles. Where wages are high, work is scarce. Where work is steady, wages starve. The world’s in confusion—beyond understanding. It needs fixing. But who’s to fix it, if all we see is what we see?”

Mr. Hale cut bread and butter. Margaret was glad—he kept the conversation light. Higgins ate mechanically, barely aware. Then, slowly, he pushed his chair back.

Suddenly, Margaret said—her voice trembling slightly—“Higgins, have you been to Marlborough Mills to seek work?”

“Thornton’s?” he asked.

“Ay, I’ve been at Thornton’s.”

“And what did he say?”

“Such a chap as me isn’t likely to see the master. Th’ overlooker bid me go and be d——d.”

“I wish you had seen Mr. Thornton,” said Mr. Hale. “He might not have given you work—but he wouldn’t have spoken like that.”

“As to language,” Higgins said, “I’m welly used to it. Doesn’t matter. But the fact—that I were na wanted—no more than anywhere else—that’s what hurt.”

“I wish you had seen Mr. Thornton,” Margaret repeated.

“Would you go again?” she asked. “It’s a lot to ask—but would you try tomorrow? I’d be so glad if you would.”

“I’m afraid it would be no use,” said Mr. Hale softly.

“It would be better if I spoke to him,” he added.

Margaret looked at Higgins. His grave, soft eyes were hard to resist.

He sighed deeply. “It would tax my pride. If it were for myself, I could bear a deal. I’d sooner knock him down than beg. I’d sooner be flogged. But yo’re not a common wench—asking pardon. Yo’r ways are different. I’ll e’en make a wry face and go at it tomorrow. Dunna yo’ think he’ll do it. That man has it in him to be burnt at the stake afore he’ll give in. I’ll do it for yo’r sake, Miss Hale. First time in my life I’ve given way to a woman. Neither my wife nor Bess could ever say that.”

“All the more do I thank you,” Margaret smiled.

“Though I don’t believe you,” she added. “I believe you’ve given way to wife and daughter as much as most men.”

“And as to Mr. Thornton,” said Mr. Hale, “I’ll give you a note. I think I may venture to say it will ensure you a hearing.”

“Thank yo’ kindly, sir,” Higgins said. “But I’d as lief stand on my own bottom. I dunnot stomach the notion of having favour curried for me by one who doesn’t know the ins and outs. Meddling ’twixt master and man is liker meddling ’twixt husband and wife than aught else. It takes wisdom to do good. I’ll stand guard at the lodge door—from six in the morning till I get speech. But I’d liefer sweep th’ streets, if paupers hadn’t taken that work. Dunna yo’ hope, miss. There’ll be more chance o’ getting milk out of a flint. I wish yo’ a very good night, and many thanks.”

“You’ll find your shoes by the kitchen fire,” Margaret said. “I took them to dry.”

He turned, looked at her steadily, brushed his hand across his eyes, and left.

“How proud that man is!” said her father, annoyed by Higgins’s refusal of help.

“He is,” Margaret said. “But what grand makings of a man there are in him—pride and all.”

“It’s amusing to see how he respects the part of Mr. Thornton’s character that’s like his own.”

“There’s granite in all these northern people, papa, is there not?”

“There was none in poor Boucher,” he said. “None in his wife.”

“I should guess by their tones they had Irish blood. I wonder what success he’ll have tomorrow. If he and Mr. Thornton could speak as man to man—if Higgins would forget Mr. Thornton was a master, and speak to him as he does to us—and if Mr. Thornton would listen with his human heart, not his master’s ears—”

“You are beginning to do Mr. Thornton justice at last, Margaret,” said her father, pinching her ear.

Margaret felt a strange choking in her chest. She couldn’t speak.

“Oh,” she thought, “if only I were a man. Then I could go to him, force him to express his disapproval, and tell him honestly that I deserved it. It seems cruel to lose him as a friend—just when I began to value him. How tender he was with dear mamma! If only for her sake, I wish he would come. Then I would know how much I am abased in his eyes.”

“Then proudly, proudly up she rose, Tho’ the tear was in her e’e, Whate’er ye say, think what ye may, Ye’s get na word frae me!” SCOTCH BALLAD.

Mr. Thornton could not shake off the belief that Margaret had lied to him—though he thought it was only because of that lie that she had fallen so deeply from his regard. But the falsehood itself carried with it, in his mind, a shadow of something more: a secret attachment, an unseen lover. He could not forget the look she had exchanged with another man—the quiet intimacy, the closeness that seemed almost tender. That image haunted him, sharp and vivid, no matter where he went or what he did. And then there was the time—dusk falling—and the place—so far from home, so seldom trodden. At first, his better nature had argued that such a walk might have been innocent, even accidental. But once he allowed her the right to love, as she plainly had refused his own, why should he deny her that? She had spoken clearly enough when she turned away his affection.
