So if she loved another, she might well have lingered longer than intended, drawn by emotion into silence and solitude. Yet the lie—oh, the lie!—that spoke of guilt, of shame hidden beneath the surface. It was unlike her. And yet, in this very unlikeness, he found proof of how deeply she was bound to someone else—a man slight and elegant, refined and handsome—while he himself was coarse, stern, and built like a man who lived by force and will. The jealousy burned within him, fierce and relentless. He remembered that look—that moment of softness and surrender—how he would have laid down his life for such glances, such gentle attention. Now he saw how she looked when truly loved, and it mocked him. He recalled her words, sharp and cold: “There was not a man in all that crowd for whom she would not have done as much, far more readily than for him.” He shared her desire to spare bloodshed, yes—but this other man, this hidden one, shared nothing. He held her gaze, her words, her hand, her secrets—all to himself.

Mr. Thornton knew he had never been so irritable in his life. He felt the urge to snap at every question, to reply in short, clipped tones like a growl. This awareness stung his pride—he had always prided himself on self-control. Yet control he would keep. So his manner remained calm, measured; but beneath it, his spirit was harder, colder than ever before. He grew quieter at home, spending evenings pacing back and forth across the room, a habit that would have annoyed his mother greatly if anyone else had shown it. It did little to soften her temper toward him, even though he was her beloved son.

“Can you stop—can you sit down for a moment?” she asked, stepping forward. “I have something to say to you—if you’d only cease that endless walking.”

He sat immediately, on a chair against the wall.

“I want to speak about Betsy. She says she must leave us—her lover’s death has broken her spirits, and she can’t give her heart to her work.”

“Very well. I suppose we can find another cook.”

“That’s just like a man,” she replied. “It’s not just the cooking—it’s that she knows every corner of this house. Besides, she told me something about your friend Miss Hale.”

“Miss Hale is no friend of mine. Mr. Hale is my friend.”

“I’m glad to hear you say that. For if she were your friend, what Betsy says would have troubled you.”

“Let me hear it,” he said, his voice quiet, subdued—as it had been for days now.

“Betsy says that the night Leonards was last seen at the station—when he was last on duty—Miss Hale was there, walking with a young man. Bessy believes he killed Leonards with a blow or a push.”

“Leonards was not killed by a blow or push.”

“How do you know?”

“Because I asked the surgeon at the Infirmary. He told me Leonards had suffered from a long-standing internal illness caused by heavy drinking. The rapid decline while intoxicated confirmed that the final attack was due to alcohol, not the fall.”

“The fall? What fall?”

“The fall caused by the blow or push Betsy mentions.”

“So there *was* a blow or push?”

“I believe so.”

“And who did it?”

“There was no inquest, because of the doctor’s opinion. So I cannot say.”

“But Miss Hale was there?”

No answer.

“And with a young man?”

Still no answer. Then, after a pause: “Mother, there was no inquest—no inquiry. No judicial one, I mean.”

“Betsy says Woolmer—some man she knows, working at a grocer’s shop in Crampton—can swear Miss Hale was at the station at that hour, walking with a young man.”

“I don’t see what concern it is of ours. Miss Hale is free to act as she chooses.”

“I’m glad you say that,” Mrs. Thornton replied eagerly. “It matters little to us—not at all to you, after what has passed! But I—I made a promise to Mrs. Hale. I vowed I would not let her daughter go astray without advising her. I shall certainly tell her what I think of such behavior.”

“I do not see any harm in what she did that evening,” said Mr. Thornton, rising and stepping near his mother. He stood by the fireplace, his face turned away from her.

“You wouldn’t have approved of Fanny being seen out after dark, in such a lonely spot, walking with a young man. I say nothing of the taste in choosing that time—when her mother lay unburied—to take such a walk. Would you have liked your sister noticed by a grocer’s assistant doing so?”

“In the first place,” he said, “since I myself was once a draper’s assistant, the fact that a grocer’s clerk sees something doesn’t change its nature. And secondly, I see a great difference between Miss Hale and Fanny. I can imagine Miss Hale has reasons—weighty ones—that make her overlook appearances. I never knew Fanny to have weighty reasons for anything. Others must protect her. I believe Miss Hale protects herself.”

“A fine character for your sister, indeed! Really, John, one would have thought Miss Hale had done enough to make you clear-sighted. She led you on to propose, with a show of interest—only to play you off against this young man, I suspect. Her whole conduct is plain to me now. You believe he is her lover, I suppose—you agree to that.”

He turned sharply to face her. His face was pale and grim. “Yes, mother. I do believe he is her lover.” Then he turned away again, twisting as though in pain. He leaned his head into his hand. Before she could speak, he turned back:

“Mother. He is her lover, whoever he is. But she may need help—womanly counsel. There may be difficulties or temptations I don’t know. I fear there are. I don’t want to know what they are. But since you’ve always been a good—yes, a kind—mother to me, go to her. Win her confidence. Tell her what must be done. I know something is wrong—some dread, some agony she carries. It must be torture.”

“For God’s sake, John!” she said, genuinely shocked. “What do you mean? What do you know?”

He did not answer.

“John! I don’t know what to think unless you speak. You have no right to say what you’ve said about her.”

“Not against her, mother! I *could* not speak against her.”

“Well, then, you have no right to say half-truths unless you finish them. These half-expressions destroy a woman’s reputation.”

“Her reputation!” he snapped, turning to face her, eyes blazing. “You dare not—” He straightened himself, composed, dignified. “I will say no more than this—what I say is simple truth, and I am sure you believe me. I have reason to believe Miss Hale is in some difficulty connected with an attachment. From what I know of her character, that attachment is innocent and proper. What my reason is, I refuse to say. But never let me hear anyone suggest anything worse than that she needs the guidance of a kind, gentle woman. You promised Mrs. Hale to be that woman!”

“No,” said Mrs. Thornton firmly. “I did not promise kindness. I felt at the time I might not be able to offer it to someone like Miss Hale. I promised counsel—advice, as I would give to my own daughter. I shall speak to her as I would to Fanny if she had gone gallivanting with a young man at dusk. I’ll speak based on what I know, without being swayed by the ‘strong reasons’ you won’t share. Then I’ll have kept my word—and done my duty.”

“She’ll never bear it,” he said, passionately.

“She will have to, if I speak in her mother’s name.”

“Well,” he broke away, “don’t tell me more. I can’t endure thinking of it. It will be better for you to speak to her—any way—than not to speak at all.” He muttered under his breath, “Oh, that look of love! And that cursed lie—showing some terrible shame behind it, hidden from the light in which I thought she lived! Oh, Margaret, Margaret! Mother, how you’ve tortured me! Oh, Margaret—couldn’t you have loved me? I am rough and hard, but I would never have led you into a lie.”

Mrs. Thornton reflected on her son’s words—his plea for mercy toward Margaret—and the more she thought, the more bitter she became. She took pleasure in imagining herself speaking plainly to her, fulfilling her duty with no hesitation. She relished the idea of showing herself untouched by the charm Margaret cast over others. She sneered at the picture of her beauty—black hair, fair skin, bright eyes—thinking they would do nothing to save her from the stern rebuke she had already rehearsed in her mind.

“Is Miss Hale within?” she asked, knowing full well she was. She had seen her at the window and was already inside the hall before Martha could answer.

Margaret sat alone, writing to Edith, recounting details of her mother’s final days. The task softened her heart, and she brushed away tears as Mrs. Thornton was announced.

She greeted her visitor with gentle grace, so ladylike that Mrs. Thornton felt momentarily disarmed. The speech she had prepared seemed ill-suited to this soft presence. Margaret’s voice was low and rich, softer than usual—because in her heart she was grateful for the visit. She made efforts to engage in conversation, praised Martha, the servant Mrs. Thornton had found for them, and mentioned asking Edith for a Greek tune she had discussed with Miss Thornton. Mrs. Thornton was thrown off balance. Her sharp tongue felt misplaced among roses. She remained silent, gathering courage. Then, a suspicion flickered—perhaps this sweetness was merely a ploy to win back Mr. Thornton, now that the other man had failed. Poor Margaret—there may have been truth in that: Mrs. Thornton was the mother of the man whose regard she valued, and feared losing. This thought, unconsciously, added to her desire to please.

Mrs. Thornton rose to leave, but paused. She cleared her throat.

“Miss Hale, I have a duty to perform. Your mother asked me to advise you if you acted wrongly—or inadvertently—without warning. I promised I would offer counsel, whether you accepted it or not.”

Margaret stood before her, blushing, eyes wide. She thought Mrs. Thornton had come to speak of the lie—perhaps Mr. Thornton had sent her to explain the danger of being caught in falsehood. Though her heart sank that he hadn’t come himself, she was humbled enough to accept blame patiently.

Mrs. Thornton continued:
“At first, when I heard from a servant that you were seen walking with a gentleman at Outwood station, so late, I hardly believed it. But my son confirmed it. It was indiscreet, to say the least. Many a young woman has lost her reputation through such actions—”

Margaret’s eyes flashed fire. This was new—this was insult. If Mrs. Thornton had spoken of the lie, she would have confessed, humbled herself. But to question her conduct, to touch on her character—by a stranger! It was unbearable. She said nothing.

Mrs. Thornton saw the defiance in her eyes—and it stirred her own.

“For your mother’s sake, I warn you. Such improprieties will degrade you in the world’s eyes—even if they don’t lead to ruin.”

“For my mother’s sake,” Margaret said, voice trembling, “I will endure much—but not this. She never meant me to be insulted.”

“Insult, Miss Hale?”

“Yes, madam,” she said steadily. “It is insult. What do you know of me to suspect—Oh!” she broke down, covering her face with her hands. “Now I understand—Mr. Thornton has told you—”

“No, Miss Hale,” Mrs. Thornton interrupted, stopping her confession. “He has told me nothing. You do not know my son. You are not worthy to know him. He said only this: ‘Go to her. I have good reason to believe she is in some strait arising from an attachment. She needs womanly counsel.’ I believe those were his exact words. Beyond confirming you were at the station with a gentleman on the twenty-sixth, he said nothing—nothing against you. If he knows anything that makes you weep, he keeps it to himself.”

Margaret’s face remained hidden, fingers wet with tears. Mrs. Thornton softened slightly.

“Come, Miss Hale. There may be circumstances—explained—that would lessen the appearance of impropriety.”

No answer. Margaret weighed her words. She wished to stand well with Mrs. Thornton—but she could not explain.

Mrs. Thornton grew impatient.
“I shall regret ending our acquaintance. But for Fanny’s sake—had she done this, we’d consider it a disgrace. Fanny might be led astray—”

“I can give no explanation,” Margaret said quietly. “I have done wrong—but not in the way you think. I believe Mr. Thornton judges me more mercifully than you—” She fought to hold back her sobs. “But I believe, madam, you mean to do what is right.”

“Thank you,” Mrs. Thornton said, lifting her chin. “I wasn’t aware my intentions were doubted. This will be the last time I interfere. I didn’t want to when your mother asked. I disapproved of my son’s feelings for you—when I only suspected them. You didn’t seem worthy of him. But when you compromised yourself during the riot, exposed to servants’ talk—then I felt it was no longer right to oppose his wish to propose—though he denied wanting to until that day.”

Margaret flinched, drawing in her breath sharply. Mrs. Thornton ignored it.

“He came. You seemed to have changed your mind. Yesterday I told him it was possible, in such a short time, you might have learned something of this other lover—”

“What must you think of me, madam?” Margaret asked, throwing her head back with proud disdain, her throat curving like a swan’s. “You can say nothing more. I refuse to justify myself. I ask you to allow me to leave.”

And she swept from the room with silent, regal grace. Mrs. Thornton, possessing enough humor, couldn’t help but see the absurdity of her situation. She left, not annoyed—she cared too little for Margaret to feel that. But she was pleased. Margaret’s passion had proven the effect of her words. “My young lady,” she thought, “you’ve a fine temper. If John and you had married, he’d have needed a tight rein to keep you in place. But I doubt you’ll walk with your beau again at dusk. You’ve too much pride. I like a girl who flies at being talked about. It shows she’s not giddy. That girl might be bold—but never giddy. I’ll give her that. As for Fanny—she’d be giddy, not bold. No courage in her, poor thing.”

Mr. Thornton spent his morning less productively than his mother. While she fulfilled her purpose, he wrestled with the damage the strike had wrought. Much of his capital was tied up in expensive new machinery. He had bought large quantities of cotton for major orders—orders now delayed beyond measure. Even with his skilled workforce, fulfilling them would be difficult. With the Irish laborers—untrained, slow—every day brought fresh frustration.

It was not a good time for Higgins to approach him. But he had promised Margaret he would try. So, despite growing irritation, pride, and sullenness, he stood leaning against the dead wall, shifting from foot to foot, hour after hour. Finally, the latch clicked, and Mr. Thornton emerged.

“I want to speak to you, sir.”

“Can’t stay now, my man. I’m too late as it is.”

“Well, sir, I reckon I can wait till you come back.”

Mr. Thornton walked halfway down the street. Higgins sighed. But there was no choice. To catch him in the street was his only chance. If he’d gone to the lodge or the house, he’d have been sent to the overlooker. So he waited, nodding curtly to familiar faces as the millhands streamed out for dinner, scowling at the newly arrived Irish workers.

At last, Mr. Thornton returned.

“What! You still here?”

“Ay, sir. I must speak to you.”

“Come in then. Wait—we’ll cross the yard. The men aren’t back. We’ll have it to ourselves. These good people,” he said, closing the lodge door, “are at dinner.”

He paused to speak to the overlooker, who whispered:
“I suppose you know, sir, that man is Higgins—one of the Union leaders. The one who gave that speech in Hurstfield.”

“No, I didn’t,” Mr. Thornton said, turning sharply. He recognized Higgins by name—known as a troublemaker.

“Come along,” he said, tone rougher. “Men like this,” he thought, “who disrupt commerce and harm their own town—mere demagogues, craving power at others’ expense.”

“Well, sir,” Mr. Thornton said, facing him in the counting-house, “what do you want?”

“My name is Higgins—”

“I know that,” he cut in. “What do you want, Mr. Higgins?”

“I want work.”

“Work! You’re a bold fellow to ask me for work. You don’t lack impudence, that’s clear.”

“I’ve got enemies and backbiters, like my betters,” Higgins replied, roused by the tone. “But I never heard one of them called over-modest.”

Mr. Thornton saw a letter on the table. He read it through. Then he looked up.

“What are you waiting for?”

“An answer to my question.”

“I gave it. Don’t waste more of your time.”

“You remarked on my impudence,” Higgins said. “But I was taught it’s manners to answer ‘yes’ or ‘no’ to a civil question. I’d be grateful if you’d give me work. Hamper can vouch for me.”

“I’ve a notion you’d better not send me to Hamper for a character, my man. I might hear things you’d prefer I didn’t.”

“I’d take the risk. The worst they could say is that I did what I thought best—even if it hurt me.”

Mr. Thornton stared at him, unreadable.

“Nay, I have done; you get no more of me;
And I am glad, yea glad with all my heart,
That thus so clearly I myself am free.”

Margaret shut herself into her room after leaving Mrs. Thornton. She paced back and forth, as she often did when agitated—but then paused, remembering how thin the walls were in this house, where every footstep echoed through the halls. She sat down instead, waiting until she heard Mrs. Thornton’s footsteps fade away. Then, slowly, she retraced their conversation, word by word, sentence by sentence, forcing her mind to recall each exchange with precision.

When she had finished, she rose and whispered aloud, her voice low and heavy with sorrow: “At least her words do not touch me. They fall away from me, like dust. I am innocent of the motives she accuses me of. But still—it is painful to think that any woman could believe such things about another so readily. It is hard, and sad. Where I’ve truly erred, she doesn’t accuse me—she doesn’t even know. He never told her. I should have known he wouldn’t.”

She lifted her chin, a quiet pride stirring within her at the thought of Mr. Thornton’s restraint, his refusal to speak ill of her. Then, suddenly, a new idea struck her—sharp and startling—and she pressed her hands together tightly. “He must think poor Frederick is some lover,” she murmured, flushing at the very word. “I see it now. It’s not only that he knows I lied—he believes someone else cares for me. And that I—Oh dear! Oh dear! What shall I do? What does it mean? Why should I care what he thinks, beyond losing his respect for my honesty? I cannot say. But I am miserable. How unhappy this year has been! I feel as if I’ve passed from childhood straight into old age. No youth, no womanhood—no future. The hopes of womanhood are closed to me. I shall never marry. Yet I carry the weight of sorrows as though I were already an old woman, weeping over a life that was never mine.”

She sighed, weary from the constant demand upon her strength. “I can bear up for Papa—that is duty, natural and pious. I could even resent Mrs. Thornton’s unjust suspicions. But it is unbearable to think how completely he misunderstands me. What has made me so sensitive today? I don’t know. Only I cannot help it. I must give way sometimes. No—I won’t,” she said, rising abruptly. “I will not. I will not dwell on myself or my position. I will not examine my feelings. It would serve no purpose. One day, perhaps, if I live to be old, I may sit by the fire, watching the embers, and see the life I might have lived.”

All the while, she was dressing quickly, preparing to go out. She stopped only to wipe her eyes, annoyed at the tears that kept coming despite her resolve. “There must be many women who make mistakes like mine—and only realize too late. And how proudly I spoke to him that day! But I didn’t know then. It crept upon me little by little. I don’t even know when it began. Now I won’t give way. I’ll find it hard to act naturally toward him, knowing this terrible truth; but I will be calm, quiet, and speak little. Still—perhaps I won’t see him at all. He avoids us plainly. That would be worse than anything. And yet—no wonder he stays away. If he believes what he must about me…”

She stepped outside, walking swiftly toward the countryside, trying to outrun her thoughts with the speed of her steps.

When she returned, her father met her at the door. “Good girl!” he said. “You’ve been to Mrs. Boucher’s. I meant to go myself before dinner.”

“No, Papa,” Margaret replied, blushing. “I haven’t. I didn’t think of her. But I’ll go right after dinner—while you’re napping.”

She kept her promise. Mrs. Boucher was seriously ill—not merely unwell, but near death. The kind neighbor who had visited earlier seemed to have taken charge. Some of the children had been sent to relatives. Mary Higgins had come to collect the youngest at lunchtime, and Nicholas had gone for the doctor. He had not returned. Mrs. Boucher was dying, and there was nothing left to do but wait.

Margaret decided she would like to hear the doctor’s opinion, but in the meantime, she thought she might visit the Higginses. Perhaps she could learn whether Nicholas had spoken with Mr. Thornton.

She found Nicholas occupied with spinning a penny on the dresser, amusing three small children who clung to him fearlessly. Both he and the children smiled at a long spin. Margaret noted the warmth in his face—the simple joy of the moment—and saw it as a good sign.

When the penny stopped, one child began to cry. “Come to me,” Margaret said, lifting the boy into her arms. She held her watch to his ear as she asked Nicholas, “Have you seen Mr. Thornton?”

His expression changed instantly. “Ay,” he said. “I’ve seen and heard enough of him.”

“He refused you?” Margaret asked, saddened.

“To be sure. I knew he would. There’s no mercy to expect from masters like him. You’re a stranger, a foreigner—you don’t know their ways. But I did.”

“I’m sorry I asked you to go. Was he angry? Did he speak to you like Hamper did?”

“He wasn’t over-civil,” Nicholas said, spinning the penny again, more for himself than the children. “Never you fret. I’m where I was. I’ll tramp tomorrow. I gave him as good as I got. I told him I wouldn’t hold him in high regard if I’d come a second time of my own accord. But you advised me, and I was obliged to you.”

“You told him I sent you?”

“I don’t know if I called you by name. Probably not. I said a woman who didn’t know better had urged me to come and see if there was a soft place in his heart.”

“And he—?”

“Said I should tell you to mind your own business. That’s the longest spin yet, lads. And those were civil words compared to what he said to me. But never mind. We’re where we were. I’ll break stones on the road before I let these little ones starve.”

Margaret gently placed the struggling boy back on the dresser. “I’m sorry I asked you to go. I’m disappointed in him.”

A sudden sound came from behind her. Both she and Nicholas turned—Mr. Thornton stood there, his face showing surprise and displeasure.

Without hesitation, Margaret stepped forward, bowing low to hide the pallor that swept over her. He bowed in return, then closed the door behind her.

As she hurried toward Mrs. Boucher’s, she heard the sharp clang of the latch—and it felt like the final blow to her humiliation. He too was disturbed to find her there.

He had tenderness in his heart—a soft place, as Nicholas had called it—but he guarded it fiercely. It was sacred to him, hidden from the world. Yet he also valued justice above all. And now, he realized, he had been unjust. A man had waited five hours—humble, patient, desperate—to speak to him. He had not given him five hours, but he had spent two of his own precious hours investigating the truth of Higgins’s story. He had learned that everything Higgins said was true. And then, something deeper stirred—the patience, the selfless concern for others. He forgot reason, logic, fairness. He was moved by instinct.

He came to offer Higgins work. But finding Margaret there was worse than hearing her last words. For then he understood—she was the woman who had urged Higgins to come. And he feared admitting that her influence had shaped his decision. He had acted out of justice alone. He could not let her think otherwise.

“So that was the lady you called a woman?” he asked, sharply, turning to Higgins.

“You might have told me who she was.”

“And then maybe you’d have spoken of her more kindly. You might have had a mother to keep your tongue in check when talking about women being the root of all plagues.”

“Of course you told Miss Hale?”

“In course I did. Or at least I reckon I did. I told her not to meddle again in anything concerning you.”

“Whose children are those—yours?”

Mr. Thornton already guessed. But he hesitated, awkwardly shifting the conversation.

“They’re not mine, and they are mine.”

“The children you mentioned this morning?”

“When you said,” Higgins replied, turning with barely concealed anger, “that my story might be true, or might not, but it was a very unlikely one. Measter, I haven’t forgotten.”

Thornton was silent for a moment. Then he said, “Neither have I. I remember what I said. I spoke to you about those children in a way I had no right to. I didn’t believe you. I couldn’t have cared for another man’s children if he had treated me as Boucher treated you. But I know now you spoke truth. I beg your pardon.”

Higgins did not turn. But when he finally spoke, his voice softened, though the words remained rough. “You’ve no business prying into what happened between Boucher and me. He’s dead, and I’m sorry. That’s enough.”

“So it is. Will you take work with me? That’s why I came.”

Higgins wavered, then steadied. He refused to answer.

Thornton did not press. His gaze fell on the children.

“You called me impudent, a liar, a mischief-maker. You might have said, with some truth, that I’ve been given to drink. And I’ve called you a tyrant, a bull dog, a cruel master. That’s where it stands. But for the children, Measter, do you think we can ever get on together?”

“Well,” Thornton said, half-laughing, “it wasn’t my proposal that we go together. But there’s one comfort—on your own admission, neither of us can think much worse of the other than we do now.”

“That’s true,” Higgins said, thoughtful. “I’ve been thinking since I saw you. It’s a mercy you didn’t take me on. I never saw a man I could less abide. But maybe that was hasty judgment. Work’s work for such as me. So, Measter, I’ll come. And more than that—I thank you. That’s a deal from me,” he added, turning fully to face Thornton for the first time.

“And this is a deal from me,” Thornton said, gripping Higgins’s hand firmly. “Now, mind you come sharp to your time. I’ll have no laggards at my mill. Fines are strictly enforced. And the first time I catch you making mischief, off you go. Now you know where you stand.”

“You spoke of my wisdom this morning. I reckon I may bring it with me. Or would you rather have me ‘bout my brains?”

“‘Bout your brains—if you use them for meddling with my business. With your brains—if you keep to your own.”

“I’ll need a lot of brains to know where my business ends and yours begins.”

“Your business hasn’t begun yet. Mine stands still for me. Good afternoon.”

Just before Thornton reached Mrs. Boucher’s door, Margaret emerged. She didn’t see him. He followed her for several yards, admiring her light step and graceful figure. Then, suddenly, the admiration curdled into jealousy. He wanted to overtake her, to speak to her, to see how she would react now that she knew he was aware of another attachment. He wanted her to know he had reversed his decision, that he had repented his harshness—and that he had done it because of her advice.

He caught up to her. She started.

“Miss Hale,” he said, “you were somewhat premature in expressing your disappointment. I have taken Higgins on.”

“I am glad of it,” she replied coldly.

“He tells me he repeated to you what I said this morning about—” Thornton hesitated.

Margaret took up the thread. “About women not meddling. You had every right to express your opinion. It was perfectly correct, I’m sure. But”—she spoke a little more urgently—“Higgins did not quite tell you the exact truth.”

The word “truth” brought her own deception rushing back. She stopped, uncomfortable.

Thornton was puzzled at first—then remembered her lie, and everything that followed.

“The exact truth?” he said. “Very few people speak it. I’ve given up hoping for it. Miss Hale, do you have no explanation to give me? You must see what I cannot help but think.”

Margaret remained silent. She wondered if any explanation would betray Frederick.

“No,” he said, “I will ask no further. I may be tempting you. For now, believe me—your secret is safe with me. But you risk much by being so indiscreet. I speak only as a friend of your father’s. If I had any other hope or thought—then, of course, it is at an end. I am entirely disinterested.”

“I am aware of that,” she said, forcing her voice to sound indifferent. “I know what I must seem to you. But the secret belongs to another. I cannot explain without harming him.”

“I have no desire to pry into the gentleman’s affairs,” he said, growing angry. “My interest in you is purely as a friend. You may not believe me, Miss Hale—but it is true. Even though I once threatened persecution—that is all past. All over. Do you believe me?”

“Yes,” she said quietly, sadly.

“Then, really, I don’t see any reason to walk together. I thought you might have something to say—but I see we are nothing to each other. If you’re convinced that any foolish passion on my part is entirely over, I wish you good afternoon.”

He walked off quickly.

“What could he mean?” Margaret thought. “Why speak as if I believed he cared for me—when I know he doesn’t? He cannot. His mother must have told him those cruel things. But I won’t care for him. I am mistress of myself. I must control this strange, wild feeling that nearly made me betray Frederick—just to regain his approval. Come, little heart—be brave. We’ll be a great deal to one another, if we are cast adrift and left alone.”

Her father was startled by her cheerfulness that morning. She talked incessantly, pushing her humor to an unusual pitch. Though some of her remarks carried a bitter edge, and her stories of Harley Street were tinged with sarcasm, he refrained from checking her—glad to see her shake off her burdens.

Later, she was summoned to speak with Mary Higgins. When she returned, her father thought he saw traces of tears on her cheeks. But no—she brought good news: Higgins had secured work at Mr. Thornton’s mill.

Her spirits sank, however. She found it difficult to keep up the pretense, let alone the liveliness she had shown earlier.

For days, her mood fluctuated wildly. Her father began to worry—until news arrived from several sources that promised change.

Mr. Hale received a letter from Mr. Bell, offering to visit. He imagined the company of his old Oxford friend would delight both himself and Margaret.

But Margaret had no interest. She was too listless to care, even if Mr. Bell were twice her godfather.

Then came a letter from Edith—full of sympathy for her aunt’s death, details of her own life, and a hopeful note at the end: the climate didn’t suit the baby, and Mrs. Shaw might return to England. Captain Lennox might sell out. They might move back into the old Harley Street house—though it would seem incomplete without Margaret.

She longed for that house, for its quiet order, its peaceful monotony. Once, she had found it dull. Now, after months of turmoil, she craved stillness—even stagnation—as a form of rest.

So she began to look forward to a long visit with the Lennoxes—not with hope, but with a sense of relief. A time, perhaps, to recover her composure, to regain control.

For now, every subject led back to Mr. Thornton. Whether visiting the Higginses, reading with her father, or hearing of Mr. Bell’s plans—his name always surfaced. Even the new lease negotiations between Bell and Thornton brought him into the conversation.

“I have no wrong, where I can claim no right,
Naught ta’en me fro, where I have nothing had,
Yet of my woe I cannot so be quite;
Namely, since that another may be glad
With that, that thus in sorrow makes me sad.”

Margaret had not expected much joy from Mr. Bell’s visit—only comfort for her father. But when the old scholar arrived, she found herself instantly at ease, as if they had known each other all their lives. He declared she had no merit in being what she was—such a girl after his own heart—because it was simply inherited, a natural gift to walk into his affections and take them by right. She smiled and praised him in return for his youthfulness under the solemn cap and gown of Oxford.

“Fresh and young in warmth and kindness,” she said. “But I must confess, I think your opinions are the oldest and most stubborn I’ve met in a long while.”

“Hear this daughter of yours, Hale!” Mr. Bell laughed. “Her time in Milton has entirely corrupted her. She’s a democrat, a red republican, a member of the Peace Society, a socialist—”

“Papa!” Margaret interrupted gently. “It’s only because I believe in progress—especially in commerce. Mr. Bell would have trade stay still, exchanging acorns for wild-beast skins.”

“No, no,” he replied with a grin. “I’d dig the ground and grow potatoes. I’d shear the beast’s hide and spin it into broadcloth. But don’t exaggerate, missy. Still, I’m tired of this constant rush—everyone trampling over everyone else in their mad race to get rich.”

“It’s not everyone who can sit comfortably in college rooms and watch their wealth grow without lifting a finger,” Mr. Hale observed. “No doubt many men here would give anything if their property increased as steadily as yours has, without effort.”

“I don’t believe they would,” Mr. Bell answered. “They like the struggle. The bustle is what excites them. As for sitting still, learning from the past, or shaping the future through honest work done with foresight—why, pooh! I don’t believe there’s a man in Milton who knows how to sit still. And that, truly, is an art.”

“Then perhaps Milton folk think Oxford men don’t know how to move,” Mr. Hale suggested. “It might be good if they mixed more.”

“It might benefit the Miltoners,” Mr. Bell countered. “Many things could help them that would be very disagreeable to others.”

“Are you not a Milton man yourself?” Margaret asked. “I should have thought you’d be proud of your town.”

“I confess,” he said, “I see little to be proud of. But come to Oxford, Margaret—I’ll show you a place worth glorying in.”

“Well,” Mr. Hale said, “Mr. Thornton is coming to tea tonight. He’s as proud of Milton as you are of Oxford. You two should try to make each other a bit more open-minded.”

“I don’t want to be more liberal-minded, thank you,” Mr. Bell replied.

“Is Mr. Thornton coming to tea, Papa?” Margaret whispered.

“Either to tea or shortly after. He didn’t say exactly. He told us not to wait.”

Mr. Thornton had resolved not to ask his mother whether she had spoken to Margaret about her conduct. He knew well enough that if such a conversation had taken place, her account of it would only wound him—distorting the truth through her own lens. He dreaded hearing her name even mentioned. Though he blamed her, though he resented her, though he rejected her—he still loved her deeply, against his will. He dreamt of her: dancing toward him with arms outstretched, light and carefree, a vision that both repelled and drew him. Yet this image, stripped of her true character—like a body possessed by an evil spirit—had become fixed in his mind. When he woke, he could barely distinguish the real Margaret from the phantom. The loathing he felt for the latter clung to the former, disfiguring her in his thoughts. Still, he was too proud to admit his weakness by avoiding her. He would neither seek nor flee her presence. To prove his self-control, he delayed every task that afternoon, forcing himself into unnatural slowness. It was past eight before he reached the Hales’ house. Then came business with Mr. Bell in the study—talk that lingered far beyond necessity, as Mr. Bell sat by the fire, weary and talkative, while Mr. Thornton grew restless, chafing at the delay. He found Mr. Bell dull, and Mr. Bell, in turn, considered him brusque and cold, lacking both wit and grace.

At last, a faint sound from above signaled the need to move upstairs. They found Margaret seated, a letter open before her, engaged in animated discussion with her father. At their entrance, she quickly closed it—but Mr. Thornton caught fragments of her father’s words.

“A letter from Henry Lennox. It makes Margaret very hopeful.”

Mr. Bell nodded. Margaret flushed crimson when Mr. Thornton looked at her. He nearly rose and fled the room then and there, never to return.

“We were thinking,” Mr. Hale said, “that you and Mr. Thornton had taken Margaret’s advice and were trying to convert each other—you were so long down there.”

“And you thought we’d end up like the Kilkenny cats, with nothing left but our opinions. Whose opinion do you suppose would survive longest?”

Mr. Thornton had no idea what they meant—and refused to ask. Mr. Hale explained.

“This morning, we accused Mr. Bell of a kind of Oxonian medievalism—his prejudice against his own town. Margaret suggested he might benefit from associating with Milton manufacturers.”

“I beg your pardon,” Mr. Bell corrected. “Margaret thought it would do the Milton people good to mix more with Oxford men. Wasn’t that it, Margaret?”

“I believe I thought it would help both sides to know each other better,” she said. “I didn’t realize it was my idea any more than Papa’s.”

“So you see, Mr. Thornton,” Mr. Hale continued, “we should have been improving each other downstairs instead of talking about vanished families of Smiths and Harrisons. Still, I’m willing to do my part now. I wonder when you Milton men intend to live. Your lives seem spent gathering materials for life.”

“By living,” Mr. Thornton said sharply, “you mean enjoyment?”

“Yes,” Mr. Hale replied. “Enjoyment—I won’t specify what, for I trust we both consider mere pleasure poor enjoyment.”

“I’d rather have the nature of the enjoyment defined.”

“Well,” Mr. Hale said, “enjoyment or leisure—the enjoyment of power and influence that money brings. You’re all striving for money. What do you want it for?”

Mr. Thornton remained silent. Then: “I really don’t know. But money isn’t what I strive for.”

“What, then?”

“It’s a personal matter. I’d have to lay myself bare to such a catechist—and I’m not sure I’m ready.”

“No,” Mr. Hale said. “Let’s not be personal. Neither of you is representative. Each is too individual.”

“I’m not sure whether that’s a compliment,” Mr. Bell said. “I’d like to stand for Oxford—its beauty, its learning, its proud history. What do you say, Margaret? Should I be flattered?”

“I don’t know Oxford,” she replied. “But there’s a difference between being a city’s representative and the representative man of its people.”

“Very true,” Mr. Bell said. “Now I recall—you were against me this morning, quite Miltonian and manufacturing in your tastes.”

Margaret saw the sudden look of surprise on Mr. Thornton’s face and winced—fearing the wrong interpretation of Mr. Bell’s words. Mr. Bell went on:

“Oh, I wish I could show you our High Street—our Radcliffe Square. I leave out the colleges, just as I ask Mr. Thornton to omit his factories when speaking of Milton’s charm. I have a right to criticize my birthplace. After all, I am a Milton man.”

Mr. Thornton was more annoyed than he ought to have been. He was not in the mood for jest. At another time, he might have enjoyed Mr. Bell’s half-irritated critique of a town so alien to his ways. But now, he felt provoked—defending something never meant to be attacked.

“I don’t set Milton up as a model.”

“Not in architecture?” Mr. Bell asked, slyly.

“No. We’ve been too busy to care for outward appearances.”

“Don’t say *mere* outward appearances,” Mr. Hale said gently. “They shape us all—from childhood onward, every day of our lives.”

“Wait a moment,” Mr. Thornton said. “We’re of a different race from the Greeks, to whom beauty was everything. Mr. Bell might speak of leisure and serene enjoyment—much of which entered through the senses. I don’t despise them, nor would I imitate them. But I belong to Teutonic blood—though it’s rare in this part of England. We keep much of their language, more of their spirit. We don’t see life as a time for enjoyment, but for action and effort. Our strength and beauty come from overcoming resistance, from conquering greater difficulties. We are Teutonic here in Darkshire in another way—we resist distant laws. We want to govern ourselves, not be governed by flawed legislation. We value self-rule and oppose centralization.”

“In short,” Mr. Bell said, “you’d like the Heptarchy back. Well, at least I retract my earlier claim—that Milton people don’t revere the past. You worship Thor.”

“If we don’t honor the past as you do in Oxford,” Mr. Thornton said, “it’s because we seek something more useful to the present. It’s fine when studying the past leads to prophecy. But for those navigating new circumstances, it would be finer if experience guided us directly—how to act in matters closest to us, full of challenges. How we meet and overcome these—not merely push them aside—shapes our future. Yet people speak easily of Utopia, but rarely of tomorrow’s duty. And when that duty is done by others, they’re quick to cry, ‘Fie, for shame!’”

“All this time,” Mr. Bell said, “I don’t see what you’re talking about. Would you Milton men send your daily troubles to Oxford? You haven’t tried us yet.”

Mr. Thornton laughed outright. “I believe I was referring to recent troubles—our strikes, painful and costly as they’ve been. And yet this latest strike, though it hurts me now, was respectable.”

“A respectable strike!” Mr. Bell exclaimed. “That sounds like deep devotion to Thor.”

Margaret sensed—rather than saw—that Mr. Thornton was wounded by the repeated mockery of what he took seriously. She tried to shift the topic—something trivial, unimportant to one, deeply personal to the other. She forced herself to speak.

“Edith says the printed calicoes in Corfu are better and cheaper than in London.”

“Does she?” Mr. Hale said. “I suspect that’s one of Edith’s exaggerations. Are you sure, Margaret?”

“I’m sure she said so, Papa.”

“Then I accept it as fact,” Mr. Bell declared. “Margaret, I trust your truthfulness so completely, I extend it to your cousin. I don’t believe a cousin of yours could exaggerate.”

“Is Miss Hale so remarkable for honesty?” Mr. Thornton asked, bitterly. The moment the words left his mouth, he regretted them. What was he? Why did he wound her with such cruelty? How ill he was tonight—tormented by delay, irritated by names, angry at the ease with which Mr. Bell joked, mocking a man whose heart was already strained. And yet—what had he said to her?

She did not rise and leave, as she once might have. She sat still, her eyes flashing briefly with hurt—like a child suddenly rebuffed—then softening into quiet sadness. Her gaze fell, and she bent over her sewing, silent. But he could not stop watching her. He saw a shiver pass through her, as if chilled by an unexpected wind. He felt like a mother abruptly called away while rocking her child—before the slow, trusting smile had returned. He gave curt answers, restless, irritable, unable to tell jest from earnest. All he wanted was a glance, a word from her—so he might kneel in penitence. But she offered neither. Her fingers moved swiftly, steadily, as if sewing were the sole purpose of her life.

He thought she couldn’t care for him—or else the fire of his desire would have made her lift her eyes, even for a second, to read his remorse. He would have struck her before leaving—just to earn the right to confess the pain gnawing at his soul. Fortunately, the long walk outside cooled his temper. He returned to resolve: henceforth, he would see her as little as possible—for her face, her voice (like gentle winds of melody) held such power to unbalance him. Yes, he had known love—a fierce, consuming flame. But through it, he would fight his way to calm maturity, richer and deeper for having known such passion.

When he left abruptly, Margaret rose and began folding her work. The seams seemed heavier than usual. Her features softened into lines of weariness. She looked as if she had endured a long, exhausting day.

As they prepared for bed, Mr. Bell muttered, “Never saw a man so spoiled by success. Can’t bear a word, a joke—everything touches his pride. Once, he was noble and simple, like daylight. You couldn’t offend him—no vanity.”

“He’s not vain now,” Margaret said, turning from the table, her voice calm. “Tonight, he wasn’t himself. Something must have troubled him before he came.”

Mr. Bell gave her a sharp look over his spectacles. She met it quietly. After she left, he turned to Mr. Hale.

“Hale—have you ever noticed that Thornton and your daughter share what the French call *tendresse*?”

“Never!” Mr. Hale stammered, startled. “No, I’m certain you’re mistaken. If there’s anything, it’s all on Mr. Thornton’s side. Poor fellow! I hope he doesn’t think of her—because I’m sure she wouldnches him.”

“Well,” Mr. Bell said, “I’m a bachelor, never touched love affairs. So my opinion may be worthless. But I’d say there are very pretty signs in her!”

“Then I’m sure you’re wrong,” Mr. Hale insisted. “He may care for her—though she’s been rude at times. But she? Margaret would never think of him. Such a thing has never crossed her mind.”

“Entering her heart would be enough. I only hinted. I dare say I’m wrong. And whether I am or not, I’m very sleepy. Having disturbed your night with my idle fancies, I’ll retire with peace of mind.”

But Mr. Hale vowed not to let such nonsense disturb him. So he lay awake, determined not to think about it.

The next day, Mr. Bell took his leave, telling Margaret to remember him as one who had a right to help and protect her in any trouble. To Mr. Hale, he said:

“That Margaret of yours has gone deep into my heart. Take care of her—she’s a precious creature, far too good for Milton—really fit only for Oxford. The town, I mean—not the men. I can’t match her yet. When I can, I’ll bring my young man to stand beside your young woman—like the genii in the Arabian Nights bringing Prince Caralmazan to match Princess Badoura.”

“I beg you not to,” Mr. Hale said. “Remember the misfortunes that followed. And besides—I can’t spare Margaret.”

“No,” Mr. Bell said. “On second thought, let’s have her nurse us ten years from now, when we’re two grumpy invalids. Seriously, Hale—I wish you’d leave Milton. It’s unsuitable for you, though I recommended it. If you would, I’d forget my doubts and take a college living. You and Margaret could come live at the parsonage—you as a sort of lay curate, handling the unwashed; she as our housekeeper—the village Lady Bountiful by day, reading us to sleep at night. I’d be happy. What do you think?”

“Never,” Mr. Hale said firmly. “My great change is made. My price of suffering paid. Here I stay, and here I’ll die, lost in the crowd.”

“I won’t give up my plan,” Mr. Bell said. “But I won’t press it now. Where’s the Pearl? Come, Margaret—give me a farewell kiss. Remember, my dear, where you may find a true friend, as far as his ability allows. You are my child, Margaret. Remember that. God bless you.”

And so they slipped back into their quiet routine. No invalid to worry over; even the Higginses, once so vivid, seemed distant. The Boucher children, orphaned and motherless, claimed Margaret’s attention. She visited Mary Higgins often, who cared for them. The two families lived together—older children in humble schools, younger ones tended by a kind neighbor whose sense had impressed Margaret at Boucher’s death. She was paid for her help. Nicholas, in managing the children’s affairs, showed surprising judgment and method—unlike his earlier erratic ways. He worked steadily, so Margaret rarely saw him. When she did, he avoided any mention of the children’s father—whom he had taken in fully, though with difficulty. He spoke little of Mr. Thornton.

“To tell the truth,” he said once, “he bamboozles me. He’s two men. One I knew before—strict master, all authority. The other—no trace of master’s flesh. How these two are joined in one body, I can’t figure. But I won’t be beaten by it. He comes here often—that’s how I know the man, not the master. And I reckon he’s as surprised by me as I am by him. He sits and stares, like some strange beast caught in the wild. But I’m not daunted. It would take a lot to daunt me in my own house, as he sees. And I tell him my mind—things I think he’d have benefited from hearing when younger.”

“And does he answer?” Mr. Hale asked.

“Well,” Nicholas said, “the advantage isn’t all his. I’ve improved him a bit. Sometimes he says rough things—hard to hear at first, but with a queer truth when you chew on them. He’ll be coming tonight about the children’s schooling. He’s not satisfied with it and wants to examine them.”

“What are they”—Mr. Hale began, but Margaret touched his arm and showed him her watch.

“It’s nearly seven,” she said. “Evenings are growing longer. Come, Papa.” She didn’t breathe freely until they were some distance from the house. Then, calming, she wished she hadn’t hurried—because now they saw Mr. Thornton so seldom. He might have come to see Higgins, and for old friendship’s sake, she would have liked to see him tonight.

Yes, he came rarely—even for lessons. Mr. Hale was disappointed in his pupil’s fading interest in Greek literature, once so passionate. Now, hurried notes from Mr. Thornton often arrived at the last moment: “Too engaged to come tonight.” Though others filled his time, none matched his first scholar in Mr. Hale’s heart. He grew melancholy, pondering why this bond had weakened.

One evening, he startled Margaret, working quietly, by suddenly asking:

“Margaret—have you ever had reason to think Mr. Thornton cared for you?”

He blushed slightly as the words left him—Mr. Bell’s suggestion returning. But he had already spoken.

Margaret didn’t answer at once. By the droop of her head, he guessed her reply.

“Yes,” she whispered. “I believe—oh, Papa, I should have told you.” She dropped her work and buried her face in her hands.

“No, dear,” he said softly. “Don’t think I’m prying. I’m sure you’d have told me if you could return his feelings. Did he speak to you?”

No answer at first. Then, slowly, a small, reluctant “Yes.”

“And you refused him?”

She nodded, her silence heavy with grief.

I see my way as birds their trackless way—
I shall arrive! what time, what circuit first, I ask not:
but unless God sends his hail
Or blinding fire-balls, sleet, or stifling snow,
In some time—his good time—I shall arrive;
He guides me and the bird. In His good time!

Winter was deepening, yet the days were slowly lengthening, though no warmth of hope accompanied the faint glow of February suns. Mrs. Thornton had long ceased to visit the house. Mr. Thornton came now and then, but only to speak with her father in the study—no longer to linger in the drawing-room or share a word with Margaret. Mr. Hale spoke of him as unchanged, even more so for being rarely seen. The infrequency of their meetings seemed to make each one more precious in his eyes. From what Margaret could gather, there was nothing in Mr. Thornton’s silence that hinted at anger or resentment. His affairs had grown tangled during the strike, demanding greater attention than he’d given them before. And sometimes, she noticed, he mentioned her—not with hesitation, nor with any trace of awkwardness, but quietly, with the same calm regard as always.

Yet Margaret found little joy in such signs. Her own spirit was weary. The quiet peace of the present had followed too many months of anxiety, strain, and sorrow. She tried to fill her days by teaching the younger Boucher children, and to practice goodness with diligence—though it felt like a burden rather than a comfort. Her heart seemed numb, unresponsive to effort. She carried out her duties faithfully, punctually, painfully, but still, cheerfulness remained just beyond reach. Life felt cold and bare. Only in tending to her father did she find true purpose. He was quiet in his needs, often hesitant to voice a wish, but Margaret anticipated them all, fulfilling them with gentle care. It was a quiet devotion, soft and complete—her soul’s quiet obedience.

Then came news from Spain: Frederick was married. He and Dolores wrote letters—she in broken English, full of Spanish charm, he with phrases shaped by his new home, hints of a life remade. When Henry Lennox’s letter arrived, revealing how unlikely it was that Frederick would clear himself at court-martial without key witnesses, Frederick replied with fierce emotion. He renounced England as his homeland. He wished he could erase his birthright. He refused pardon if offered, and vowed never to live here again. The words tore at Margaret—so unnatural, so extreme—but when she reflected, she saw they were born of shattered dreams. There was nothing left but patience. In his next letter, Frederick spoke with radiant joy of his future, leaving the past behind. And Margaret, who had been begging God to grant patience for him, found herself needing it now—for herself.

Dolores’s letters, timid and sweet, began to warm both Margaret and her father. The young Spaniard clearly wanted to please Frederick’s family. Her careful writing, her erasures, her delicate attempts at politeness—they all revealed a tender heart eager to impress. With the announcement of the marriage came a black lace mantilla, chosen by Dolores herself for her sister-in-law, whom Frederick had described as a perfect woman—beautiful, wise, virtuous. Margaret smiled, then sighed. Here was her noble brother, once a romantic idealist, now a merchant—a trader! But she quickly checked herself. Wasn’t there a difference between a Spanish merchant and a millowner like Milton? Still, trade or no, Frederick was happy. Dolores must be lovely. The mantilla was exquisite. And so she returned to the present.

This spring, her father had begun to struggle with breathlessness—brief episodes that frightened him deeply. Margaret was less alarmed, since the symptoms passed entirely between attacks. Yet she urged him strongly to accept Mr. Bell’s invitation to visit Oxford in April. Mr. Bell had invited her too—and even written directly, commanding her to come. But Margaret felt she needed rest, not responsibility. To remain at home, free from duty, to simply breathe, to think, to grieve—this was what she craved after two years of constant care.

When her father left for the train, Margaret stood alone in the house. The sudden freedom stunned her. No one relied on her now. No invalid needed her planning or presence. She could be idle. Silent. Forgetful. And best of all—she could be unhappy, if she chose. For months, her own sorrows had been locked away in a dark cupboard. Now she opened it. She sat motionless in the drawing-room, going over every painful memory with steady resolve. Only once did she cry aloud—when the memory struck her: the falsehood, born of faithlessness, that had humiliated her so deeply.

She would not allow the temptation to claim power over her again. Her plans for Frederick had failed. The temptation was dead—mockery now, never real. The lie had been foolish, exposed by events. And truth, she now saw, was infinitely wiser.

In her agitation, she opened a book of her father’s lying on the table. The words that caught her eye seemed meant for this moment:

“Je ne voudrois pas reprendre mon cœur en ceste sorte: meurs de honte, aveugle, impudent, traistre et desloyal à ton Dieu, et sembables choses; mais je voudrois le corriger par voye de compassion. Or sus, mon pauvre cœur, nous voilà tombe dans la fosse, laquelle nous avions tant resolu d’eschapper. Ah! revelons-nous, et quittons-la pour jamais, reclamons la misericorde de Dieu, et esperons en elle qu’elle nous assistera pour desormais estra plus fermes; et remettons-nous au chemin de l’humilitè. Courage, soyons meshuy sur nos gardes, Dieu nous aydera.”

“The way of humility.”
Ah, thought Margaret, that is what I have missed.
But courage, little heart. We will turn back.
By God’s help, we may find the lost path.

She rose, determined to begin something that would carry her beyond herself. As she passed the drawing-room door, she called Martha in. She wanted to know the woman beneath the stiff, obedient exterior—the person hidden under years of servitude. It was hard to draw her out. But when Margaret mentioned Mrs. Thornton, Martha’s face lit up. A story unfolded—of how her father had once helped Mrs. Thornton’s husband, though she couldn’t say exactly how, for it happened when she was very young. Later, when her father fell into poverty and her mother died, Martha and her sister would have been “lost” without Mrs. Thornton’s intervention. She found them, cared for them, brought them into her home.

“I had the fever, and was weak,” Martha said. “Mrs. Thornton and Mr. Thornton—they never rested until I was nursed back to health, sent to the sea, treated properly. The doctor said it was catching, but they didn’t care. Only Miss Fanny—she went visiting those people she’s marrying. She was afraid, but it all turned out well.”

“Miss Fanny is getting married?” Margaret asked.

“Yes. To a rich gentleman—older than she is. Mr. Watson. His mills are near Hayleigh. A good match, though he has gray hair.”

Margaret was silent, letting the news settle. Then Martha returned to her duties, sweeping the hearth, asking about tea, leaving with the same wooden expression. Margaret pulled herself back—she had lately fallen into the habit of imagining how every news about Mr. Thornton might affect him. Whether he would approve or disapprove. She stopped herself.

The next day, she taught the Boucher children, took a long walk, and visited Mary Higgins. To her surprise, Nicholas was already home from work. The evening light had fooled her into thinking it was earlier. He seemed quieter, less forceful—like someone learning humility.

“So th’ oud gentleman’s away on his travels, is he?” he said. “Little ’uns telled me. Eh! but they’re sharp ones. I almost think they beat my own wenches for sharpness, though m’appen it’s wrong to say so—one of ’em in her grave. There’s summut in th’ weather, I reckon, as sets folk a-wandering. My measter, him at th’ shop yonder, is spinning about th’ world somewhere.”

“Is that why you’re home so early?” Margaret asked.

“Thou know’st nought about it, that’s all,” he said, contemptuously. “I’m not one wi’ two faces—one for my measter, and t’other for his back. I counted a’ th’ clocks in the town striking afore I’d leave my work. No! yon Thornton’s good enough to fight wi’, but too good to be cheated. It were you as getten me the place, and I thank yo’ for it. Thornton’s is not a bad mill, as times go.”

Then he called the boy forward.

“Stand down, lad, and say yo’r pretty hymn to Miss Marget. That’s right; steady on thy legs, and right arm out as straight as a skewer. One to stop, two to stay, three mak’ ready, and four away!”

The child sang a Methodist hymn—too complex for his understanding, but its rhythm had captured him. He delivered it with the solemnity of a statesman. Margaret clapped warmly. Nicholas called for another, and another. She was surprised—he seemed genuinely engaged, drawn into something sacred he had once mocked.

It was past tea-time when she returned home. No one waited. She could think freely, without watching others to gauge mood. After tea, she sorted through a large packet of letters, deciding which to destroy. Among them were several from Mr. Henry Lennox, concerning Frederick’s case. She read them carefully, intending only to assess the chance of clearing his name. But as she finished, she noticed something else—the man’s character. The tone was stiff, formal. He had never forgotten his connection to her, yet his concern felt distant. The letters were clever, yes—but cold. They lacked warmth. Still, they were valuable. She set them aside.

Then she slipped into thought. Her father was gone. And she had felt relief at being alone. She almost reproached herself for it. But these two days had renewed her strength. Plans that once seemed burdens now looked like joys. The fog had lifted. She saw her life more clearly. If only Mr. Thornton would return—just to visit, to ease her father’s loneliness—even if she never saw him again, she believed her path could be clear, steady. She sighed as she rose to bed. Despite her vow—"One step’s enough for me"—despite her duty to her father—there remained a quiet ache, a lingering sorrow.

And Mr. Hale, that April evening, thought of Margaret just as persistently. He had walked among old friends, revisiting familiar places. He had expected their reactions to change, given his departure from the church. Some might have been shocked, angry, disappointed. But when they saw him—the man they once loved—their opinions faded. They remembered not his beliefs, but his kindness. Mr. Hale had not been widely known. He had belonged to a small college, and was reserved by nature. But those who knew him well had seen beneath the silence—a depth of feeling, a tenderness. They welcomed him back with gentle affection, as if protecting a fragile thing.

“It’s too much,” said Mr. Bell. “You’re worn out by that Milton air.”

“I am tired,” said Mr. Hale. “But it’s not the air. I’m fifty-five. That explains it.”

“Nonsense! I’m over sixty, and feel no loss. Don’t talk like that. Fifty-five? You’re a young man.”

Mr. Hale shook his head. “These last few years…” He paused, then raised himself from the easy chair, speaking with trembling earnestness.

“Bell—you must not think that if I could have foreseen all that came from my resignation, even if I had known how _she_ would suffer—I would undo it. I would not have taken back my act of leaving the church. Even knowing that cruel suffering, I would have done the same. I might have handled things better for my family. But I don’t believe God gave me much wisdom or strength.”

Mr. Bell blew his nose loudly before replying.

“He gave you strength to do what your conscience told you was right. That’s all the strength or wisdom we need. I’ve none of it. Yet men call me wise, strong-minded—such nonsense. The simplest fool who obeys his own sense of right—wiping his shoes on a mat—is stronger than I. How blind men are!”

There was silence. Then Mr. Hale continued.

“About Margaret.”

“Well? What about her?”

“If I die—”

“Nonsense!”

“What will become of her? I often wonder. I suppose the Lennoxes will invite her to live with them. I try to believe they will. Aunt Shaw loved her, in her quiet way—but she forgets the absent.”

“A common fault. Who are the Lennoxes?”

“He’s handsome, fluent, agreeable. Edith—sweet, spoiled, beautiful. Margaret loves her deeply. And Edith—with as much heart as she can spare.”

“Hale,” said Mr. Bell, “you know that girl has nearly all my heart. I told you before. I’ve always cared for her—your daughter, my goddaughter. But this visit to you at Milton made me her slave. I went, a willing old victim, following the car of the conqueror. She looks—grand and serene—as if she has fought, and may still be fighting, but sees victory ahead. Yes, despite her troubles, that was her face. So everything I have is hers—if she needs it. And it will be hers when I die. And I myself—sixty and gouty though I am—will be her preux chevalier. Seriously, old friend, your daughter shall be my chief charge. All the help I can give—my wit, my wisdom, my heart—shall be hers. I won of course choose her as a subject for worry. You must have something to fret about to be happy. But you’ll outlive me by many years. Spare, thin men always cheat death. It’s the stout, florid fellows like me who go first.”

If Mr. Bell had seen into the future, he might have glimpsed the torch turning, the angel standing near, beckoning. That night, Mr. Hale laid his head on the pillow where it would never stir again. The servant entered the room in the morning, called out—received no reply. He approached the bed and saw the face—calm, beautiful, white, sealed in death. No pain. No struggle. The heart had simply stopped as he lay down.

Mr. Bell was stunned. He recovered only when it came time to rage at the idea of a coroner’s inquest.

“A coroner? Pooh. You don’t think I poisoned him! Dr. Forbes says it’s natural—heart failure. Poor old Hale. You wore out that tender heart too soon. Poor old friend. How he talked of his—— Wallis, pack a carpet-bag in five minutes. Here I’ve been talking. Pack it up. I must go to Milton by the next train.”

The bag was packed. The cab ordered. The railway reached. Twenty minutes later, Mr. Bell boarded the train. The London express whizzed past, drew back, and he was hurried aboard by the guard. He sank back, eyes closed, trying to grasp how a man alive yesterday could be dead today. Tears crept from beneath his grizzled lashes. He opened his eyes sharply, forcing a stern cheerfulness. He wouldn’t cry before strangers. Not he.

There were no strangers—only one man seated far across the carriage. Mr. Bell glanced at him, wondering if he’d been watching. Behind a broad sheet of the *Times*, he recognized Mr. Thornton.

“Why, Thornton! Is that you?” he said, moving closer. He gripped Mr. Thornton’s hand fiercely—then suddenly let go, needing it to wipe his tears. He had last seen him with Mr. Hale.

“I’m going to Milton. On a sad errand. I must tell Hale’s daughter he’s dead.”

“Dead? Mr. Hale dead?”

“Ay. I keep saying it—‘Hale is dead!’ But it doesn’t make it real. He went to bed well last night. This morning, my servant found him cold.”

“Where? I don’t understand.”

“At Oxford. He came to stay with me. Hadn’t been here in seventeen years. And this is the end.”

No words were spoken for over fifteen minutes. Then Mr. Thornton said:

“And she…”

“Margaret, you mean. Yes. I’m going to tell her. Poor fellow! How full his thoughts were of her last night! Good God! Last night only. And now—how far away he is! But I take Margaret as my child—for his sake. I said last night I would take her for her own. Well, I take her for both.”

Mr. Thornton struggled to speak. At last:

“What will become of her?”

“I fancy two people will be waiting: myself, for one. I’d take a live dragon into my house, if hiring such a chaperon and setting up an establishment would let me grow old with Margaret as my daughter. But there are those Lennoxes!”

“Who are they?” Mr. Thornton asked, voice trembling.

“Oh, smart London people. Likely to think they have the best claim. Captain Lennox married her cousin—the girl she grew up with. Good enough folk, I suppose. And there’s her aunt, Mrs. Shaw. I could marry that lady—though that would be a _pis aller_. And then—there’s that brother.”

“What brother? A brother of her aunt’s?”

“No, no. A clever Lennox—the captain’s a fool, you understand. A young barrister. He’s had his eye on Margaret for five years or more. One of his friends told me. He was held back by lack of fortune. Now that’s changed.”

“How?” asked Mr. Thornton, too eager to notice the intrusion.

Mr. Bell hesitated, then said softly:

“He’s inherited a fortune.”

"When some beloved voice that was to you
Both sound and sweetness, faileth suddenly,
And silence, against which you dare not cry,
Aches round you like a strong disease and new—
What hope? what help? what music will undo
That silence to your sense?"

The blow had struck with terrible force. Margaret sank into a state of utter collapse—not marked by tears or sobs, nor even by words. She lay upon the sofa, eyes closed, motionless, breathing faintly, speaking only when addressed, and then in whispers so soft they seemed scarcely human. Mr. Bell stood helpless beside her. He could not leave her; yet he dared not suggest she accompany him back to Oxford, for her exhaustion was too profound to endure such a journey—let alone the agony of facing the scene of her father’s death. He sat before the fire, lost in thought, his mind racing over what must be done.

Margaret remained still, almost breathless, as if suspended between life and oblivion. He refused dinner, though Dixon brought it up, weeping as she served him, eager to coax him to eat. The devilled chicken tasted like sawdust. He minced some meat for Margaret, seasoned it well, but when Dixon tried to feed her, a quiet shake of the head told all—she could not eat. Her body was too weak, too numb.

Mr. Bell sighed deeply. He rose stiffly from his chair, his limbs aching from travel, and followed Dixon out of the room.

"I can't leave her," he said. "I must write to Oxford, make sure preparations are made. They can work on while I'm gone. Can't Mrs. Lennox come? I'll send word—she must come. The girl needs a woman near her, someone to talk her into crying properly."

Dixon was already weeping—more than enough for two. After composing herself, she murmured that Mrs. Lennox was too close to childbirth to travel.

"Then Mrs. Shaw—she's returned to England, hasn’t she?"

"Yes, sir, she has—but I don’t think she’ll want to leave Mrs. Lennox now, at such a time," Dixon added, her tone tinged with disapproval. She resented the idea of a stranger stepping into her role as guardian of Margaret.

"Interesting time be——!" Mr. Bell broke off with a cough. "She could have been content in Venice or Naples, those Popish places, during her last 'interesting time'—which was in Corfu, I believe. And what does that little prosperous woman’s 'interesting time' matter compared to this poor creature—this helpless, homeless, friendless Margaret—lying there like a stone statue on an altar tomb? I tell you, Mrs. Shaw shall come. See that a room is ready for her by tomorrow night. I’ll see to it."

He wrote a letter, firm and urgent. When Mrs. Shaw read it, she wept, calling it so like her late husband’s voice when he faced a gout attack that she vowed to keep it always. Had he asked her gently, perhaps she wouldn’t have come—though her heart ached for Margaret. But the sharp command stirred her resolve. It broke through her natural inertia, and she allowed her maid to pack her things.

At the top of the stairs, Edith, wrapped in shawl and cap, tears streaming down her face, called after her mother:
"Don’t forget, mamma—Margaret must come and live with us. Sholto goes to Oxford on Wednesday. Send word by Mr. Bell when we’re to expect you. And if you need Sholto, he can go on from Oxford to Milton. Don’t forget, mamma—you’re bringing Margaret home!"

Edith reentered the drawing-room. Mr. Henry Lennox sat there, cutting open the pages of a new review. Without looking up, he said, “If you don’t like Sholto being away from you so long, Edith, I hope you’ll let me go down to Milton and offer what help I can.”

“Oh, thank you,” Edith replied. “But I’m sure Mr. Bell will do everything—and more help may not be needed. Still, one doesn’t expect much savoir-faire from a resident Fellow. Dear, darling Margaret—won’t it be lovely to have her here again? You were great allies once, weren’t you?”

“Were we?” he asked, indifferent, his eyes fixed on the page. “Well, perhaps not—I forget. I was so full of Sholto. But doesn’t it suit well that if my uncle died now, it should be just when we’ve returned and settled in the old house, ready to welcome Margaret? Poor thing—what a change it will be from Milton! I’ll have new chintz for her bedroom, make it bright and cheerful, lift her spirits.”

In the same spirit, Mrs. Shaw journeyed to Milton, often dreading the moment she would meet Margaret, wondering how she might break through the silence. More often, she planned how soon she could take Margaret away from “that horrid place” and restore her to the comfort of Harley Street.

“Oh dear!” she said to her maid. “Look at those chimneys! My poor sister Hale! I couldn’t have rested in Naples if I’d known what awaited me here. I must have come and fetched her and Margaret away.”

To herself, she admitted she’d always thought her brother-in-law weak—but never so weak as now, seeing what he’d traded the lovely Helstone home for.

Margaret remained unchanged—pale, still, silent, tearless. They told her her aunt was coming, but she showed no sign of surprise, joy, or sorrow. Mr. Bell, whose appetite had returned, tried again to tempt her with sweetbreads stewed with oysters. She shook her head, as quietly as before. He ate them all himself, finding solace in the food where she found none.

But Margaret heard first—the clatter of the cab arriving at the door. Her eyelids fluttered. Her lips trembled, colored slightly. Mr. Bell went down to greet Mrs. Shaw. When they came back, Margaret stood unsteadily, trying to hold herself upright. And when she saw her aunt, she stepped forward into the arms that waited, and finally, after weeks of silence, burst into passionate tears—her grief released at last upon her aunt’s shoulder.

All the quiet love, the years of tenderness, the echoes of her mother’s presence—everything that bound them together by blood and memory—flooded her heart. She wept freely, her numb soul thawing under the warmth of kinship.

Mr. Bell slipped out of the room and descended to the study. He lit a fire, took down books, and tried to distract himself. Each volume recalled his dead friend. The task was different from watching Margaret, but the thoughts remained the same.

He was glad when he heard Mr. Thornton’s voice at the door. Dixon, annoyed by the arrival of Mrs. Shaw’s maid—whose very presence reminded her of former grandeur, of Beresford blood, of “station”—was curt in her dismissal. She had been sharing stories with the maid about Sir John Beresford’s coach and six, reliving past glory, and felt superior to any inhabitant of Milton. So when Thornton arrived, she told him sharply he could not see anyone that night.

But Mr. Bell opened the study door and called out: “Thornton! Is that you? Come in for a minute—I want to speak to you.”

Thornton entered. Dixon retreated to the kitchen, regaining her dignity with a tale of Sir John’s grand procession.

“I don’t know what I wanted to say after all,” Mr. Bell began. “Only it’s dull sitting in a room where everything speaks of a dead friend. Yet Margaret and her aunt must have the drawing-room to themselves.”

“Is Mrs.—is her aunt come?” asked Thornton.

“Come? Yes—maid and all. One would think she might have come alone at such a time. Now I’ll have to find my way to the Clarendon.”

“You mustn’t go to the Clarendon,” said Mr. Bell. “We have five or six empty rooms at home.”

“Well aired?”

“I think you may trust my mother for that.”

“Then I’ll just run upstairs, wish that wan girl good-night, bow to her aunt, and go with you straight.”

Mr. Bell lingered upstairs longer than expected. Thornton grew impatient—he had business to attend to, and had barely spared time to visit Crampton. When they walked out, Bell said:

“I was held up by the women in the drawing-room. Mrs. Shaw wants Margaret to leave immediately—says she must return to London, see friends, say good-bye. And she worries Margaret about old claims, whether she remembers old friends. Then Margaret breaks down, cries out she’d be glad to leave a place where she’s suffered so much. I must return to Oxford tomorrow. I don’t know whose side to take.”

He paused, expecting a response. But Thornton said nothing. His mind echoed: *Where she had suffered so much.*

Alas—that was how these eighteen months in Milton would be remembered. To him, every day had been precious, even in its bitterness—worth more than all the sweetness of life elsewhere. Neither the loss of father nor mother could taint the memory of those hours when a two-mile walk brought him closer to her, each step rich with anticipation, each moment away from her filled with longing for her grace, her wit, her presence.

Yes—whatever had happened outside their connection, he could never call that time suffering. It had been a royal luxury, with all its stings and rebuffs, compared to the poverty of a future stripped bare, without hope or fear.

Mrs. Thornton and Fanny were in the dining-room. Fanny fluttered with excitement, holding up silks and satins, testing wedding dresses by candlelight. Her mother tried to sympathize, but couldn’t. Fashion wasn’t her domain. She wished Fanny had accepted her brother’s offer to hire a London dressmaker—avoiding endless debates and indecision.

Mr. Thornton, meanwhile, was pleased to support any man who could be captivated by Fanny’s modest charms, giving her ample funds for her finery—finery that, in her estimation, rivalled even her fiancé.

When Bell and Thornton entered, Fanny blushed and simpered, fluttering about her work. But Bell barely noticed. His thoughts were with the pale figure left behind—still, silent, head bent, hands folded, in a room so still it seemed the rush of air in his ears was the breath of spirits lingering near their beloved.

When Bell had first gone upstairs, Mrs. Shaw slept on the sofa. No sound disturbed the hush.

Mrs. Thornton greeted Bell formally, warmly—always most gracious when receiving her son’s friends. “How is Miss Hale?” she asked.

“About as broken down as she can be.”

“I’m certain it’s a great comfort to her to have such a friend as you.”

“I wish I were her only friend, madam. I daresay it sounds brutal—but I’ve been displaced, turned out of my post by a fine lady aunt. Cousins claim her in London as if she were a lapdog. And she’s too weak to care for herself.”

“She must indeed be weak,” Mrs. Thornton said, meaning more than she spoke.

“But where,” she continued, “have these relations been all this time? Miss Hale has seemed almost friendless, and certainly borne great anxiety.”

She didn’t wait for an answer, leaving to attend to household matters.

“They’ve lived abroad,” Bell replied. “They have claims—some justice. The aunt raised her. She and the cousin were like sisters. What troubles me is that I wanted to take her as my own child. I’m jealous of these people who don’t seem to value the privilege.”

“And Frederick?” Thornton asked abruptly.

“Frederick?” Bell looked surprised. “Don’t you know? He’s her brother—the one involved in the mutiny.”

“I never heard his name before. Where is he? Who is he?”

“Surely I told you when the family first came to Milton. He lives in Spain. He risks arrest if he sets foot in England. Poor fellow—he’ll grieve not to attend his father’s funeral. We’ll have to settle for Captain Lennox. I don’t know of another relation.”

“I hope I may be allowed to go?”

“Certainly—thankfully. You’re a good fellow, Thornton. Hale liked you. He spoke of you only the other day. He regretted seeing so little of you lately. I’m grateful you wish to honor him.”

“But Frederick—does he never come to England?”

“Never.”

“He wasn’t here when Mrs. Hale died?”

“No. Why, I was here then. I hadn’t seen Hale in years. And if you remember, I came—no, it was after that. But Frederick wasn’t here. What made you think he was?”

“I saw a young man walking with Miss Hale once,” Thornton said. “I think it was around that time.”

“Oh—that would be Captain Lennox’s brother. A lawyer. They corresponded regularly. Mr. Hale told me he thought he might come down.”

Bell wheeled around, squinting one eye shut, studying Thornton’s face closely. “Do you know,” he said, “I once thought you had a little tenderness for Margaret?”

No reply. No flicker of emotion.
