“And so did poor Hale. Not at first—only after I planted the idea in his head.”

“I admired Miss Hale,” Thornton said, cornered. “Everyone must. She is a beautiful creature.”

“Is that all? You speak of her in such measured terms—just a ‘beautiful creature,’ something to catch the eye. I hoped you had nobleness enough to pay her homage from the heart. Even if she rejected you—still, to love her without return would have lifted you higher than any man who ever knew her love. ‘Beautiful creature’ indeed! Do you speak of her as you would a horse or a dog?”

Thornton’s eyes glowed like embers.

“Mr. Bell,” he said, “before you speak so, remember not all men are free to express what they feel as you are. Let us talk of something else.”

Though his heart leapt at every word, though he knew Bell’s words would forever bind him to the deepest parts of his soul, he refused to be forced into expression. He was not a mocking bird, singing louder because another praised what he loved.

So he turned to business—landlord and tenant matters.

“What is that pile of brick and mortar in the yard? Repairs needed?”

“No, none. Thank you.”

“Are you building for yourself?”

“I’m building a dining-room—for the men. The hands.”

“I thought you were hard to please, if this room wasn’t good enough for a bachelor.”

“I’ve met a strange sort of chap. I put one or two children he cares about to school. One day, passing his house, I stopped to settle a small payment. I saw a dreadful meal—a greasy cinder of meat. That started me thinking. Then, when provisions rose this winter, I realized how much could be saved—how much comfort gained—by buying wholesale and cooking in bulk.”

“I see. But you mentioned a man—Higgins?”

“Yes. I laid aside my plan, thinking it impractical, and fearing I’d interfere with my men’s independence. Then Higgins came to me, approved the scheme—so similar to mine I could have claimed it—and several of his coworkers backed it. I was irritated by his manner, thought of abandoning it. But it seemed childish to give up a wise plan just because I didn’t get credit.”

“So you took the steward’s role?”

“Yes. I buy in bulk, hire a cook.”

“And you taste the food?”

“At first, I confined myself to purchasing, obeying the men’s orders through the housekeeper. But once, when the beef was too large, the mutton too lean, I saw they valued my caution. One day, several men—including Higgins—invited me to join them. I went. It was the best meal I’d ever eaten. I told them how much I enjoyed it. From then on, whenever that dish appeared, they’d say, ‘Master, hot-pot today—will you come in?’”

“You must restrain their conversation. They can’t criticize masters when you’re there.”

“We’ve avoided vexed questions. But if old disputes arise, I’ll speak my mind next hot-pot day. You don’t know our Darkshire men—they have humor, a lively tongue. I’m beginning to know them well. They talk freely before me.”

“Nothing like eating to equalize men. Dying is nothing to it. The philosopher dies sententiously, the pharisee ostentatiously, the simple-hearted humbly, the fool blindly. But all eat alike—given good digestion. There’s theory for theory!”

“I hate theories.”

“Then accept this ten-pound note—toward your marketing. Give the men a feast.”

“Thank you—but I’d rather not. They pay rent for the oven and cooking space. They’ll pay more for the new room. I don’t want charity. Once donations start, people will talk, spoil the simplicity.”

“People will talk anyway.”

“My enemies may make a fuss—call it philanthropy. But you’re a friend. I expect your silence. It’s a new broom now—sweeps clean. But soon, I’ll meet stumbling blocks.”

“The meanest thing to which we bid adieu,
Loses its meanness in the parting hour.” — ELLIOTT.

Mrs. Shaw could not bear Milton. Not that she was naturally a harsh judge of places or people—quite the contrary—but Milton, with all its smoke and noise, its ragged streets and over-dressed ladies, seemed to her a place that had no room for peace or decency. The men wore clothes ill-fitted and slovenly; the poor looked weary and unclean; even the air itself felt heavy and oppressive. She feared for Margaret’s health, and worse still, for her own nerves. She insisted, with gentle but firm urgency, that Margaret must leave Milton at once and return with her to London. Margaret, weak and worn down by grief and illness, gave in at last, promising that as soon as the funeral on Wednesday passed, she would prepare to go, leaving Dixon to manage the house, settle debts, and oversee the sale of their belongings.

Before that somber day—when Mr. Hale would be laid to rest far from either home he had known, and far from his grieving wife who now lay alone among strangers—Margaret received a letter from Mr. Bell.

“MY DEAR MARGARET:—I meant to return to Milton on Thursday, but it turns out I’m needed in Plymouth this week, one of those rare occasions when duty calls. Captain Lennox and Mr. Thornton are here. The former seems a decent fellow, though perhaps too eager to help—offering to come to Milton and assist you in searching for the will. Of course, there is no will, or you’d have found it already if you followed my instructions. Still, he insists on taking you and your mother-in-law back to London. Given his wife’s condition, he can’t stay beyond Friday. But don’t worry—your Dixon is reliable. She’ll hold things together until I arrive. If no will exists, I’ll entrust matters to my Milton solicitor. Though I doubt the captain has much head for business. Still, his moustache is magnificent.

There will need to be a sale. Choose what you wish to keep—or send me a list later. Two other points, and I’m done. You know, or if you don’t, your father did, that upon my death, my money and property will pass to you. Not that I intend to die soon—I merely state this so you understand what’s coming. The Lennoxes seem fond of you now—whether they will remain so is another matter. So let us begin with an agreement: you shall pay them two hundred and fifty pounds a year, as long as you both find it agreeable to live together. (This includes Dixon—don’t let yourself be pressured into paying more for her.) That way, if one day the captain decides he wants his house to himself, you won’t be left stranded. You can take your two hundred and fifty pounds and go elsewhere—unless I’ve claimed you first to come and keep house for me.

As for dress, personal expenses, confectionery—every young lady eats sweets until wisdom arrives—I’ll consult a woman I know to determine how much your father left you, and then fix these matters accordingly.

Now, Margaret—have you already flown off in anger before reading this far, wondering what right this old man has to dictate your life so freely? I expect you have. Yet I do have a right. I’ve loved your father for thirty-five years. I stood beside him at his wedding. I closed his eyes in death. I am your godfather—and since I cannot give you spiritual comfort, knowing full well you surpass me in that regard, I would at least try to provide material support. And I have no living kin. Who will mourn Adam Bell? My whole heart is set on this one purpose. And Margaret Hale is not the girl to say no.

Write back, even just two lines, to tell me your answer. But _no thanks_.”

Margaret picked up a pen and, with trembling fingers, wrote:
“Margaret Hale is not the girl to say him nay.”

She couldn’t think of anything else. Her strength was gone. Even this small act exhausted her. She had to lie down again, shutting her eyes against the weight of thought.

“My dearest child,” Mrs. Shaw said softly, “did that letter upset you?”

“No,” Margaret whispered. “I shall feel better once tomorrow is over.”

“I’m certain you won’t truly feel better until you’re out of this dreadful air. How you’ve endured it for two years, I cannot imagine.”

“Where could I have gone? I couldn’t leave papa and mamma.”

“Well, don’t fret about it. It may have been best, though I never dreamed how poorly you lived. Our butler’s wife lives in a better house than this.”

“It’s lovely in summer—you can’t judge it now. I’ve been very happy here,” she murmured, closing her eyes to end the conversation.

The house was now warm and comfortable, fires lit in every bedroom as Mrs. Shaw insisted. She doted on Margaret, buying every delicacy, every soft luxury she herself would have cherished. But Margaret cared little for them—only noting them as signs of her aunt’s kindness. She was restless, yet drained. All day, she wandered from room to room, sorting through familiar objects, touching them gently, as if reluctant to part with memories. Dixon followed her, ostensibly to receive orders, but secretly instructed to soothe her into quiet.

“These books, Dixon, I’ll keep. Send the rest to Mr. Bell—they’ll mean something to him, as well as to papa. This one—I’d like you to give this to Mr. Thornton after I’m gone. Wait—let me write a note.”

She sat down quickly, afraid to dwell on thoughts, and scribbled:

“DEAR SIR,—The book enclosed I am sure you will value for the sake of my father, to whom it belonged.
Yours sincerely,

She resumed her walk through the house, pausing at each object from childhood, touched by affection and sorrow. She spoke little. Dixon reported to Mrs. Shaw that “Miss Hale didn’t seem to hear a word I said, though I talked the whole time to distract her.”

By evening, the exhaustion had brought her some relief—her first good night’s sleep since hearing of her father’s death.

At breakfast the next morning, Margaret asked to visit a few friends before leaving.

Mrs. Shaw hesitated. “My dear, I don’t think you have any friends here close enough to justify calling on them so soon—before church.”

“But today is my only chance. If Captain Lennox arrives this afternoon, and if we really must go tomorrow…”

“Oh, yes—we must go. I’m more convinced than ever this air harms you. You look pale and frail. Edith expects us, and she may be waiting. And you cannot be left alone, my dear. If you insist on visiting, I’ll go with you. Dixon can arrange a coach.”

So Mrs. Shaw accompanied her, along with the maid, to ensure shawls and cushions were properly managed. Margaret’s face remained solemn, untouched by the effort of such a journey. She dreaded one stop above all—Nicholas Higgins’ cottage. She hoped her aunt might refuse to alight, especially given the damp, wind-swept court where wet clothes flapped between houses.

But Mrs. Shaw, torn between comfort and propriety, chose comfort. With many warnings about catching cold, she allowed the visit.

Nicholas was out. Only Mary and a few of the Boucher children were home. Margaret regretted not timing her visit better. Mary, though kind-hearted, had a blunt mind. As soon as she understood why Margaret came, she burst into tears, sobbing uncontrollably. Margaret could not speak—her words dissolved in the flood of emotion. She could only offer vague hopes of future meetings, and ask Mary to tell Nicholas how much she wished he could come see her after work.

As she turned to leave, she paused. “I’d like something small to remember Bessy by.”

Mary’s face lit up. “Oh, take something better—this cup only cost fourpence!”

“That will do, thank you,” Margaret said, and hurried away, while Mary smiled, pleased to give.

“Now to Mrs. Thornton’s,” Margaret thought. “It must be done.”

But the thought made her stiffen. She struggled to explain to her aunt who Mrs. Thornton was and why she wished to bid farewell.

They were shown into the drawing-room, where a fire had just been lit. Mrs. Shaw wrapped herself tightly in her shawl and shivered. “What an icy room!” she muttered.

They waited. When Mrs. Thornton entered, she was softer than usual—perhaps because Margaret was leaving. She remembered not only Margaret’s endurance, but her spirit. Her manner held a rare tenderness as she saw the pale, tear-streaked face and the quiver in Margaret’s voice.

“Allow me to introduce my aunt, Mrs. Shaw. I’m leaving Milton tomorrow. I wanted to see you once more—to apologize for my behavior last time we met, and to say I know you meant well, however much we misunderstood each other.”

Mrs. Shaw looked puzzled—apologies for rudeness, gratitude for kindness? Unusual.

Mrs. Thornton replied, “Miss Hale, I am glad you acknowledge my intentions. I acted as I believed was my duty. I always sought to be a friend to you.”

“And,” Margaret said, blushing, “will you believe me when I say that though I cannot—nor choose to—explain my actions, I did not behave as you imagined?”

Her voice was gentle, her eyes pleading. For once, Mrs. Thornton was moved—by the charm Margaret had always possessed, yet which she had refused to admit.

“Yes, I believe you. Let us say no more.”

“Where will you live?” Mrs. Thornton asked.

“With my aunt,” Margaret replied.

“My niece will live with me in Harley Street. She is almost like a daughter,” Mrs. Shaw said, looking proudly at Margaret. “And I am grateful for any kindness shown her. Should you and your husband ever come to London, my son and daughter—Captain and Mrs. Lennox—will join me in welcoming you.”

Mrs. Thornton thought silently: Margaret hadn’t explained the relationship between Mr. and Mrs. Thornton—nor had she mentioned that Mr. Thornton was her son. She answered curtly: “My husband is dead. Mr. Thornton is my son. I never go to London. Your offers are kind, but I shall not be able to accept them.”

At that moment, Mr. Thornton entered. He had just returned from Oxford. His mourning suit marked the reason for his absence.

“John,” his mother said, “this is Mrs. Shaw, Miss Hale’s aunt. I’m sorry to say, Miss Hale is bidding us farewell.”

“You are going?” he asked, quietly.

“Yes,” Margaret said. “We leave tomorrow.”

“My son-in-law arrives this evening,” Mrs. Shaw added.

Mr. Thornton turned away, as if absorbed in something on the table—perhaps a letter he had just noticed. He didn’t even seem aware when they rose to leave. But as they reached the door, he stepped forward to help Mrs. Shaw into the carriage.

They stood side by side on the doorstep. The memory of the riot flooded both their minds—the chaos, the speeches, her fierce declaration that no man in that crowd mattered less to her than he did. Now, seeing her pale beauty and stony composure, he clenched his jaw.

“No,” he whispered. “I tested it once—and lost it all. Let her go—with her cold heart and her beauty. She fears I’ll say something she cannot endure. Let her go. Beauty and heiress as she may be, she will find it hard to meet a truer heart than mine. Let her go.”

His voice held no sorrow, no emotion. The hand she offered was taken firmly, then dropped as if it were nothing.

No one in the house saw him again that day. He said he was busy.

Margaret was utterly spent by the visits. Her aunt watched over her, sighing “I-told-you-so” with tender concern. Dixon declared she looked as ill as the day she heard of her father’s death. They debated delaying the journey—but when Mrs. Shaw suggested it, Margaret writhed in pain and said, “Oh, let us go. I cannot wait. I won’t recover here. I want to forget.”

The arrangements continued. Captain Lennox arrived, bringing news of Edith and the baby. His calm, indifferent talk—though kind—soothed Margaret. She grew stronger. By the time Nicholas Higgins came, she was able to sit quietly in her room.

“Eh!” he said, stepping in. “To think the old gentleman passed so suddenly! I nearly fell over when I heard. ‘Mr. Hale?’ I asked. ‘The parson?’ ‘Ay,’ they said. ‘Then,’ I replied, ‘there’s a better man gone than ever lived on this earth!’ I came to see you, to tell you how sorry I was—but the women in the kitchen wouldn’t let me in. Said you were ill. Butter me, but you don’t look like the same girl. And you’re going to be a grand lady in London, aren’t you?”

“Not a grand lady,” Margaret said, half-smiling.

“Well,” Higgins continued, “Thornton said the other day, ‘Higgins, have you seen Miss Hale?’ ‘No,’ I said, ‘a pack of women won’t let me near her. But I’ll wait.’ We’ve known each other well enough. And I won’t pretend I’m not grieved for the old gentleman—just because I can’t get to tell you so.”

“And he said, ‘You won’t have long to try. She’s not staying a day longer than necessary. She’s got rich relations taking her away. We shan’t see her again.’”

“Measter,” I said, “if I don’t see her before she goes, I’ll make it my business to come up to London next Whitsuntide. I won’t be stopped by any relation. But bless you, I knew you’d come. I only pretended I thought you might leave without seeing me.”

“You’re quite right,” Margaret said. “You only do me justice. And you’ll not forget me, I’m sure. If no one else remembers me, I know you will—and papa too. You know how gentle and kind he was. Look, Higgins—here is his Bible. I’ve kept it for you. I can hardly spare it, but I know he’d want you to have it. I’m sure you’ll care for it and study it—for his sake.”

“You may say that,” Higgins replied. “If it were the devil’s own writing, and you asked me to read it for your sake and the old gentleman’s, I’d do it. What’s this, wench? I’m not taking your money—don’t think it. We’ve been honest friends—no coin passed between us.”

“For the children—for Boucher’s children,” Margaret said quickly. “They may need it. You’ve no right to refuse it for them. I wouldn’t give you a penny,” she added, smiling. “Don’t think there’s any for you.”

“Well, wench,” Higgins said, “I can only say—Bless you! And bless you!—and amen.”

“A dull rotation, never at a stay,
Yesterday’s face twin image of to-day.” — COWPER
“Of what each one should be, he sees the form and rule,
And till he reach to that, his joy can ne’er be full.” — RÜCKERT

For Margaret Hale, the stillness of the Harley Street house during Edith Lennox’s recovery offered more than mere quiet—it gave her the rest her body and spirit so desperately needed. In those weeks, she found herself transplanted into a world of comfort and ease, far removed from the familiar rhythms of Helstone and Milton. The home was spacious, well-ordered, and polished—its machinery of daily life running with such smooth precision that it seemed untouched by hardship or care. The servants moved like shadows, attending only when summoned, their lives hidden in a silent underground realm. Mrs. Shaw and Edith doted on Margaret, treating her return as a long-awaited homecoming. Yet, deep within, Margaret felt a quiet dissonance.

She could not help but remember the small vicarage at Helstone, with its modest rooms and humble routines—the scent of pipe smoke mingling with the damp earth outside, her father’s thoughtful silences, her mother’s frail strength. Even the cramped little house in Milton, where worries were shared and meals eaten in earnest conversation, now seemed more real, more *home*, than this gilded cage of London elegance.

Edith, eager to make Margaret feel welcome, rushed through her convalescence to rearrange her room—filling it with soft fabrics, delicate trinkets, and all the finery she deemed necessary for a lady’s comfort. Mrs. Shaw and her maid busied themselves restoring Margaret’s wardrobe to a state of refined variety, as though every thread must reflect the dignity of her new station. Captain Lennox, gentle and courteous, passed his days between his wife’s dressing room, playful hours with his son Sholto, and idle afternoons at his club. He treated Margaret with kindness, almost like a younger sister, though she sometimes resented his overzealous attention to Edith’s appearance—his desire to present her as the perfect specimen of beauty before society.

Margaret had barely begun to adjust to this tranquil existence when Edith returned to her usual routine. Once again, Margaret fell into the old pattern of watching, admiring, and caring for her cousin. She willingly took on tasks Edith left behind: answering letters, reminding her of appointments, tending to her when fatigue made her feign illness. But while Edith and her husband plunged into the whirl of London’s social season, Margaret often found herself alone—left to wander through empty rooms, her thoughts drifting back to Milton.

There, life had been lived with purpose, with struggle, with meaning. Here, everything flowed effortlessly, without effort or direction. The ease began to weigh upon her. She feared becoming numb, softened into forgetfulness, her soul dimmed by luxury. There were no voices from the streets, no cries from the factories, no faces worn by labor—only the polite murmurs of drawing-room talk and the distant clatter of carriages. The servants existed in silence, emerging only when summoned, their lives invisible, their hopes unknown.

A hollow space opened in her heart. She tried once to express this unease to Edith, who, languid after a night of dancing, lay back on the sofa, stroking Margaret’s cheek with a half-sleepy affection.

“Poor child,” Edith murmured. “It must be lonely for you, sitting here night after night while the world dances. But soon—when Henry comes back from circuit—we’ll have dinner parties. That will cheer you up, won’t it? No wonder you’re moping, dear.”

But Margaret did not believe dinner parties would mend the emptiness. Edith prided herself on these gatherings—how different they were, she said, from the stiff, formal affairs under her mother’s rule. Mrs. Shaw, too, seemed to take equal pleasure in this new style of entertaining, just as she once had in the grand, solemn dinners of her youth.

Captain Lennox remained kind, even protective, toward Margaret. She liked him—except when he became overly attentive to Edith’s dress, as if her beauty were a performance to be perfected for public approval. Then, a spark of defiance stirred within her—the same Vashti who had once refused a king’s command. She bit her tongue, but her eyes burned with unspoken protest.

Her days followed a set rhythm: a quiet morning before late breakfast; an unhurried meal attended by people still half-asleep, yet expected to join in the endless discussions of plans and invitations that followed; a flood of letters to write—Edith always handing them over with compliments on Margaret’s “eloquence du billet”; time spent playing with Sholto; watching over the children during the servants’ meal; a carriage ride or callers; then, evening engagements that left her free but drained, her spirits low.

She looked forward, silently, to the return of Dixon from Milton. The servant had been managing the Hale household since their departure, and her letters had become a lifeline—though brief and business-like. They mentioned Mr. Thornton occasionally, offering advice on furniture or leases, but never with warmth or depth. The names of Milton’s people rarely appeared. And so, one evening, as Margaret sat alone in the drawing-room, holding Dixon’s latest letter without reading it, her mind wandered to the past—remembering the busy life she had left behind, wondering if anyone missed her there.

Suddenly, the door opened, and Mr. Bell was announced.

Margaret started, blushing as she hastily tucked the letter into her workbasket.

“Oh, Mr. Bell! I didn’t expect to see you!”

“But you give me a welcome, I hope—besides that very pretty start of surprise.”

“Have you dined? How did you come? Let me order you something.”

“If you’re having dinner, I might join you. Otherwise, I don’t care much for eating. Where are the others? Out?”

“Yes—gone to dinner. It’s a relief, actually. I was just thinking—” she paused, then added, “Would you like some tea instead?”

“I’d rather have tea. And now—what were you thinking of? You were about to tell me. Whose letters were those, god-daughter, that you hid so quickly?”

“Only Dixon’s,” she replied, growing red.

“Whew! Is that all? Who came up with me on the train?”

“I don’t know,” she said, refusing to guess.

“Your—what do you call him? Cousin-in-law’s brother?”

“Mr. Henry Lennox?” she asked.

“Yes. You knew him before?”

“I liked him once,” she said, glancing down. Then she lifted her eyes. “We’ve corresponded about Frederick since—but I haven’t seen him in nearly three years. Has he changed?”

“I don’t know. He was too busy trying to figure out who I was—and what I was—to reveal anything about himself. Unless,” he added, “that veiled curiosity was part of his character. Do you think he’s good-looking?”

“No. Not at all. Do you?”

“Not I. But I thought perhaps you might. Is he here often?”

“I suppose so when he’s in town. He’s been away on circuit since I arrived. But—Mr. Bell—did you come from Oxford or from Milton?”

“From Milton. Don’t you see I’m smoke-dried?”

“Certainly. But I thought it might be the effect of Oxford’s antiquities.”

“Come now, be sensible! In Oxford, I could have managed the landlords with half the trouble your Milton landlord caused me—and still failed. He won’t take the house off our hands until next June. Luckily, Mr. Thornton found a tenant. Why don’t you ask after Mr. Thornton, Margaret? He’s been a most active friend of yours. Took more than half the burden off my shoulders.”

“And how is he? How is Mrs. Thornton?” she asked, her voice low, breathless.

“I suppose they’re well. I stayed at their house until I was driven out by the constant chatter about the Thornton girl’s marriage. Too much for even Thornton himself, though she was his own sister. He used to retreat to his room. He’s past the age for such things. I was surprised to find the old lady swept along by her daughter’s excitement over orange-blossoms and lace. I thought Mrs. Thornton had stronger stuff in her.”

“She would pretend feeling to cover her daughter’s weakness,” Margaret whispered.

“Perhaps. You’ve studied her, haven’t you? She doesn’t seem to care much for you.”

“I know,” Margaret said quietly. “Oh—here’s the tea!” she exclaimed, relieved.

And just as the tea arrived, Mr. Henry Lennox entered, having walked up from dinner. He clearly expected to find his brother and sister-in-law at home. Margaret sensed his gratitude at seeing another person present—especially after their last meeting, when he had proposed and she had refused, in the garden at Helstone.

She could not speak at first, grateful for the distractions of the tea table. He, too, seemed to need the moment to compose himself. He had come to Harley Street deliberately, knowing the awkwardness of the encounter, and now, finding her alone, he felt doubly exposed.

She recovered first. “Mr. Lennox,” she said, “I’ve been so grateful for all you’ve done regarding Frederick.”

“I’m sorry it hasn’t succeeded,” he replied, glancing toward Mr. Bell as if measuring how much he could say.

Margaret understood. She turned to Mr. Bell. “That Horrocks—the last witness—proved useless, just like the others. Mr. Lennox discovered he sailed for Australia only last August—two months before Frederick was in England.”

“Frederick in England? You never told me that!” Mr. Bell exclaimed.

“I thought you knew. It was a secret, and perhaps I shouldn’t have said it now,” she admitted, embarrassed.

“I never mentioned it to my brother or your cousin,” Mr. Lennox said, dryly.

“Never mind,” Mr. Bell said, laughing. “I’m not living in a world of gossip, nor among people who pry. You needn’t look so alarmed. I won’t betray your secret. I’m a faithful old hermit—I’ll keep it safe. But wait—was it at your mother’s funeral?”

“He was with mamma when she died,” Margaret said softly.

“To be sure! I remember someone asking me if he’d been over then. I denied it—just a few weeks ago. Who could it have been?” He paused, then stopped himself from naming the person.

Margaret longed to know if it had been Mr. Thornton—but she could not ask.

After a pause, Mr. Lennox addressed her directly. “Now that Mr. Bell knows the circumstances of your brother’s situation, I may as well inform him of the current state of our investigation. If he will do me the honor of breakfasting with me tomorrow, we can go over the list of missing witnesses.”

“I’d like to hear all about it,” she said. “Can’t you come here? I wouldn’t dare invite both of you to breakfast, though I’m sure you’d be welcome. Just tell me what you can—even if there’s no hope.”

“I have an engagement at half-past eleven,” he replied, “but I’ll come if you wish.”

His tone held a hint of eagerness that made her shrink inwardly, wishing she hadn’t asked.

Mr. Bell rose to leave, searching for his hat. “Well, I don’t know what Mr. Lennox intends, but I’m ready to go home. A journey today has tired me at my sixty and odd years.”

“I think I’ll stay and see my brother and sister,” Mr. Lennox said, remaining seated.

Margaret felt a sudden dread—of being left alone with him. The memory of that terrace at Helstone flooded back, vivid and undeniable.

“Don’t go yet, please, Mr. Bell,” she said quickly. “I want you to meet Edith. Please!”

She laid a light hand on his arm. He looked at her, saw the flush of emotion, and sat back down as if compelled.

“You see how she commands me, Mr. Lennox,” he said. “And notice her clever choice of words—she wants me to ‘see’ Edith, whom I’m told is beautiful—but when it comes to me, she says Mrs. Lennox is to ‘know’ me. I suppose I’m not much to ‘see,’ eh, Margaret?”

He joked to ease her tension, and she responded with a smile.

Mr. Lennox, watching her, wondered how his brother could have claimed she had lost her beauty. In her simple black dress, she stood in sharp contrast to Edith—dancing in white crape, her golden hair flowing. Yet Margaret’s presence was quiet, graceful. When introduced to Mr. Bell, she dimpled and blushed, aware of her reputation, determined not to let a man like him—old, learned, obscure—disregard her charm.

Mrs. Shaw and Captain Lennox welcomed Mr. Bell warmly, their sincerity winning him over. He saw how naturally Margaret fit into the family, and began to admire her.

“What a shame we weren’t home to receive you,” Edith said. “You too, Henry! Though I don’t know if we’d have stayed for you. And Mr. Bell—for Margaret’s Mr. Bell—”

“There’s no telling what sacrifices you wouldn’t make,” Henry said. “Even skipping a dinner party! Or wearing this lovely dress.”

Edith hesitated between frown and smile, but Henry chose not to push. “Will you show your readiness to sacrifice tomorrow? First, by inviting me to breakfast to meet Mr. Bell, and second, by ordering it at half-past nine instead of ten? I have papers to show Miss Hale and Mr. Bell.”

“I hope Mr. Bell will treat our house as his own,” Captain Lennox said. “Though I’m sorry we can’t offer a bedroom.”

“Thank you,” Mr. Bell replied. “But I’d decline, even with such pleasant company. It would be like meat without salt. I’m glad they don’t have a bed.”

Outside, walking side by side, Mr. Bell suddenly remembered Margaret’s pleading glance. He also recalled hints from a mutual acquaintance about Henry Lennox’s feelings for her.

“You’ve known Miss Hale a long time,” he said. “How does she look? Pale, I think.”

“I thought her remarkably well. At least, when she spoke, she looked as vibrant as ever.”

“She’s endured much,” Mr. Bell said.

“Yes. I’ve heard of her sorrow—not just death, but the distress her father’s actions must have caused. And then—”

“Her father’s actions?” Mr. Bell interrupted.

“You must have heard a false account. He acted with great conscience. More strength than I’d have credited him with.”

“Perhaps I was misinformed. But I’ve heard from his successor—a capable clergyman—that there was no need for Mr. Hale to resign. The bishop offered him another living. If he’d doubted, he could have stayed. These country clergymen live isolated lives—cut off from men of equal culture. They often torment themselves with imagined doubts, throwing away opportunities for uncertain whims.”

“I disagree,” Henry said calmly. “I don’t think many do what Mr. Hale did.”

Mr. Bell bristled. “I meant ‘very apt’ generally. But yes, their lives often breed either arrogance or morbid conscience.”

“And lawyers?” Mr. Bell challenged. “No self-sufficiency? No guilt?”

He was losing patience.

Henry, seeing the irritation, changed course. “To be sure, there’s something noble in a man of Mr. Hale’s age leaving his home of twenty years, giving up settled habits, for an idea—perhaps mistaken—but that doesn’t matter. One can’t help admiring him, mixed with pity. Like Don Quixote. Such a gentleman! I’ll never forget his hospitality at Helstone.”

Mr. Bell grumbled. “Aye. But you don’t know Milton. Such a change from Helstone! It’s standing there, unchanged—every stone as it was a century ago. But Milton! I go there every few years—I was born there—yet I often lose my way, among warehouses built on my father’s orchard.”

They reached their parting point.

“Well, good-night, sir. We’ll meet tomorrow in Harley Street.”

“Where are the sounds that swam along
The buoyant air when I was young;
The last vibration now is o’er,
And they who listened are no more;
Ah! let me close my eyes and dream.”

The name of Helstone had stirred in Mr. Bell’s mind during his talk with Mr. Lennox, and from that moment it haunted his dreams all night long. He found himself once more the young tutor at the college where he now stood as a Fellow—a man of rank and years, yet in spirit still the eager scholar of his youth. It was again the long vacation, and he was staying with his friend, newly married, proud in his role as Vicar of Helstone, and blissfully devoted to his wife. Together, they leapt across babbling brooks with impossible grace, suspended in midair for what felt like endless days. Time and space dissolved; only emotion remained real. The world existed not in its physical form but in the warmth of memory—the rustling leaves of autumn trees, the sweet scent of herbs and flowers carried on the breeze, the gentle movements of the young wife through the house, her manner a blend of quiet pride in her husband and a subtle irritation at their modest means—just as Mr. Bell remembered her from twenty-five years past.

The dream was so vivid, so deeply alive, that when he awoke, the reality around him seemed thin, unreal. Where was he? In a narrow, well-appointed room of a London hotel, surrounded by silence and cold order. Who were those voices he had heard, those hands that had touched him, those faces that had smiled? They were gone—dead, buried, lost beyond recall. And here he was, an old man, once full of vigor and purpose, now standing alone in a life stripped of companionship. The weight of solitude pressed upon him, unbearable in its clarity.

He rose quickly, determined to drown the ache in routine. Dressing hurriedly, he prepared for breakfast in Harley Street, seeking refuge in the mundane. But even there, the world refused to offer comfort. As Mr. Lennox read aloud the legal documents, each sentence stripping away another thread of hope for Frederick’s innocence, Margaret’s face grew pale, her eyes wide with growing dread. She had known the outcome already, yet the relentless unfolding of evidence—each detail a blow—left her trembling. At last, she could hold back no longer, and tears fell freely.

Mr. Lennox paused, his voice softening. “I had better not go on,” he said quietly. “It was a foolish idea of mine.”

“Lieutenant Hale,” he added, using the title that had been stripped from him, “is happy now. More secure in fortune and future than he ever could have been in the navy. He has, no doubt, made his wife’s country his own.”

“That is it,” Margaret whispered, forcing a smile. “It seems selfish to regret it—and yet… he is gone from me. And I am so lonely.”

Mr. Lennox turned over his papers, silent, wishing he possessed the wealth and peace he believed might one day be his. Mr. Bell wiped his nose with a handkerchief, saying nothing. But within moments, Margaret had regained her composure. She thanked Mr. Lennx with quiet dignity, her gratitude tempered by awareness that her sorrow might have seemed excessive to him. Yet she would not have spared herself the pain—it was part of what made her whole.

Then Mr. Bell stepped forward, adjusting his gloves. “Margaret,” he said, his voice thick with emotion, “I’m going down to Helstone tomorrow—to see the old place. Would you like to come with me? Or would it be too painful? Speak plainly. Don’t hold back.”

“Oh, Mr. Bell,” she murmured, unable to speak further. But she reached out and gently kissed his gnarled, gouty hand.

“Come, come,” he said, blushing with discomfort. “That’s enough.”

“I suppose your aunt Shaw will trust you with me,” he continued. “We’ll leave early tomorrow morning—we should arrive about two o’clock. We’ll take a light meal, order dinner at the little inn—the Lennard Arms, as it used to be called—and walk through the woods to work up an appetite. Can you manage it, Margaret? I know it will be hard for both of us—but it will be a joy for me, at least. We’ll dine on venison if we can get it, then I’ll rest while you visit old friends. I’ll bring you back safe and sound—unless the railway fails, which I won’t risk. I’ll insure your life for a thousand pounds before we start, just to ease your family’s mind. Otherwise, I promise to return you to Mrs. Shaw by lunchtime on Friday. So—if you say yes—I’ll go upstairs and tell her.”

“It’s no use trying to express how much I’d love it,” Margaret said, her voice breaking.

“Well then,” Mr. Bell replied, “prove your gratitude by keeping those tearful springs dry for the next two days. If you don’t, I’ll feel strange myself about the lachrymal ducts—and I don’t care for that.”

“I won’t cry a single drop,” she promised, blinking rapidly to clear her lashes, and forcing a smile.

“There’s my good girl,” he said, pleased. “Then let’s go upstairs and settle it.”

Margaret stood beside him, her heart fluttering with excitement and sorrow, as he discussed the plan with Aunt Shaw. The older woman was at first startled, then uncertain—weighing propriety against affection, duty against desire. She hesitated, unable to decide whether it was right or wrong, proper or improper. Only after Margaret’s safe return and the joyful success of the journey would she feel confident enough to declare: “I’m certain it was a kind thought of Mr. Bell’s—and exactly what I’d hoped for Margaret, a change she truly needed after all this anxiety.”

“So on those happy days of yore
Oft as I dare to dwell once more,
Still must I miss the friends so tried,
Whom Death has severed from my side.

But ever when true friendship binds,
Spirit it is that spirit finds;
In spirit then our bliss we found,
In spirit yet to them I’m bound.”

Margaret was ready long before the appointed hour, and had time enough to weep quietly when no one watched, and to smile brightly when someone looked her way. Her last worry was that they might be too late for the train—but no! They arrived in good time, and she sat down at last, heart light and breath free, opposite Mr. Bell in the carriage, as the train pulled away from the station. Past familiar landmarks they raced: southern towns and hamlets bathed in golden sunlight, their red-tiled roofs glowing like embers beneath a clear blue sky—a world apart from the gray slate roofs of the north. Pigeons drifted lazily around crooked gables, fluffing their feathers in the warmth, as if soaking up every ray. At each stop, only a few people lingered—no crowds, no rush. It felt almost as though these quiet souls were too content with home to want to travel. Later in the year, this line would buzz with holidaymakers, but now it moved slowly, gently, unlike the relentless pace of northern rails. At nearly every halt, a man or two stood idle, hands in pockets, staring at the track as if watching the train pass was the most important thing in life. Margaret wondered what he’d do when the train was gone—what could possibly hold his attention beyond the empty platform, the sheds, and distant fields?

The air shimmered over the sun-drenched land. Farm after farm slid past, each one stirring memories—of German idylls, of *Herman and Dorothea*, of *Evangeline*. Then, suddenly, she was roused from her reverie. This was the stop for Helstone. The moment came—and with it, emotions sharp and tangled, neither joy nor sorrow, but something deeper. Every mile carried echoes of the past, cherished beyond measure, yet mourned for being gone. She had last traveled this road with her father and mother—on a bleak day, her own heart heavy with despair, but at least they were there. Now she was alone. An orphan. They had slipped from her life, vanished from the world. And here, the road shone in full summer glory, unchanged, untouched by time. The trees, the turns, the very light—all exactly as they had been years ago. Nature did not age. It only renewed itself.

Mr. Bell sensed what stirred within her and said nothing. He knew better than to intrude.

They drove to the Lennard Arms—part inn, part farmhouse—set slightly apart from the road, as if its owner preferred solitude to show. The house faced the village green, where an ancient lime tree stood, its branches thick with shade, its roots cradling the weathered crest of the Lennard family. The door hung open, but no one rushed forward to greet them. When the landlady finally appeared—after they’d already begun unpacking—she welcomed them warmly, as if they were old friends, and apologized for her delay: “It’s hay-time,” she explained, “and I’ve been packing baskets for the men. Didn’t hear the wheels, the road’s soft grass.”

“Why, bless me!” she exclaimed, catching sight of Margaret in the dim parlour. “It’s Miss Hale, Jenny!” she called to her daughter. “Come here quick—it’s Miss Hale!” Then she stepped forward, clasping Margaret’s hand with maternal warmth.

“And how are you all? How’s the Vicar? Miss Dixon? The Vicar above all! God bless him—we’ve never stopped missing him since he left.”

Margaret tried to speak of her father’s death—her mother’s passing, she saw, the woman already knew. But the words caught in her throat. She touched her black dress and whispered, “Papa.”

“Surely not, sir?” Mrs. Purkis turned to Mr. Bell, eyes wide with dread. “A gentleman passed through last winter—maybe spring—who told us about Mr. Hale and Miss Margaret. Said Mrs. Hale had gone—poor lady. But never a word of the Vicar being ill!”

“It is so,” Mr. Bell replied gently. “He died suddenly while visiting me in Oxford. A good man. Many of us would give much to end as calmly as he did. Come, Margaret, my dear—your father was my oldest friend, and you’re my goddaughter. That’s why I thought we’d come together to see the old place. And I know you’ll find comfort here—good rooms, a fine dinner. You don’t remember me, I see, but my name is Bell. I’ve stayed here once or twice when the parsonage was full. Your mother always kept the best ale.”

“To be sure,” Mrs. Purkis said, stepping back. “I beg your pardon—I was so taken with Miss Hale. Let me show you to your room, miss, where you can take off your bonnet and wash your face. Just this morning I dropped fresh roses into the water jug—thought perhaps someone might come. Nothing smells sweeter than rose-scented water in spring. To think the Vicar is gone! Well, we must all die, but that gentleman said he was recovering after Mrs. Hale’s passing.”

“Come down later, Mrs. Purkis,” Mr. Bell said. “After you’ve seen to Miss Hale, I’d like to talk about dinner.”

Margaret’s bedroom had a small casement window almost blocked by climbing vines and roses. But pushing them aside, she could glimpse the tops of the parsonage chimneys rising above the trees, the lines of the roof still familiar through the leaves.

“Aye,” Mrs. Purkis said, smoothing the bed and sending her daughter for lavender-scented towels. “Times have changed, miss. Our new Vicar has seven children, and he’s building a nursery—right where the arbour and tool-house used to stand. New grates, plate-glass windows in the drawing-room. He and his wife are lively folk—do a great deal of good, or so they say. If not, I’d call it turning things upside down for little reason. The new Vicar’s a teetotaller and magistrate. His wife has recipes for saving money—makes bread without yeast. They both talk at once, like a storm, and knock one down. Only when they’re gone, and peace returns, do you realize what you might have said. He’ll be checking the men’s cans in the hayfield—then there’ll be trouble because it’s not ginger-beer. But I can’t help it. My mother and grandmother sent strong ale to the workers, and took salts when ill. I’ll go on in their ways, even if Mrs. Hepworth wants to give me comfits instead of medicine—pleasant, she says, but I don’t trust it. But I must go, miss, though I’d love to hear more.”

Mr. Bell had strawberries and cream, brown bread, milk, and a Stilton cheese waiting for Margaret when she came downstairs—along with a bottle of port for himself. After their simple meal, they set out walking, uncertain which path to choose, so many memories tugged at them.

“Shall we go past the vicarage?” Mr. Bell asked.

“No, not yet,” Margaret said. “Let’s go this way—make a circle, and return that way.”

Here and there, trees had fallen in the autumn before. A rough cottage, once squat and crumbling, had disappeared. Margaret missed each one—each felt like a lost friend. They passed the spot where she and Mr. Lennox had sketched. The great beech tree, scarred by lightning, was gone. The old man who lived in the ruinous cottage was dead. The cottage had been torn down, replaced by a neat, tidy house. A small garden now grew where the tree had stood.

“I didn’t think I’d grown so old,” Margaret murmured, sighing.

“Yes,” Mr. Bell said softly. “It’s the first changes that make time seem mysterious to the young. Later, we grow numb to it. I accept change as natural. Instability is familiar to me. To you, it’s fresh and painful.”

“Let’s go see little Susan,” Margaret said, leading him up a grassy path under the shadow of a forest glade.

“With all my heart,” he replied. “Though I have no idea who little Susan is. But I have a fondness for Susans—for simple Susan’s sake.”

“My little Susan was disappointed when I left without saying goodbye. It’s haunted me—how I might have spared her pain with just a little effort. But it’s far. Are you sure you won detail?”

“Quite sure. Unless you walk too fast. Here, there’s no view worth stopping for. You’d think it romantic to walk with a person ‘fat and scant o’ breath’—if I were Hamlet, Prince of Denmark. Have mercy on my frailties for his sake.”

“I’ll walk slower—for your sake. I like you twenty times better than Hamlet.”

“On the principle that a living ass is better than a dead lion?”

“Perhaps. I don’t analyze my feelings.”

“I’m content to accept your liking me—without dissecting it. But we needn’t crawl along.”

“Very well. Walk at your own pace. I’ll follow. Or stop and meditate, like the Hamlet you compare yourself to—if I go too fast.”

“Thank you. But since my mother hasn’t murdered my father and married my uncle, I wouldn’t know what to ponder—unless it’s whether we’ll have a well-cooked dinner.”

“What do you think?”

“I’m hopeful. She was famed for cooking, according to Helstone opinion.”

“But have you considered the distraction of haymaking?”

Margaret felt his kindness in trying to keep conversation light, to distract her from the weight of memory. Yet she would have preferred silence—perhaps even solitude—over this gentle companionship.

They reached the cottage where Susan’s widowed mother lived. Susan wasn’t there—she’d gone to school. Margaret was disappointed. The woman noticed and began to apologize.

“Oh, it’s quite right,” Margaret said quickly. “I’m glad to hear it. I should have known. She used to stay home.”

“She did,” the woman said. “And I miss her. I taught her what little I knew at night. Not much, to be sure. But she was becoming such a helpful girl—now I miss her sore. But she’s far ahead of me in learning.” She sighed.

“I’m all wrong,” Mr. Bell muttered. “Don’t mind me. I’m a hundred years behind the times. But I’d say the child was getting a better, simpler, more natural education staying home—helping her mother, reading a chapter of the New Testament each night—than any schooling under the sun.”

Margaret didn’t reply—she didn’t want to prolong the debate in front of the mother. Instead, she asked, “How is old Betty Barnes?”

“I don’t know,” the woman said sharply. “We’re not friends.”

“Why not?” Margaret asked—once the village peacemaker.

“She stole my cat.”

“Did she know it was yours?”

“I don’t know. I reckon not.”

“Well, couldn’t you get it back when you told her?”

“No! For she’d burnt it.”

“Burnt it?” Margaret and Mr. Bell gasped.

“Roasted it!” the woman clarified.

It was no explanation. After questioning, Margaret learned the horrifying truth: Betty Barnes, frightened that her husband would discover she’d lent his Sunday clothes to a gipsy fortune-teller (promising they’d be returned before he missed them), resorted to a local superstition. According to belief, the screams of a cat boiled or roasted alive could compel dark forces to grant a wish. So she sacrificed the animal. The woman believed it worked. Her only regret was that her cat had been chosen. Margaret listened in horror, trying to reason with her—failing utterly. She guided the woman through logical steps, but at the end, she simply repeated: “It were cruel, sure. I wouldn’t do it myself. But nothing like it for getting what you want. I’ve heard it all my life. But it were cruel, for all that.”

Margaret gave up, heart sick.

“You’re a good girl not to mock me,” Mr. Bell said.

“How? What do you mean?”

“I admit I’m wrong about schooling. Anything rather than raise that child in such practical paganism.”

“Oh! Poor little Susan. I must see her. Would you mind calling at the school?”

“Not at all. I’m curious to see what she’s learning.”

They walked on in silence, through shaded glens whose soft greenery could not ease the shock of what they’d heard—the cruelty, the utter lack of empathy for suffering.

As they emerged from the woods, the murmur of voices rose like bees in a hive. The school stood on the village green. The door was open. A brisk woman in black greeted them—Mrs. Hepworth, the current Vicar’s wife, and Margaret’s mother’s successor. Margaret hesitated, then stepped forward, meeting bright eyes and whispers: “It’s Miss Hale.”

Mrs. Hepworth smiled warmly. “Your father, I presume, Miss Hale. I see it in the likeness. I’m delighted to see you, sir, and so will the Vicar be.”

Margaret corrected her gently: “It’s not my father. He’s passed. I’m afraid…”

She trailed off, wondering how Mr. Hale could have borne returning, if he’d believed it was his own daughter. She didn’t hear what Mrs. Hepworth said—left it to Mr. Bell to respond. She scanned the room, looking for old faces.

“Ah! I see you’d like to teach a class, Miss Hale,” Mrs. Hepworth said. “First class, stand up for a parsing lesson with Miss Hale.”

Poor Margaret—her visit was sentimental, not inspecting. But seeing the eager faces, some baptized by her father, she sat down, tracing features, holding Susan’s hand briefly, unseen. The class searched for books. Mrs. Hepworth chatted with Mr. Bell, explaining the Phonetic system, showing him a conversation with the Inspector.

Margaret leaned over her book, hearing only the hum of voices. Memories flooded in. Tears filled her eyes—until a girl stumbled over the word “a.”

“A—an indefinite article,” Margaret said softly.

“I beg your pardon,” Mrs. Hepworth snapped, alert. “We are taught by Mr. Milsome to call ‘a’ an—anyone remember?”

“An adjective absolute!” half a dozen voices shouted.

Margaret sat abashed. The children knew more than she did. Mr. Bell turned away, smiling.

She spoke no more during the lesson. Afterward, she visited a few old favorites—girls growing fast, slipping from childhood into youth. She was fading from their memories as quickly as they were from hers. Still, she was glad to see them again—even if sadness tinged her joy.

When school ended, it was early afternoon. Mrs. Hepworth invited them to the parsonage—to see “alterations” (though “improvements” had nearly slipped out). Margaret cared little for the changes—they clashed with her memories. But she longed to see the old house once more, even knowing the pain it would bring.

The parsonage was transformed—inside and out. The garden, once perfectly trimmed, was scattered with children’s toys: marbles, hoops, a straw hat crushed onto a rosebush, snapping a long flowering stem. The hall, once quiet and orderly, now bore signs of boisterous, healthy childhood.

“Ah!” Mrs. Hepworth said. “You must excuse the mess, Miss Hale. Once the nursery is finished, I’ll insist on order. We’re converting your room into a nursery. How did you manage without one?”

“We were only two,” Margaret said. “You have seven, I suppose?”

“Seven. Look—here we’re adding a window facing the road. Mr. Hepworth is spending a fortune. The house was barely livable when we came—for a family like ours, of course.”

Every room was altered. The former study—Mr. Hale’s—was now open to the road. The new window offered views of sheep straying toward the “Jolly Forester.” The Vicar, ever vigilant, watched from his desk, hat and stick ready to chase them back. The whole family was energetic, loud, kind-hearted, but lacking in subtlety. Margaret feared Mrs. Hepworth would notice Mr. Bell’s irony in praising everything that offended him. But no—she took every word seriously. Margaret reproached him as they walked back to the inn.

“Don’t scold, Margaret. It was all for you. If she hadn’t shown every change with such pride, I could have behaved better. But if you must preach, wait till after dinner. It’ll put me to sleep—and aid digestion.”

They were both tired. Margaret, especially, was weary. She no longer wanted to wander through the woods near her childhood home. And somehow, the visit to Helstone had not been what she expected. Change was everywhere—small, but deep. Families shifted with absence, death, marriage. Time moved on, carrying people from childhood to youth, manhood to age, until they fell, ripe, into the earth. Places changed—trees gone, paths narrowed, light streaming where shadows once reigned. Progress, they called it. But Margaret sighed for the old charm, the quiet gloom, the untamed lanes of her youth.

She sat by the window on the little settle, watching twilight gather, matching the quiet ache in her heart. Mr. Bell slept soundly after his long day. Finally, he stirred as the tea tray arrived, brought by a flushed country girl—evidently taking a break from hay-making.

“Experience, like a pale musician, holds
A dulcimer of patience in his hand;
Whence harmonies we cannot understand,
Of God’s will in His worlds, the strain unfolds
In sad, perplexed minors.”

It was around this time that Dixon returned from Milton, resuming her place as Margaret’s maid. She arrived laden with news—endless, vivid tales of life in the industrial town. She spoke of Martha, who had moved into Miss Thornton’s home following her marriage, recounting every detail of the wedding: the bridesmaids’ gowns, the breakfasts, the guests, and how some thought Mr. Thornton had gone too far in splendour, especially after the losses from the strike and the costly failure of several contracts. Then came stories of the auction—how certain cherished household items, once prized by Dixon herself, fetched paltry sums, which she found deeply unjust given the wealth of Milton’s elite. Mrs. Thornton had appeared one day, securing a few fine bargains; the next, Mr. Thornton himself came, so eager for certain pieces he ended up outbidding himself—a scene that delighted onlookers. “That,” Dixon declared with satisfaction, “made things even. If Mrs. Thornton paid too little, Mr. Thornton paid too much.”

Mr. Bell, meanwhile, had sent a flurry of orders regarding the books—so particular and exacting that it was nearly impossible to follow. “If he’d come himself,” Dixon sighed, “it would have been plain enough. But letters—ah! they’re always more trouble than they’re worth.”

She had little to say about the Higginses. Her memory, though sharp when it came to matters of status and society, failed her entirely when it touched upon those beneath her notice. Nicholas, she said, was well enough—had called several times to inquire after Miss Margaret, the only person who ever did, save once, Mr. Thornton. As for Mary? “Oh, she’s very well,” Dixon declared, “a great, stout, slatternly thing!” She then added, hesitating, perhaps remembering a dream or a half-heard rumour, that Mary might have taken work at Mr. Thornton’s mill—because, she supposed, her father wanted her to learn how to cook. But what sense that made, Dixon couldn’t say. Margaret, hearing this, found it no less absurd—yet not entirely unbelievable. Still, it was comforting to have someone now with whom she could speak of Milton, its people, its ways.

Dixon, however, was not particularly fond of such talk. She preferred to dwell instead on the speeches of Mr. Bell, from which she had formed a theory: that he meant to make Margaret his heir. She hinted at this often, cloaked in suspicion or quiet assertion, but Margaret gave her no encouragement. No matter how subtly she probed, Margaret remained silent, leaving Dixon’s hopes unfulfilled.

All this while, Margaret carried within her a strange, nameless longing—to hear that Mr. Bell had gone to Milton. It had been agreed between them, during their conversation at Helstone, that any explanation she desired should be delivered personally to Mr. Thornton, and never forced upon him. Yet Mr. Bell had not yet made that journey. He wrote occasionally—sometimes long letters, sometimes brief—but none mentioned Milton. Though Margaret did not admit it even to herself, each letter brought a quiet disappointment. He was not going. At least, he said nothing of it.

His letters lately seemed unlike him—shorter, more weary, tinged with a bitterness unfamiliar to his usual tone. He spoke little of the future, more often lamenting the past, as if tired of the present. Margaret began to suspect he was unwell. When she asked about his health, he replied with a short note: “An old-fashioned complaint called the spleen. I’m suffering from it—whether mental or physical, you may decide. But I shall indulge myself in grumbling without sending bulletins every time.” After that, she said no more.

Then one day, Edith let slip a fragment of a conversation she’d had with Mr. Bell during his last visit to London. It sparked in Margaret a sudden, vivid idea: he had considered taking her to visit her brother Frederick and his new wife in Cadiz during the autumn. She questioned Edith relentlessly until the younger woman grew exhausted, insisting there was nothing more to recall. All he had said, she repeated, was that he half-thought he might go—to hear Frederick’s own account of the mutiny—and that it would be a good chance for Margaret to meet her new sister-in-law. “He always travels somewhere during the holidays,” Edith added, “and why not Spain?” That was all.

But Margaret could not contain her excitement. The mere possibility of Spain—of seeing Frederick again, of meeting his wife, of escaping the quiet monotony of her days—filled her with a quiet joy. She tried to hide it, but when she dressed for dinner, she could not resist asking Dixon, “Wouldn’t you like to see Master Frederick and his new wife very much indeed?”

“Oh, Miss,” Dixon replied, “she’s a Papist, isn’t she?”

“I believe—yes, certainly,” Margaret answered, her enthusiasm dimmed slightly by the thought.

“And they live in a Popish country?”

“Yes.”

“Well then,” Dixon declared, “I must say my soul is dearer to me than even Master Frederick himself. I should be in constant terror of being converted.”

“Oh,” Margaret said gently, “I don’t know that I’m going. And if I do, I’m not so delicate a lady as to need you by my side. No, dear old Dixon—you’ll have a long holiday, if we go. But I’m afraid it’s a long ‘if.’”

Dixon did not like this. First, she disliked the affectionate “dear old Dixon”—a term Miss Hale used freely for anyone she liked, but which stung Dixon, who felt she was still in the prime of life, barely fifty. Second, she resented being so easily convinced. For all her piety, she harboured a secret curiosity about Spain—the Inquisition, the mysteries of Catholicism. So, after clearing her throat as if to signal resolve, she asked, “Do you think, Miss, if I were careful never to see a priest or enter one of their churches, I’d be safe from conversion?”

Master Frederick, after all, had changed without warning.

“I fancy it was love that first turned him,” Margaret said, sighing.

“Indeed, Miss!” Dixon exclaimed. “Well, I can avoid priests and churches—but love sneaks in unawares! I think it’s best I stay behind.”

Margaret avoided dwelling too long on the idea of Spain. It was a bright distraction, a château en Espagne, perhaps—but it lifted her mind from the restless ache of wanting clarity from Mr. Thornton. Mr. Bell remained in Oxford, showing no signs of moving to Milton. A quiet restraint held Margaret back—she could not ask, could not hint, could not even mention Edith’s revelation. He had never spoken of Spain at Helstone, not during that golden afternoon. It might have been a fleeting whim. Yet if true, what a relief it would be—from the slow weight of waiting, the silence, the uncertainty.

One of the greatest joys in Margaret’s life at present was Edith’s son. He was a delight to both parents when well-behaved, but when his temper flared, Edith would collapse in despair, calling out, “Oh dear, what shall I do with him? Do, Margaret, please ring for Hanley.”

But Margaret loved him most in those storms. She would carry him away to a private room, where they wrestled not with force, but with quiet strength. She held firm, using every charm, every gentle persuasion, until he calmed—then rubbed his hot, tear-streaked face against hers, kissing her, clinging to her, often falling asleep in her arms. These moments were precious—tasting, as she believed, of a love she would never know.

And then there was Mr. Henry Lennox. His frequent presence added a new dimension to the household. Margaret found him colder than before, yet sharper, more brilliant. His intellect sparkled, his knowledge wide-ranging, enriching conversations that might otherwise have been dull. She noticed, too, a faint disdain in his manner toward his brother and sister-in-law—what he saw as frivolity, lack of purpose. Once or twice, in her presence, he questioned Captain Lennox sharply: “Do you mean to give up your profession entirely?” When the captain replied that he had enough to live on, Mr. Lennox curled his lip. “And is that all you live for?”

Yet the brothers were close—bound in that way two men are when one leads and the other willingly follows. Mr. Lennox advanced steadily in his career, cultivating connections with cold precision, keen-eyed, ambitious, proud, and sarcastic. Since their long talk about Frederick, the only real conversation they’d had, Margaret had seen little of him beyond the routine of shared family life. But that was enough to ease the awkwardness between them—her shyness, his pride, both softened by time and proximity.

They met often, of course, but she sensed he avoided being alone with her. They had drifted apart—no longer aligned in opinion or taste. Yet when he made a particularly clever remark, she noticed his glance seeking hers—brief, fleeting, but unmistakable. And in the quiet exchanges of family life, she knew he listened to her—not out of affection, but with a reluctant respect, hidden beneath a mask of indifference.

“My own, my father’s friend! I cannot part with thee! I ne’er have shown, thou ne’er hast known, How dear thou art to me.” —ANON.

The dinner parties hosted by Mrs. Lennox were a well-ordered symphony of society: her guests brought grace and charm; Captain Lennox offered an easy mastery of current affairs; and Mr. Henry Lennox, along with the select circle of rising men welcomed into their fold, contributed wit, intellect, and a refined fluency that never descended into pedantry. Conversation flowed like wine—lively, polished, sparkling. Yet for Margaret Hale, even in this glittering world, there was no peace. Every gift, every sentiment, every learned pursuit was reduced to mere ornament. The soul beneath—the quiet fire of truth, reverence, or moral depth—was consumed in fleeting brilliance. Art was discussed not for its meaning, but for its surface beauty; lofty ideas were celebrated in company, then forgotten in solitude. Their minds, sharp and capable, were spent on elegance rather than insight, on words instead of feeling.

One evening, after the gentlemen had joined the ladies in the drawing-room, Mr. Lennox approached Margaret. It was the first time since her return to Harley Street that he had spoken to her without formality or hesitation.

“You did not look pleased at what Shirley said during dinner,” he observed.

“Didn’t I? My face must be very expressive,” she replied, smiling faintly.

“It always was. It has not lost its eloquence.”

“I didn’t like,” she said quickly, “his manner of defending something he knew to be wrong—so clearly wrong—even in jest.”

“But it was clever. Each word landed perfectly. Did you catch the felicity of his phrases?”

“Yes.”

“And yet you despise them, I suppose?” he added, with a quiet smile. “Please don’t hesitate, even though he is my friend.”

There—she paused. He waited, sensing she might continue. But she only flushed, turned away, and walked toward the window. As she did, she heard him speak again, low and clear:

“If your disapproval lies in my tone, or in how I think, will you do me the justice to tell me plainly? That way, I may learn to please you.”

For weeks, there had been no word from Mr. Bell about his journey to Milton. He had mentioned it briefly at Helstone, as something likely to happen soon. But perhaps he had settled matters through letters. Margaret believed he would avoid Milton if he could—he disliked the place, and worse, he would not grasp the deep significance she attached to speaking with him in person. She knew he would feel the necessity of doing so, but whether in summer, autumn, or winter made little difference to her. Now it was August. Still, no mention had been made of the Spanish trip he’d alluded to Edith. Margaret began to accept that the hope of seeing him again might fade into nothingness.

Then one morning, a letter arrived. He wrote that he intended to come to London the following week. He wanted to see her about a plan he had formed—and, in a lighter tone, confessed he meant to consult a doctor. He had begun to agree with her: when irritability overtook him, it was easier to blame ill health than temper.

There was a forced cheerfulness in the letter, which Margaret noticed only later. At the time, her thoughts were caught up in Edith’s exclamations.

“Coming to town!” Edith gasped. “Oh dear! And I’m so exhausted by the heat—I can’t possibly host another dinner. Besides, everyone’s gone except us two poor fools who can’t decide where to go. There’ll be nobody to greet him!”

“I’m certain he’d prefer dining with us alone,” Margaret said firmly, “to meeting any strangers, however pleasant. If he’s unwell, he won’t want invitations. I’m glad he finally admitted it. I’ve known he was ill ever since reading his letters—but he wouldn’t answer me when I asked. I had no one else to turn to.”

“Oh, he’s not seriously ill,” Edith insisted. “If he were, he wouldn’t be thinking of Spain.”

“He never mentions Spain,” Margaret said.

“No—but his plan must concern it. Still, would you really travel in such weather?”

“Oh, it will cool down soon enough. Yes—just think. I’m afraid I’ve wished and thought too much—too passionately, too wilfully. That kind of longing either brings disappointment or fulfillment, down to the letter, but never true joy.”

“That’s superstition, Margaret,” Edith said gently.

“No,” Margaret replied, “I don’t believe it is. But it should warn me. It should stop me from giving in to such intense desires. It’s like saying, ‘Give me children, or else I die.’ Mine is, ‘Let me go to Cadiz, or else I die.’”

“My dear Margaret!” Edith exclaimed. “You’ll be persuaded to stay there forever—and then what shall I do? Oh, I wish I could find someone here for you to marry—someone I could count on!”

“I shall never marry,” Margaret declared.

“Nonsense! Double nonsense! Sholto says you’re such a draw, he knows half a dozen men who’d visit just to see you next year.”

Margaret stiffened. “Do you know, Edith, I sometimes think your life in Corfu has taught you—”

“Well?”

“Just a shade or two of coarseness.”

Edith burst into tears, sobbing violently, declaring that Margaret no longer loved her, that she was no longer her friend. Margaret, struck by the sudden intensity of her sister’s distress, realized she had spoken too harshly in her own wounded pride. She ended up spending the rest of the day tending to Edith, who lay on the sofa like a broken thing, sighing deeply, until she finally fell asleep.

Mr. Bell did not arrive on the day he had rescheduled. The next morning came a letter from Wallis, his servant. His master had been unwell for some time—that was why he had delayed his journey. But just as he was preparing to leave for London, he suffered a sudden apoplectic seizure. The doctors believed he would not survive the night. By the time Miss Hale received the letter, they feared, he would already be dead.

Margaret read the letter at breakfast and turned deathly pale. Without a word, she handed it to Edith and left the room.

Edith was devastated, weeping in a frightened, childlike way that upset her husband deeply. Mrs. Shaw was eating alone in her room, and thus Sholto found himself tasked with calming his wife, who was overwhelmed by the sudden proximity of death—a concept she had never truly faced before. Here was a man they expected to dine with them today now lying dying—or dead.

It took some time before she could think of Margaret. Then she rose, hurried upstairs, and found her in her bedroom. Dixon was packing a few personal items. Margaret was frantically putting on her bonnet, tears streaming down her face, her hands trembling so badly she could barely tie the strings.

“Oh, dear Margaret! This is terrible! What are you doing? Are you going out? Sholto could telegraph—do anything you want!”

“I’m going to Oxford,” she said, voice firm despite her distress. “There’s a train in half an hour. Dixon offered to go with me, but I could have gone alone. I must see him again. He may still be alive, needing care. He’s been like a father to me. Don’t stop me, Edith.”

“But I must,” Edith insisted. “Mamma won’t approve at all. Come talk to her, Margaret. You don’t know where you’re going. I wouldn’t mind if he had a house of his own—but in his Fellow’s rooms? Please come to mamma and ask her first. It’ll take only a minute.”

Margaret hesitated, then gave in. The moment passed. Mrs. Shaw, thrown into confusion and hysteria by the news, could not act decisively. Time slipped away.

But another train was due in a couple of hours. After heated debate about propriety and decorum, it was decided that Captain Lennox would accompany Margaret—because she was resolute: she would go, by herself or with someone, on the next train, regardless of social conventions. Her father’s friend, her own beloved mentor, lay near death. The thought struck her with such force that she surprised herself with the strength of her resolve.

Five minutes before departure, she sat across from Captain Lennox in the railway carriage.

She found comfort in knowing she had gone—even if only to hear that he had died that night. She saw the rooms he had occupied, and from that moment forward, they became sacred in her memory, linked forever to her father and to the man who had stood beside him in loyalty and affection.

They had promised Edith they would return for dinner if the worst had happened. So, after a long, silent farewell to the familiar, gentle face that had once smiled and joked with her father, they left.

Captain Lennox slept during the journey home. Margaret sat quietly, allowing herself to grieve. She reflected on the year that had brought so many sorrows—loss upon loss, each one reopening wounds not yet healed. But as she stepped into the warm, welcoming light of her aunt’s home, hearing Edith’s tender voice, Sholto’s joyful greeting, and seeing the cheerful rooms filled with love and sorrowful kindness, she stirred from her numb despair.

She took Edith’s usual seat on the sofa. Sholto was carefully instructed to bring Aunt Margaret’s tea. By the time she went upstairs to dress, she could whisper a prayer of gratitude—for having spared her dear friend a prolonged or painful end.

“And down the sunny beach she paces slowly,
With many doubtful pauses by the way;
Grief hath an influence so hushed and holy.”

“Is not Margaret the heiress?” Edith whispered to her husband one evening, after they had returned from their sorrowful journey to Oxford. She had drawn his tall head down toward hers, standing on tiptoe, as if afraid of what she was about to say. “Don’t be shocked,” she pleaded, “but I must ask.”

Captain Lennox, however, was entirely in the dark. If he’d ever heard of such a thing, it had slipped from memory. A Fellow of a modest college could hardly leave much behind—certainly not enough to matter. And besides, he’d never expected her to pay for her board. Two hundred and fifty pounds a year seemed absurd, especially since she didn’t drink wine.

Edith stepped back, her spirits slightly dimmed, her romantic dreams shattered. A week later, she bounded into the room with a low, playful curtsey. “I was right, and you were wrong, noble Captain! Margaret has received a letter from a solicitor—she’s the residuary legatee. The specific legacies amount to about two thousand pounds, and the remainder, at current values in Milton, is close to forty thousand.”

“Indeed!” he replied, surprised. “And how does she bear this sudden fortune?”

“Oh, she says she knew she would inherit something—but not this much. She looks pale, almost frightened. But that’s only nerves, you know. It will pass. I left Mama pouring out congratulations like water, and slipped away to tell you.”

It was assumed, without question, that Mr. Henry Lennox should now become Margaret’s legal advisor. She knew nothing of business matters and relied upon him for nearly every decision. He selected her solicitor, brought documents for her signature, and took great delight in instructing her in the mysteries of law—explaining each clause, each phrase, as though unveiling sacred symbols.

One day, Edith said mischievously, “Henry, do you know what I hope and expect all these long talks with Margaret will end in?”

“No,” he answered, flushing. “And I wish you wouldn’t tell me.”

“Oh, very well,” she said, smiling. “Then I won’t warn Sholto to stop inviting Mr. Montagu so often.”

“Just as you please,” he replied, forcing calmness. “What you imagine may or may not come to pass. But before I commit myself, I want to see my ground clear. Ask whom you will—it may not be courteous, but if you interfere, you’ll ruin it. She’s been distant with me for a long time, only just beginning to thaw from her Zenobia-like pride. She has the makings of a Cleopatra—if only she were a little more pagan.”

“For my part,” Edith retorted, a little tartly, “I’m glad she’s Christian. I know so few!”

That autumn, there was no Spain for Margaret. Though she clung to the faint hope that Frederick might be summoned to Paris—where she could have joined him under safe escort—no such chance came. Instead of Cadiz, she found herself bound for Cromer. Her aunt Shaw and the Lennoxes were going there, and though they had always encouraged her to join them, they made only half-hearted efforts to support her own desire to travel separately. Yet perhaps Cromer was best suited to her needs. She required both rest and physical renewal. Among the vanished hopes was the belief that Mr. Bell would one day reveal to Mr. Thornton the true circumstances surrounding the tragic accident that led to Leonards’ death. She had longed for him to understand—not merely to forgive, but to grasp the full truth of what she had done and why. Such knowledge would have brought peace, a quiet certainty she feared she would carry through life unless she could resolve never to dwell on it again.

But time had passed too long. With Mr. Bell gone, the only way to explain those events had vanished with him. She could submit to being misunderstood, as so many others did. Reason told her this was common fate; yet her heart still ached with longing—that one day, years hence, before he died, he might know how deeply she had been tempted. She didn’t need to hear that everything was explained—only to know, with absolute certainty, that he understood.

But the wish remained unfulfilled, like so many others. When she accepted this, she turned fully to the present, determined to make the most of the life before her.

She spent long hours sitting on the beach, watching the waves crash endlessly against the pebbled shore. Sometimes she gazed beyond, at the distant swell where sea met sky, listening unconsciously to the ceaseless rhythm—the eternal psalm rising up from the ocean. She felt calmed, though she couldn’t say why.

She sat listlessly, hands clasped around her knees, while her aunt ran errands and Edith and Captain Lennox rode far and wide along the coast and into the countryside. Nurses passing with children would whisper among themselves, wondering what could possibly hold her attention so steadily, day after day.

At dinner, Margaret remained silent and withdrawn. Edith declared her moped, and welcomed her husband’s suggestion with relief: that Mr. Henry Lennox be invited to stay at Cromer for a week upon his return from Scotland in October.

Yet during these quiet hours, Margaret reorganized her thoughts, placing past events in their proper order—understanding their causes and meanings, both for her past and her future. Those solitary moments by the sea were not wasted. Anyone who could read her face would have seen the subtle transformation taking place.

Mr. Henry Lennox noticed it immediately. “The sea has done Miss Hale an immense deal of good,” he remarked when she first left the room after his arrival. “She looks ten years younger than she did in Harley Street.”

“That’s the bonnet I got her!” Edith exclaimed triumphantly. “I knew it would suit her the moment I saw it.”

“I beg your pardon,” Mr. Lennox replied, with the half-contemptuous, half-indulgent tone he often used toward Edith. “But I believe I can distinguish between the charm of a dress and the beauty of a woman. No mere bonnet could have made Miss Hale’s eyes so luminous and gentle, her lips so full and red, her face so radiant with peace and light. She is like—and yet more than—” he lowered his voice, “the Margaret Hale of Helstone.”

From that moment, the ambitious and clever man set his mind fully upon winning her. He admired her delicate grace, her intelligence—qualities he believed could be guided toward the goals he himself pursued. He saw her fortune not as separate from her worth, but as part of her complete character and position. Still, he was keenly aware of the rise it would bring him—a poor barrister suddenly elevated by her wealth. In time, he thought, he would achieve success and honors sufficient to repay her, with interest, for the advantage she had given him.
