He had visited Milton on business related to her estate, returning from Scotland. With the sharp eye of a skilled lawyer, he observed how the value of her land and property increased yearly in the thriving town. He was pleased to see that the old awkwardness of their meeting at Helstone was fading, replaced by a natural relationship between client and adviser—one that allowed for greater intimacy.

Margaret was eager to listen whenever he spoke of Milton, even though he had not met the people she knew best. Her aunt and cousin had spoken of the town with disdain—just as Margaret had once felt, ashamed to recall. But Mr. Lennox admired Milton more than she did. He was captivated by its energy, its strength, its relentless drive. Its vivid, almost feverish existence fascinated him. He never tired of describing it. Only Margaret, in her quiet honesty, pointed out the flaw beneath the nobility—the materialism that often lay hidden behind their fierce industry.

Still, when other topics bored her and she gave brief answers, a question about some peculiar trait of Darkshire life would bring back the sparkle to her eyes, the warmth to her cheeks.

When they returned to London, Margaret carried out one of her seaside resolutions: she took charge of her own life. Before going to Cromer, she had obeyed her aunt’s rules as docilely as a child, remembering the night she wept herself to sleep in the Harley Street nursery. But the long hours of reflection had taught her that she must one day answer for her own choices. She began to grapple with the difficult question for any woman: how much of oneself should be surrendered to authority, and how much preserved for personal freedom?

Mrs. Shaw was kindly and good-natured; Edith inherited this quality. Margaret, perhaps, had the most volatile temper of the three—her sharp mind and vivid imagination making her quick to act, her early isolation fostering pride. Yet beneath it all lay an undeniable sweetness, a childlike tenderness that made even her rare moments of defiance endearing. Now, tempered by what the world called her good fortune, she charmed her reluctant aunt into accepting her independence.

She gained recognition of her right to define her own duties.

“Only don’t be strong-minded,” Edith begged. “Mamma wants you to have your own footman—and honestly, I think you’re welcome. They’re such nuisances. But please, darling, don’t go and be strong-minded. That’s the only thing I ask. Footman or no footman, just don’t be strong-minded.”

“Don’t worry, Edith,” Margaret replied. “I’ll faint in your arms at the servants’ dinner the very first chance I get. Then, with Sholto playing with the fire and the baby screaming, you’ll wish for a strong-minded woman—someone who can handle anything.”

“And you won't grow too serious? You’ll still joke and laugh?”

“Not I. I shall be merrier than ever—now that I’ve taken my own path.”

“And you won’t become a figure? Will you let me buy your dresses?”

“Indeed, I mean to buy them myself. You can come with me if you like—but no one else can choose for me.”

“Oh!” Edith laughed. “I was afraid you’d dress in brown and dust-color, so no dirt would show. I’m glad you’re keeping a few vanities—just to remind us of the old Adam.”

“I’ll be exactly the same, Edith—if you and Aunt could only believe it. Only now, since I have neither husband nor child to give me natural duties, I must create my own—besides choosing my own clothes.”

In private conversation, Edith, her mother, and her husband agreed that Margaret’s plans might only strengthen her bond with Henry Lennox. By distancing herself from other potential suitors, she limited her circle. And it was noted that she rarely showed pleasure in anyone’s company except Henry’s—outside their family.

Other admirers, drawn by her beauty or her fortune, were gently repelled by her unconscious air of disdain, drifting instead to other beauties less selective, or heiresses with larger dowries.

Henry and Margaret grew closer, slowly, naturally. Neither was one to allow their feelings to be openly discussed. They walked the fine line between friendship and something deeper—each careful, each guarded, yet unmistakably drawn together by the quiet currents of shared understanding.

“Here we go up, up, up;
And here we go down, down, downee!”

In Milton, the chimneys belched smoke into the grey sky, and the ceaseless thunder of machinery echoed through the streets like a never-ending heartbeat. Iron, steam, and wood moved in endless cycles, driven by no purpose but their own mechanical will. Yet beside them stood men and women—strong, restless, and full of intent—each striving with quiet desperation to carve out a place in a world that offered little comfort or certainty. The town hummed with tension, not from industry alone, but from fear. The air was thick with unspoken questions: *Who has failed? Who is next? What will this mean for me?*

There were few who walked without a goal. No idle strollers, no carefree callers. Every face bore the weight of anxiety. In the Exchange and the Mart, men jostled past one another, eyes sharp, voices low, each eager for news that might shift the balance between survival and ruin. Credit had grown brittle; even the most solid businesses now trembled under the shadow of collapse. Though Milton had so far escaped failure, whispers carried from distant ports—the fall of great shipping houses, the crash of American ventures—spreading dread like a contagion. One man’s downfall could pull others into the abyss.

"Thornton is safe," someone muttered, half to himself. "His business grows every year. He's bold, yes—but he's got sense too."

But then, a step aside, a hushed voice: “He’s spent every penny of his profits on expansion. No reserves. New machinery—cost him a fortune. And if the market turns again… well, let’s just say, the wise know what they’re looking at.”

Mr. Harrison, known as a pessimist, spoke these words. Once a modest inheritor of his father’s trade, he’d clung to old ways, fearing change. He resented those who dared more, who reached farther. But truth was, Mr. Thornton was not as secure as he seemed.

He felt it deeply—not in his pocketbook alone, but in his pride. His reputation, built over years of hard work and shrewd decisions, was now under threat. He had believed that commerce was a level field: any honest, brave man could rise, see the game clearly, and command influence across continents. That was the dream his mother once read aloud to him, as if it were prophecy: *“Her merchants be like princes.”* He had taken it to heart. He wanted his name known in foreign lands, his word trusted as gold. He had labored long and silently toward that end—and now, in his own town, among his own people, he saw how much he truly valued what he had built.

Yet until recently, he had lived in parallel with the lives around him—close, yet separate. Then came Higgins. Not as master and worker, but as two men suddenly face to face, stripped of roles. And in that moment, something shifted. They began to recognize the same human heart beating beneath different clothes. It was a small crack in the wall of separation—a wedge that opened slowly, painfully, but irrevocably.

At first, Thornton had resented Higgins. The strike, a year and a half before, had disrupted orders, wasted time, and damaged his standing. He had imported Irish laborers—unskilled, careless—and their work had ruined shipments meant to be flawless. For months, the fallout weighed on him. When his eyes met Higgins’s, anger stirred—though there was no cause. But he fought it. He resolved not to avoid the man. Instead, he allowed Higgins access, listened when he spoke, treated him with fairness. And slowly, resentment gave way to curiosity.

How, he wondered, could two men working the same trade, striving for the same goals, see their duties so differently? From that question arose a new kind of understanding. Not friendship—yet—but respect. A mutual awareness that neither side knew the whole truth. Each had blind spots. Now, both were learning.

Then came the downturn. Markets crashed. Stocks lost half their value. Orders dried up. Capital tied up in machinery and raw cotton could not be liquidated. Payments for completed work dragged. Bills for cotton came due. Money was scarce. Interest rates soared. Borrowing became desperate. And still, expenses rolled on.

Thornton did not break. He worked night after night, poring over ledgers in his private room long after his family slept. He kept his composure. At home, he remained gentle, calm. At the mill, he said little—but the workers noticed. They saw the strain in his eyes, the shortness of breath, the rare sighs caught behind closed doors. And though he had once inspired fear, now some felt pity.

One evening, Higgins lingered after hours, quietly finishing a task left undone. No one knew. Thornton never learned that it was Higgins, not the overlooker, who had done it. But the thought crossed Higgins’s mind: *I reckon I know who’d have been sorry to see our master sitting there like a piece o’ grey calico.* The old parson would have grieved, he mused, seeing such sorrow on a man’s face.

The next day, Higgins stopped Thornton in Marlborough Street.

“Measter,” he said, halting the man mid-stride. “Have yo’ heerd aught of Miss Marget lately?”

“Miss—who?” Thornton replied, startled.

“Miss Marget—Miss Hale—the oud parson’s daughter. Yo’ know who I mean.”

“Oh!” Thornton’s face softened instantly. The cold rigidity melted. A warmth spread across his features. “She’s my landlord now, Higgins. I hear of her through her agent. She’s well. Among friends. Thank you, Higgins.”

That “thank you” hung in the air. It wasn’t just politeness—it carried feeling. Higgins sensed it. He followed it, like a flicker of light in the dark.

“And she’s not getten married, measter?”

“No,” Thornton said, his expression clouding again.

“There’s talk of it—with a connection of the family.”

“Then she’ll not be coming back to Milton?”

“No.”

Higgins stepped closer. “Stop a minute, measter. Is th’ young gentleman cleared?”

He winked—an odd gesture that only deepened the mystery.

“The young gentleman—I mean Master Frederick. Her brother. He was over here, yo’ know.”

“Over here?”

“Ay. At th’ missus’s death. Yo’ need na be feared o’ my telling. Mary and me, we knew all along. We held our peace—got it through Mary working in th’ house.”

“And he was over. It was her brother.”

“Sure enough. And I reckoned yo’ knowed it, or I’d never ha’ let on. Yo’ knowed she had a brother?”

“Yes. I know all about him. And he was over at Mrs. Hale’s death?”

“Nay! I’m not going to tell more. Maybe I’ve already brought trouble. They kept it close. I nobbut wanted to know if they’d getten him cleared?”

“Not that I know of. I know nothing. I only hear of Miss Hale now—through her lawyer.”

Thornton turned away, returning to his urgent tasks. Higgins stood alone, baffled.

*It was her brother,* Thornton thought, walking quickly. *I am glad.* He had never seen her again since the days of the strike, but knowing this—knowing she had not been alone—brought relief. A small thread of hope woven into the dark fabric of his present despair.

Yet the darkness deepened. His agent had trusted a firm in the American trade. That firm collapsed. Others fell with it. Like dominoes. One failure pulled down the next. How could he stand? Could he survive?

Night after night, he worked. Until dawn crept in through the shutters, pale and cold. He had not slept. He sat at his desk, weary beyond measure.

Then the door opened.

His mother stood there, dressed as she had been the day before. She had not slept either.

Their eyes met. Both were pale, drawn, tired.

“Mother,” he said, “why are you not in bed?”

“Son John,” she replied, “do you think I can rest while you sit awake, burdened with care? You haven’t told me what troubles you. But I see it. These many days, you’ve been tormented.”

“Trade is bad.”

“And you dread——”

“I dread nothing,” he said, lifting his head. “I know no man shall suffer because of me. That was my worry.”

“But how do you stand? Will it be a failure?”

Her voice trembled—unusual for her.

“No,” he said. “Not a failure. I must give up the business. But I will pay everyone. I might redeem myself—I am sorely tempted.”

“How?” she asked, stepping forward, laying her hand on his arm. “John, keep your name! Try all risks for that. How would you redeem it?”

“By a speculation—offered to me. Full of risk. But if successful, it would lift me high above water. No one would ever know I was in straits. Still—if it fails—”

“And if it fails,” she whispered, “what then?”

“Honest men are ruined by rogues,” he said grimly. “As I stand now, every farthing of my creditors’ money is safe. But I don’t know where my own is. It may be gone. I may be penniless. So it is their money I would risk.”

“But if it succeeds—no one need know. Is it so desperate?”

“It would make me rich,” he said. “But I would lose my peace of conscience. I would have run the risk of ruining many for my own gain. Mother, I have decided. You won’t grieve much over leaving this house, will you?”

“No,” she said, tears rising. “But to see you not what you are—this will break my heart. What can you do?”

“Be the same John Thornton. Do right. Make mistakes. Then try again. But it is hard, mother. I have worked. I have planned. I have discovered new powers in my position—too late. All is over. I am too old to begin again with the same heart. It is hard.”

He turned away, covering his face.

“I cannot understand,” she said, voice heavy. “Here is my boy—good son, just man, tender heart—and he fails in everything he sets his mind to. Finds a woman to love—and she does not return it. Works hard—and it comes to nothing. Others grow rich and hold their names high. Why? Where is justice?”

“Shame never touched me,” he murmured.

“But God has been very hard on you,” she said, shaking with sobs. “Very hard.”

She fell upon his neck, kissing him through her tears.

“Mother,” he said, holding her gently. “Who sent me this lot in life—both good and evil?”

She shook her head. No religion now. Too much pain.

“Mother,” he continued, “I, too, have rebelled. But I strive no longer. Help me, as you helped me when I was a child. When father died, and we had little, you spoke noble, trusting words. I remember them. Say them again. Let us not believe the world has hardened our hearts. If you speak them now, I will feel the pious simplicity of my childhood. I say them to myself—but they come differently from you.”

“I have had many trials,” she sobbed. “But none so sore as this. To see you cast down from your rightful place! I could say it for myself, John—but not for you. Not for you. God has seen fit to be very hard on you.”

She wept uncontrollably—old grief released.

Then silence.

She looked. He sat by the table, arms across it, head bent.

“Oh, John!” she said, lifting his face.

The pallor, the depth of sorrow—she thought for a moment he was dying. But then the rigidity softened. Color returned. He was alive. Alive.

She thanked God for that—for his simple existence, for the fact that he was still hers.

He rose, opened the shutters. Dawn flooded in—ruddy, cold, bleak. The wind blew from the east. The weather was harsh. There would be no demand for summer goods this year. That last hope—of revival—was dead.

Yet it was a comfort to have spoken with her. To know they understood each other, even if they did not agree. They were not in harmony—but they were not enemies.

Fanny’s husband, angered by Thornton’s refusal to join his speculative venture, withdrew any offer of financial aid. The speculator needed cash—and Thornton could not provide it.

So it came to pass: after weeks of dread, Thornton had to surrender the business he had built with honor and sweat. He would seek a subordinate role.

Marlborough Mills and its dwelling were leased long-term. They must be relet. Offers came swiftly.

Mr. Hamper wished to partner with him—his son was being set up with capital in a neighboring town. But the young man was half-educated, wholly self-serving, brutal in pleasure and pain. Thornton declined. He would rather be a manager—where he could retain some control—than submit to a tyrannical partner whose whims he knew he would oppose within months.

So he waited. Humble. Silent.

And as news swept through the Exchange—of the enormous fortune made by his brother-in-law, Mr. Watson—Thornton stood apart. The world admired Watson. Called him wise, bold, visionary. But Thornton felt nothing but a quiet ache.

Success, he knew, had its price. And sometimes, it was paid in the quiet dignity of those who refused to gamble with others' futures.

He watched the tide turn—and waited.

“Bear up, brave heart! we will be calm and strong;
Sure, we can master eyes, or cheek, or tongue,
Nor let the smallest tell-tale sign appear
She ever was, and is, and will be dear.”

It was a sweltering summer evening when Edith entered Margaret’s bedroom—first in her usual attire, then again, fully dressed for dinner. At first, the room was empty. On her second visit, she found Dixon carefully arranging Margaret’s gown upon the bed—but no Margaret herself. Edith lingered, restless, hands fluttering about as if to tidy something that didn’t need tidying.

“Oh, Dixon!” she exclaimed, “not those dreadful blue flowers with that dull gold dress. What taste! Wait just a moment—I’ll fetch you some pomegranate blossoms.”

“It’s not dead gold, ma'am,” Dixon corrected, stiffly. “It’s straw-colour. And blue always goes with straw-colour.”

But before Dixon could finish, Edith had already returned with the vivid scarlet blooms, tossing them down with an air of triumph.

“Where is Miss Hale?” Edith asked, stepping back to assess the effect of the flowers now tucked into the dark hair of the absent woman.

“I can’t say,” she continued, pettishly. “I simply don’t understand how my aunt allowed her to fall into such careless habits in Milton. I’m always expecting news of some dreadful accident—some scrape among those wretched alleys she insists on wandering through. I’d never venture down half those streets without a servant. They’re not fit for ladies.”

Dixon, still smarting from the insult to her sense of fashion, replied curtly: “It’s no wonder, if you ask me, when ladies talk so much about other ladies—and such delicate, dainty ladies at that. I say it’s no wonder there are no saints left on earth.”

“Oh, Margaret! Here you are!” Edith cried, catching sight of her cousin at last. “I’ve been longing for you. But look at your cheeks—how flushed you are, poor thing! Only think what Henry has done—really, he’s gone beyond brother-in-law limits. Just when I’d arranged my party so perfectly—everything fitted just right for Mr. Colthurst—along comes Henry, apologizing, of course, and using your name as an excuse, asking if he may bring Mr. Thornton from Milton—your tenant, you know—who’s in London on some legal business. It ruins my numbers completely.”

“I don’t care about dinner,” Margaret murmured, her voice low. “I don’t want anything. Dixon can bring me tea here. I’ll be in the drawing-room by the time you come up. I’d really like to lie down.”

“No, no—you mustn’t do that,” Edith insisted. “You do look terribly pale, true enough, but it’s only the heat. We simply can’t manage without you. (Lower those flowers a bit, Dixon. They look like flames in your black hair, Margaret.) You remember—we planned for you to talk about Milton to Mr. Colthurst. Oh! Of course—this man is from Milton too. I believe it might turn out quite well after all. Mr. Colthurst can quiz him thoroughly on everything that interests him, and it’ll be delicious fun to see how your experiences compare with Mr. Thornton’s wisdom in his next speech in Parliament. Honestly, I think Henry hit upon a stroke of genius. I asked him if he was a man one would be ashamed of, and he said, ‘Not if you’ve any sense in you, my little sister.’ So I suppose he can sound his h’s—which isn’t common in Darkshire, is it? Eh, Margaret?”

“Did Mr. Lennox mention why Mr. Thornton came to town?” Margaret asked, her tone strained. “Was it legal business related to the property?”

“Oh! He’s failed, or something like that—the very thing Henry told you about the day you had such a headache. What was it again? (There—that’s perfect, Dixon. Miss Hale does us credit, doesn’t she?) I wish I were as tall as a queen and as brown as a gipsy, Margaret.”

“But about Mr. Thornton?” Margaret pressed.

“Oh, law matters—such a dreadful subject for me. Henry will enjoy telling you all about it. The impression he made on me was that Mr. Thornton is in bad straits, but still a respectable man, and that I should be particularly civil to him. Since I didn’t know how, I came to you to ask for help. Now, come downstairs with me and rest on the sofa for a few minutes.”

The privileged brother-in-law arrived early. Margaret, blushing faintly as she spoke, began questioning him about Mr. Thornton.

“He came up regarding the sub-letting of the property—Marlborough Mills, the house, and the premises adjacent. He’s unable to maintain it himself. There are deeds and leases to review, agreements to draft. I hope Edith will receive him properly, though I could see she was slightly annoyed by the liberty I took in securing an invitation for him. But I thought you’d appreciate some attention being paid to him. One would be especially careful to show respect to a man who’s falling from fortune.”

He had spoken quietly, seated beside her, but at the end of his words, he rose abruptly and introduced Mr. Thornton—just as he stepped into the room—to Edith and Captain Lennox.

Margaret watched him closely as he engaged in conversation. More than a year had passed since she had seen him, and time had visibly altered him. His tall, commanding figure still set him apart from ordinary men, lending him an air of distinction through natural grace and ease. Yet his face bore the marks of age and worry. Still, a noble composure rested upon it, a quiet dignity that struck others—especially those who had heard of his decline—as evidence of inner strength and moral fiber.

From the moment he entered, he had noticed her. He saw her absorbed expression as she listened to Mr. Lennox and approached her with the composed manner of a long-standing friend. The moment he spoke, a sudden flush swept across her cheeks—a color that remained throughout the evening.

She did not speak much to him. Her questions were brief, practical—about familiar faces in Milton—what seemed to him merely formal inquiries. But others joined in, more intimate figures within the household, and he slipped into the background, exchanging occasional remarks with Mr. Lennox.

“You think Miss Hale looks well,” said Mr. Lennox, “don’t you? Milton didn’t agree with her, I imagine. When she first came to London, I thought I’d never seen anyone so changed. Tonight, she’s radiant. And stronger too. Last autumn, a two-mile walk tired her out. On Friday evening, we walked to Hampstead and back. Yet Saturday, she looked as fresh as she does now.”

“We?” Who? They alone?

Mr. Colthurst, a sharp-minded rising politician, was keenly observant. He caught a remark Mr. Thornton made during dinner and, curious, turned to Edith. “Who is that gentleman?” he asked.

To her surprise, the tone of his “Indeed!” suggested Mr. Thornton of Milton was not as obscure a name as she had assumed. Her dinner proceeded smoothly. Henry was in high spirits, his dry wit sparkling. Mr. Thornton and Mr. Colthurst discovered shared interests, briefly touched upon them, then saved deeper discussion for after dinner.

Margaret looked exquisite in the pomegranate blossoms. Though she leaned back in her chair and spoke little, Edith was content—conversation flowed effortlessly without her. Margaret, however, kept her gaze fixed on Mr. Thornton. He never looked at her, which allowed her to study him unobserved, noting the changes even this short time had wrought. Only once—when Mr. Lennox made an unexpected joke—did the old spark return: a flash of laughter in his eyes, a hint of the brilliant smile from days past. For a fleeting instant, his glance sought hers, as if craving her recognition. But their eyes met, and instantly, his expression hardened. He became grave, distant, and deliberately avoided looking near her again.

Only two other ladies were present, both engaged in conversation with Edith and her aunt. When the guests moved into the drawing-room, Margaret sat quietly, occupied with her embroidery. Soon, the gentlemen followed, Mr. Colthurst and Mr. Thornton deep in discussion. Mr. Lennox drew near to Margaret and whispered:

“I really think Edith owes me thanks for my contribution to her party. You have no idea how agreeable and sensible your tenant is. He’s been exactly the person Colthurst needed to get the facts he wanted. I can’t imagine how he managed to ruin his affairs.”

“With his talents and opportunities,” Margaret replied, “you would have succeeded.”

He did not entirely like the tone, though the sentiment echoed thoughts he had himself entertained. Silence followed, then they heard a swell in the voices near the fireplace—Mr. Colthurst and Mr. Thornton speaking.

“I assure you,” Colthurst said, “I heard your name mentioned frequently during my brief stay in the area. With great interest—perhaps curiosity about its outcome.”

Then the words faded. When they could hear again, it was Mr. Thornton speaking.

“I have not the qualities for popularity—if they spoke of me that way, they were mistaken. I take time to embrace new ventures, and find it difficult to reveal myself, even to those I wish to know, and with whom I desire no reserve. Yet, despite these shortcomings, I felt I was on the right path. Starting from a kind of friendship with one individual, I began to grow acquainted with many. The benefits were mutual—we were teaching each other, unconsciously and consciously.”

“You say ‘were.’ I trust you intend to continue?”

“I must stop Colthurst,” Henry Lennox interrupted hastily, redirecting the conversation with a clever, timely question to spare Mr. Thornton the embarrassment of admitting his failure and change in status.

But as soon as the new topic ended, Mr. Thornton resumed where he had left off.

“I have failed in business and had to relinquish my position as a mill owner. I am now seeking employment in Milton—any role where I may work under someone willing to let me pursue my own methods in certain matters. I can rely on myself not to impose reckless theories. My only desire is the chance to foster genuine relationships with the workers—beyond mere transactions. But it might be the point Archimedes sought to move the world, given how seriously some manufacturers regard it. They shake their heads and grow solemn whenever I mention even one or two experiments I’d like to try.”

“You call them ‘experiments,’ I notice,” Colthurst remarked, his manner subtly respectful.

“Because I believe them to be such. I am uncertain of the outcomes. But I am certain they ought to be tried. I’ve come to realize that no institution, however wise or meticulously designed, can truly unite classes unless its implementation brings individuals into direct personal contact. Such interaction is life itself. A worker cannot grasp how much effort his employer has spent in his study planning for the workers’ benefit. A plan emerges polished, mechanical, seemingly perfect. But the hands accept it like machinery—without understanding the mental labor behind it. Yet, if I were to introduce an idea requiring personal involvement—messy at first, perhaps—each obstacle would draw more men into interest. Eventually, success would become everyone’s desire, because all had helped shape it. Even then, I know it would lose vitality the moment it ceased to be driven by shared concern, by the instinctive ways people find to meet, to know each other’s characters, quirks, and speech. We would understand each other better—and, I dare say, grow to like each other more.”

“And you think this might prevent strikes?”

“Not at all. My highest hope is only that they cease to be bitter, venomous sources of hatred. A more hopeful man might dream that closer ties could abolish strikes altogether. But I am not a hopeful man.”

Suddenly, as if struck by a new thought, he crossed to where Margaret sat and began, without preamble, as if he knew she had been listening:

“Miss Hale, I received a round-robin from some of my men—I suspect written by Higgins—stating their willingness to work for me again, should I ever be in a position to employ them. That was good, wasn’t it?”

“Yes,” she said softly, lifting her eyes to meet his—clear, steady, full of meaning. “Just right. I’m glad of it.”

She lowered her gaze beneath the weight of his eloquent stare. He looked back at her for a long moment, as if unsure of his own purpose. Then he sighed.

“I knew you would like it,” he said quietly, and turned away. He did not speak to her again until he bid her a formal “good night.”

As Mr. Lennox prepared to leave, Margaret, her face flushing uncontrollably, hesitated, then said:

“Can I speak to you tomorrow? I need your help with—something.”

“Certainly,” he replied, his voice warm. “I’ll come at any time you choose. Nothing would give me greater pleasure than to be of use. Eleven o’clock? Very well.”

His eye lit with quiet exultation. How she was learning to depend on him! It seemed every day brought him closer to certainty—the certainty he had vowed never to seek again without.

“For joy or grief, for hope or fear,
For all hereafter, as for here,
In peace or strife, in storm or shine.”

Edith moved through the house on tiptoe, her voice hushed, checking Sholto whenever he raised his tone. It was as if every sudden sound might shatter the quiet tension that had settled over the drawing-room, where two figures sat in silence behind closed doors. The clock struck two. Still no movement. Then, a swift footstep descended the stairs, and Edith slipped to the doorway, peering cautiously inside.

“Well, Henry?” she asked, her eyes searching his face.

“Well,” he replied, curtly. “Come in to lunch.”

“No, thank you,” he said, turning away. “I’ve already wasted too much time.”

Her brow fell. “Then it’s not settled?”

“No,” he answered flatly. “Not at all. And it never will be, if by ‘it’ you mean what I think you do. That won’t happen, Edith. So stop dreaming about it.”

“But it would be so good for us all,” she pleaded. “I’d feel at ease knowing Margaret was close by. As it is, I’m always afraid she’ll vanish off to Cadiz again.”

“I’ll try to find a wife who understands children,” he said with a hint of weariness. “That’s all I can promise. Miss Hale refuses me. And I won’t press her.”

“Then what did you talk about?” she asked, stepping forward.

“A thousand things you wouldn’t care about—investments, leases, land values.”

“Oh, go away then,” she snapped. “You and she must be terribly dull if you’ve spent all morning on such tedious matters.”

He smiled faintly. “Very well. I’ll return tomorrow—with Mr. Thornton. We’ll have more to discuss with Miss Hale.”

“Mr. Thornton? What does he have to do with this?”

“He’s Miss Hale’s tenant,” Henry said, walking away. “And he wants to give up his lease.”

“Oh,” she said, dismissively. “Well, I don’t understand these details. Don’t bother explaining them to me.”

“The only detail I want you to know,” he said, pausing at the door, “is that we need the back drawing-room undisturbed—just as it was today. With the children and servants coming and going, I can never get anything properly explained. And the plans we have for tomorrow are important.”

No one ever learned why Henry Lennox failed to appear the next day. But Mr. Thornton arrived punctually. After waiting nearly an hour, Margaret entered the room, pale and visibly agitated.

“I’m so sorry Mr. Lennox isn’t here,” she began quickly. “He could have handled this far better than I can. He’s my advisor in such matters.”

“I’m sorry I came if it troubles you,” Thornton said gently. “Shall I go to his chambers and see if I can find him?”

“No, thank you,” she replied. “I wanted to tell you how deeply I regret losing you as a tenant. But Mr. Lennox says things will improve…”

“Mr. Lennox knows little of what I feel,” Thornton interrupted quietly. “Happy and fortunate in everything a man values, he cannot grasp what it means to stand at the edge of life, no longer young, yet forced to begin again—back at the starting point that demands the energy of youth. To feel half a lifetime gone, nothing achieved, nothing left but the bitter memory of wasted chances… Miss Hale, I would rather not hear Mr. Lennox’s opinion on my affairs. Those who are content often trivialize the struggles of others.”

“You’re unjust,” she said softly. “Mr. Lennox only believes there’s great likelihood you’ll recover—perhaps even surpass—what you’ve lost. Please—don’t interrupt me.”

She gathered herself, then hurriedly flipped through legal documents and financial statements, her hands trembling slightly. “Ah! Here it is!” she exclaimed. “He drew up a proposal—I wish he were here to explain it—but it shows that if you took eighteen hundred fifty-seven pounds from my bank account—money currently earning only two and a half percent—you could repay me at a higher interest rate and continue operating Marlborough Mills.”

Her voice had steadied. Thornton remained silent. She searched for another paper—the one detailing security terms—eager to frame the entire matter as a business transaction, one clearly favorable to her.

As she looked, her breath caught. His voice, when it came, was thick, raw with emotion.

“Margaret.”

She glanced up—then lowered her head, pressing her forehead into her hands to hide her face.

He stepped closer. “Margaret!”

Her head sank lower, her face almost touching the table.

He knelt beside her, bringing his face level with her ear, and whispered—almost gasped—his words:

“Take care. If you don’t speak, I shall claim you—some strange, presumptuous way. Send me away now, if I must go. Margaret!”

At the third call, she turned her face toward him, still hidden beneath her small white hands, and rested it against his shoulder, burying her cheek in his coat.

It was too sweet to see—too sweet to bear—to feel her soft skin against his. He held her tightly. They said nothing.

After a long moment, she murmured, brokenly: “Oh, Mr. Thornton, I am not good enough.”

“Not good enough?” he whispered. “Don’t mock my own sense of unworthiness.”

A minute passed. Then, gently, he lifted her hands from her face and placed her arms around him just as she had once done to shield him from the mob.

“Do you remember, love?” he murmured.

“And how I repaid you with my insolence the next day?”

“I remember how wrong I was,” she said. “That’s all.”

“Look here,” he said. “Lift your head. I have something to show you.”

Slowly, she faced him, glowing with tender shame.

“Do you know these roses?” he asked, pulling out his pocketbook and revealing dried flowers carefully preserved within.

“No,” she replied, curious. “Did you give them to me?”

“No,” he said. “Vanity. You didn’t. But you may have worn similar ones.”

She studied them, then smiled faintly. “They’re from Helstone, aren’t they? I recognize the deep indentations on the leaves. Oh—have you been there? When?”

“I went there,” he said, “on my return from Havre. I needed to see the place where Margaret grew into who she is—even at the darkest time, when I had no hope of ever calling her mine.”

“You must give them to me,” she said, reaching for them with gentle urgency.

“Only if you pay me for them,” he teased.

After a while, in delicious silence, she whispered: “How shall I ever tell Aunt Shaw?”

“Let me speak to her,” he said.

“Oh, no,” she murmured. “I owe her so much—but what will she say?”

“I can guess,” he said. “Her first words will be, ‘That man!’”

“Hush,” she whispered, smiling. “Or I’ll try to imitate your mother’s voice saying, ‘That woman!’”
